In this chapter, we covered the following ES6 features:

*   We learned about the Default Parameters in TypeScript, which allowed us to provide the default values to the function parameters.
*   We spoke about the Arrow functions that let us create stateless functions in TypeScript.
*   We talked about object destructuring that will allow us to extract the values of an Array on a JavaScript object into the individual variables.

We are getting closer to Angular. I hope your excitement is on the top. See you in part 3.

# [CHAPTER 4](#toc.xhtml_c04)

# [ES6 Features – Part 3](#toc.xhtml_c04)

# [Introduction](#toc.xhtml_s37a)

In part 3 of the Station 2 journey, we will explore about the Promise in ES6.

# [Promise](#toc.xhtml_s38a)

To understand **`promise`** properly, first, we need to understand **synchronous** and **asynchronous** programming. Let’s do that with the help of some coding.

First, let’s prepare all the ingredients needed for the demos. For all our demos, we will need a function that will run slow. For that purpose, I have created a function **`SlowFunction`** as follows:

1.  `function SlowFunction() {`
2.  `   let ms = 3000;`
3.  `   let start = new Date().getTime();`
4.  `   let end = start;`
5.  `   while (end < start + ms) {`
6.  `     end = new Date().getTime();`
7.  `  }`
8.  `}`

Do not worry much about the preceding code. If you cannot understand the logic, ignore it, and copy it as it is. Just remember that it’s slow.

Now assume that we have the following code:

1.  `function MyFunction(){`
2.  `     SlowFunction();`
3.  `     return 100;`
4.  `}`
5.  *`//some logic 1`*
6.  *`//some logic 2`*
7.  `let returnValue=MyFunction();`*`//some logic 3`*
8.  *`//some logic 4`*
9.  *`//some logic 5`*
10.  `console.log(returnValue);`*`//some logic 6`*
11.  `console.log(returnValue+100);`*`//some logic 7`*

In the preceding code, you can assume that every logic is a unique logic needed in the application.

For the demonstration purpose, let’s use **`console.log`** instead of comments.

1.  `function MyFunction(){`
2.  `     SlowFunction();`
3.  `     return 100;`
4.  `}`
5.  `console.log(“Logic 1”);`*`//some logic 1`*
6.  `console.log(“Logic 2”);`*`//some logic 2`*
7.  `let returnValue=MyFunction();`*`//some logic 3`*
8.  `console.log(“Logic 4”);`*`//some logic 4`*
9.  `console.log(“Logic 5”);`*`//some logic 5`*
10.  `console.log(returnValue);`*`//some logic 6`*
11.  `console.log(returnValue+100);`*`//some logic 7`*

The output will be relatively straightforward.

![](images/Image43702.jpg)

**Figure 4.1**

Upon execution, you will find out that after logic 2, there will be a 3-seconds delay, and then logic 4 will get displayed.

We are ready with all the boilerplate code. Let’s do our very first demo.

**Demo 1: Need for callback**

If you carefully examine the code, you can easily conclude that **logic 6** and **logic 7** is dependent on **logic 3**, whereas **logic 4** and **logic 5** are entirely independent of **logic 3**.

![](images/Image43754.jpg)

**Figure 4.2**

So, the question is, should we wait for 3 seconds after logic 2 to get the result of logic 4 and logic 5?

The answer is No. We should not.

It can be accomplished by making the code inside **`MyFunction`** execute asynchronously, and for that we will use the JavaScript’s **`setTimeout`** function. **`setTimeout`** queues the code to run once the current call stack is finished executing. It merely breaks the normal synchronous flow.

1.  `function MyFunction(){`
2.  `     setTimeout(() => {`
3.  `         SlowFunction();`
4.  `    }, 1);`
5.  `}`

Now, the question is how to return the value from the **`MyFunction`** function?

That can be accomplished using the **callback** functions.

The callback functions are an old and proven methodology to deal with asynchronous programming.

The following is the final code.

1.  *`//Demo - Callback into action`*
2.  `function MyFunction(callbackFn){`
3.  `     setTimeout(() => {`
4.  `         SlowFunction();`
5.  `         callbackFn(100);    `
6.  `    }, 1);`
7.  `}`
8.  `console.log(“Logic 1”);`*`//some logic 1`*
9.  `console.log(“Logic 2”);`*`//some logic 2`*

11.  `MyFunction(returnValue=>{`
12.  `     console.log(returnValue);`*`//some logic 6`*
13.  `     console.log(returnValue+100);`*`//some logic 7`*
14.  `});`*`//some logic 3`*

16.  `console.log(“Logic 4”);`*`//some logic 4`*
17.  `console.log(“Logic 5”);`*`//some logic 5`*

Let’s understand the flow:

*   **logic 1** runs, and the message is displayed on the screen.
*   **logic 2** runs, and the message is displayed on the screen.
*   **logic 3** runs, and it will make a call to **`MyFunction`**.
*   In **`MyFunction`**, **`setTimeout`** is used, which will queue the inside code to run only after the current call stack completes execution. In short, in this case, **`MyFunction`** executes and quickly finishes its execution.
*   **logic 4** runs, and the message is displayed on the screen.
*   **logic 5** runs, and the message is displayed on the screen.
*   This makes the call stack empty. All instructions in the current call stack are executed. So, now the queued code starts running. It means that after 3 seconds of waiting, the callback function is invoked.
*   **logic 6** runs, and the message is displayed on the screen.
*   **logic 7** runs, and the message is displayed on the screen.

As you can see, now there is no need to wait for 3 seconds to get **logic 4** and **logic 5**.

So, that is why we need the callback functions.

![](images/Image43792.jpg)

**Figure 4.3**

**Demo 2: Exceptions**

Now, let’s say that **`MyFunction`** expects an even number as an input parameter. An odd number will be considered as an invalid input. Let’s implement the changes:

1.  `function MyFunction(inputValue,callbackFn){`
2.  `     if(inputValue%2==0){`
3.  `         setTimeout(() => {`
4.  `                 SlowFunction();`
5.  `                 callbackFn(100);`
6.  `        }, 1);`
7.  `    }    `
8.  `     else{`
9.  `         return “Invalid Input”;`
10.  `    }`
11.  `}`

13.  `let fnReturnValue=MyFunction(100,returnValue=>{`
14.  `     console.log(returnValue);//some logic 6`
15.  `     console.log(returnValue+100);//some logic 7`
16.  `});`
17.  `if(fnReturnValue!=undefined){`
18.  `     console.log(fnReturnValue);`
19.  `}`

Unlike the static languages, it’s not mandatory to return the value from every part of the function in JavaScript. **`MyFunction`** has a simple **`if…else`** block, and in the **`if`** block, we are returning nothing, so the function **`ReturnValue`** will have the value as undefined, whereas, in the **`else`** block, we are returning a string value.

Although it works, it is not a clean way of coding. There is another approach, and that will be using two callback functions – one for success and one for failure.

1.  `function MyFunction(inputValue,callbackFn,errorCallbackFn){`
2.  `     if(inputValue%2==0){`
3.  `         setTimeout(() => {`
4.  `                 SlowFunction();`
5.  `                 callbackFn(100);`
6.  `        }, 1);`
7.  `    }    `
8.  `     else{`
9.  `         errorCallbackFn(“Invalid Input”);`
10.  `    }`
11.  `}`

13.  `MyFunction(100,returnValue=>{`
14.  `     console.log(returnValue);//some logic 6`
15.  `     console.log(returnValue+100);//some logic 7`
16.  `},error=>{`
17.  `     console.log(fnReturnValue);`
18.  `});`

As you can see, we are passing two functions as an argument to **`MyFunction`**. The first one represents success, and the second one represents failure. It is a much cleaner approach than before.

**Drawbacks of the preceding approach**

*   There is no standard industry practice defining which function is a success and which is a failure. For instance, in our case, a success callback is expected before a failure callback. You may find a different sequence in some other place.
*   Let’s say the developers who created **`MyFunction`** make a logical mistake. They make a call to the **`errorCallback`** function immediately after the callback function.

1.  `setTimeout(() => {`
2.  `                 SlowFunction();`
3.  `                 callbackFn(100);`
4.  `                 errorCallbackFn(“Invalid Input”);`
5.  `        }, 1);`

What will happen? Both will execute nothing else.

Is it logical? The answer is No.

We can have other similar scenarios, too, such as invoking the callback function after the **`errorCallback`** function or invoking the **`errorCallback`** function more than once or invoking the callback function more than once.

All these four points ultimately make no sense when we talk in terms of success and failure. Logically, the success and failure won’t be possible simultaneously, and secondly, both the success and f the ailure should happen only once. Even if we invoke any of them multiple times, it should execute only once.

**Demo 3: Callback Hell**

Let’s assume that we want to call multiple asynchronous methods one after the other. I mean, invoke one, and once that is over, make the call to another.

For the demo purpose, let’s try out with only three methods.

1.  `function MyFunction(inputValue,callbackFn){`
2.  *`//some code`*
3.  `     callbackFn(100);`
4.  `}`
5.  `function MyFunction2(inputValue,callbackFn){`
6.  *`//some code`*
7.  `     callbackFn(100);`
8.  `}`
9.  `function MyFunction3(inputValue,callbackFn){`
10.  *`//some code`*
11.  `     callbackFn(100);`
12.  `}`

14.  `MyFunction(100,returnValue=>{`
15.  *`//Some Logic`*
16.  *`//Some Logic`*
17.  `     MyFunction2(200,returnValue2=>{`
18.  *`//Some Logic`*
19.  *`//Some Logic`*
20.  `         MyFunction2(200,returnValue2=>{`
21.  *`//Some Logic`*
22.  *`//Some Logic`*
23.  `        });`
24.  `    })`
25.  `});`

The preceding phenomenon is called the **`callback hell`**.

The code becomes more and more complex and unreadable as more and more asynchronous methods are invoked.

![](images/Image43848.jpg)

**Figure 4.4**

**Demo 4: Promise**

In Demo 1, we understood the importance of *callback*. We realized how much it is needed. Then, in Demo 2 and Demo 3, we spoke about its limitations.

Let’s revise those limitations:

1.  There is no standard way to identify success and failure.
2.  Callback leads to the callback hell when the number of asynchronous methods is more.

A *promise* is a solution to these problems. It gives us the standard interface to deal with the asynchronous methods.

Let’s do the demo and understand the same.

1.  `Function MyFunction(inputValue){`
2.  `     return new Promise((resolve,reject)=>{`
3.  `         if(inputValue%2==0){`
4.  `             setTimeout(() => {`
5.  `                     SlowFunction();`
6.  `resolve(100);` *`//success`*
7.  `            }, 1);`
8.  `        }    `
9.  `         else{`
10.  `reject(“Invalid Input”);` *`//fail`*
11.  `        }`
12.  `    });`
13.  `}`

In the preceding code, we created an instance of promise passing a callback as an argument with two parameters. The first represents success and the second failure. **`resolve`** and **`reject`** are just the parameter names; it can be anything. The best thing is, the first one is success and the second one is failure.

Let’s look at the invoking code:

1.  `MyFunction(12).then(result=>{`
2.  `     console.log(“Result is”,result);`
3.  `},result=>{`
4.  `     console.log(“Error is”,result);`
5.  `});`

As you know, **`MyFunction`** is returning the **`Promise`** object. The **`Promise`** object gives us a method called then, which expects two callback functions. The first one will be a success, and the second will be a failure.

![](images/Image43905.jpg)

**Figure 4.5**

Execute it using the node to check the output.

![](images/Image43947.jpg)

**Figure 4.6**

Let’s also test it for the error.

1.  `MyFunction(11).then(result=>{`
2.  `     console.log(“Result is”,result);`
3.  `},result=>{`
4.  `     console.log(“Error is”,result);`
5.  `});`

The output will be as follows:

![](images/Image43985.jpg)

**Figure 4.7**

**Success and Failure**

Now we have a standard way to represent the success and failure. The first callback is a success, and the second is a failure.

**Is it success and failure?**

Change **`MyFunction`** as follows:

1.  `function MyFunction(inputValue){`
2.  `     return new Promise((resolve,reject)=>{`
3.  `         if(inputValue%2==0){`
4.  `             setTimeout(() => {`
5.  `                     SlowFunction();`
6.  `                     resolve(100);`
7.  `                     reject(“Invalid Input”);`
8.  `            }, 1);`
9.  `        }`
10.  `         else{`
11.  `             reject(“Invalid Input”);`
12.  `        }`
13.  `    });`
14.  `}`

Now, test the code:

1.  `MyFunction(12).then(result=>{`
2.  `     console.log(“Result is”,result);`
3.  `},result=>{`
4.  `     console.log(“Error is”,result);`
5.  `});`

Execute and check the output.

![](images/Image44030.jpg)

**Figure 4.8**

As you can see, **`reject`** ignored as **`resolve`** is invoked before that. It means once it succeeds, there is no failure.

*   I also recommend you try invoking resolve after reject. It will prove there is no failure after success.
*   Try invoking resolve multiple times, which will confirm that it cannot succeed again once you succeed.
*   Finally, try to reject numerous times. Every time you will see only one getting executed, which will prove that it cannot fail again once it’s failed.

So, with promise, we do have real success and failure here.

**What about the callback hell?**

The Promise solves that issue as well. Check the following code:

1.  `function MyFunction(input){`
2.  `     return new Promise((resolve,reject)=>{`
3.  *`//some logic`*
4.  `         resolve(input+1);`
5.  `    });`
6.  `}`
7.  `function MyFunction2(input){`
8.  `     return new Promise((resolve,reject)=>{`
9.  *`//some logic`*
10.  `         resolve(input+2);`
11.  `    });`
12.  `}`
13.  `function MyFunction3(input){`
14.  `     return new Promise((resolve,reject)=>{`
15.  *`//some logic`*
16.  `         resolve(input+3);`
17.  `    });`
18.  `}`

20.  `MyFunction(100).then(result=>{`
21.  *`//some logic`*
22.  `     return MyFunction2(result);`
23.  `}).then(result=>{`
24.  `     return MyFunction3(result);`
25.  `}).then(result=>{`
26.  `     console.log(result);`
27.  `});`

The code is simpler and more readable now.

![](images/Image44077.jpg)

**Figure 4.9**

# [Summary](#toc.xhtml_s39a)

In this chapter, we learned about Promises:

*   We learned why the promise is considered a better replacement for callbacks.
*   We learned that the ES6 promise provides a standard way to represent the success and failure.
*   We learned that a promise could be resolved or rejected, and only once.
*   We learned that a Promise also solves the problem of a callback hell.

See you in the next and final chapter of the ES6 part, which will take you to Station 2.

# [CHAPTER 5](#toc.xhtml_c05)

# [ES6 Features – Part 4](#toc.xhtml_c05)

# [Introduction](#toc.xhtml_s40a)

By the end of this chapter, you will reach Station 2, and your journey to the ES6 feature ends with that. This chapter is all about Modules in ES6.

# [JavaScript Modules](#toc.xhtml_s41a)

**What are the Modules?**

Almost every technology supports the modules somehow. What is a module? Well, I will say, a module represents a small piece of an entire application.

**Why do we need Modules?**

For now, let’s not worry about how to create modules. First, let’s focus on how much a modular development is essential.

Here are the advantages of the modules:

*   Modular development
*   Isolated coding
*   Dependency management

![](images/Image44118.jpg)

**Figure 5.1**

**1: Modular development**

Breaking the applications into multiple modules makes it more manageable and readable.

**2: Isolated coding**

Every code that we write belongs to some module, and will be unaffected by every other code belonging to some other module. The code written inside each module will be private by default to that module. It won’t be accessible outside (to the other modules). In case we want to make something available, it should be done explicitly.

![](images/Image44166.jpg)

**Figure 5.2**

So, a developer working on one module won’t be worried about the code written in another module. He/she won’t be worried that he/she will replace/overrides someone else’s functionality, or someone else will replace/override his/her functionality.

**3: Dependency management**

Let’s say we are developing an application, and our app needs a reusable module (module1) for some purpose. Now, let’s say module1 is dependent on two other modules (module2 and module3). Finally, module2 is dependent on module4.

(In this case, the word module is used only for the explanation purposes. It merely means some library.)

**Regular libraries**

Let’s consider that module1, module2, module3, and module4 are regular libraries. I mean, they are not created in a modular fashion. To make module1 work properly, we must make sure that all the other dependent modules, that is, module2, module3, and module4 are also included in the application. In short, we need to know the entire dependency hierarchy. It is not the only challenge here. We also need to make sure that the modules are included in a proper order. In this case, we should have the module4 first and then the module2 and module3, and finally the module1.

![](images/Image44200.jpg)

**Figure 5.3**

**Modular libraries**

Now, the good news is that it would be the case with regular libraries. If all those modules are developed in a modular fashion, then we will follow a different approach.

In our application, we will only reference module1, and everything else will be indirectly referenced.

That’s the second advantage of using the modules.

**How to create Modules in JavaScript?**

Next, let’s learn how to create modules in JavaScript.

It depends on your runtime environment:

1.  With the Node runtime.
2.  With the browser runtime and the ES6 Modules.
3.  With the browser runtime and without the ES6 Modules.

Let’s see all the three areas one by one.

**With Node Runtime**

If you use Node.js runtime to execute your JavaScript, every file becomes one module. By default, every function and variable declared in one file will be hidden from another file. We must explicitly make things visible to the outside world.

Let’s do a demo.

Create a JavaScript file **`lib1.js`** and **`lib2.js`** as follows:

1.  *`//lib1.js`*
2.  `function getString(){`
3.  `     return getString2();`
4.  `}`
5.  `function getString2(){`
6.  `     return “Just Compile”;`
7.  `}`

It will create a Node.js module called **`lib1`** automatically for us. Both the methods inside the **`lib1`** module will be private and won’t be accessible outside.

1.  *`//lib2.js`*
2.  `function getString(){`
3.  `     return getString2();`
4.  `}`

6.  `function getString2(){`
7.  `     return “Train IT”;`
8.  `}`

It will create a Node.js module called **`lib2`** automatically for us. Both the methods inside the **`lib2`** module will be private, and won’t be accessible outside.

As you can see, both the files contain the functions with the same name, **`getString`**, and **`getString2`**.

Next, create one more JavaScript file, **`test.js`**, with the simple code as follows:

1.  *`//test.js`*
2.  `console.log(getString());`

It will create a new module called **`test`**.

Execute the **`test.js`** using the node command.

![](images/Image44245.jpg)

**Figure 5.4**

As you can see, we got a runtime error as the test module is unaware of the **`getString`** method.

To resolve this error, we have to make the methods visible to the outside world explicitly. It can be done using the **`exports`** keyword.

Simply add the following line at the end of both **`lib1.js`** and **`lib2.js`**.

1.  *`//lib2.js and lib1.js`*
2.  `exports.myFunction=getString;`

With the preceding code, we made the **`getString`** method public, and now it will be accessible to the outside world.

In **`test.js`**, to access the stuff exported by the other modules, we need to import it first, and for that, we use the required keyword.

Change the **`test.js`** contents to the following:

1.  `let lib1=require(‘./lib1’);`
2.  `let lib2=require(‘./lib2’);`

4.  `console.log(lib1.myFunction());`
5.  `console.log(lib2.myFunction());`

As you can see, we are importing both the **`lib1`** and the **`lib2`** modules separately, and the local variables **`lib1`** and **`lib2`** will give us the access to the function exposed inside the respective modules.

Execute it once again using the node command.

![](images/Image44298.jpg)

**Figure 5.5**

From the preceding demo, we can conclude the following points:

1.  The modules break the application into multiple smaller chunks, each encapsulating a particular functionality and thus achieving abstraction.
2.  The modules allow us to do Isolated coding.

Let’s do one more demo.

Create three more JavaScript files, and call it **`lib3.js`**, **`lib4.js`**, and **`test.js`**. Put the following contents inside it:

1.  *`//lib3`*
2.  `let lib4=require(“./lib4”);`
3.  `function getString(){`
4.  `     return lib4.getString();`
5.  `}`
6.  `exports.getString=getString;`

1.  *`//lib4`*
2.  `function getString(){`
3.  `     return “Sukesh Marla”;`
4.  `}`
5.  `exports.getString=getString;`

1.  `let lib3=require(‘./lib3’);`
2.  `console.log(lib3.getString());`

As you can see, **`test2.js`** is only referring to **`lib3.js`**; it is completely unaware of **`lib4.js`**.

Execute **`test2.js`** using the node command to get the following output:

This second demo proves that with the modules, we are not required to remember the entire dependency tree. The module system will automatically take care of it.

![](images/Image44346.jpg)

**Figure 5.6**

Both of the preceding demos together proved all the three advantages of Modules – Modular development, Isolated coding, and Dependency management.

**With browser runtime and without ES5 Modules**

With the previous lab, we learned to create the Modules in the Node.js environment. Next, let’s learn how to get it done in the browser runtime.

Unfortunately, the browser runtime doesn’t support the built-in JavaScript modules. (At least not before ES6 is introduced. We will talk about the ES6 modules in the next section.)

I know the developer inside you won’t accept it unless it’s proved with the demo. Let’s do it.

Create three JavaScript files, **`lib1.js`**, **`lib2.js`**, **`test.js`**, and **`index.html`** as follows:

1.  *`// lib1.js`*
2.  `function getLib1Message(){`
3.  `     return getMessage();`
4.  `}`
5.  `function getMessage(){`
6.  `     return “Just Compile”;`
7.  `}`

1.  *`// lib2.js`*
2.  `function getLib2Message(){`
3.  `     return getLib1Message() +” & “+ getMessage();`
4.  `}`
5.  `function getMessage(){`
6.  `     return “Train IT”;`
7.  `}`

1.  *`//test.js`*
2.  `alert(getLib2Message());`

1.  `<html>`
2.  `<head>`
3.  `     <title>`
4.  `         JavaScript Module Demo`
5.  `     </title>`
6.  `     <script src=lib1.js></script>`
7.  `     <script src=”lib2.js”></script>`
8.  `     <script src=”test.js”></script>`
9.  `</head>`
10.  `</html>`

We need a development purpose server where we can host the preceding HTML and JavaScript files.

For that, we will be using **`lite-server`**. Open the Command Prompt, and execute the following command:

1.  `npm install lite-server -g`

After that, execute the **`lite-server`** command in the folder where the preceding files are saved.

![](images/Image44433.jpg)

**Figure 5.7**

It will automatically open the browser, requesting to the **`http://localhost:3000`** file.

Sometimes the browser may not start automatically. In that case, please do it manually.

![](images/Image44481.jpg)

**Figure 5.8**

Aren’t you expecting the output “*Just Compile & Train IT*”?

In the preceding case, **`lib2.js`** replaced the **`getMessage`** method in **`lib1.js`**.

**lib1.js and lib2.js are not modules; hence they affected each other when included in the HTML file.**

We don’t have the default support modules in JavaScript (before ES6), but we can bring in using the **Module Loaders** and **Module Formatters**.

***Note:*** **This entire series is about Angular. We are discussing and covering all these terminologies so that your basics get stronger. The Module Loaders and Module Formatters are itself a vast topic. Please don’t get into the depth of it for now.**

**Module Formatters**

It’s the standard way of writing code. *AMD*, *CommonJS*, *UMD* are some examples of the Module Formatters. For our demo, we will be using the AMD format.

So, as the first step, let’s rewrite all the three JavaScript files in the AMD format.

Let’s start with **`lib1.js`**.

When it comes to writing the code in an AMD format, we must invoke the define function.

It accepts two parameters; first of type array, and second a function.

1.  *`//lib1.js`*
2.  `define([], function() {`
3.  `});`

I firmly believe that the first thing which must have caught your curiosity was the define function. You must be asking yourself, “*What is this define function? I never saw it before in JavaScript.*”

Keep that thought aside for now and focus on the demo. You will eventually understand it.

Next, write every code of yours inside that anonymous function.

1.  *`//lib1.js`*
2.  `define([], function() {`
3.  `     function getLib1Message(){`
4.  `         return getMessage();`
5.  `    }`
6.  `     function getMessage(){`
7.  `         return “Just Compile”;`
8.  `    }`
9.  `});`

With the preceding code, we created a JavaScript module called **`lib1`**.

Secondly, as both **`getLib1Message`** and **`getMessage`** are defined inside another function, they become private to that function, and there is no way that someone overrides it, or it overrides someone else.

But if you remember our previous demo, **`getLib1Message`** is invoked by another module **`lib2`**. That means we have to make **`getLib1Message`** public.

For that, we will use the exports module. In your **`lib1`** module, you will get access to the exports module. That’s where the first parameter in the define function comes into the picture. Look at the following code:

1.  *`//lib1.js`*
2.  `define([‘exports’],`
3.  `function(e) {`
4.  `     function getLib1Message(){`
5.  `         return getMessage();`
6.  `    }`
7.  `     function getMessage(){`
8.  `         return “Just Compile”;`
9.  `    }`
10.  `     e.getMessage=getLib1Message;`
11.  `});`

“*Earlier define, now exports, what’s going on here*.”

Don’t worry. Just relax and continue with the demo.

Let’s analyze the preceding code.

*   That array specifies the other modules on which your module is dependent.
*   In the preceding code, exports is a module name. Specifying it in an array indicates that our module depends on it. Parameter **`e`** in the function holds the reference to the exports module.
*   The preceding code indicates that **`getLib1Message`** will be available to the other modules as **`getMessage`**.

Now that we are done with **`lib1.js`**, let’s proceed with **`lib2.js`**.

1.  *`// lib2.js`*
2.  `define([‘exports’,”./lib1.js”],`
3.  `function(e,lib1) {`
4.  `     function getLib2Message(){`
5.  `         return lib1.getMessage() +” & “+ getMessage();`
6.  `    }`
7.  `     function getMessage(){`
8.  `         return “Train IT”;`
9.  `    }`
10.  `     e.getMessage=getLib2Message;`
11.  `});`

As you can see, this time, we have not only specified the exports but also **`./lib1.js`** inside the array. With the exports, we make **`getLib2Message`** available in another module, and with **`lib1`**, we get access to the **`getMessage`** method of **`lib1.js`**.

Next, lets modify **`test.js`**.

1.  `define([`
2.  `     ‘./lib2.js’,`
3.  `], function(lib2) {`
4.  `     alert(lib2.getMessage());`
5.  `});`

In **`test.js`**, we only have **`./lib2.js`** as a dependency.

**Module loaders**

The module loaders are the third party libraries that make the browser understand that the Module **`Formatters.requireJS`** and **`systemJS`** are some examples of the module loaders.

**In simple words, download the module loader, include them in the application, and only after that the browser will start recognizing the define and exports keywords.**

So, let’s download the module loader using **`npm`**.

1.  `npm install requirejs`

In the preceding command, download the **`requireJS`** module loader, and keep it in the **`node_modules`** folder.

Now that we have everything ready, let’s do our final change. Let’s change **`index.html`**.

1.  `<html>`
2.  `<head>`
3.  `     <title>`
4.  `         JavaScript Module Demo`
5.  `     </title>`
6.  `     <script data-main = “test.js” src = “\node_modules\requirejs\require.js”></script>`
7.  `</head>`
8.  `</html>`

As you can see, we have only included **`require.js`** in our HTML file, and we have attached a special attribute to our script tag **`data-main`** which is set to **`test.js`**. That is how we specify the startup file in case of **`require.js`**.

Now, as you know, **`test.js`** depends on **`lib1.js`**, which is dependent on **`lib2.js`**. Hence, both of these will be loaded dynamically into the HTML file by the Module Loader.

Execute the **`lite-server`** command in the folder where all the preceding files are kept and start the server.

This time we will get the correct output.

![](images/Image44490.jpg)

**Figure 5.9**

![](images/Image44587.jpg)

**Figure 5.10**

It was a demo using AMD as a Module Formatter and **`requireJS`** as a Module Loader. You can try some other combination as well. The purpose of this demo was to realize the importance and fundamentals of the JavaScript modules.

**With browser runtime and with ES6 Modules**

So far, we have learned that Node.js runtime, by default, has support for the modules, and the Browser runtime doesn’t.

The good news is, unlike ES5, ES6 has the support for the JavaScript Modules.

So, if your browser is compatible with the ES6 JavaScript Module, you can get out of the complexity of the Module Formatters and the Module Loaders. You can follow the standard syntax of the ES6 module.

Already the modern browsers started supporting the ES6 JavaScript Modules.

For our demo, we will be using Google Chrome, which has the complete support for it.

So, without wasting time, let’s start creating **`lib1.js`**, **`lib2.js`**, and **`test.js`** just like before.

1.  *`//lib1.js`*
2.  `export function getLib1Message(){`
3.  `     return getMessage();`
4.  `}`
5.  `function getMessage(){`
6.  `     return “Just Compile”;`
7.  `}`

Did you notice the export keyword attached to the **`getLib1Message`** method? It means **`lib1.js`** is a JavaScript module with two methods, out of which **`getLib1Message`** will be public, and **`getMessage`** will be private.

1.  *`// lib2.js`*
2.  `import {getLib1Message} from “./lib1.js”`
3.  `export function getLib2Message(){`
4.  `     return getLib1Message() +” & “+ getMessage();`
5.  `}`
6.  `function getMessage(){`
7.  `     return “Train IT”;`
8.  `}`

Look at the **`import`** statement. I believe that is self-explanatory.

We will dig more into the export and import after this demo. For now, let’s get our initial output.

1.  *`//test.js`*
2.  `import {getLib2Message} from “./lib2.js”`
3.  `     alert(getLib2Message());`

The code is much simpler and readable.

One last thing, lets create **`index.html`** file.

1.  `<html>`
2.  `<head>`
3.  `     <title>`
4.  `         JavaScript Module Demo`
5.  `     </title>`
6.  `     <script type=”module” src = “test.js”></script>`
7.  `</head>`
8.  `</html>`

Did you notice, there is no Module Loader involved? We don’t need it. It’s ES6 and it’s a standard syntax. Also, you can see a special attribute value module to type attribute. That makes JavaScript understand that **`test.js`** is a JavaScript Module.

Now it’s time for execution. Execute the **`lite-server`** command in the folder where the preceding files are kept and start the server. Here is the output:

![](images/Image44600.jpg)

**Figure 5.11**

Isn’t it cool? It is, without any doubt.

Before we wrap this chapter, let’s learn a little more about these ES6 Modules.

# [More on ES6 Modules](#toc.xhtml_s42a)

**Can we use** the **ES6 Modules with Node.js?**

With the Node.js version 13 or higher, we can use the ES6 module syntax without any problem.

**Can we export multiple things from one ES6 module?**

Yes, using the same syntax, we can do that.

1.  *`//module1.js`*
2.  `export function a(){`
3.  `     …`
4.  `}`
5.  `export function b(){`
6.  `     …`
7.  `}`
8.  `function c(){`
9.  `     …`
10.  `}`
11.  *`//module2.js`*
12.  `import {a,b} from “./module1.js”`

**Can we export variables?**

Yes, check the following syntax:

1.  *`//module1.js`*
2.  `export const ABCD=10;`
3.  *`//module2.js`*
4.  `import {ABCD} from “./module1.js”`

**What if the imported functionality already exists?**

We can put an alias while importing. Check the following syntax:

1.  *`//module1.js`*
2.  `export function a(){`
3.  `     …`
4.  `}`
5.  *`//module2.js`*
6.  `import {a as mya} from “./module1.js”`
7.  `function a(){`
8.  `     mya();`
9.  `}`

**What if we want to import everything exported by a Module but don’t want to specify the import statement for each exported thing explicitly?**

You can use “*” as follows:

1.  *`//module1.js`*
2.  `export function a(){`
3.  `     …`
4.  `}`
5.  `export function b(){`
6.  `     …`
7.  `}`
8.  `function c(){`
9.  `     …`
10.  `}`

12.  *`//module2.js`*
13.  `import * as lib2 from “./lib2.js”`
14.  `console.log(lib2.a());`
15.  `console.log(lib2.b());`

**Is there any other way to export?**

The export syntax used in the preceding demos is called the Named export.

There is something called the Default export. Check the following code:

1.  *`//module1.js`*
2.  `export default function a(){`
3.  `     …`
4.  `}`
5.  `export function b(){`
6.  `     …`
7.  `}`

9.  *`// module2.js`*
10.  `import xyz,{b} from “./module1.js”`
11.  `function m1(){`
12.  `     return xyz();`
13.  `}`
14.  `function m2(){`
15.  `     return b();`
16.  `}`

In the preceding code, **`module1`** is having both the named export (**`function b`**) and the default export (**`function a`**).

*   The Named exports must be specified inside {and}, whereas the default exports without it.
*   Only one default export is possible per module.
*   We can use an alias with the default exports. In the preceding code, the function is imported as the function xyz.

# [Summary](#toc.xhtml_s43a)

Finally, we have completed our Station 2 journey and explored the ES6 features. Don’t worry if anything got confusing or complicated. Once we start with Angular, you will see most of them in action again, and then things will become much simpler. Let’s revise what we have learned in this chapter:

*   We spoke about the advantage of Modules – Modular development, Isolated coding, and dependency management.
*   Modular development means breaking the application into multiple smaller modules, thus improving the code maintenance and readability.
*   Isolated coding means every developer writes the code independent of what the other developers are writing. The code written inside one module will be private to that particular module, unless it is explicitly made public. So, there is no chance of one developer overriding the functionality written by another developer.
*   Dependency management means application dependencies will be managed easily. We are not required to know the entire dependency graph.
*   We learned to create the JavaScript modules for the Node.js runtime.
*   We learned to create the JavaScript modules for the browser runtime without ES6.
*   We learned to create the JavaScript modules for the browser runtime with ES6.
*   We also discussed the default export and named export in the ES6 Modules

# [Section 2](#toc.xhtml_c05b)

# [Summary](#toc.xhtml_c05b)

[Section 2](#p02.xhtml) was all about ES6. ES6 is the new standard of JavaScript. Learning ES6 makes the foundation more robust, and TypeScript becomes easier too.

It had a total of four chapters, starting with *[Chapter 2: ES6 Features Part 1](#c02.xhtml)* and ending with *[Chapter 5: ES6 Features Part 4](#c05.xhtml)*. In this journey, we explored the various features of ES6.

**Chapter 2**

*   We learned that learning ES6 is not very different from learning JS. It is the same language. We will only be learning new features.
*   We spoke about the new **`let`** and **`const`** keywords that let us declare the block-level variables in JavaScript, which never hoist up.
*   We saw a demonstration of the object-oriented programming support in JavaScript.
*   We learned how the String Interpolation makes life easy when it comes to the concatenation of strings.
*   We saw the enhanced object literal syntax in JavaScript that makes object creation easy when the object’s property name matches the variable name.
*   We spoke about the Spread Operator that let us spread objects and arrays.
*   We explored the new mighty **`for…of`** loop.
*   We ended this chapter with a demo on the REST parameters that can be an alternative for arguments in the previous JavaScript version.

**Chapter 3**

*   We learned about the Default Parameters in TypeScript, which allowed us to provide default values to the function parameters.
*   We spoke about the Arrow functions, which let us create the stateless functions in TypeScript.
*   We talked about object de-structuring that will allow us to extract the values of an Array on a JavaScript object into the individual variables.

**Chapter 4**

*   We learned that ES6 promise provides a standard way to represent success and failure.
*   We learned that a promise could be resolved or rejected, and only once.
*   We learned that a Promise also solves the problem of a callback hell.

**Chapter 5**

*   We spoke about the advantage of Modules – Modular development, Isolated coding, and dependency management.
*   Modular development means breaking the application into multiple smaller modules, thus improving the code maintenance and readability.
*   Isolated coding means every developer writes the code independent of what the other developers are writing. The code written inside one module will be private to that particular module, unless it is explicitly made public. So, there is no chance of one developer overriding the functionality written by another developer.
*   Dependency management means application dependencies will be managed easily. We are not required to know the entire dependency graph.
*   We learned to create the JavaScript modules for the Node.js runtime.
*   We learned to create the JavaScript modules for the browser runtime without ES6.
*   We learned to create the JavaScript modules for the browser runtime with ES6.
*   We also discussed the default export and named export in the ES6 Modules

# [Section 3](#toc.xhtml_p03)

# [CHAPTER 6](#toc.xhtml_c06)

# [Webpack](#toc.xhtml_c06)

# [Introduction](#toc.xhtml_s44a)

My dear Angular lovers, I believe you have enjoyed your journey to Station 2 so far.

I 100% guarantee that by now you have the basic idea behind **Node.js**, **npm**, and have an excellent notion behind ES2015, also known as ES6.

In this chapter, we will discuss the fundamentals of **webpack**. This article will take you to Station 3 from Station 2, and when you reach there, I promise your understanding of the **webpack** will be upgraded.

# [Module Bundlers](#toc.xhtml_s45a)

In the previous chapter, we spoke about **module loaders**. The module loaders load modules dynamically at run-time on demand.

This on-demand feature comes with its own issue. Imagine a situation where our application comprises hundreds of modules, and they are getting loaded dynamically. It will impact the performance of the application.

A module loader is the right solution for a traditional application. For SPA, where a large amount of JavaScript code will be written across multiple files, we need a better solution, and that solution is the Module Bundler.

A Module Bundler is a node module that takes a JavaScript file as the input, and then identifies all the other JavaScript files that the input file is dependent on (directly or indirectly), and finally merges all the files and generates one single JavaScript file. It joins them in such a way that logic inside those JavaScript files won’t get affected; that one generated bundle file will be included in the HTML file.

![](images/Image44714.jpg)

**Figure 6.1**

# [Webpack](#toc.xhtml_s46a)

There are a lot of bundlers available in the community. **webpack** is one of the most famous bundlers in the market. It is also the default Module Bundler included in the Angular application.

Hence, it is essential to know and learn the basics about webpack before getting into Angular.

We will do three demos here:

1.  Using the Node.js as run-time.
2.  Using the browser as run-time with the AMD modules.
3.  Using the browser as run-time with the ES6 modules.

Before we start with the first demo, let’s complete the installation first.

To get started with our bundling process, we will need two node modules, **`webpack`**, and **`webpack-cli`**, both of which are node modules, and can be downloaded with **`npm`**.

1.  `npm install webpack -g`

1.  `npm install webpack-cli -g`

As you can see, both the node modules are installed at the global level using the **`-g`** flag. In case you miss the **`-g`** flag, it will get installed locally, and then you will need the **`npm`** scripts to use them.

Now that we are done with our installation, let’s start with our demos.

# [Using Node.js as run-time](#toc.xhtml_s47a)

Create three JavaScript files **`lib1.js`**, **`lib2.js`**, and **`test.js`** with the following code:

1.  *`//lib1.js`*
2.  `function getLib1Message(){`
3.  `     return getMessage();`
4.  `}`
5.  `function getMessage(){`
6.  `     return “Just Compile”;`
7.  `}`
8.  `exports.getMessage=getLib1Message;`

1.  *`//lib2.js`*
2.  `let lib1=require(“./lib1”);`
3.  `function getLib2Message(){`
4.  `     return lib1.getMessage() +” & “+ getMessage();`
5.  `}`
6.  `function getMessage(){`
7.  `     return “Train IT”;`
8.  `}`
9.  `exports.getMessage=getLib2Message;`

1.  *`//test.js`*
2.  `let lib2=require(‘./lib2’);`
3.  `console.log(lib2.getMessage());`

Out of these three files, **`test.js`** is going to be our main file. It is dependent on **`lib2.js`**, which is, in turn, dependent on **`lib1.js`**.

Also note, **`lib1.js`** and **`lib2.js`** contain a method with the same name **`getMessage`**.

For bundling, execute the following command in the folder where the preceding three files are kept:

1.  `webpack test.js`

As you can see, we have provided **`test.js`** as an input parameter to the **`webpack`** command.

![](images/Image44767.jpg)

**Figure 6.2**

Once the preceding command is executed, you will find a new folder called **`dist`** inside the root folder with a file **`main.js`**. It is a bundled file created by a **`webpack`**. It combined everything in each file into one file in such a way that nothing got replaced.

![](images/Image44813.jpg)

**Figure 6.3**

Let’s test the output by executing **`main.js`** using the node command as follows:

As you can see, the expected output is generated.

![](images/Image44849.jpg)

**Figure 6.4**

I hope you now know what bundling is, and how to do it with a webpack.

# [Using Browser as run-time with AMD modules](#toc.xhtml_s48a)

Create three JavaScript files **`lib1.js`**, **`lib2.js`**, and **`test.js`** with the following code:

1.  *`//lib1.js`*
2.  `define([‘exports’],`
3.  `function(e) {`
4.  `     function getLib1Message(){`
5.  `         return getMessage();`
6.  `    }`
7.  `     function getMessage(){`
8.  `         return “Just Compile”;`
9.  `    }`
10.  `     e.getMessage=getLib1Message;`
11.  `});`

1.  *`// lib2.js`*
2.  `define([‘exports’,”./lib1.js”],`
3.  `function(e,lib1) {`
4.  `     function getLib2Message(){`
5.  `         return lib1.getMessage() +” & “+ getMessage();`
6.  `    }`
7.  `     function getMessage(){`
8.  `         return “Train IT”;`
9.  `    }`
10.  `     e.getMessage=getLib2Message;`
11.  `});`

1.  *`//test.js`*
2.  `define([`
3.  `     ‘./lib2.js’,`
4.  `], function(lib2) {`
5.  `     alert(lib2.getMessage());`
6.  `});`

Everything is the same as the previous demo, except the code is written in the AMD format this time.

![](images/Image44888.jpg)

**Figure 6.5**

Let’s do the bundling using the same procedure as the previous demo.

Then create the HTML file for the testing as follows:

1.  `<html>`
2.  `<head>`
3.  `     <title>`
4.  `         JavaScript module Demo`
5.  `     </title>`
6.  `     <script  src = “./dist/main.js”></script>`
7.  `</head>`
8.  `</html>`

As you can see, unlike the demo we did with the module loaders, this time, we have not included any module loader in the HTML file, plus we have included **`main.js`** as an ordinary JavaScript file.

Now, start the web-server in the root folder using the **`lite-server`** command.

(**`lite-server`** is a development purpose web-server that we are using. It was downloaded and installed in *[Chapter 5: ES6 Features Part 4](#c05.xhtml)*. In case you haven’t installed it, do it now.)

![](images/Image44933.jpg)

**Figure 6.6**

Check the output in the browser:
