# [Using Browser as run-time with ES6 modules](#toc.xhtml_s49a)

Repeat the previous demo exactly. The only difference will be, instead of the AMD, follow the ES6 module syntax.

1.  *`//lib1.js`*
2.  `export function getLib1Message(){`
3.  `     return getMessage();`
4.  `}`
5.  `function getMessage(){`
6.  `     return “Just Compile”;`
7.  `}`
8.  *`// lib2.js`*
9.  `import {getLib1Message} from “./lib1.js”`
10.  `export function getLib2Message(){`
11.  `     return getLib1Message() +” & “+ getMessage();`
12.  `}`
13.  `function getMessage(){`
14.  `     return “Train IT”;`
15.  `}`

1.  *`//test.js`*
2.  `import {getLib2Message} from “./lib2.js”`
3.  `     alert(getLib2Message());`

Repeat the same bundling and execution process as the previous demo, and you will get the same output.

# [Webpack configuration file](#toc.xhtml_s50a)

So far, in our demos, we passed the name of our main JavaScript file as a parameter to the **`webpack`** command, and as a result, we got a bundle file called **`main.js`**.

In a real project, you may expect an output file name to be different.

**`webpack`** has provisions for it. We have to execute the **`webpack`** command as follows:

1.  `webpack --entry ./test.js --output-filename ./dist/main.js`

But you know what, the webpack is way more capable than bundling just the JavaScript files. It can bundle the CSS files too, and for that, we need the webpack plugins.

Supplying all those configurations via the command line won’t be an elegant solution. We need a cleaner way, and that’s where the webpack config file comes into the picture.

Let’s look into it with a quick demo.

Let’s reuse the BrowserModule example from the preceding demo for this demo. Create a new file in the root folder with the name **`webpack.config.js`** and the following content:

1.  `const path = require(‘path’);`

3.  `module.exports = {`
4.  `     entry: {`
5.  `         main: “./test.js”`
6.  `    },`
7.  `     output: {`
8.  `         path: path.resolve(__dirname, ‘dist’),`
9.  `         filename: “[name].js”`
10.  `    }`
11.  `};`

The preceding code is the Node.js code.

1.  **`__direname`** is a global variable in the Node.js, which holds the current folder’s path as the value. Let’s assume that the current folder is **`C://SourceCode`**.
2.  **`path`** is a local node module given to us by the Node.js run time. It helps to concatenate paths. For example, **`path.resolve(__direname, ‘dist’)`** will return **`C://SourceCode/dist`**.
3.  We are exporting a JavaScript object with two properties – entry and output – one is used for representing the input file name, and the other is for specifying the output file name.
4.  In the entry section, the main is not a keyword. It can be anything. It decides the bundle file name, which will be **`main.js`** in our case as we have specified the key as main.

***Note:* In case you are facing difficulty in understanding the preceding code, then do not worry.**

**Just try to get the feel of a webpack. Focus more on Angular. All these initial labs are there to make your fundamentals stronger. You can even skip if you wish.**

Now, open the Command Prompt, and execute the following command:

1.  `webpack`

As you can see, there is no parameter supplied to the **`webpack`** command. It will generate the bundle based on the settings in the **`webpack.config.js`**.

You will notice that the **`main.js`** file gets generated inside the **`dist`** folder.

# [Webpack for CSS bundling](#toc.xhtml_s51a)

Did I say Webpack could bundle CSS files too? Let’s see it in action this time.

First, let’s create the project.

Create two CSS files and HTML files as follows:

1.  *`/* file2.css */`*
2.  `header span{`
3.  `     color: red;`
4.  `}`

1.  *`/* file.css */`*
2.  `@import url(“file2.css”);`
3.  `div{`
4.  `     color: green;`
5.  `}`

1.  `<html>`
2.  `<head>`
3.  `     <title>CSS bundle demo</title>`
4.  `</head>`
5.  `<header>`
6.  `     <span>Hello from header span</span>`
7.  `</header>`
8.  `<div>`
9.  `     Hello from Div`
10.  `</div>`
11.  `</html>`

As you can see, we are importing **`file2.css`** in **`file.css`**.

Next, let’s create the **`webpack.config.js`** as follows:

1.  `const path = require(‘path’);`

3.  `module.exports = {`
4.  `     entry: {`
5.  `         style: “./file.css”`
6.  `    },`
7.  `     output: {`
8.  `         path: path.resolve(__dirname, ‘dist’),`
9.  `         filename: “[name].css”`
10.  `    },`
11.  `}`

Now, let’s execute our magical **`webpack`** command:

![](images/Image45075.jpg)

**Figure 6.8**

Unfortunately, the webpack expects every file to be a valid JavaScript module, and **`file.css`** is not a JavaScript module. To make it work, first, we need to convert the CSS modules into the JavaScript modules, and that’s where a **`webpack`** plugin called **`css-loaders`** comes into the picture.

Download it using the **`npm install css-loader`** command, and then configure the **`webpack`** to use it as follows:

1.  `const path = require(‘path’);`

3.  `module.exports = {`
4.  `     entry: {`
5.  `         style: “./file.css”`
6.  `    },`
7.  `     output: {`
8.  `         path: path.resolve(__dirname, ‘dist’),`
9.  `         filename: “[name].css”`
10.  `    },`
11.  `     module: {`
12.  `         rules: [`
13.  `             {`
14.  `                 test: /\.css$/,`
15.  `                 use: [‘css-loader’]`
16.  `            }`
17.  `        ]`
18.  `    }`
19.  `}`

Now, re-run the **`webpack`** command.

Did it work?

Let’s examine the bundled **`style.css`** file.

![](images/Image45084.jpg)

**Figure 6.9**

![](images/Image45128.jpg)

**Figure 6.10**

Does it look like a CSS file to you?

**`webpack`** is a JavaScript bundler. It bundled all your CSS files and created a bundled JavaScript file.

As it generates a JavaScript file, not a CSS, it does not make sense to set the extension as CSS.

Open the webpack configuration file, and set the extension as **`js`** instead of **`css`** for our bundled file.

1.  `……`
2.  `    },`
3.  `     output: {`
4.  `         path: path.resolve(__dirname, ‘dist’),`
5.  `         filename: “[name].js”`
6.  `    },`
7.  `     module: {`
8.  `……`

Re-run the **`webpack`** command. It will generate **`style.js`** instead of **`style.css`** and then include it in the HTML file.

1.  `……`
2.  `    <title>CSS bundle demo</title>`
3.  `     <script src=”dist/style.js”></script>`
4.  `</head>`
5.  `<header>`
6.  `     <span>Hello from header span</span>`
7.  `</header>`
8.  `……`

Run the file. I don’t think it is the expected output.

The problem is, we included the bundled JavaScript file in HTML.

![](images/Image45172.jpg)

**Figure 6.11**

We have to somehow inject the CSS styles into the DOM.

The **`css-loader`** helped the webpack converting the CSS module into a JavaScript module, so that it can be bundled. We also need one more plugin called the **`style-bundle`**, which will add a special code inside the bundle that will extract the CSS from the generated JavaScript bundle, and inject it into the DOM.

Install the **`style-loader`** using the **`npm install style-loader`** command.

Then, add the **`style-loader`** to the loader collection in the webpack configuration file.

1.  `……`
2.  `     module: {`
3.  `         rules: [`
4.  `             {`
5.  `                 test: /\.css$/,`
6.  `                 use: [‘style-loader’,’css-loader’]`
7.  `            }`
8.  `        ]`
9.  `    }`
10.  `……`

Once done, re-run the webpack command, and then execute the HTML file.

Inspect HTML using the Chrome developer tools. You will notice that the CSS is injected into the DOM.

![](images/Image45252.jpg)

**Figure 6.12**

# [Module loaders versus Module bundlers](#toc.xhtml_s52a)

I believe that now you have a clear idea about the Module Bundlers, and how they are different from the Module Loaders.

A Module loader is a library that has to be included in the HTML file explicitly. It loads modules dynamically at run-time on demand.

A Module Bundler is a node module that is needed only in the development machine. It’s kind of an application that bundles all the modules into one file without affecting the code inside each other.

A module bundler is the way to go. It removes the complexities of the module loaders, and on top of that, it provides better performance.

# [webpack-dev-server](#toc.xhtml_s53a)

Don’t you feel that using a webpack adds a little overhead during development? Every time we change something in any of the JavaScript files, we have to re-create the bundles.

![](images/Image45213.jpg)

**Figure 6.13**

The solution to this problem is the **`webpack-dev-server`**. Just like **`lite-server`**, it is a development purpose web-server, but it also provides an in-memory bundling feature. Let’s understand it with the help of a demo.

Take one of the browser examples from the previous section. You can pick any of the two demos – either the AMD or the ES6 module. Delete the dist folder inside it explicitly and keep everything as it is, including the Index.html file which looks as follows:

1.  `<html>`
2.  `<head>`
3.  `     <title>`
4.  `         JavaScript module Demo`
5.  `     </title>`
6.  `     <script src = “./dist/main.js”></script>`
7.  `</head>`
8.  `</html>`

First, install **`webpack-dev-server`** using **`npm`**:

1.  `npm install webpack-dev-server -g`

Now, launch the following command in the root folder:

1.  `webpack-dev-server --entry ./test.js --output-filename ./dist/main.js`

![](images/Image45291.jpg)

**Figure 6.14**

As you can see, while executing the **`webpack-dev-server`** command, we have used the **`--entry`** flag with the value as **`test.js`** and the **`--output-filename`** flag with the value as **`./dist/main.js`**.

The **`--entry`** flag is used to specify the input file name, and the **`--output-filename`** flag is used to specify the bundle file name.

![](images/Image45329.jpg)

**Figure 6.15**

Let’s understand what the preceding command does:

*   It will create a bundle but not in the disk; instead, it will keep it in the memory.

It will launch a development purpose web-server just like **`lite-server`**.

Now, check the output in the browser.

Are you wondering how the demo is working if **`main.js`** doesn’t exist on the disk?

Well, that’s the power of **`webpack-dev-server`**.

Now, keep the **`webpack-dev-server`** in the background and change **`lib1.js`** as follows:

1.  `………`
2.  `function getMessage(){`
3.  `         return “Just Compile123”;`
4.  `}`
5.  `………`

You will notice that automatically the bundle gets recreated, and the browser gets refreshed, and the updated output is displayed.

I hope it solved your development time bundling issue.

![](images/Image45365.jpg)

**Figure 6.16**

# [Summary](#toc.xhtml_s54a)

We explored webpack in this chapter, and this much knowledge is enough for an Angular learner. Once you start with Angular, you will not even see a webpack anywhere. Everything will be abstracted from you, all the bundling, and the other complication will be hidden from you, and your focus will be entirely on building the applications.

Internally, angular-cli will use the webpack or **`webpack-dev-sever`** depending on the situation (building or developing), along with **`css-loader`** and **`style-loader`**. It also uses a webpack plugin called **`html-webpack-plugin`**, which, after bundling, auto injects the bundled JavaScript files into the HTML file.

It’s always great if you know the internals of the technology that you are learning. So, knowing all of these things makes your Angular development journey entertaining and better.

Let’s quickly revise what we learned in this chapter:

*   We discovered how the module bundlers improve the performance by merging multiple JavaScript files into one, and thus reducing the number of requests.
*   We learned about the Webpack, which is a module bundler that Angular internally uses.
*   We spoke about the Webpack CLI, which is a command-line interface for dealing with the bundling.
*   We did a demo of the Webpack.
*   We saw the demonstration Webpack configuration file.
*   We learned to bundle the CSS files using Webpack.
*   We finally explored the Webpack Dev Server, and learned how to deal with Webpack in the development environment.

So finally, the journey to Station 3 is over. Your Station 4 journey will take you through TypeScript.

# [CHAPTER 7](#toc.xhtml_c07)

# [TypeScript](#toc.xhtml_c07)

# [Introduction](#toc.xhtml_s55a)

We have finished our journey to Station 3, and we know all the basics behind Angular learning.

With our journey to Station 4, we will be putting our first step towards Angular development. While developing the Angular applications, we will be using the TypeScript language for writing the code.

By the end of this chapter, you will reach Station 4, and then you will get a clearer picture of the difference between JavaScript, ES6, and TypeScript.

# [What is TypeScript? And Why?](#toc.xhtml_s56a)

We already had a quick discussion about it in the very first chapter. Let’s do it again.

*   It is a programming language that has a compiler. Upon compilation, the corresponding JavaScript code will be generated.
*   The perfect terminology should be the Transpiler, not the compiler, as it is creating one language from another language. We will use both the words throughout the series, as compiling is the most popular word in the community, and transpiling is the actual terminology. Both mean the same with respect to TypeScript.
*   Instead of writing in JavaScript, we can think of writing the code in TypeScript because TypeScript makes a developer’s life very easy. How? That’s what we will be learning in this chapter.

# [Type-Safe and compiled environment](#toc.xhtml_s57a)

It is one of the greatest benefits that we get from the TypeScript over JavaScript. *It provides us a compiled environment.* Let’s do a couple of demos to understand the same.

First, install TypesScript by executing the following command in the Command Prompt:

1.  `npm install typescript -g`

**Demo 1: Type-Safety**

Create a new TypeScript file **`comp_env.ts`**, and put the following content:

1.  `var myName=”Sukesh”;`
2.  `myName=2.0; /invalid code – It will throw compile error.`

Compile (transpile) using the **`tsc`** command:

![](images/Image45407.jpg)

**Figure 7.1**

As you can see, after compilation, we are getting an error.

The variable **`myName`** is a string, but we are trying to assign a number to it, and that is not permittable.

If you are thinking about how **`myName`** becomes the string type, then it’s because of the Type inference. The Type inference means that the data type will be set based on the first assignment.

In our case, the first assigned value was Sukesh; hence it becomes a string type.

If you want, you can assign data types explicitly.

1.  *`//Demo 1.2`*
2.  `var myName:string=”Sukesh”;`
3.  `myName=2.0;` *`//invalid code – It will throw compile error.`*

We will not discuss every data type in TypeScript one by one. It will be auto-learned once we start with Angular.

But there is one data type worth discussing. That is **`any`**. Declaring a variable with **`any`** datatype makes it a dynamic type variable, and it becomes open for all types of values.

1.  *`//Demo 1.3`*
2.  `var myName:any=”Sukesh”;`
3.  `myName=2.0;` *`//valid code – It will not throw any compile error.`*

Although it’s not recommended to use, it exists to support the backward compatibility. The old JavaScript code, which is written without type-safety, can be migrated by setting the variable type to **`any`**.

**Demo 2: Functions**

Let’s create a new function as follows:

1.  `function myFunction(x:number,y:number):string{`
2.  `     var result=x+y;`
3.  `}`

Our function in the preceding code expects two parameters of the type number, and have the return type as string, but we are not returning anything. Let’s see what will happen when it’s compiled.

![](images/Image45460.jpg)

**Figure 7.2**

Let’s change the function to the following:

1.  `function myFunction(x:number,y:number):string{`
2.  `     return (x+y).toString();`
3.  `}`

Next, let’s invoke the function, passing the wrong type of parameters.

1.  `myFunction(“a”,123);`

Let’s compile and check the output.

![](images/Image45504.jpg)

**Figure 7.3**

These two demos are more than enough to prove the compiled environment in TypeScript.

This feature will make the life of all those developers amazingly easy, who have extreme experience with static languages, and are now going to start extensively in the JavaScript world.

Before we wrap up this compiled world explanation, there is one thing worth discussing. That is, what happens when the compilation fails?

First, let’s try with a proper code:

![](images/Image45541.jpg)

**Figure 7.4**

As you can see, upon a successful compilation, a JavaScript file got created.

Now, let’s try with an invalid code.

![](images/Image45578.jpg)

**Figure 7.5**

Is it surprising? When an invalid code is compiled, we get the error in the console, but the JavaScript file also gets generated.

Upon both the successful and the failed compilation, the JavaScript file gets created by TypeScript.

Though it seems terrible, it is the default behavior, and it can be turned off by using a flag, **`noEmitOnError`**.

1.  `tsc comp_env.ts --noEmitOnError`

# [Latest features of JavaScript](#toc.xhtml_s58a)

This is the second feature of TypeScript that makes it superior to JavaScript. In TypeScript, we can do everything which we can with JavaScript. It supports every syntax and feature in JavaScript, including the latest ES features, such as **`let`**, **`const`**, spread operator, and so on.

![](images/Image45619.jpg)

**Figure 7.6**

Typescript will also provide some features, which are otherwise not possible in JavaScript, such as generics, interfaces, and so on.

# [A curiosity of one of my clients](#toc.xhtml_s59a)

I am willing to share a wonderful conversation with one of my clients during the consulting work.

![](images/Image45658.jpg)

**Figure 7.7**

My client asked – “I don’t want a type-safe and compiled environment. We have been using JavaScript for such a long time. We are very much used to its dynamic behavior, and we are happy with it. Why shouldn’t I go with ES6 instead of TypeScript in such a case?”.

Here is what I answered.

*“You have a very valid question. Just tell me, can you guarantee that ES6 features will work in your client browser? You cannot guarantee it. It may or it may not.*

*With TypeScript, you can guarantee that every feature will work in all browsers. As of now, TypeScript, by default, generates the ES5 code on compilation/transpilation. It will create logic on behalf of you. Let me explain to you with the help of one simple demo.”*

I opened a file from my laptop with the following content, and asked him what it was.

1.  `let a=10;`
2.  `console.log(a`
3.  `if(true){`
4.  `   let a=9;`
5.  `   console.log(a);`
6.  `}`
7.  `console.log(a);`

![](images/Image45698.jpg)

**Figure 7.8**

He quickly replied, “*It’s a JavaScript code using the ES6 let keyword*”. I replied with a smile, “*It’s a TypeScript code. Check the extension of the file.*”

I continued, “*TypeScript inherits its syntaxes from JavaScript. Everything you write in JavaScript can be written in TypeScript.*”

Then, I compiled (transpiled) the TypeScript file as follows:

1.  `   tsc Var_Dec.ts`

Then, I opened the generated JavaScript file, and compared it with the TypeScript code.

![](images/Image45767.jpg)

**Figure 7.9**

I said, *“It converted let to var as let is an ES6 keyword, but also renamed the variable inside the if block. Renaming ensured that although is it is var, it will generate the same output as let. So I got the benefit of both the worlds. One is that I get to use the let keyword and second, I don’t need to think about browser compatibility.”*

I continued, *“The TypeScript compiler will take care of the new ES6 features. Whenever needed, it generates custom logic and ensures that output remains unaffected.”*

He got impressed, but he is a client. He countered with another question. He asked, *“It sounds good to me, but what if once ES6 becomes completely compatible across all browsers? What will be the scenario by then?”*

He is smart but I am too.

The following was my answer:

*“We get one more benefit by using Typescript over JavaScript. TypeScript always stays ahead of the JavaScript version. By the time all the browsers become compatible with ES6, TypeScript starts supporting features in the next version of JavaScript.”*

I continued, *“Plus, the additional benefit is, here, you will get the provision to change the target of JavaScript to be generated. As of now, TypeScript gets transpiled to ES5, but by just setting one flag, we can configure it to generate ES6\. So, when ES6 becomes completely compatible, we will set it to ES6, so no extra custom logic.”*

1.  `tsc Var_Dec.ts --target ES6`

I ended my conversation with, *“To convince you on TypeScript, let me tell you one last advantage. TypeScript will provide you some features which are not even available in ES6\. Such as generics, interfaces, and so on. I think these reasons are more than enough to make you feel comfortable with TypeScript over ES6.”*

He gets convinced, and I get a new contract for my company.

# [Unique features in TypeScript](#toc.xhtml_s60a)

Now, as I said before, every JavaScript feature is a TypeScript feature. Including all the ES6 features. So, we will not repeat all those features.

We also said that TypeScript has some features that are not even there in the latest JavaScript standard. Let’s explore a couple of them.

# [Interfaces](#toc.xhtml_s61a)

Unlike JavaScript, TypeScript does support interfaces.

Let’s do a demo and understand it.

Create a TypeScript file **`interface.ts`** with the following content:

1.  `interface MyInterface{`
2.  `     MyMethod(x:string,y:number):string;`
3.  `     MyMethod2():number;`
4.  `}`

Let’s compile the preceding file, and see what output is generated.

![](images/Image45809.jpg)

**Figure 7.10**

It’s empty.

If you are from an object-oriented programming background, then you may recollect the definition of the interface as *“It’s a contract everybody must follow”.*

JavaScript is a dynamic language, so no contract needs to be followed. Hence, you get nothing for the interfaces by the TypeScript compiler.

This feature is for the TypeScript programmers. You will see a lot of interfaces in action once we start with Angular.

Let’s use it in some code, and then let’s see how JavaScript got generated.

Create a new function **`myFunction`** as follows, and then compile it.

1.  `function myFunction(type1:MyInterface,type2:MyInterface){`
2.  `     let a=type1.MyMethod(“A”,12);`
3.  `     let b=type1.MyMethod2();`
4.  `     let c=type2.MyMethod(“B”,12);`
5.  `     let d=type2.MyMethod2();`
6.  `}`

![](images/Image45816.jpg)

**Figure 7.11**

**When you are using TypeScript, don’t worry much about what is getting generated. Just try to understand the development-time benefit you get because of that.**

For instance, let’s create a class **`Customer`**, which won’t implement **`MyInterface`**, then let’s pass an instance of it to **`myFunction`**, and finally, let’s compile, and let’s see what happens.

1.  `interface MyInterface{`
2.  `     MyMethod(x:string,y:number):string;`
3.  `     MyMethod2():number;`
4.  `}`

6.  `function myFunction(type1:MyInterface,type2:MyInterface){`
7.  `     let a=type1.MyMethod(“A”,12);`
8.  `     let b=type1.MyMethod2();`
9.  `     let c=type2.MyMethod(“B”,12);`
10.  `     let d=type2.MyMethod2();`
11.  `}`
12.  `class Customer{`
13.  `}`
14.  `let c=new Customer();`
15.  `myFunction(c,c);`

![](images/Image45857.jpg)

**Figure 7.12**

As you can see, it is enforcing you to pass the instance which is following the contract, and the contract says it must contain a definition for the methods **`MyMethod`** and **`MyMethod2`**.

# [Generics](#toc.xhtml_s62a)

Generic is a programming methodology where we write our code that will work for multiple data types. We won’t explicitly specify the data type at the time of writing that code. Instead, we specify the datatype at the time of using that code in some other code.

Let’s do a demo, and understand the same.

I believe, as a programmer, you know what an array is. It’s a collection of elements of similar types.

But in JavaScript, the concept is a little different. In JavaScript, a single array can hold the items of different data types.

As you know, TypeScript is a typed language, and we have a generic class called **`Array`** here. It will let us create a collection that can hold the items of a similar data type.

Look at the following code:

1.  `let myArray:Array<number>=new Array<number>();`

The preceding code will create an array of numbers called **`myArray`**.

Let’s try to add some items to it.

1.  `myArray.push(1);` *`// valid code`*
2.  `myArray.push(1);` *`// valid code`*
3.  `myArray.push(“Sukesh”);` *`// invalid code - compile error`*

Now, let’s use the same **`Array`** class for creating an array of string values.

1.  `let myStrings:Array<string>=new Array<string>();`
2.  `myStrings.push(“Sukesh”);  `*`// valid code`*
3.  `myStrings.push(“Marla”);  `*`// valid code`*
4.  `myStrings.push(1);` *`// invalid code - compile error`*

It is generic programming, and it is giving us the following two advantages:

1.  The Array class can be used for multiple data types. It’s not bound to one specific datatype.
2.  It is type-safe. It can work with the various data types, but it doesn’t make it type-unsafe.

Now that you know TypeScript has support for Generics, let’s create our generic custom function.

Look at the following code:

1.  `function IsEqual(x:number,y:number):boolean{`
2.  `     if(x==y){`
3.  `         return true;`
4.  `    }`
5.  `     else{`
6.  `         return false;`
7.  `    }`
8.  `}`

The preceding function is bound to only numbers. We cannot use the same for strings. So what to do to make it work for multiple types?

**Solution 1: No parameter type solution**

Change the preceding code to the following:

1.  `function IsEqual(x,y):boolean{`
2.  `     if(x==y){`
3.  `         return true;`
4.  `    }`
5.  `     else{`
6.  `         return false;`
7.  `    }`
8.  `}`
9.  `console.log(IsEqual(1,2));`
10.  `console.log(IsEqual(“A”,”B”));`

Let compile it using the **`tsc`** command.

![](images/Image45908.jpg)

**Figure 7.13**

It seems like it’s working. But is it really working? Let’s test the code with one more possibility.

1.  `function IsEqual(x,y):boolean{`
2.  `     if(x==y){`
3.  `         return true;`
4.  `    }`
5.  `     else{`
6.  `         return false;`
7.  `    }`
8.  `}`

10.  `console.log(IsEqual(1,2));`
11.  `console.log(IsEqual(“A”,”B”));`
12.  `console.log(IsEqual(“A”,2));`

We haven’t changed anything. We only added one more function call but this time we passed one string and one number. Let’s see if it compiles properly:

![](images/Image45996.jpg)

**Figure 7.14**

Yes, it’s compiling. Is it an expected output? The answer is No.

By removing the datatypes from the function parameter signature, we simply made it any type, and hence it can accept all types of values now.

We expected to have a function where both the parameters must be of the same data type.

It can be achieved by making it a generic code.

1.  `function IsEqual<T>(x:T,y:T):boolean{`
2.  `     if(x==y){`
3.  `         return true;`
4.  `    }`
5.  `     else{`
6.  `         return false;`
7.  `    }`
8.  `}`

10.  `console.log(IsEqual<number>(1,2));`*`// valid code`*
11.  `console.log(IsEqual<string>(“A”,”B”));`*`// valid code`*

13.  `console.log(IsEqual<number>(“A”,”B”));`*`// invalid code - compile error`*
14.  `console.log(IsEqual<number>(1,”B”));`*`// invalid code - compile error`*

Compile it, and you will see that now it will work as per expectation.

I believe with this, the advantages of generic programming must be clear to you.

# [Enums](#toc.xhtml_s63a)

Enums are named constants.

Many a times, in programming, we come across a situation where we have to declare constants. For instance, look at the following code:

1.  *`//Constants - May be declared in some different file`*
2.  `const AddMode:string=”Add”;`
3.  `const EditMode:string=”Edit”;`

5.  `let currentMode=GetCurrenMode();`
6.  `if(currentMode==AddMode){`
7.  *`//Do Save operation`*
8.  `}`
9.  `else if(currentMode==EditMode){`
10.  *`//Do Update Operatopm`*
11.  `}`

Now, let’s define **`GetCurrentMode`** function:

1.  `function GetCurrenMode():string{`
2.  *`//Some logic`*
3.  `     return “Veg Burger”;`
4.  `}`

Now, if you compile the preceding TypeScript code, it will compile without any error.

I think you are smart enough to realize the problem in the preceding code. The value Veg Burger is not valid for the modes, but there is no way to identify it at the compiled time.

It can be solved using **`enums`**.

Let’s re-write the same code using **`enums`**:

1.  `const enum Mode{`
2.  `     AddMode=”Add”,`
3.  `     EditMode=”Edit”`
4.  `}`
5.  `let currentMode:Mode=GetCurrenMode();`
6.  `if(currentMode==Mode.AddMode){`
7.  *`//Do Save operation`*
8.  `}`
9.  `else if(currentMode==Mode.EditMode){`
10.  *`//Do Update Operatopm`*
11.  `}`

13.  `function GetCurrenMode():Mode{`
14.  `     return Mode.VegBurger;`
15.  `}`

Now, let’s compile it.

![](images/Image46006.jpg)

**Figure 7.15**

The Enums make working with the constant easier, plus it makes the code more readable.

# [Modules](#toc.xhtml_s64a)

Working with the modules is super easy if you know the ES6 modules.

We already discussed it in detail in *[Chapter 6: Webpack](#c06.xhtml)*.

Let’s do a quick demo here.

Create an independent folder (to avoid confusion) called **`Module-demo`** and create two TypeScript files, **`simple.ts`**, and **`reusable.ts`** as follows:

1.  *`//reusable.ts`*
2.  `export function getString():string{`
3.  `     return getString2();`
4.  `}`
5.  `function getString2():string{`
6.  `     return “Just Compile”;`
7.  `}`

1.  *`//simple.ts`*
2.  `import {getString as myFunction} from “./reusable”;`
3.  `console.log(myFunction());`

Now, compile **`simple.ts`** using the **`tsc`** command.

![](images/Image46103.jpg)

**Figure 7.16**

Please note, we have not compiled **`reusable.ts`** explicitly. It got compiled too as **`simple.ts`** has imported it.

![](images/Image46117.jpg)

**Figure 7.17**

Let’s look at the generated code—open **`simple.ts`**.

1.  `“use strict”;`
2.  `exports.__esModule = true;`
3.  *`//simple.ts`*
4.  `var reusable_1 = require(“./reusable”);`
5.  `console.log(reusable_1.getString());`

What you see in the preceding code is the **`CommonJS`** format. If you remember, in our discussion on the Module Formatters, we said that there are a lot of Module Formatters, and we did our demo with AMD.

TypeScript, by default, generates the **`CommonJS`** format.

We can instruct the TypeScript compiler to generate the AMD format code using the --module flag.

![](images/Image46187.jpg)

**Figure 7.18**

Let’s check what code is generated after compiling:

1.  `define([“require”, “exports”, “./reusable”],`
2.  `function (require, exports, reusable_1) {`
3.  `     “use strict”;`
4.  `     exports.__esModule = true;`
5.  `     console.log(reusable_1.getString());`
6.  `});`

Does it seem like a familiar code to you? It is an AMD code.

# [Using existing JavaScript files in TypeScript](#toc.xhtml_s65a)

In my many years of experience, I have never seen a web project where no external JavaScript files have been used.

If we are planning to write the code in TypeScript, what about the external JavaScript files (which are not compiled from TypeScript)? How do we use them?

Let’s consider jQuery for the demonstration purpose.

Create a TypeScript file **`simple.ts`** as follows:

1.  `$(document).ready(function() {`
2.  `         $(‘#myElement’).text(“Just Compile”);`
3.  `    }`
4.  `);`

The preceding code is the jQuery code that will get the element with the ID as **`myElement`**; change the inner text. (Do not worry about jQuery now. Just know that $, text, and ready are functions provided by the jQuery library.)

Let’s compile the code using the **`tsc`** command:

![](images/Image46195.jpg)

**Figure 7.19**

Unfortunately, $ is not a known keyword for TypeScript. Hence, the compilation failed.

The solution for this is the Type Definition files.

**Type Definition files**

The Type Definition files are the TypeScript files with **`d.ts`** as an extension. It will contain only the declarations of all the artifacts of a JavaScript library. For instance, jQuery’s type definition file will include a declaration of all the available functions inside jQuery.

Once we have the type definition files, we can import from those Type Definition files.

Lets’ see a demo.

(You can skip the demo if you wish, and jump to the next section directly, as it is not very important in Angular learning. It will be your call.)

The good news is, the Type Definition files are available for almost all the major reusable community libraries that can be downloaded using the **`npm`** command.

So, let’s download the Type Definition file of jQuery.

1.  `   npm install @types/jquery`

After executing the preceding command, you will notice the @types folder gets created inside the **`node_modules`** folder.

Now, change your TypeScript code to the following code:

1.  `import $ from ‘jquery’;`
2.  `$(document).ready(function() {`
3.  `         $(‘#myElement’).text(“Just Compile”);`
4.  `    }`
5.  `);`

Now, compile it using the **`tsc`** command.

The compilation will be successful as the TypeScript compiler recognizes the $ function.

![](images/Image46240.jpg)

**Figure 7.20**

Now, let’s try to execute the preceding file by including it in the HTML file. We are going to use a **`webpack-dev-server`** for this purpose that was discussed in *[Chapter 6: Webpack](#c06.xhtml)*.

Create a new HTML file **`index.html`** as follows:

1.  `<html>`
2.  `<head>`
3.  `     <title>External library demo</title>`
4.  `     <script src = “dist/main.js”></script>`
5.  `</head>`
6.  `<body>`
7.  `     <h1>Welcome to, <span id=”myElement”>Train IT Solutions</span></h1>`
8.  `</body>`
9.  `</html>`

Let’s execute the **`webpack-dev-server`** using the following command:

1.  `webpack-dev-server --entry ./simple.js --output-filename ./dist/main.js`

Let’s check the output.

If you are expecting to get the working output now, then it will be a bit disappointing. You will find the HTML text unchanged, and on top of that, it will throw an error that can be examined using the developer tools.

![](images/Image46281.jpg)

**Figure 7.21**

Let’s understand the reason for this error.

We downloaded jQuery Type Definition files, but we have not downloaded the jQuery library file – **`jquery.js`**.

The Type Definition files only made the TypeScript aware of jQuery, so that it can be compiled. The jQuery library needed to be downloaded explicitly and included in the HTML file to make it work.

In short, the Type Definition files make sure that the compilation works, and the actual library ensures that the execution works.

Let’s keep **`webpack-dev-server`** running in the background. Open another Command Prompt and download jQuery using **`npm`**.

Now, include it in the HTML file.

1.  `npm install jquery`

Include jQuery in the HTML file as follows:

1.  `<html>`
2.  `<head>`
3.  `     <title>External library demo</title>`
4.  `     <script src=”./node_modules/jquery/dist/jquery.js”></script>`
5.  `     <script  src = “dist/main.js”></script>`
6.  `</head>`
7.  `<body>`
8.  `     <h1>Welcome to, <span id=”myElement”>Train IT Solutions</span></h1>`
9.  `</body>`
10.  `</html>`

Let’s check the output:

![](images/Image46324.jpg)

**Figure 7.22**

**What if we don’t have Type Definition files?**

It’s 100% possible. The Type Definition files are not available for every external JavaScript file.

For instance, let’s say we have a JavaScript file called **`myLib.js`** with the following code:

1.  `function getAuthorName(){`
2.  `     return “Sukesh Marla”;`
3.  `}`

Let’s say we want to use **`getAuthorName`** from the preceding JavaScript file in our TypeScript code. How do we do that?

Create a TypeScript files **`imple.ts`**, and directly try to use the preceding function in the TypeScript file.

1.  *`//simple.ts`*
2.  `alert(getAuthorName());`

Now, let’s compile it.

![](images/Image46370.jpg)

**Figure 7.23**

We can solve this problem with a logical solution. Change the **`simple.ts`** code as follows:

1.  *`//simple.ts`*
2.  `var getAuthorName:any;`
3.  `alert(getAuthorName());`

Now you will be able to compile it.

![](images/Image46406.jpg)

**Figure 7.24**

Let’s test it in the HTML file, and for that, create an HTML file as follows:

1.  `<html>`
2.  `<head>`
3.  `     <title>External library demo</title>`
4.  `     <script  src = “dist/main.js”></script>`
5.  `</head>`
6.  `<body>`
7.  `</body>`
8.  `</html>`

Let’s execute the **`webpack-dev-server`** using the following command:

1.  `webpack-dev-server --entry ./simple.js --output-filename ./dist/main.js`

Let’s check the output.

Just like the last lab, we have to include **`myLib.js`** in the HTML file explicitly.

![](images/Image46447.jpg)

**Figure 7.25**

Let’s change the HTML file to the following:

1.  `<html>`
2.  `<head>`
3.  `     <title>External library demo</title>`
4.  `     <script src=”myLib.js”></script>`
5.  `     <script  src = “dist/main.js”></script>`
6.  `</head>`
7.  `<body>`
8.  `</body>`
9.  `</html>`

Check the output:

![](images/Image46447.jpg)

**Figure 7.26**

We are still not getting the output.

To understand the reason for this, open the generated **`simple.js`** file.

1.  *`//simple.ts`*
2.  `var getAuthorName;`
3.  `alert(getAuthorName());`

Did you notice **`var getAuthorName;`**?

It replaced the **`getAuthorName`** function in the library.

We should somehow instruct the TypeScript compiler to skip this line while transpiling. It can be done with the declare keyword.

Change the **`simple.ts`**. to the following:

1.  *`//simple.ts`*
2.  `declare var getAuthorName:any;`
3.  `alert(getAuthorName());`

Now, recompile the TypeScript file, and examine the generated JavaScript file. It should like the following:

1.  `alert(getAuthorName());`

As you can see, that declaration statement is skipped.

Switch to the browser and check the output.

![](images/Image46534.jpg)

**Figure 7.27**

# [TypeScript configuration](#toc.xhtml_s66a)

There are the following two real-time problems that we need to address, which we haven’t so far:

1.  When it comes to the real-time project, we will have many TypeScript files, and we cannot compile them individually. We need a shortcut way to transpile all of them at one go.
2.  We expect many TypeScript settings to be set at the project level, not at the file level. For instance, we want to set the target to ES6.

Both can be addressed with the help of the configuration file.

Create a folder and execute the **`tsc --init`** command.

After the command, you will find a new JSON file, called the **`tsconfig.json`** that will get generated inside the folder with some default settings.

![](images/Image46610.jpg)

**Figure 7.28**

Next, create two TypeScript files, **`one.ts`**, and **`two.ts`**, with some random TypeScript code.

![](images/Image46617.jpg)

**Figure 7.29**

Now, open the Command Prompt and navigate to the folder, and execute **`tsc`** without any parameter.

Can you imagine what happened?

![](images/Image46654.jpg)

**Figure 7.30**

It compiled every file in the folder. It works only if the **`tsconfig.json`** file exists.

On top of that, while compiling, it took the settings inside **`tsconfig.json`** into consideration while transpiling.

You can check **[https://www.typescriptlang.org/docs/handbook/compiler-options.html](https://www.typescriptlang.org/docs/handbook/compiler-options.html)** and read about each setting in case you are interested.

# [Decorators](#toc.xhtml_s67a)

Decorators are one more important feature in TypeScript. We will not do any demo for the Decorator. You will see them in action once we start with Angular, but let’s have a quick discussion on it.

Decorators let us add some new behavior to the functions, classes, properties, and so on, without modifying the existing code.

We share similar features in other technologies too. In Java, we have the Annotation, in C#, there is Attributes, and in Python, it is called Decorators.

Let’s not worry about Decorators right now. We will see them in action in Angular, and I promise that you will like them, and understand the benefit of it as well.

# [Summary](#toc.xhtml_s68a)

You have reached Station 4, and it’s time to celebrate. With the next chapter, we will start our journey to the next level. We have finally reached a point where we can begin with Angular. Keep your excitement to the top, as I’ll see you in the following chapter.

Let’s revise what we learned in this chapter:

*   We learned that TypeScript is a language that will be transpiled into JavaScript.
*   TypeScript provides a compiled environment, and thus lets us avoid a lot of run time errors.
*   We learned that TypeScript lets us take advantage of the latest ES standards without worrying about browser compatibility.
*   We explored features such as Generics, Interfaces, and enums, which are not even there in ES6.
*   We spoke about Type Definition files, and learned how we could use the third-party JavaScript libraries with TypeScript.
*   We spoke about the **`tsconfig.json`** file, which is a configuration file for TypeScript.
*   We had a quick discussion on Decorators that will add additional behavior to the functions, classes, and properties.

# [Section 4](#toc.xhtml_p04)

# [CHAPTER 8](#toc.xhtml_c08)

# [Angular: The Beginning](#toc.xhtml_c08)

# [Introduction](#toc.xhtml_s69a)

Hello Angular learners, now that we are done with all the necessary prerequisites, it’s time to get into some real business. It’s time for Angular. We have crossed our Station 4, and are on our way to Station 5\. By the end of Station 5, you will have a clearer picture of creating a basic Angular application.

# [What is Angular?](#toc.xhtml_s70a)

Let’s start understanding what Angular is?

It is a framework for building a SPA.

# [How to create an Angular application?](#toc.xhtml_s71a)

First, I will explain the steps, and then I will create our first Hello World Angular application. If you get confused about any step, then don’t get stressed. Proceed with the next step.

Just keep moving until you reach the next module, where we will create our first Angular application.

It involves the following three simple steps:

![](images/Image46738.jpg)

**Figure 8.1**

Let’s elaborate on each step.

# [Step 1: Download and configure](#toc.xhtml_s72a)

Let’s list down all the sub-steps that we are supposed to finish in this step:

1.  **Download and configure TypeScript**

    The Angular applications are recommended to be developed in TypeScript, so the first thing we do is download TypeScript using the **`npm`** command. After that, we will create the **`tsconfig.json`** file, and set it up.

2.  **Download Angular Framework**

    Angular is a JavaScript framework, and if we are willing to develop an application using it, we need to download it first.

    Now, the most significant challenge that we have in our hands is that it’s not one library. It is a framework comprising of 7 individual modules – compiler, core, common, animation, forms, m router, platform-browser, and platform-browser-dynamic.

    We have to download each one of them.

    So create **`package.json`** and mention each of these seven modules. Finally, download them using the **`npm`** install command.

    One relief is, we are not required to explicitly download the Type Definition files of these modules as it will be downloaded with the respective module.

3.  **Download Dependent Libraries**

    Angular is dependent on three libraries internally, **`RxJS`**, **`tslib`**, and **`zone.js`**.

    **RxJS** is the reactive extension library for JavaScript.

    **`zone.js`** provides the execution context for Angular.

    **`tslib`** is a runtime helper of TypeScript that contains some helper functions.

    Relax, don’t worry about these libraries now. Angular uses them internally, and they can be downloaded again using the **`npm`** command.

4.  **Download and configure webpack**

    So far, we have not written a single line code, but soon we will. We already know how vital the Module bundler is when it comes to complex JavaScript applications. So next, we need to download and configure the Module Bundler – preferably Webpack.

5.  **Download and configure the web-server**

    It’s a web application, and we need a web-server to run it. You can use any that you like or even think of using the **`webpack-dev-server`** provided by Webpack.

# [Step 2: Write startup code](#toc.xhtml_s73a)

In the Angular application, Components are the basic building blocks.

An Angular application consists of a lot of Angular Components logically grouped into one or more Angular Modules.

Relax, don’t worry much about Components and Modules for now. We will discuss them in detail in the future chapters. For now, get the gist of it.

Now, out of those multiple Angular Modules, one will be considered as the Main or Root Module, and similarly, out of all the components of the Main Module, one will be regarded as the Main or Root Component.

For creating an Angular project, first, we need to create a Root Module and a Root Component, and that’s what “*Writing startup code is all about.*”

We also need an HTML file, which is going to be our startup file. Create it using the editor of your choice. You must be feeling dizzy by now.

Too many things need to be done to just get Hello World.

![](images/Image46786.jpg)

**Figure 8.2**

# [Step 3: Create the bundle](#toc.xhtml_s74a)

Now, it’s a natural step. Use the Module Bundler downloaded in Step 1, and bundle everything into one JavaScript file.

# [Step 4: Use the Component](#toc.xhtml_s75a)

Now, it involves the following two steps:

1.  **Using Component**

    As I said, Components will be discussed in detail later in this book.

    For now, keep one simple thing in mind.

    *Creating a new Component = creating a new HTML tag.*

    First, in the startup file, include the bundled JavaScript file, and then inside the body tag, use the root Component.

    If you are wondering how to use it, then as I already said, creating a new Component means creating a new HTML tag, and I believe you know how to use tags in HTML.

2.  **Run the server**

    Launch the web-server and execute the startup HTML file.

    Creating an Angular application will be a simple 4 step process.

# [Creating the first Angular application](#toc.xhtml_s76a)

So finally, it’s time to follow the preceding steps, and create our first application.

Or maybe I can teach you a simplified way to do it.

All the steps mentioned earlier are encapsulated inside a command-line interface called **Angular CLI**.

Let’s learn about it with a demo:

**Step 1: Install Angular CLI**

For that, execute the following command:

1.  `npm install @angular/cli -g`

**Step 2: Create the first project**

Once Angular CLI is installed, a new command becomes available **`– ng`**.

Using it, create a new project, **`myapp`** as follows.

1.  `ng new myapp`

It will ask a question in between. Ignore this question for now. Type N for all the Yes/No questions for now and then press the *Enter* key.

![](images/Image46852.jpg)

**Figure 8.3**

In case of Angular v.11, you will find a special prompt like the following:

*Do you want to enforce stricter type checking and stricter bundle budgets in the workspace?*

*This setting helps improve the maintainability and catch the bugs ahead of time.*

*For more information, see [https://angular.io/strict](https://angular.io/strict) (y/n)*

Ignore that for now too. Type **`N`** and press the *Enter* key.

![](images/Image46906.jpg)

**Figure 8.4**

It will be continued with another prompt. Please ignore it and enter.

![](images/Image46991.jpg)

**Figure 8.5**

The installation will take some time.

Once the installation is done, you will notice a new folder, **`myapp`**, with a couple of files.

![](images/Image46999.jpg)

**Figure 8.6**

You must be wondering what just happened. The simple answer is that out of the four steps we mentioned earlier, the first two steps are done: “*Download and configure*” and “*Write startup code*.”

![](images/Image47008.jpg)

**Figure 8.7**

Now, you must be feeling relieved and happy as **`ng`** made the project creation so easy.

**Step 3: Execute the project**

Navigate to the **`myapp`** folder and then execute the following command:

1.  `ng serve --open`

![](images/Image47051.jpg)

**Figure 8.8**

You will notice something like what is shown in the preceding figure, and in the end, you will see a browser automatically getting launched with the following output:

![](images/Image47093.jpg)

**Figure 8.9**

Are you wondering what happened? Step 3 and Step 4 happened.

![](images/Image47127.jpg)

**Figure 8.10**

With Angular CLI, creating the first Angular project is a piece of cake.

# Angular Hello World

If you are an older person like me, you miss Hello World – the first lab of every new coding.

I won’t disappoint you. Before we start exploring what got created by the **`ng new`**, let’s get our Hello World first.

# Editor for coding

Wait!!! Before you proceed, do you have a proper editor with you? Trust me. Everyone needs a perfect editor. Having a perfect editor is like having a perfect wife (or husband) (where I am lucky enough). It makes life a lot easier.

I prefer the *Visual Studio Code* which you can download from the following link:

**[https://code.visualstudio.com/](https://code.visualstudio.com/)**

**Back to coding**

Next, let’s get our Hello World.

Open the **`app.component.html`** file located in the **`myapp/src/app`** folder.

![](images/Image47164.jpg)

**Figure 8.11**

You will notice a lot of things written inside it. Delete everything and put the following HTML code:

1.  `<h1>Hello World</h1>`

Save the file, and that’s it.

There is no need to re-execute the **`ng`** serve command, or even refresh the browser, as it will be auto-refreshed. Switch to the browser, and you will notice the updated output.

![](images/Image47206.jpg)

**Figure 8.12**

**The Root Component**

Now, let’s take a look at our Root Component first.

Please note, we will discuss both the Component and the Module in much detail further in this book.

As of now, let’s explore our first project.

Inside the app folder, you will find **`app.component.ts`** and **`app.component.html`**. Those two files together make our Root Component. Let’s open the TypeScript file, and study it.

1.  `import {Component} from ‘@angular/core’;`
2.  `@Component({`
3.  `   selector: ‘app-root’,`
4.  `   templateUrl: ‘./app.component.html’,`
5.  `   styleUrls: [‘./app.component.css’]`
6.  `})`
7.  `export class AppComponent {`
8.  `   title = ‘myapp’;`
9.  `}`

For now, let’s remove the **`styleUrls`**. We will have a separate chapter dedicated to the same. It has no impact on the output as of now.

![](images/Image47243.jpg)

**Figure 8.13**

The following three points are worth discussing:

*   We have a TypeScript class called **`AppComponent`**, decorated with a decorator called **`component`**, imported from **`@angular/core`**.
*   Once again, I am repeating the same statement about component – creating component = creating a new HTML tag. By writing the preceding code, we created a new HTML tag called **`app-root`**.
*   Using the **`app-root`** tag, we will get the contents of **`app.component.html`** in response – our Hello World.

Next, you must be thinking about where this newly generated component (HTML tag) is used.

In the **`src`** folder, you will find an **`index.html`** file with the following content:

1.  `<html lang=”en”>`
2.  `<head>`
3.  `   <meta charset=”utf-8”>`
4.  `   <title>Myapp</title>`
5.  `   <base href=”/”>`
6.  `   <meta name=”viewport” content=”width=device-width, initial-scale=1”>`
7.  `   <link rel=”icon” type=”image/x-icon” href=”favicon.ico”>`
8.  `</head>`
9.  `<body>`
10.  `   <app-root></app-root>`
11.  `</body>`
12.  `</html>`

As you can see, the **`app-root`** tag is used inside the body tag.

Angular CLI internally uses Webpack and **`Webpack-dev-server`**. It dynamically injects the bundled in-memory file into this HTML file using the **`html-webpack-plugin`** discussed in *[Chapter 6: Webpack](#c06.xhtml)*.

Let’s conduct one small experiment. Remove the **`app-root`** tag from this HTML file, and check the output.

![](images/Image47285.jpg)

**Figure 8.14**

We will get nothing but a big white screen. But it will be the strangest thing you will find in the developer console of the browser.

*It is a must to use the app-root in the index.html as the AppComponent is a Root Component.*

You must be curious to know how **`AppComponent`** becomes the root component.

Let’s learn the same now.

# [The Root Module](#toc.xhtml_s77a)

In the same **`src`** directory, you will see a file **`app.module.ts`**. It will contain the definition for **`AppModule`**, which is an Angular Module.

1.  `import {BrowserModule} from ‘@angular/platform-browser’;`
2.  `import {NgModule} from ‘@angular/core’;`
3.  `import {AppComponent} from ‘./app.component’;`
4.  `@NgModule({`
5.  `   declarations: [`
6.  `     AppComponent`
7.  `  ],`
8.  `   imports: [`
9.  `     BrowserModule`
10.  `  ],`
11.  `   providers: [],`
12.  `   bootstrap: [AppComponent]`
13.  `})`
14.  `export class AppModule {}`

![](images/Image47369.jpg)

**Figure 8.15**

The following three points are worth discussing:

*   We have a TypeScript class called **`AppModule`**, which is decorated with a Decorator called **`NgModule`**, imported from **`@angular/core`**.
*   Can you see the declarations section? It’s an array, and inside it, you will notice **`AppComponent`** is mentioned. It means **`AppComponent`** is part of **`AppModule`**.
*   Just like that is another section called **bootstrap**. It represents the components, which will be used directly in the startup HTML file that is **`index.html`**. In short, it represents the root component.

We will talk about the other things inside **`NgModule`** later in this book.

Suppose a single Angular application is going to be comprised of multiple components, and modules. Doesn’t that mean more than one module in an application can have a bootstrap section defined?

It can, but only the bootstrap section in the **`AppModule`** will be considered.

Do you know why? Because **`AppModule`** is a Root Module.

Considering that, next, we will learn how an AppModule becomes a Root Module.

# [Root Module](#toc.xhtml_s78a)

Inside the **`src`** folder, you will find the **`main.ts`** file.

1.  `import {enableProdMode} from ‘@angular/core’;`
2.  `import {platformBrowserDynamic} from ‘@angular/platform-browser-dynamic’;`

4.  `import {AppModule} from ‘./app/app.module’;`
5.  `import {environment} from ‘./environments/environment’;`

7.  `if (environment.production) {`
8.  `   enableProdMode();`
9.  `}`

11.  `platformBrowserDynamic().bootstrapModule(AppModule)`
12.  `   .catch(err => console.error(err));`

*   This is the file that will start everything. Both the compilation and the bundling start from this file.

    It contains the statements which will start executing line by line.

*   Look at the last line. That’s what made the AppModule a Root Module.

# [Deployment](#toc.xhtml_s79a)

It’s much simpler than what you think. Execute the following command:

1.  `  ng build --prod`

Once the preceding command completes its execution, you will find a new folder called **`dist`**.

Inside that folder, you will see the **`Index.html`** file and a couple of JavaScript files. That’s what we have to deploy.

![](images/Image47376.jpg)

**Figure 8.16**

We will explore the files generated by the ng build command in a later chapter.

# [Other files in the project](#toc.xhtml_s80a)

The other files are self-explanatory:

*   **`package.json`**: Managing dependencies, metadata, and npm scripts.
*   **`tsconfig.json`**: TypeScript configuration files. You will notice multiple versions of these files.
    *   **`tsconfig.json`**: Contains standard TypeScript settings.
    *   **`tsconfig.app.json`**: Extends **`tsconfig.json`** and defines an additional setting for the development mode.
    *   **`tsconfig.app.json`**: Extends **`tsconfig.json`** and defines an additional setting for the testing mode.
*   **`angular.json`**: It’s a configuration file for Angular CLI. Based on the settings defined in this file, your **`ng`** command behaves. You will see it in action in this book from time to time.

# [Summary](#toc.xhtml_s81a)

We have crossed the first step of Angular learning and reached Station 5\. In this chapter, we have only covered the basics of creating an Angular Project:

*   In this chapter, we spoke about the manual process that we need to follow to create an Angular application.
*   We saw Angular CLI in action, and learned how easily we could create, execute, and build the Angular applications using CLI.
*   We decided to use the Visual Studio Code as a code editor for Angular coding.
*   We learned that the AppComponent would be considered the root component of our application by default, as mentioned in the bootstrap section of the AppModule.
*   We also learned that the AppModule is the root module of our application by default, as it was bootstrapped in the **`main.ts`** file.
*   We spoke about the **`ng build --prod`** command that compiles the application, and generates the optimized deployment-ready files.

Moving further, we will do so many labs, that this chapter will feel like “*A for apple, B for ball, C for cat….*”

# Discussion

The preceding project contains AppModule, which is our Angular root Module, and AppComponent, which is our Angular root Component.

Let’s have some bullet points about Components.

*   Components are the basic building blocks of an Angular application. The entire Angular application is built as a combination of reusable and compostable web components.
*   The first component, which serves as a container for all other future ones, is called the root component. By convention, it’s named AppComponent.

    For instance, check the composition in the following Angular application. AppComponent holds the Customer Component. The Customer Component is composed of CustomerAdd Component, Header Component, and Footer Component. Next, the CustomerAdd Component is formed by Section1 Component, Section2 Component2, and Section3 Component. Finally, Section3 Component is composed of Widget1 Component and Widget2 Component.

    ![](images/Image47536.jpg)

    **Figure 9.3**

*   Creating the components is equivalent to teaching your browser about a new HTML tag. By building it, you will be making a new HTML element.

Throughout this book, we will be creating a lot of components. So believe me when I say, “*by the end of this book, you will become a master of Component oriented design.*”

Don’t worry about the Angular Modules as of now.

# Lab – Continued

**Step 3: Create a new Component**

Inside the **`app`** folder, create a new folder called **`customer`**.

Now inside that folder, create a TypeScript file **`customer.component.ts`** with the following content:

1.  `import {Component} from ‘@angular/core’;`

3.  `@Component({`
4.  `   selector: ‘customer’,`
5.  `   templateUrl: ‘./customer.component.html’`
6.  `})`

8.  `export class CustomerComponent {`
9.  `}`

# Discussion – Continued

**`CustomerComponent`** class created in the preceding step is referred to as *Component class* or *Template class*. It is connected to a template file using the **`templateUrl`** property. Both of them together make the Angular Component.

![](images/Image47584.jpg)

**Figure 9.4**

There are the following three points worth discussing:

*   You may ask, “*Why folder?*” We created our component inside the Customer folder. Keeping the components inside in an independent folder is an industry-standard.
*   Secondly, you must have noticed **`component.ts`** in the class file name and **`component.html`** in the template file name. You may ask, is it a must? Once again, it’s just a practice followed in the industry. **`.ts`** and **`.html`** are there because it is the extension and **`.component`** is to identify that the file contains the component. Please note that it’s not a strict rule. You can name your file anything you wish.
*   **`templateUrl`** is not the only way to attach the template to the component class. Sometimes, you may find *template* instead of *templateUrl*. If your HTML is simple, you can use the *template* instead of *templateUrl* to reduce the overhead of creating another HTML file and connecting both. In real-time, for most of the scenarios, we always have *templateUrl*.

# Discussion – Continued

It’s time for **Directives**.

A Directive is an Angular concept which extends our HTML.

There are the following three kinds of directives:

1.  **Component Directives**

    You have already seen this one. **`AppComponent`** and **`CustomerComponent`** are component directives, also called components. Creating them is like creating a new HTML tag.

2.  **Attribute Directives**

    The name itself says what it is. It’s an attribute. We attach it to other HTML elements or Angular components, and its default behavior will get changed.

3.  **Structural Directives**

    They are attributes too, but they won’t change the HTML element or Angular component’s default behavior like the attribute directives. Instead, they will change the default structure of that element.

    Throughout this book, we will see all the three directives in action, including our custom ones.

For now, let’s look into ***`ngFor`**. Don’t miss that *.

*** is an indication that it’s a *structural directive*. We attach it to an element, and its structure will change. Let’s understand it with the demo.

# Lab – Continued

**Step 14: Display array**

Change the content of the **`customer.component.html`** to the following code:

1.  `<h1>Customer List</h1>`
2.  `<table border=”1”>`
3.  `   <tr>`
4.  `     <th>FName</th><th>LName</th>`
5.  `   </tr>`
6.  `   <tr *ngFor=”let item of Customers”>`
7.  `     <td>{{item.FName}}</td><td>{{item.LName}}</td>`
8.  `   </tr>`
9.  `</table>`

**Step 15: Check the output**

Execute the application and make sure that the list of customers is displayed.

![](images/Image47831.jpg)

**Figure 9.11**

# Discussion – Continued

***`ngFor`**

As you know, **`tr`** represents a single HTML row, but because we have attached the *****`ngFor`** directive to it, its structure got changed. Instead of one row, we got three rows as the collection had three elements.

Look at the expression assigned to *****`ngFor`***.*

That is – let the item of customers

*Item* will hold the individual *Customer* object and will act as the data to a particular row. Its values can be displayed dynamically using interpolation.

I request you to play with this demo a little bit more before reading further.

**Angular Module**

You already know the basic concept of the *module* in general.

*Anything defined inside one module will be private to that particular module and won’t be accessible in any other Module. We have to import and export it explicitly, in case we want to make anything available. We have seen it in the ES6 chapter.*

*Modules are almost everywhere. ES6 had it, Typescript had it, and even Angular has it. They all share the same principle of abstracting unnecessary things from the outside world.*

In the world of Angular, Angular modules are a logical encapsulation over components, directives, and pipes.

(You haven’t seen pipes yet, so don’t worry about it for now.)

If that’s the case, then it brings out the following two questions:

1.  ***`ngFor`** directive is encapsulated inside which Angular module?
2.  How come we can use *****`ngFor`** in the CustomerComponet because CustomerComponent is defined as part of AppModule, whereas *****`ngFor`** is not.

The answer is that it’s possible as the CustomerComponent is defined inside the AppModule, which imports **`BrowserModule`**, which exports *****`ngFor`**.

***Note:*** When I said, **`BrowserModule`** exported *****`ngFor`**, you must have got the impression that *****`ngFor`** is defined as part of **`BrowserModule`**.

Surprisingly that is not true, but at the same time, *BrowserModule* is indeed the one that made *****`ngFor`** accessible inside CustomerComponent.

We will talk about it in detail very soon.

In Angular, all the DOM events such as click, focus, and so on, are internally overridden. At the end of these events, Angular fires *Change Detection*, which re-renders the component based on the new state of the UI.

As of now, don’t worry much about *Change Detection*. We have a dedicated chapter for the same.

For implementing the first requirement, we will use an Angular concept called **Property binding**. **Property binding** lets us create a binding between the property of a control in the template and a component class property.

Property binding also introduces a concept, **input property**.

![](images/Image48059.jpg)

**Figure 9.15**

In the preceding figure, can you see a *href* written within the square brackets?

*href* is an input property of the *anchor* element. It is assigned to *MyURL,* where *MyURL* is a property defined in the Component class, and this concept is called **property binding**.

Have a confusion between **Property binding** and **interpolation**?

If yes, then relax. We will discuss it very soon.

# Discussion – Continued

*Property binding* and *interpolation* both let us display the latest value in the UI.

**Why interpolation?**

Interpolation makes binding simpler.

Let’s say we have a variable **`CompanyName`** in our component class, and we want to display it in a textbox after concatenating it with some hardcoded string.

With property binding, the syntax will be as follows:

1.  `<input type=”text” [value]= “’Hello, the company name is’ + CompanyName” />`

Now, the same code can be written with the interpolation as follows.

1.  `<input type=”text” value=”Hello, the company name is {{CompanyName}}” />`

You can easily say that with interpolation, it becomes easy. That left us with the question, why do we need property binding?

**Why property binding?**

First of all, you should know that *interpolation* gets converted to *property binding* in the behind.

You will have a hard time with interpolation when the value is a non-string value. To prove it, do one thing. Change *Property binding* to *interpolation* in our lab and check the output.

I am sure that it is not working. No matter what value the **`IsAddNew`** variable has, you must have noticed the **`data-entry`** div being invisible all the time.

Hence, if the value is a non-string value, then the input property is preferred as the interpolation interprets everything as a string.

# Lab – Continued

**Step 25: Connect the component properties to Form controls**

First, in the component class, create two properties.

1.  `……`
2.  `     FirstName:string;`
3.  `     LastName:string;`
4.  `     constructor() {`
5.  `…..`

Next, modify the controls in the template file as follows.

1.  `……`
2.  `  <h1>Add New Customer</h1>`
3.  `  First Name:`
4.  `  <input type=”text” name=”FName” (change)=”ChangeFirstName($event.target.value)”>`
5.  `  <br>`
6.  `  Last Name:`
7.  `  <input type=”text” name=”LName” (change)=”ChangeLastName($event.target.value)”>`
8.  `  <br><br>`
9.  `  <input type=”button” value=”Save” (click)=”Save()”> &nbsp;`
10.  `……`

Next, define **`ChangeFirstName`** and **`ChangeLastName`** in the component class as follows:

1.  `ChangeFirstName(value){`
2.  `       this.FirstName=value;`
3.  `    }`
4.  `     ChangeLastName(value){`
5.  `       this.LastName=value;`
6.  `    }`

In the preceding code, **`$event.target`** represents the control, which triggered the event, and that is the corresponding textbox.

Now, in the component class, the FirstName and LastName hold the latest state of the respective control all the time. We can call it the reverse one-way binding. Every-time the textbox value is changed, the variables get updated.

**Step 31: Modify Component class for Reset**

Create the **`Reset`** function as follows, and invoke them in the **`Save`** and **`Cancel`** method.

4.  `       this.LastName=””`
5.  `    }`

7.  `       this.Customers.push(new Customer(this.FirstName,this.LastName));`
