**Step 32: Check the output**

![](images/Image48369.jpg)

**Figure 9.22**

One more sad output. What happened? Let’s discuss this.

# Discussion – Continued

We created a **property binding** *(***one-way binding***)* between the **FirstName** textbox in the template and the **`FirstName`** property in the component class by writing the preceding code.

Because of this, the value of the **`FirstName`** property will be displayed in the **FirstName** textbox. Every time the **`FirstName`** property gets changed, the textbox value gets refreshed too.

The same applies to the **`LastName`** property too.

Now the thing is, both the variables are *unassigned* initially. Hence, the value is **`undefined`**. Let’s fill that gap by assigning them to an *empty* string initially.

**Step 33: Initialise controls with empty values**

For that, invoke the same **`Reset`** function in the constructor.

1.  `……`
2.  `     constructor() {`
3.  `       this.IsAddNew=false;`
4.  `       this.Reset();`
5.  `       this.Customers=new Array<Customer>();`
6.  `……`

**Step 34: Check the output**

![](images/Image48421.jpg)

**Figure 9.23**

# Discussion – Continued

In the preceding output, we have managed form functionality manually.

But that is not needed. Angular has inbuilt support for forms.

There are the following two approaches in Angular with regards to forms:

1.  Template-driven approach
2.  Model-driven approach, aka Reactive approach

*In the* ***Template-driven approach****, we will control the entire Form in the template file, and in the case of the* ***Reactive approach****, aka* ***Model-driven approach,*** *it happens in the Component class.*

In this lab, we will follow the Template-driven approach. Later, we will see a Model-driven approach.

**Two-way binding**

The template-driven approach provides us with **`ngModel`**, which is a directive for implementing the two-way binding.

1.  **`ngModel`** takes care of the binding between the control to properties.
2.  **`ngModel`** takes care of the undefined.

**Two-way binding** is a binding methodology where the UI and the Model are connected in both the directions.

The value of the model will be displayed in the UI. The UI gets the change for any model change, and if the UI gets changed, the model gets that change.

*ngModel* directive is defined inside the Angular module called the *FormsModule.* Hence, to use *ngModel* in our component, we need to import *FormsModule* in our AppModule.

# Lab – Continued

**Step 35: Import FormsModule**

Import **`FormsModule`** in the **`AppModule`** as follows:

1.  `……`
2.  `import {CustomerComponent} from ‘./Customer/customer.component’;`
3.  `import {FormsModule} from ‘@angular/forms’;`
4.  `@NgModule({`
5.  `   declarations: [AppComponent, CustomerComponent],`
6.  `   imports: [BrowserModule, FormsModule],`
7.  `   providers: [],`
8.  `……`

**Step 36: Remove unnecessary code**

Remove **`ChangeFirstName`** and **`ChangeLastName`** methods from the component class.

Also, remove the call to the **`Reset`** function in the constructor.

1.  `constructor() {`
2.  `     this.IsAddNew = false;`
3.  *`//this.Reset();`*
4.  `     this.Customers=new Array<Customer>();`
5.  `     this.Customers.push(new Customer(“Sukesh”,”Marla”));`
6.  `     this.Customers.push(new Customer(“Just”,”Compile”));`
7.  `     this.Customers.push(new Customer(“Ram”,”Lakhan”));`
8.  `  }`
9.  *`// ChangeFirstName(value){`*
10.  *`//   this.FirstName=value;`*
11.  *`//}`*
12.  *`// ChangeLastName(value){`*
13.  *`//   this.LastName=value;`*
14.  *`//}`*

**Step 37: Implement ngModel**

Change both the text boxes in the template to the following:

1.  `……`
2.  `   <h1>Add New Customer</h1>`
3.  `   First Name: <input type=”text” name=”FName” [(ngModel)]=”this.FirstName”><br>`
4.  `   Last Name: <input type=”text” name=”LName”`
5.  `   [(ngModel)]=”this.LastName”><br><br>`
6.  `   <input type=”button” value=”Save” (click)=”Save()”> &nbsp;`
7.  `……`

**Step 38: Check the output**

You will get the same output as before.

# Discussion – Continued

Next, we will implement validation. We will do it in the following two stages:

**Stage 1**: We will put a red border around the control if validation is failed.

**Stage 2**: Display an error message.

To complete stage 1, we will use CSS, and the best thing is that **`ngModel`** dynamically applies the CSS classes to controls based on the condition. We just have to define those classes.

![](images/Image48430.jpg)

**Figure 9.24**

You can notice the following three CSS classes attached to the control:

1.  **`ng-untouched`**: It means the control is not yet touched. Put a cursor inside the textbox and focus out. **`ng-untouched`** will get changed to **`ng-touched`**. Once it is touched, it cannot be untouched.
2.  **`ng-pristine`**: It means the control values are not modified. Try to change it to anything, and it will change to **`ng-dirty`**. Once it is dirty, it cannot be pristine again.
3.  **`ng-valid`**: It means the control contains a valid value, and it will be changed to **`ng-invalid`** when the validation is failed. Unlike the other two, this one keeps switching between valid and invalid.

**How to attach validation?**

For that, we will use the HTML attributes such as *required*, *minlength*, *pattern*, and so on.

# Lab – Continued

**Step 39: Define styles**

Open **`style.css`** in the **`src`** folder and put the following content:

1.  `.ng-invalid.ng-touched.ng-dirty{`
2.  `   border: 1px solid red;`
3.  `}`

**Step 40: Attach validation to controls**

Modify the content in the template as follows:

1.  `……`
2.  `<div [hidden]=”!IsAddNew”>`
3.  `   <h1>Add New Customer</h1>`
4.  `   First Name: <input type=”text” name=”FName”`
5.  `   [(ngModel)]=”this.FirstName” required minlength=”3”><br>`
6.  `   Last Name: <input type=”text” name=”LName”`
7.  `   [(ngModel)]=”this.LastName” required minlength=”3”><br><br>`
8.  `   <input type=”button” value=”Save” (click)=”Save()”> &nbsp;`
9.  `   <input type=”button” value=”Cancel” (click)=”Cancel()” >`
10.  `</div>`
11.  `……`

As per the preceding code, both the textboxes won’t accept empty values, and the value must contain a minimum of 3 characters.

**Step 41: Check the output.**

We have not completed the validation entirely yet. So, for now, don’t click the **`Save`** button.

Instead, just check the formatting, and for that, try the following:

1.  Make sure that the border is not displayed at the beginning, as the controls are not touched yet.
2.  Put a cursor in any textbox and focus out. The border should not be displayed as controls are not dirty yet.
3.  Put a cursor in any textbox, put some values with more than three characters, and focus out. The border should not be displayed as the controls are valid.
4.  Put a cursor in any textbox, put some values with less than three characters or leave it empty, and focus out. The border should be displayed as the controls are touched, dirty, and validation has failed.

# Discussion – Continued

Now that we are done with formatting the controls for validation, it’s time to display the error messages.

For that, we can use the properties exposed by the **`ngModel`** directive.

As you know, **`ngModel`** is a directive, and conceptually it’s a **`TypeScript`** *class*.

Every time we attach a directive to an element, one instance of that directive gets created and associated with that element. We can get the reference of that instance in our template using “*template reference variables.*”

For instance, look at the following code:

![](images/Image48553.jpg)

**Figure 9.25**

The **`ngModel`** class instance will provide us with the following seven properties representing the control state:

1.  **`touched`** – Will be true if the control is touched, or else false.
2.  **`untouched`** – Will be true if the control is untouched yet, or else false.
3.  **`pristine`** – Will be true if the control’s value is not changed yet, or else false.
4.  **`dirty`** – Will be true if the control’s value is not changed ever, or else false.
5.  **`valid`** – Will be true if the control’s value is valid, or else false.
6.  **`invalid`** – Will be true if the control’s value is invalid, or else false.
7.  **`error`**- Will be null if the control’s value is valid. It will be an object with the properties such as **`required`**, **`minlength`**, **`pattern`**, and so on, when the control value is invalid – each of which will be a boolean property indicating which validation failed.

Let’s do the demo and understand it.

# Lab – Continued

**Step 42: Attach validation message**

Add the error messages to both the controls as follows:

1.  `<div [hidden]=”!IsAddNew”>`
2.  `   <h1>Add New Customer</h1>`
3.  `   First Name:`
4.  `   <input type=”text” name=”FName”`
5.  `   [(ngModel)]=”this.FirstName” required minlength=”3”>`
6.  `   <span>Enter First Name</span>`
7.  `   <span>Minimum 3 characters needed for First Name</span>`
8.  `   <br>`
9.  `   Last Name:`
10.  `   <input type=”text” name=”LName”`
11.  `   [(ngModel)]=”this.LastName” required minlength=”3”>`
12.  `   <span>Enter Last Name</span>`
13.  `   <span>Minimum 3 characters needed for Last Name</span>`
14.  `   <br><br>`
15.  `   <input type=”button” value=”Save” (click)=”Save()”> &nbsp;`
16.  `   <input type=”button” value=”Cancel” (click)=”Cancel()” >`
17.  `</div>`

**Step 43: Control the error message with the ngModel instance**

As discussed, get the **`ngModel`** directive’s instance in the template reference variable, and control the error messages’ visibility.

1.  `……`
2.  `   <input type=”text” name=”FName”`
3.  `   [(ngModel)]=”this.FirstName” required minlength=”3” #F=”ngModel”>`
4.  `   <span [hidden]=”!(F.dirty && F.errors?.required)”>Enter First Name</span>`
5.  `   <span [hidden]=”!(F.dirty && F.errors?.minlength)”>Minimum 3 characters needed for First Name</span>`
6.  `……`

The **`LastName`** error messages will be an assignment to you. Make sure you try and finish it.

Now, if you are confused about what is assigned to the hidden, read the next discussion. Or else, directly jump to the next step.

# Discussion – Continued

Let’s talk about the value assigned to the **`hidden`** attribute in the preceding code:

1.  It will hide the element if the assigned expression yields to the *true* value. So, we need the final result as *false* to see the error message.
2.  As the entire expression is negated, we want the result of the expression inside the parenthesis to be *true* to see the error message.
3.  The parenthesis expression yields to *false* if the control value is never changed. In short, the control is not dirty. To see the error message, it has to be dirty.
4.  The parenthesis expression yields to *false* if the values are valid. If they are invalid, the *error* will return an object with either *required* as *true* or **`minlength`** as *true*.

# Lab – Continued

**Step 44: Check the output**

Save click validation is still pending. So, don’t jump to it.

Instead, just check the error messages, and for that, try the following:

1.  Make sure that error messages are invisible at the beginning as controls are not dirty.
2.  Put a cursor in any textbox and focus out. The error messages should remain invisible as the controls are not yet dirty.
3.  Put a cursor in any textbox and put some values. Now it’s dirty. Now, it should start displaying the error messages if any of the validation has failed.

# Discussion – Continued

Next, we have to control the **`Save`** button.

For that, we will use the **`ngForm`** directive.

We attach **`ngModel`** to the individual controls, whereas **`ngForm`** will be attached to the entire container.

The reference of it can be obtained using the *template reference variables*, and it will expose the similar properties as **`ngModel`**, but it represents the entire container state.

For example, as soon as any of the controls gets dirty, the container will get dirty.

Similarly, the container will be valid only if all the controls are valid.

**Step 45: Control the button**

Attach the **`ngForm`** directive to our top div element, which encapsulates the entire data-entry screen.

Next, get the reference of that directive using the template reference variables.

Finally, control the **`Save`** button using the **`ngForm`** properties as follows:

1.  `<div [hidden]=”!IsAddNew” ngForm #myForm=”ngForm”>`
2.  `   ………`
3.  `   <input type=”button” value=”Save” (click)=”Save()” [disabled]=”myForm.invalid”>   &nbsp;`
4.  `   <input type=”button” value=”Cancel” (click)=”Cancel()” >`
5.  `</div>`

You can clearly notice in the preceding code that we attached the **`ngForm`** directive first and then got the reference of it in the **`myForm`** variable, and finally disabled the **`Save`** button, if anything was invalid.

**Step 46: Check the output**

Execute the application, and you will notice a small issue. You will also see a red border around div in case of a validation error.

**Step 47: Remove the red border from the Form**

Just like **`ngModel`**, **`ngForm`** also attaches those six CSS classes dynamically depending on the condition. So, the red border was very much expected.

Change the **`style.css`** to the following:

1.  `.ng-invalid.ng-touched.ng-dirty:not([ngForm]){`
2.  `   border: 1px solid red;`
3.  `}`

**Step 48: Check the output**

Now you will be able to see the expected output. You will notice the **`Save`** button gets disabled when any of the control contains an invalid value.

The CSS combination that I used for the error messages was for demonstration purposes. I strongly recommend you to play with the different combinations and understand it correctly.

# Discussion – Continued

I believe you all are following and practicing every step genuinely and dedicatedly. In that situation, you must have noticed one small issue in the system that we’ve created so far.

*The data-entry Form is not resetting the state after Save and Cancel.*

Follow the following steps:

*   Open the application and click on **`AddNew`**.
*   Put some invalid value. You will see a red border around the textbox with the corresponding error messages.
*   Now click on **`Cancel`**.
*   Again, click on **`AddNew`**, and you will still notice those red borders and error messages.

![](images/Image48566.jpg)

**Figure 9.26**

It can be solved by using the **`reset`** function in the **`ngForm`** class.

So, we need the instance of **`ngForm`** in our component class to invoke its **`reset`** method.

To get the **`ngForm`** instance inside the component class, we can use the **`ViewChild`** decorator.

Let’s see the demo.

# Lab – Continued

**Step 49: Reset the complete form state**

Get access to the **`ngForm`** directive attached to the **`form`** tag and redefine the **`Reset`** function as follows:

1.  `import {Component, ViewChild} from ‘@angular/core’;`
2.  `……`
3.  `  LastName: string;`
4.  `   @ViewChild(‘myForm’) CustomerForm: NgForm;`
5.  `   constructor() {`
6.  `     this.IsAddNew = false;`
7.  `……`
8.  `   Reset() {`
9.  *`// this.FirstName = ‘’;`*
10.  *`// this.LastName = ‘’;`*
11.  `     this.CustomerForm.reset();`
12.  `  }`
13.  `   Save() {`
14.  `……`
15.  `……`

**Step 50: Check the output**

Now you will see everything working correctly.

![](images/Image48957.jpg)

**Figure 11.2**

# Discussion

The last time when we had the input controls, we followed a **template-driven approach**.

This time we will follow the **model-driven approach***,* also known as the **reactive approach**. It is a much cleaner approach, and unlike the **template-driven**, you will notice that the validation decisions will be made in the component class.

# Lab – Continued

**Step 6: Prepare the LoginComponent class for validation**

Validation in the reactive approach involves the following two steps:

1.  Creating a FormGroup.
2.  Creating FormControls inside FormGroup.

Change the code in the **`LoginComponent`** class to the following:

1.  `import {Component} from ‘@angular/core’;`
2.  `import {FormGroup, FormControl, Validators} from ‘@angular/forms’;`
3.  `@Component({`
4.  `   selector: ‘login’,`
5.  `   templateUrl: ‘./login.component.html’`
6.  `})`
7.  `export class LoginComponent {`
8.  `   loginForm = new FormGroup({`
9.  `     UserName: new FormControl(‘’,[Validators.required,Validators.minLength(6)]),`
10.  `     Password: new FormControl(‘’,[Validators.required,Validators.minLength(6)]),`
11.  `  });`
12.  `}`

***Note:*** Every **`FormControl`** property in the preceding code represents one control in our UI (template), and you will notice that the validations are mentioned in the component class instead of the template.

# Discussion – Continued

Hence, to make these directives accessible in **`CustomerComponent`**, we have to import **`ReactiveFormsModule`** in **`AppModule`**.

# Discussion – Continued

Now that we are ready with the Initial UI stuff, including what should happen if the credentials are invalid, the next question is, what should happen in case of the valid credentials?

The definite answer should be – after a successful login, we should redirect to **`CustomerComponent`** and maintain some state indicating that the user has authenticated.

It’s time to introduce a new Angular concept called **`Angular Routing`**.

Routing is a common terminology used in many technologies. The core idea behind it is displaying the different things in UI, based on the different URLs. We provide some data to the routing engine; using that routing engine, it displays UIs for different URLs.

# Lab – Continued

**Step 13: Define Routes**

First, create a folder in the app folder and call it **`Routes`**. Then create a file inside it **`app.routes.ts`** with the following code:

1.  `import {Routes} from ‘@angular/router’;`
2.  `import {LoginComponent} from ‘../Login/login.component’;`
3.  `import {CustomerComponent} from ‘../Customer/customer.component’;`
4.  `export const AppRoutes:Routes=[`
5.  `   {path:’login’,component:LoginComponent},`
6.  `   {path:’customer’,component:CustomerComponent}`
7.  `];`

Isn’t the code intuitive enough? We have two simple routes for now – when the URL is login, **`LoginComponent`** will be displayed, and when it is customer, **`CustomerComponent`** will be displayed.

**Step 14: Import RouterModule**

Now, to configure routing in Angular, we need to import **`RouterModule`** in our **`AppModule`**.

1.  `……`
2.  `import {RouterModule} from ‘@angular/router’;`
3.  `import {AppRoutes} from ‘./Routes/app.routes’;`
4.  `@NgModule({`
5.  `   declarations: [AppComponent, CustomerComponent,LoginComponent],`
6.  `   imports: [BrowserModule, FormsModule,ReactiveFormsModule`
7.  `   ,RouterModule.forRoot(AppRoutes)],`
8.  `   providers: [],`
9.  `   bootstrap: [AppComponent],`
10.  `})`
11.  `export class AppModule {}`

As you can see, while importing **`RouterModule`**, we have passed our **`Routes`** to it.

# Discussion – Continued

As you can see, this time, the import is a little different. This time, we have not mentioned the class names in the import section of the **`NgModule`** decorator. Instead, we invoked the **`forRoot`** method of the **`RouterModule`** class, passing our **`Routes`** object as a parameter. It will configure the routing in the application.

**Where will these different components be displayed?**

We configured the routes in the application specifying what component will be displayed for what URL, but the question is, at what location in the UI will those Components be shown?

That’s where an Angular component **`RouterOutlet`** comes into the picture.

**`RouterOutlet`** will act as a placeholder for the other components.

Is it too much text, and is it confusing you? Let’s jump to the lab and discuss it over there.

2.  *`<!-- <login></login> -->`*

# Discussion – Continued

Let’s understand the entire flow:

![](images/Image49394.jpg)

**Figure 11.11**

1.  In the case of a SPA, the first request will be sent to the Server. It will send the initial *HTML* response to the client. In the case of the Angular application, it is an **`index.html`** file with **`AppComponent`**.
2.  As the routing is configured at the client-side, the Routing system searches the marching Route based on the URL.
3.  Based on the route information, the appropriate component will be loaded into the UI. In the case of the Angular application, the component will be loaded into the **`RouterOutlet`**.

5.  `   {path:’’,redirectTo:’login’,pathMatch:’full’}`
6.  `];`

# Discussion – Continued

**What is pathMatch?**

You can skip this explanation as it is not that important.

When **`pathMatch`** is set to **`full`**, the whole URL path needs to match and is consumed by the route matching algorithm.

# Discussion – Continued

We have finished our setup process, but we haven’t learned yet how to navigate. That can be done using the **`Router`** service.

We have seen the *Components*, *Directives*, and *Modules* in Angular. Now it’s time for *Services*.

Soon in this book, we will be creating our custom services, and at that time, we will discuss in more detail about the services. For now, try to get as much as you can and jump to the next step in the lab.

So, let’s start with understanding what exactly a *Service* is?

*A* ***Service*** *is an Angular feature which represents the Injectable Application logic.*

Let’s separate both the words.

**Application logic**

It can be any application logic.

**Example**: It can be a logic, which will log the messages somewhere, or it can be one that will help us communicate with the external APIs, or it can be one that will help us create the files in the Server.

As the best practice in Angular Application, logics should not be written as a part of the components. It should be moved into Services. We will soon move all the logic in the **`LoginComponent`** to an independent service.

**Injectable**

Look at the following image:

A patient needs Medicine.

That Medicine is injected into his body by the Doctor.

According to you, who is more knowledgeable with Medicine, the Doctor or the Patient? The answer is the Doctor (unless the Patient is also a doctor).

![](images/Image49508.jpg)

**Figure 11.14**

Imagine if the patients are supposed to be injecting themselves. Can you think of a problem? They may not realize the amount of Medicine to be injected. They may forget to change the needle.

By giving that responsibility to the doctors, a patient’s life becomes easier.

In the programming world, it’s called the **Dependency Injection** *–* the dependency will be injected into the various parts of the application externally.

![](images/Image49587.jpg)

**Figure 11.15**

The one who needs the object will not initialize the object; instead, it will only declare it, and the framework will initialize and inject the object at runtime.

In this case, the framework which will do this injection work is our Doctor. The needy part in the application is the Patient, and the Medicine is the object that the Patient needs.

Angular has an inbuilt support for the **Dependency injection**, aka *DI*, and the Angular services are *injectable*.

In short, we won’t create the instance of the Angular Service explicitly. Instead, Angular will take care of the instantiation of the service. Those service instances are injected wherever it is needed.

Still not very clear? Let’s get back to the lab.

We will talk more about it, once we start with our custom Service.

# Discussion – Continued

We have completed almost all the UI and navigation steps. Now, let’s get our Server (REST API) into action, and do some real-time authentication.

First of all, let’s understand how authentication exactly works in SPA:

**1 – Get the Authentication token from the Server**

![](images/Image49684.jpg)

**Figure 11.18**

![](images/Image49720.jpg)

**Figure 11.19**

**2 – Securing Routes at the client side**

![](images/Image49754.jpg)

**Figure 11.20**

![](images/Image49788.jpg)

**Figure 11.21**

The user will not be allowed to access secured Routes unless they have a proper token.

**3 – Securing Server resources**

So far, by using a REST API call, we got the Authentication token. That’s the only server call we had till now. A real-time application will have more API calls, such as very soon, we will make a REST call in **`CustomerComponent`** to bind the Customer list with the dynamic data.

Soon, you will see that too. The difference between these APIs and the authentication API will be security.

The Authentication API will be accessible to everyone, whereas the other APIs will be accessible to only the authenticated users. Hence, it is necessary that while doing the REST calls, we pass the token in the headers to the Server. The Server will send back the response only if the token is valid, or else it will return the error.

![](images/Image49849.jpg)

**Figure 11.22**

**What is a token in the preceding step?**

It is a string value created by the Server application and returned to the client. A Token acts as the *ID card* for the client. The client sends that token with every subsequent request to the Server.

The Server validates the token and returns a response only if the token is valid and not expired.

**How to assure that the token is secured?**

The first thing you need is the SSL, which makes sure that whatever is exchanged across the wire is encrypted.

Secondly, it depends on the standard that we follow for the token. The most used one is JWT, aka JSON web tokens.

The official definition of JWT is as follows:

*“****JSON Web Token*** *(****JWT****) is an open standard (RFC 7519) that defines a compact and self-contained way for securely transmitting information between parties as a JSON object. This information can be verified and trusted because it is digitally signed. JWTs can be signed using a secret (with the HMAC algorithm) or a public/private key pair using RSA or ECDSA.”*

As it is signed, it cannot be modified in between the transfers by anyone.

The token also has an expiry period. The Server will consider the token as invalid after the expiry period.

**Are we going to create a REST API for creating a token?**

We won’t build a REST API from scratch. We will provide you a sample demo-purpose REST API code, which will validate the username and password against some static values and then return the JWT token in response, in case of a valid user.

**Where is the demo server code?**

You will learn that in the next step.

# Discussion – Continued

Every Component and Directive in Angular belongs to some Angular Module.

**But what about the Services?**

The Services only work if **`AngularInjectors`** are configured with **`providers`**.

**What are Injector and Provider?**

The *injector* is the one who will create and inject the instance of service wherever needed based on the *provider* configured.

We will talk more about the *injector* in the next chapter.

A *provider* is an instruction to the *Dependency Injection* system of the Angular Framework to decide on how to obtain a dependency value.

**How to configure injectors with the provider?**

There are the following three ways to do it:

1.  The *Component* decorator lets us do that.
2.  The *NgModule* decorator enables us to do that.
3.  The *Injectable* decorator enables us to do that.
4.  Create an independent *Angular Module* and configure the *injector* in its **`NgModule`** decorator and then *import* that *Angular Module* in our *Angular Module*.

We will talk about all the four approaches in this book. This time let’s look into the fourth one.

**Custom Angular Module and import approach**

The first three approaches are pretty simple and straight forward approaches. The fourth one is a little strange. It asks us to create a custom *Angular module* and then using the third approach, we will configure the *injector* and finally import that *module* in our *Angular module*. When we import one *Angular module* in another, their *providers* get merged.

Those modules are logically termed as **Service Modules**.

You must be wondering why such a long cut. Let me give you a scenario:

1.  Let’s say, we have a *ServiceX* on which our *ABCDComponent* is dependent. To use *ServiceX* in the *ABCDComponent*, we must configure the *injector* using one of the first three approaches.
2.  Now the surprise is, *ServiceX* is dependent on *ServiceY* and *ServiceZ*. Hence, configuring the *injector* only for *ServiceX* won’t be enough. We also need to configure the *Injector* for *ServiceY* and *ServiceZ,* and that’s true for every other dependency on which *ServiceY* and *ServiceZ* are dependent.

Isn’t it too much work for the consumers of the service, that is for the component *ABCDComponent*?

1.  Hence, the programmer who creates *services* will create an *Angular module*. Let’s call it *ServiceModule*.
2.  He will configure the *injector* for *ServiceX* and all the other *Services* in the hierarchy in the *NgModule* decorator of that *ServiceModule*.
3.  The programmer, who will work on the *ABCDComponent* will just *import* that *ServiceModule* in the *module*, which holds the *ABCDComponent*.
4.  Internally, all the providers defined in the *ServiceModule* will get merged with the *ABCDComponent’sModule* and thus make the developers’ life easier.

It’s a handy approach when it comes to the reusable libraries.

Usually, whenever you think about using a reusable third-party service, you always find an *Angular module* which needs to be imported into the target *Angular module*.

Most importantly, let me tell you, this concept is not new to you. You already imported the Browser Module in **`AppModule`**, responsible for configuring the *injector* for the DOM manipulation services.

Also, the same solution is followed by you when it comes to the *RouterModule*. It is imported in the *AppModule,* so the *injector* is able to inject the instance of *RouterService* in the Customer Component.

**Do we have a similar ServiceModule shortcut for HttpClient?**

*   Internally, **`HttpClient`** Service is dependent on some other Angular services.
*   So registering the *injector* for **`HttpClient`** won’t be enough. We also need to register for all the other dependent Services.

Hence, the Angular team has made our job easy by having the **`HttpClient`** Module. Simply, import it in our **`AppModule`**, and it will be done.

It is common to have some confusion in understanding the preceding query. Revisit it after completing the next chapter, and it will get brighter.

# Lab – Continued

**Step 31: Import HttpClientModule in AppModule**

**Step 33: Invoke the API**

Change the login method in the **`LoginComponent`** to the following code:

1.  `……`
2.  `   login() {`
3.  `     this.httpClient.post(“http://localhost:4000/signin”,this.loginForm.value)`
4.  `     .subscribe((response:{Token:string})=>{`
5.  `       if(response.Token==undefined){`
6.  `         this.IsAuthenticatiionFailed = true;`
7.  `      }`
8.  `       else{`
9.  `         this.router.navigate([‘customer’]);`
10.  `      }`
11.  `    });`
12.  `  }`

# Discussion – Continued

The HTTP calls in Angular returns *Observables*.

In the preceding code, **`this.httpClient.post(……)`** returns *Observable*.

I assume, in your web development experience, you must have seen *callbacks* while doing asynchronous programming in the JavaScript world. Later, *Promise* came to the picture with more significant features. (You can check the comparison between those two in the ES6 chapter.)

Angular uses *RxJS Observables* for this purpose.

I believe you remember RxJS. It is one of the libraries that the Angular Framework is dependent on.

Don’t get confused with *RxJS and ReactJS*. ReactJs is different.

Secondly, RxJS is not a part of Angular. It’s an independent library and can be used in any other JavaScript-based applications as well.

RxJS is a vast topic. Covering it in this book will not be possible. I strongly recommend you learn Rxjs some time in the future using some online tutorials. It will make your Angular life a lot better. It’s not mandatory to know RxJS, but it’s better to learn it.

To get the preceding API call result, we need to subscribe to the observable, which we can see in the previous code.

# Lab – Continued

**Step 34: Check the output**

Test the applications for two set of values:

1.  Try some random username and password – you will get the “*Invalid Credentials*” error.
2.  Try with the user name as Admin1 and the password as Admin1 or with the user name as Admin2 and the password as Admin2\. Both are valid credentials, and you will be redirected to **`CustomerComponent`**.

But unfortunately, it won’t be as expected. No matter what credentials we put, we will always get “*Invalid Credentials*” as a response.

# Discussion – Continued

To understand the reason for the preceding problem, we need to debug our Angular Code.

It’s time to learn how to do it.

Fortunately, there is not much effort to be put here.

*Webpack* used by the *Angular CLI* is configured to export the *source map files* on the compilation.

The source map files are JSON files that contain information to map the transpiled code back to the source files.

**As a developer, what should I do? How will I debug?**

You won’t be doing anything extra. Everything is already configured.

For debugging, open the developer tools. In the sources tab, where you usually see our JavaScript files, you will find a tree with a **`webpack`** as a tree item. Expand it, and then you will find a tree item with the name “.”, inside which you will find your entire Angular application source code. Open any TypeScript file and put the breakpoints in a usual way.

![](images/Image50049.jpg)

**Figure 11.25**

In the following step, we will debug the **`LoginComponent`** to identify the cause for our error.

# Lab – Continued

**Step 35: Troubleshoot**

Open the developer tools in your browser and put a breakpoint in the login method of **`LoginComponent`**.

Next, put proper credentials, which are, Admin1 as username and Admin1 as password, and click on the **`Login`** button, and check the breakpoint.

![](images/Image50099.jpg)

**Figure 11.26**

I think you can see the reason now. We are trying to read the Token property from the response object where, in reality, it has a token property. (The case is different.)

2.  *`// .subscribe((response:{Token:string})=>{`*
3.  *`//   if(response.Token==undefined){`*
4.  `       .subscribe((response:{token:string})=>{`
5.  `         if(response.token==undefined){`

# Discussion – Continued

**Are we done with authentication?**

Give yourself 5 to 10 mins and try to find out what is missing. (No cheating. Don’t read the next line. Start thinking.)

**Did we really protect the Customer Component from an unauthorized access?**

Although we have **`LoginComponent`** communicating with a real-time API, our application is not indeed protected. You can still access **`CustomerComponent`** without logging in.

Try to do that. Execute the application. Now, without completing the login, try to access **`CustomerComponent`**.

To make it wholly protected, we have to persist the JWT token received from the API call at the client-side, then we have to secure the **`CustomerComponent`** route from an unauthorized access.

For persisting a JWT token, we can use the **local storage** at the client-side.

For protecting the route, we will introduce a new Angular concept, **Route Guard**.

Let’s finish the storing of the token part first.

# Lab – Continued

**Step 38: Store the token**

Modify the login method in **`LoginComponent`** to the following code:

1.  `……`
2.  `login() {`
3.  `     this.httpClient.post(“http://localhost:4000/signin”,this.loginForm.value)`
4.  *`// .subscribe((response:{Token:string})=>{`*
5.  *`//   if(response.Token==undefined){`*
6.  `       .subscribe((response:{token:string})=>{`
7.  `         if(response.token==undefined){`
8.  `         this.IsAuthenticatiionFailed = true;`
9.  `      }`
10.  `       else{`
11.  `         localStorage.token=response.token;`
12.  `         this.router.navigate([‘customer’]);`
13.  `      }`
14.  `    });`
15.  `  }`
16.  `}`

# Discussion – Continued

**What is Route Guard?**

Angular’s route guards are interfaces that can tell the router whether or not it should allow the navigation to a requested route. They make this decision by looking for a *true* or *false* return value from a class which implements the given guard interface.

In our application, we need to protect the **`customer`** route from direct access in the following two scenarios:

1.  When the user is not logged in, which means the token won’t exist.
2.  When the user is logged in, but the token is expired.

Let’s start with the first point.

For that, we will use the **`CanActivate`** interface. We create the guard by creating a class implementing this interface. If the guard returns *true*, the navigation will continue, and if it returns *false*, the navigation will be cancelled. Let’s see it in action.

# Lab – Continued

**Step 39: Create AuthGuard**

Create a new folder **`Guards`** in the app folder and then create a file **`auth.guard.ts`** inside it with the following content. For now, we will return true. Later, we will put the dynamic logic.

1.  `import {ActivatedRouteSnapshot, CanActivate, RouterStateSnapshot}`
2.  `from ‘@angular/router’;`
3.  `export class AuthGuard implements CanActivate {`
4.  `   canActivate(route: ActivatedRouteSnapshot, state: RouterStateSnapshot) {`
5.  `     return true;`
6.  `  }`
7.  `}`

**Step 40: Attach Guard to Route**

Next, attach the route guard to the **`CustomerComponent`** in the **`Route.ts`** file as follows:

1.  `……`
2.  `import {AuthGuard} from ‘../Guards/auth.guard’;`
3.  `export const AppRoutes:Routes=[`
4.  `   {path:’login’,component:LoginComponent},`
5.  `   {path:’customer’,component:CustomerComponent,canActivate:[AuthGuard]},`
6.  `   {path:’’,redirectTo:’login’,pathMatch:’full’},`
7.  `……`

**Step 41: Check the output**

As of now, we don’t have any dynamic logic written. So, don’t expect any magic.

Switch back to the browser and enter **`http://localhost:[portnumber]/customer`** in the address bar.

![](images/Image50106.jpg)

**Figure 11.27**

Strangely, instead of getting the Customer Component, you will get a white screen.

Open the developer tools and check the error.

# Discussion – Continued

Have you seen **`AuthGuard`** object explicitly created anywhere?

Like **`Services`**, **`RouteGuards`** are *injectable*. The Angular DI system will create and inject the instance when needed.

As I said before, “*to get DI into action, we need to configure the Injector with the provider.*”

**How will we configure the** *injector* **this time?**

If you remember, sometime back, we discussed four ways to configure Injectors.

They are **Component**, **NgModule**, **Injectable decorator**, and **ServiceModule**.

The Route Guard instances need to be available globally and have to be a **singleton**. It means only one instance needs to be created and maintained across the application.

That can be achieved using the **Injectable** decorator or using the **`NgModule`** decorator of **`AppModule`**.

We will choose the *Injectable* decorator for now. Why? We will answer that later.

# Lab – Continued

**Step 42: Register the Route Guard**

Attach the **`Injectable`** decorator to the **`AuthGuard`** class as follows:

1.  `…..`
2.  `import {Injectable} from ‘@angular/core’;`
3.  `@Injectable({providedIn:’root’})`
4.  `export class AuthGuard implements CanActivate {`
5.  `……`

**Step 43: Check the output**

You will see everything is back to work. Also, test the application by returning false from the **`CanActivate`** method.

**Step 44: Add dynamic code in AuthGuard**

It’s time to stop the users from accessing **`CustomerComponent`** without a proper login.

For that, modify **`AuthGuard`** to the following:

1.  `import {ActivatedRouteSnapshot, CanActivate, RouterStateSnapshot, Router}`
2.  `from ‘@angular/router’;`
3.  `import {Injectable} from ‘@angular/core’;`
4.  `@Injectable({providedIn:’root’})`
5.  `export class AuthGuard implements CanActivate {`
6.  `   constructor(private router:Router){`
7.  `  }`
8.  `   canActivate(route: ActivatedRouteSnapshot, state: RouterStateSnapshot) {`
9.  `     if(!this.isAuthenticated()){`
10.  `       this.router.navigate([‘login’]);`
11.  `       return false;`
12.  `    }`
13.  `     return true;`
14.  `  }`
15.  `   isAuthenticated(): boolean {`
16.  `     const token = localStorage.token;`
17.  `     return token!=null || token!=undefined;`
18.  `  }`
19.  `}`

Spend some time and try to analyze the code.

# Discussion – Continued

Next, let’s focus on the second point with regards to protecting Routes – we have to stop the navigation if the token is expired.

**How to check if the JWT token is expired?**

We can achieve it with the help of a library called **`@auth0/angular-jwt`**.

# Lab – Continued

**Step 49: Install library for checking the Token expiry**

Using the **`npm install`**, download **`@auth0/angular-jwt`** as follows:

1.  `  npm install @auth0/angular-jwt --save`

**Step 50: Create IsExpired method**

Create the **`IsTokenExpired`** method in the **`AuthGuard`** class as follows:

1.  `import {JwtHelperService} from “@auth0/angular-jwt”;`
2.  `……`
3.  `   isTokenExpired():boolean{`
4.  `     const helper = new JwtHelperService();`
5.  `     return helper.isTokenExpired(localStorage.token)`
6.  `  }`

Next, change the **`canActivate`** method as follows:

1.  `……`
2.  `   canActivate(route: ActivatedRouteSnapshot, state: RouterStateSnapshot) {`
3.  `     if(!this.isAuthenticated() || this.isTokenExpired()){`
4.  `       this.router.navigate([‘login’]);`
5.  `       return false;`
6.  `    }`
7.  `     return true;`
8.  `  }`
9.  `……`

**Step 51: Check the output**

To test the preceding change, you need to change the server code.

1.  Stop the Server.
2.  Open the **`server.js`** file and change the value of **`jwtExpirySeconds`** to some small number.
3.  Start the Server.
4.  Now check the Angular application. Finish the login and route to **`CustomerComponent`**, and after the specified second, refresh the page. As the token is auto-expired, you will be redirected to the login component.

***Note:* Once your testing is done, make sure to set the expiration to some higher number such as 1200 and restart the Server. It makes our life easy. Or else you have to log in again and again.**

# Discussion – Continued

The next question is how will we get the logged in user’s first name. There are different ways to achieve it.

**API Call**

One way to get it is by performing another API call. As soon as the component is initialized in the Customer Component, we will make an API call passing the token and input, and the API will return the “*FirstName*” in the response.

**FirstName in the token**

The second approach involves the Server, including *FirstName,* as part of the token’s payload. The token will be decoded at the client-side and then used.

**IDToken**

It’s the third and one of the widely used approaches in the industry.

After successfully validating the credentials, the Server will create two tokens — one is the Authentication token, which will be needed for validating future requests. The client will include this token in the header of every request. The second is the ID token. The ID token will be a JWT token again, and it will contain the information about the user, such as *FirstName*.

For our demo, we will use the second approach. Our token will have *FirstName* embedded into it.

The next question is how to decode the token.

For that, we will use *jwt-decode,* which is a library for decoding the JWT tokens.

# Lab – Continued

**Step 54: install jwt-decode**

Install it using the “**`npm install jwt-decode@2.2.0 --save`**” command.

**Step 55: Decode token and retrieve FirstName**

Import **`jwt-decode`** in **`CustomerComponent`**, followed by decoding in the constructor as follows:

1.  `………`
2.  `import {Customer} from ‘../Models/customer’;`
3.  `import * as jwt_decode from “jwt-decode”;`
4.  `@Component({`
5.  `   selector: ‘customer’,`
6.  `   templateUrl: ‘./customer.component.html’`
7.  `})`
8.  `………`
9.  `   LoginUserFirstName:string=”DummyFName”;`
10.  `   constructor() {`
11.  `     const decodedToken = jwt_decode(localStorage.token);`
12.  `     this.LoginUserFirstName=decodedToken.firstName;`
13.  `     this.IsAddNew = false;`
14.  `………`

**Step 56: Check the output**

Check the output in the browser and enjoy the dynamic greeting message.

# [CHAPTER 12](#toc.xhtml_c12)

# [Authentication Service](#toc.xhtml_c12)

# [Introduction](#toc.xhtml_s92a)

In the previous chapter, we covered a couple of Angular inbuilt services such as Router, HTTP, and so on.

In our journey to Station 9, we will explore the custom services and answer many unanswered questions. The Station 9 journey is going to be the longest of all, and span across multiple chapters. So, have some patience and keep reading.

# [What is dependency?](#toc.xhtml_s93a)

Throughout the Station 9 journey, we will encounter the word dependency again and again.

So, let’s understand what it is first.

![](images/Image50300.jpg)

**Figure 12.1**

# [Understand Tightly coupled](#toc.xhtml_s94a)

The best way to understand tight coupling is through the human body.

![](images/Image50338.jpg)

**Figure 12.2**

Can you imagine a human replacing his hand with stronger hands when he/she wants to do some heavy work or replace his brain with an extraordinary one at the exam time?

It’s not possible because our body is tightly connected with the other body parts, but can you imagine how great it would be if that were possible?

The same principle applies to the programming world. Look at the following code:

1.  `class EmailLayer{`
2.  `     sendEmail(){}`
3.  `}`

5.  `class LogicLayer{`
6.  `     Save(){`
7.  `         let b:EmailLayer=new EmailLayer();`
8.  `         b.sendEmail();`
9.  `    }`
10.  `}`

12.  *`//UI Code`*
13.  `let l:LogicLayer=new LogicLayer();`
14.  `l.Save();`

In the preceding code, you can see that the class **`LoginLayer`** directly connects with the class **`EmailLayer`**.

Now, try to answer the following questions:

1.  What if there is an error in the **`EmailLayer`** class? Will you be able to test the save method of the **`LogicLayer`** class?
2.  Imagine another UI where the same **`LogicLayer`** class can be reused, but instead of Email, it wants SMS notification. Could it be possible to achieve it?

The answer to both the preceding questions is simply no, because, similar to the human body parts, **`LoginLayer`** and **`EmailLayer`** are tightly coupled.

# [Understand loosely coupled](#toc.xhtml_s95a)

Imagine a motor vehicle.

![](images/Image50376.jpg)

**Figure 12.3**

Unlike the human body, a motor vehicle is more loosely connected with its parts.

The tyres can be replaced with the others, or the engine can be swapped with some other based on the need.

It’s possible because a motor vehicle is loosely connected with its parts.

Imagine a situation where they were tightly connected. If a tyre gets damaged, then we have to replace the motor vehicle itself. It will not at all be cost-effective.

The same principle applies to the programming world.

Look at the following code:

1.  `abstract class NotificationLayer{`
2.  `     abstract Notify():void;`
3.  `}`

5.  `class EmailLayer extends NotificationLayer{`
6.  `     Notify() {`
7.  *`//Send email code`*
8.  `    }`
9.  `}`
10.  `class SMSLayer extends NotificationLayer{`
11.  `     Notify() {`
12.  *`//Send SMS code`*
13.  `    }`
14.  `}`

16.  `class LogicLayer{`
17.  `     constructor(private notificationLayer:NotificationLayer){`
18.  `    }`
19.  `     Save(){`
20.  `         this.notificationLayer.Notify();`
21.  `    }`
22.  `}`
23.  *`//UI Code`*
24.  `let l:LogicLayer=new LogicLayer(new EmailLayer());`
25.  `l.Save();`

In the preceding code, **`LogicLayer`** is loosely connected with the **`NotificationLayer`**.

As a result, the same **`LogicLayer`** can be used with both **`EmailLayer`** and **`SMSLayer`**. It will be compatible with all the derived classes of **`NotificationLayer`**.

# [Understanding DI](#toc.xhtml_s96a)

*DI*, or *dependency injection*, is a pattern of injecting the dependency instance into the system by the external framework known as **IOCcontainers** or **DI framework**.

![](images/Image50453.jpg)

**Figure 12.4**

Nowadays, most of the modern technologies are equipped with **DIframeworks**. We already saw the demonstration of DI in *[Chapter 11: Authentication](#c11.xhtml)*, where the instances of **`HttpClient`** and Router service were injected into **`LoginComponent`** by the Angular DI system and made available to us.

DI makes the application loosely coupled and thus makes it testable and maintainable.

In this Station 9 journey, you will learn DI in Angular in detail.

# [Angular Services](#toc.xhtml_s97a)

Let’s quickly revise the definition of the Angular service that we learned in the previous chapter.

They are an injectable application logic. It can be any logic, and the Angular DI system handles the instance creation, and injecting at the right place for us.

# [Lab 4: Angular services in the project](#toc.xhtml_s98a)

As of now, **`LoginComponent`** contains some code that should not be there as a best practice. As a best practice, a component should not be equipped with any business logic. Every business logic in the application should be moved into services. Let’s do the same.

**Step 1: Rename the project**

Create a new folder, called Services, in the **`app`** folder and then create a new file **`authentication.service.ts`** as follows:

1.  `export class AuthenticationService{`
2.  `}`

4.  `……`
5.  `   constructor(private authenticationService:AuthenticationService,`
6.  `     private router: Router,private httpClient:HttpClient) {`
7.  `     this.IsAuthenticatiionFailed = false;`
8.  `  }`
9.  `   login() {`
10.  `……`

**Step 4: Check the output**

It will break the application. Check the error in the developer tools.

![](images/Image50495.jpg)

**Figure 12.5**

# Discussion

It’s time to get back to the previous chapter’s statement once again.

“To make *DI* work, we have to configure the *injector* with the *provider*.”

**Injector**

It’s a theory, so don’t bother much about it. Go through it without much stress and later revise it again.

In Angular, at the core of the *DI framework*, we have an *Injector*:

*   The Angular Injector is responsible for instantiating and injecting the dependency into the various parts of the application, such as components and services.
*   As an application developer, we will not create the injectors manually. We only configure it with the provider*.*
*   Then the injector creates the instance of service using that provider*.*
*   Angular creates two kinds of injectors – **Module Injector** and **Element Injector**.
*   When the application is bootstrapped, Angular creates a *Module injector*, referred to as *root injector*.

    (There are other *child Module injectors* that also get created over time, but don’t worry about that for now as we haven’t seen multiple *Angular Modules* in action yet. For now, assume that there is only one *Module injector* getting created, and that is *root Injector.*)

*   Angular also creates an Element Injector for every component and maintains a tree hierarchy similar to the application’s component tree. In the current application, as of now, we have a straightforward component hierarchy. We have an **`AppComponent`**, which has two children – **`CustomerComponent`** and **`LoginComponent`**. Hence, we will have an element injector for **`AppComponent`** that will be a parent to the element injectors created for **`CustomerComponent`** and **`LoginComponent`**.
*   When a dependency is expected in the Angular application, the first request will be sent to the connected component’s element injector. If it cannot be resolved, it will be forwarded to the parent component’s element injector, and so on, until the dependency is resolved.
*   In case even the **`AppComponent`** injector fails to resolve the dependency, it will be forwarded to the module injector. The module injector also maintains a tree that we haven’t discussed yet. In case the module injector tree also fails to resolve the instance, Angular will throw the “*no provider…*” error.

**Provider**

A provider is an object which links a *DI token* with a *dependency*. You will see it in the next demo.

**DI Token**

*   The tokens are used to locate the provider.
*   We can create three types of tokens – *Type Token*, *String token*, and *Injection Token*.

    ![](images/Image50540.jpg)

    **Figure 12.6**

# Lab – Continued

**Step 5: Configure the injector for AuthenticationService**

Add **`AuthenticationService`** to the providers collection in the Component decorator as follows:

1.  `import {AuthenticationService} from ‘../Services/authentication.service’;`
2.  `@Component({`
3.  `   selector: ‘login’,`
4.  `   templateUrl: ‘./login.component.html’,`
5.  `   providers:[{provide: AuthenticationService, useClass: AuthenticationService}]`
6.  `})`
7.  `export class LoginComponent {`

# Discussion – Continued

Let’s understand what we did here.

![](images/Image50586.jpg)

**Figure 12.7**

By writing the preceding code, we added a new provider object to the *providers* collection of **`CustomerComponent`**, which is going to be used by the *CustomerComponent’s* element injector.

In the code, the *DI token* used is of the type *Type Token*.

In the preceding example, both **`provide`** and **`useClass`** hold the same value, but both can be different if needed.

Based on the current configuration, the injector will inject the instance of **`CustomerService`** (because that’s what is mentioned in **`useClass`**) when we expect **`CustomerService`** (because that’s what is mentioned in the *provide*). The instance created will be destroyed when the **`CustomerComponent`** instance is destroyed.

**Short syntax**

When both the attributes (**`provide`** and **`useClass`**) share the same value, we can use a simpler syntax.

1.  `……`
2.  `import {AuthenticationService} from ‘../Services/authentication.service’;`
3.  `@Component({`
4.  `   selector: ‘login’,`
5.  `   templateUrl: ‘./login.component.html’,`
6.  `   providers[AuthenticationService]`
7.  `})`
8.  `export class LoginComponent {`

We will explore the other types of *DI tokens* later in this book.

This time we have used a generic version of the post method instead of the non-generic one.

The non-generic version returns **`Observable<object>`**, whereas the generic one returns **`Observable<T>`**. Using the generic one is the best practice.

# Discussion – Continued

Now, something is fascinating.

*We must add an Injectible decorator to AuthenticationService as well.*

Or else we will get an error like the following:

![](images/Image50597.jpg)

**Figure 12.8**

In the previous chapter, we mentioned three ways of configuring the injector – using the *Component decorator*, *ngModule decorator*, and the *Injectable decorator*.

While creating **`AuthGuard`**, we followed the **`Injectable`** decorator methodology.

This time, for the **`AuthenticationService`**, we followed the *Component decorator* methodology, but it is also demanding us to add the Injectable decorator.

Why so?

Because in Angular, *“every subscriber of the service must be decorated with the Injectable decorator.”*

It means everyone who wants to use the service must be decorated with an Injectable decorator.

An injectable decorator generates the necessary metadata about the constructor’s parameters that will be needed by Angular. Hence, it is essential to add the Injectable decorator to every Angular service that is dependent on some other service.

**If that’s the case, then why is the LoginComponent not decorated with the Injectable decorator?**

It’s because the preceding rule applies to only those subscribers who are otherwise not decorated with any other decorator.

**Does it mean every custom service in the application will be decorated with the Injectable decorator?**

Technically, *no*. We have to attach the Injectable decorator to the custom services only if they are dependent on some other service/s. The truth is that mostly all custom services are dependent on the other services. Hence, in a real-time project, you will find most of the services decorated with the **`Injectable`** decorator.

**After attaching the Injectable decorator, do we still need the providers in the Component decorator?**

Does configuring the *injectors* using the *Component* decorator seem useless to you as it still demands us to add the *Injectable* decorator to the *service*?

If yes, then there is a slight confusion in your understanding.

While configuring the injector for the **`AuthGuard`** service, we passed a parameter to the decorator.

1.  `  @Injectable({providedIn:’root’})`

By doing so, we are configuring the **root Injector**.

This time, we will only attach the decorator, but we won’t pass any parameter.

![](images/Image50687.jpg)

**Figure 12.9**

So, it won’t configure any injector. By specifying the provider at the component level, we configured the **element injector** of that component.

**What is the difference between the two configuration methods?**

When the root injector is configured to provide an instance of Service:

1.  Only one instance of that service will be created for the entire application.
2.  The same instance will be injected wherever it is expected.
3.  This instance will have the life span of the application. It will get destroyed only when the application is terminated.
4.  Services such as **`Logger`**, **`Authentication`**, **`RouteGuards`** are recommended to be configured at the root injector level. Those services will be used again and again in the system. Hence, it’s better to be maintained as a singleton (only one instance).

![](images/Image50694.jpg)

**Figure 12.10**

When the element injector is configured to provide an instance of service:

1.  One instance of the service will be created per component instance, and shared across all child components.
2.  The life of the service will depend on the life of a component instance. When that component is destroyed, the service instance also gets terminated.
3.  The Feature level services such as **`CustomerService`**, **`ProductServices`** are recommended to be configured in this way, as they won’t be needed after a specific component is destroyed.

![](images/Image50732.jpg)

**Figure 12.11**

**Isn’t it appropriate to configure the root injector for AuthenticationService instead of the element injector?**

Yes, it is. **`AuthenticationService`** is a service needed throughout the application, and hence it is recommended to have a singleton instance throughout the application. By configuring the injector at the component level, we made a mistake, which we will soon correct.

**What is unique about configuring the injectors using the NgModule decorator?**

Both the **`Injectable`** decorator and the **`NgModule`** decorator lets us configure the **Module Injectors**.

We will answer this question later. For now, continue with the lab. So far, we have learned about configuring the injectors only using the *Component decorator* and the *Injectable decorator*.

# Discussion – Continued

**Do you think we achieved our target?**

If you did some thinking, you will realize that our **`LoginComponent`** still contains some logic, such as the redirect logic, the decision-making logic, and so on.

It is a bad practice, and we will take it out using *Rxjs*.

Imagine the following process:

1.  **`AuthenticationService`** will make an HTTP call.
2.  If the credentials are invalid, the **`LoginComponent`** will be notified about it, and the **`LoginComponent`** will display an error message in UI.
3.  If the credentials are valid, then **`AuthenticationService`** will store the response in a *local storage* and redirect to the **`CustomerComponent`**.

What do you think about the preceding process?

Don’t you think we will get rid of every logic from **`LoginComponent`** and make it simpler if we do it?

It’s not a complicated task. It will be done using *RxJS* very quickly.

Let’s do it step by step.

# Lab – Continued

**Step 12: Handle Authentication success**

Let’s handle the successful authentication on the service side itself. Change the authenticate method in **`AuthenticateService`** as follows:

1.  `@Injectable()`
2.  `export class AuthenticationService {`
3.  *`// Change no 1 - Router Service injected`*
4.  `   constructor(private router: Router, private httpClient: HttpClient) {}`

6.  *`// Change no 2 - Return type is void`*
7.  `   authenticate(user: User): void {`

9.  *`// Change no 3 - Observable is subscribed`*
10.  `     this.httpClient`
11.  `       .post<AuthenticationResponse>(‘http://localhost:4000/signin’, user)`
12.  `       .subscribe((response: {token: string}) => {`
13.  `         if (response.token !== undefined) {`
14.  `           localStorage.token = response.token;`
15.  `           this.router.navigate([‘customer’]);`
16.  `        }`
17.  `      });`
18.  `  }`
19.  `}`

You will notice the following three changes:

1.  We are no more returning the observables.
2.  We are subscribing to the observables in the service itself.
3.  We are routing to **`CustomerComponent`** in the service itself.

# Discussion – Continued

A part of the work is done. Next, we need to notify the **`LoginComponent`** in case of invalid credentials.

For that, we will use the **`Subject`** class in *RxJS*. We will discuss the **`Subject`** in more detail after the demo.

Let’s do a demo and then talk more about it.

# Lab – Continued

**Step 13: Handle Invalid credentials**

Handle the else part of the authenticate method as follows:

1.  `……`
2.  `import {Subject} from ‘RxJS’;`
3.  `……`
4.  `export class AuthenticationService {`
5.  *`// Change 4 - Subject for dealing with invalid credentials`*
6.  `   isAuthenticationFailedSubject: Subject<boolean>;`
7.  *`// Change no 1 - Router Service injected`*
8.  `   constructor(private router: Router, private httpClient: HttpClient) {`
9.  `     this.isAuthenticationFailedSubject = new Subject<boolean>();`
10.  `  }`

12.  *`// Change no 2 - Return type is void`*
13.  `   authenticate(user: User): void {`
14.  *`// Change no 3 - Observable is subscribed`*
15.  `     this.httpClient`
16.  `       .post<AuthenticationResponse>(‘http://localhost:4000/signin’, user)`
17.  `       .subscribe((response: {token: string}) => {`
18.  `         if (response.token !== undefined) {`
19.  `           localStorage.token = response.token;`
20.  `           this.router.navigate([‘customer’]);`
21.  `        }else{`
22.  *`// Change 5 - Notify subscriber that credentials are invalid`*
23.  `           this.isAuthenticationFailedSubject.next(false);`
24.  `        }`
25.  `      });`
26.  `  }`
27.  `}`

In the preceding code, you can notice the following three new things:

1.  At the top, we declared an instance of the Subject passing Boolean as an argument.
2.  In the constructor it has been initialized.
3.  The subscribers are notified in the else part by invoking the next method of the Subject.

# Discussion – Continued

Why Boolean with *Subject*?

Well, in our use case, *Boolean* makes sense. It can be any data type, including the user-defined objects.

# Lab – Continued

There is no more need for logic in the login method. Simply invoke the authenticate function as follows:

1.  `login() {`
2.  `     this.authenticationService.authenticate(this.loginForm.value);`
3.  `  }`

**Step 15: Handle invalid credentials**

Subscribe to **`isAuthenticationFailedSubject`** in the constructor as follows:

1.  `constructor(private authenticationService: AuthenticationService) {`
2.  `     this.IsAuthenticatiionFailed = false;`
3.  `     this.authenticationService.isAuthenticationFailedSubject.subscribe((x) => {`
4.  `       this.IsAuthenticatiionFailed = x;`
5.  `    });`
6.  `  }`

**Step 16: Check the output**

Execute the application and check the updated output. Check with the invalid credentials.

Sadly you won’t get the expected output. I don’t want you to check the next step. Instead, try to debug the TypeScript file, and try to find out the issue by yourself.

**Step 17: Solve the error**

The issue is, in **`AuhenticationService`**, we are passing false as the value to the next method in case of the invalid credentials. It should be true in our case.

1.  `……`
2.  `    else{`
3.  *`// Change 5 - Notify subscriber that credentials are invalid`*
4.  `           this.isAuthenticationFailedSubject.next(true);`
5.  `        }`
6.  `……`

**Step 18: Check the output**

Now you will get the expected output.

# Discussion – Continued

Let’s understand the difference between *Observable* and *Subject*. But first, let’s explore the concept of *Observable* and *Observer* in *reactive programming*.

**Observable and Observer**

In the *reactive programming* world, we have a concept of *Observable* and *Observer*.

Think of Twitter. Let’s say you visit my Twitter account (**`@sukeshmarla`**), and click on the Follow button. What exactly happens then? Twitter adds you to my follower list. Whenever I tweet, all my followers get the notification. In this case, I (who you followed) am the *Observable*, and Twitter is the *Observer*, and the followers are the *consumers* or *Subscribers*.

The one which you observe is called the *Observable*. The *subscriber* subscribes for its changes.

The one who feeds new data into the observable is called the *Observer*. The *subscriber* gets a subscription because the *observer* feeds something into the *Observable*.

![](images/Image50855.jpg)

**Figure 12.12**

*Such programming methodology is called reactive programming – the needy won’t ask for the data continuously. He will subscribe to the changes. The program which is responsible for the data will react and publish the data whenever available.*

**Subject in Rxjs**

We have introduced *Rxjs* in one of the previous labs. It’s a *JavaScript library* for the *reactive kind of programming*.

In *RxJSJS*, we have a class called **`Subject`**, which represents both the reactive programming, *Observable* and *Observer*.

Let’s recollect our previous lab:

1.  We created the *Subject*.
2.  We subscribed to it in the *LoginComponent*. Hence, it is an *Observable***.**
3.  We fed data to it with the *next* method. Hence, it is an *Observer*.

**Rxjs Subject vs. Rxjs Observable**

In Rxjs, we also have a class called *Observable*, which represents the reactive programming *Observable***.**

RxJS *Observable* can only be subscribed, whereas the RxJS *Subjects* can be subscribed and can emit new values.

RxJS *Observables* won’t expose any method to feed the new data to it. In simple words, we do have the **`Subject.next`** method, but we don’t have **`Observable.next`**.

**Extracting Observable from Subject**

In the real-time projects, as the best practice, we never expose the subjects to the subscribers directly.

Imagine multiple subscribers subscribe to a subject, and one of the subscribers invokes the **`next`** method. Can you imagine what happens now? Every other subscriber will get notified of that.

Hence, it is recommended to declare *Subject* as private, and its underlying *Observable* is exposed to the *subscriber* for subscribing. The **`Subject`** class has a method *as Observable*, which will let us extract the underlying *Observable* from it.

Let’s see the solution.

# Lab – Continued

**Step 19: Convert the Subject to Observables**

Open **`AuthenticationService`** and mark the Subject as private and expose the Observables instead.

1.  *`// Change 4 - Subject for dealing with invalid credentials`*
2.  `   private isAuthenticationFailedSubject: Subject<boolean>;`
3.  *`// Change 6 - Declare Observable`*
4.  `   invalidCredentialsObservable$: Observable<boolean>;`

6.  *`// Change no 1 - Router Service injected`*
7.  `   constructor(private router: Router, private httpClient: HttpClient) {`
8.  `     this.isAuthenticationFailedSubject = new Subject<boolean>();`
9.  *`// Change 7 - Get Observable from Subject`*
10.  `     this.invalidCredentialsObservable$ = this.isAuthenticationFailedSubject.asObservable();`
11.  `  }`

***Note:* Adding “$” to the observable variables is a naming convention that is followed in the industry.**

**Step 20: Subscribe for Observables**

Now, in the Login Component, instead of Subject, subscribe to the Observable.

1.  `……`
2.  `   constructor(private authenticationService: AuthenticationService) {`
3.  `     this.IsAuthenticatiionFailed = false;`
4.  `     this.authenticationService.invalidCredentialsObservable$.subscribe((x) =>`
5.  `     {`
6.  `       this.IsAuthenticatiionFailed = x;`
7.  `    });`
8.  `  }`
9.  `……`

It’s a happy output again.

Step 21 is not the last in this lab. The lab will continue in the next chapter. We just finished one part of a long lab.

Let’s revise all the things that we have learned in this lab:

*   We revised the DI concept again, and learned that it makes our code loosely coupled, leading to more maintainable and testable code.
*   We Spoke about the Angular Injector, which is responsible for injecting the service instances into the Angular application.
*   We discussed the two kinds of Injectors – **Module** and **Element**.
*   We learned that both the Injectors maintain a hierarchy.
*   We learned how to configure service using an Injectable decorator and Component decorator.
*   We learned that both the **`NgModule`** and the **`Injectable`** decorator let us configure the module injectors, whereas the Component decorator configures the element injector.
*   We learned that we have to add the Injectable decorator to every Angular service dependent on some other service regardless of what injector is configured.
*   We learned that the root Injector creates a global singleton instance, whereas the element injector creates the instances that will be specific to the component instance (and its children).
*   We spoke about the short syntax for configuring the injectors when both the provide and the use Class have the same value.
*   We learned about reactive programming and how to implement it in Angular with the help of RxJS.
*   We learned that in reactive programming, the Observable is subscribed by a Subscriber, and the Observer feeds the data into the Observables.
*   We have seen a demonstration of the RxJS Subjects, which represents both the Observable and the Observer in reactive programming.
*   We learned that the RxJS Subjects would be marked as private in the real-time project, and the underlying observable will be exposed as a public member.

# [CHAPTER 13](#toc.xhtml_c13)

# [Unsubscribe](#toc.xhtml_c13)

# [Introduction](#toc.xhtml_s100a)

In this chapter, we will learn the importance and the steps to unsubscribe from an observable.

# [Why unsubscribe?](#toc.xhtml_s101a)

One important thing is that no matter what technology stack you are using, when it comes to the observer pattern, unsubscription is as vital as a subscription. Imagine that a couple of subscribers have subscribed to our observable, and some of the subscribers are no more interested in receiving the notifications.

Or better, let’s say some subscribers don’t even exist anymore. Does active subscription make sense in such a case? Definitely not. Keeping all those unnecessary subscriptions alive may lead to a memory leak.

*The best practice is, once the subscription is no more needed, it should be unsubscribed. Otherwise, it will lead to a memory leak.*

# [When and how to unsubscribe in Angular?](#toc.xhtml_s102a)

**How to unsubscribe RxJS observable subscriptions?**

Fortunately, the RxJS Subscription provides a way to unsubscribe it. We will see the demo soon.

**When should we unsubscribe?**

The next question is, in the case of our application, when should we unsubscribe.

In our case, once we do a successful login, we redirect to **`CustomerComponent`**, which will destroy the **`LoginComponent`**. Once **`LoginComponent`** has been destroyed, no point in keeping a subscription to **`invalidCredentialsObservable$`** afterwards.

Angular provides us the various life-cycle events, and one of them is **`ngOnDestroy`**, which will be executed when the component is destroyed. It is the perfect place to perform unsubscription.

We will explore the other life-cycle events as we move on.

# [Lab – Continued – Unsubscribing subscription](#toc.xhtml_s103a)

**Step 22: Implement ngOnDestroy method**

It’s a two-step process. First, implement the **LoginComponent** class with the **`OnDestroy`** interface, which will force you to define the **`ngOnDestroy`** method.

1.  `import {Component, OnDestroy} from ‘@angular/core’;`
2.  `……`
3.  `export class LoginComponent implements OnDestroy {`
4.  `……`
5.  `ngOnDestroy(): void {`
6.  `}`
7.  `……`

**Step 23: Unsubscribe from subscription**

Again it’s a two-step process. First, hold the subscription in a global variable and then, in the **`ngOnDestroy`** method, unsubscribe it.

1.  `……`
2.  `import {FormControl, FormGroup, Validators} from ‘@angular/forms’;`
3.  `import {subscription} from ‘RxJS’;`
4.  `……`
5.  `export class LoginComponent implements OnDestroy {`
6.  `invalidCredentialsSubscription: Subscription;`
7.  `……`
8.  `constructor(private authenticationService: AuthenticationService) {`
9.  `this.invalidCredentialsSubscription = this.authenticationService.` `invalidCredentialsObservable$.subscribe(`
10.  `(x) => {`
11.  `this.IsAuthenticatiionFailed = x;`
12.  `}`
13.  `);`
14.  `}`
15.  `ngOnDestroy(): void {`
16.  `this.invalidCredentialsSubscription.unsubscribe();`
17.  `}`
18.  `……`

# Discussion – Continued

Now that you understood how vital unsubscribing a subscription is, it’s time to look into a better approach to achieve the same. It can be done using an **async pipe**.

**What is the Angular pipe?**

*The pipe* is an Angular concept that will be used in the component templates to transform the data.

**What is the async pipe?**

Async pipe is an Angular pipe which internally subscribes to the *observable* and returns the latest value that **observable** has emitted. When the component is destroyed, the best part is the async pipe unsubscribes from the subscription automatically to avoid potential memory leaks.

For instance, let’s say we have an observable *ABCD*, which will return some value, and that value has to be displayed in UI. The known approach will be as follows.

1.  *`//In the Component class`*
2.  `this.somesubsctiption=ABCD.subscribe(`
3.  `(x) => {`
4.  `this.myValue = x;`
5.  `}`
6.  `);`
7.  `……`
8.  `ngOnDestroy(): void {`
9.  `this.invalidCredentialsSubscription.unsubscribe();`
10.  `}`
11.  `<!-- In Template -->`
12.  `<span>{{myValue}}</span>`

Using the async pipe, it will be as follows:

1.  `<span>{{ABCD | async}}</span>`

Which approach seems easier to you out of the preceding two?

Let’s implement the better one in our application.

# Lab – Continued

**Step 24: Implement Async pipe**

It will be a 3-step process:

*   In step 1, we will remove the unnecessary code from the **`LoginComponent`**.
*   In step 2, we will create a new global variable in the **`LoginComponent`** to hold the observable for representing the invalid credentials.
*   Finally, in step 3, we will use the async pipe in the template.

Let’s implement it.

1.  `export class LoginComponent` *`// implements OnDestroy`*
2.  *`/* Read: Implementing OnDestroy is not needed`*
3.  `as unsubscribing will be handled by async pipe */`
4.  `{`
5.  *`/*Read: No need to hold the subscription as observable`*
6.  `will be auto unsubscribed when the component is destroyed */`

8.  *`// invalidCredentialsSubscription: Subscription;`*

10.  `loginForm = new FormGroup({`
11.  `UserName: new FormControl(‘’, [`
12.  `Validators.required,`
13.  `Validators.minLength(6),`
14.  `]),`
15.  `Password: new FormControl(‘’, [`
16.  `Validators.required,`
17.  `Validators.minLength(6),`
18.  `]),`
19.  `});`

21.  *`/* Read: no boolean variable needed`*
22.  `instead we need Observable boolean */`
23.  `IsAuthenticatiionFailed$: Observable<boolean>;`
24.  *`// IsAuthenticatiionFailed: boolean;`*

26.  `constructor(private authenticationService: AuthenticationService) {`

28.  *`// this.IsAuthenticatiionFailed = false;`*
29.  `this.IsAuthenticatiionFailed$ = this.authenticationService.` `invalidCredentialsObservable$;`

31.  *`/* Read: No need for a manual subscription,`*
32.  `the async pipe will take care of the subscription */`

34.  *`// this.invalidCredentialsSubscription = this.authenticationService.` `invalidCredentialsObservable$.subscribe(`*
35.  *`// (x) => {`*
36.  *`// this.IsAuthenticatiionFailed = x;`*
37.  *`//}`*
38.  *`//);`*
39.  `}`
40.  *`// ngOnDestroy(): void {`*
41.  *`// this.invalidCredentialsSubscription.unsubscribe();`*
42.  *`//}`*
43.  `login() {`
44.  `this.authenticationService.authenticate(this.loginForm.value);`
45.  `}`
46.  `}`

Finally, let’s use the async pipe in the template.

1.  `<div class=”alert alert-danger”`
2.  `*ngIf=”IsAuthenticatiionFailed$|async” >`
3.  `Invalid User Name or Password`
4.  `</div>`

# [Summary](#toc.xhtml_s104a)

It was a very simple chapter that discussed unsubscription. We have covered the following topics in Angular in this chapter:

*   We learned that unsubscribing is very important as it will lead to a memory leak otherwise.
*   We learned how to unsubscribe from the RxJS subscriptions.
*   We learned about the **`ngOnDestroy`** life cycle event of the Angular component.
*   We had a quick talk on the Angular pipe. It is used to encapsulate the transformation logic in Angular (there is a separate chapter dedicated to it).
*   Finally, we discussed the async pipe.
