**Step 28: Check the output**

Execute the application. Try to login with proper credentials, but strangely, the application won’t redirect to **`CustomerComponent`**.

Open the developer tools. You will find the “*No provider for AuthenticationService*” error.

# Discussion – Continued

Currently, we have configured the element injector of **`LoginComponent`** for **`AuthenticationService`**.

Hence, **`LoginComponent`** has access to the **`AuthenticationService`***,* but that will not let **`CustomerComponent`** get access to **`AuthenticationService`***.*

**What to do to make that possible?**

That will be possible only if we do one of the following in the existing application:

1.  Configure the element injector of **`CustomerComponent`** with the **`AuthenticationService`** provider*.*
2.  Instead of configuring the element injector of the **`LoginComponent`***,* configure the element injector of **`AppComponent`**.
3.  Configure the root Injector with the **`AuthenticationService`** provider using the **`NgModule`** decorator.
4.  Configure the root Injector with the **`AuthenticationService`** provider using the **`Injectable`** decorator with the **`{providedIn: ‘root’}`** parameter.

**Way 1**

If we go with the first approach, we will end up with two instances of *AuthenticationService* being created – one for **`LoginComponent`** and one for **`CustomerComponent`**.

**`AuthenticationService`** is a kind of service that will be needed again and again in the application. Hence, it is recommended to keep one application-wide instance instead of multiple. Therefore, this approach is not recommended.

**Way 2, 3, 4**

Suppose we follow any of the other ways mentioned earlier. In that case, we don’t need a provider in the **`LoginComponent`***.* All the three approaches lead to a singleton instance of **`AuthenticationService`**, which will be available to the entire application.

**Way 2 – How it is singleton?**

As I said earlier, technically, the element injector makes the service instances available to a particular component and all of its child components. That service instance will get destroyed when the component instance gets destroyed. The interesting point is, **`AppComponent`** is our root Component, and hence it will never get destroyed. So ultimately, the services registered at the root Component logically become a global singleton instance.

But using the element injector for singleton instances is not a practice followed in the industry (although you can). Hence, we have to choose either way-3 or way-4.

**Way 3 vs. Way 4**

Both of these will serve the purpose. It will configure the root injector, and therefore maintains a single service.

The biggest question is, which one to choose between way-3 and way-4.

*provided in root* is introduced in Angular 6, and it is recommended. Before that, the only available option to configure the root injector was using the **`NgModule`** decorator.

**Why is the Injectable decorator recommended?**

When **`Injectable`** is used for configuring the injectors, then the service won’t be included in the final build if it is never used anywhere in the application. This concept is called **Tree Shaking***.*

We will talk about the importance of configuring the injectors using **`NgModule`** very soon.

**Step 32: Refactor Guard**

Now that we are refactoring everything, let’s refactor our guards too.

Move the **`isAuthenticated`** and **`isTokenExpired`** methods from the **`AuthGuard`** class to the **`AuthenticationService`** class.

1.  `import {JwtHelperService} from “@auth0/angular-jwt”;`
2.  `@Injectable({providedIn: ‘root’})`
3.  `export class AuthenticationService {`
4.  `……`
5.  `   isAuthenticated(): boolean {`
6.  `     const token = localStorage.token;`
7.  `     return token!=null || token!=undefined;`
8.  `  }`
9.  `   isTokenExpired(): boolean{`
10.  `     const helper = new JwtHelperService();`
11.  `     return helper.isTokenExpired(localStorage.token)`
12.  `  }`
13.  `   logout(){`
14.  `     localStorage.clear();`
15.  `  }`
16.  `}`

After that, refactor **`AuthGuard`** using **`AuthenticationService`** as follows:

1.  `@Injectable({providedIn: ‘root’})`
2.  `export class AuthGuard implements CanActivate {`
3.  `   constructor(`
4.  `     private router: Router,`
5.  `     private authenticationService: AuthenticationService`
6.  `  ) {}`
7.  `   canActivate(route: ActivatedRouteSnapshot, state: RouterStateSnapshot) {`
8.  `     if (!this.authenticationService.isAuthenticated() ||`
9.  `       this.authenticationService.isTokenExpired()`
10.  `    ) {`
11.  `       this.router.navigate([‘login’]);`
12.  `       return false;`
13.  `    }`
14.  `     return true;`
15.  `  }`
16.  `}`

Simply attach the autofocus attribute to any input control, and automatically the focus will be set on that control when the page is loaded.

1.  `  <label for=”UserName”>User Name: </label>`
2.  `         <input class=”form-control” autofocus`

4.  `……`

**Step 36: Enter on submit**

Another irritating thing in the application right now is the **`Login`** click. We have to take the click manually every time we want to submit. The end users usually prefer the enter key. Again it can be done purely with HTML.

It will be a two-step process:

First, convert the login button to the submit button from the standard button.

1.  `<input type=”submit” class=”btn btn-success” value=”Login”`
2.  `       (click)=”login()”  [disabled]=”loginForm.invalid” >`

Next, Instead of using **`div`** as a container for all the form controls, use the **`form`** tag.

1.  `    ……`

(Don’t try to implement the same solution in **`CustomerComponent`** as of now. It won’t work. We will do it shortly.)

# [Summary](#toc.xhtml_s107a)

This chapter was all about making the code better. We still covered some exciting things. Let’s look into them again:

*   We learned about the importance of writing logic in services by moving the existing code into services.
*   We again revised the element and the module injector in this chapter. As the element injector was not serving our requirement, we switched it to the module injector.
*   We learned that the element injector of **`AppComponent`** would indirectly create a singleton global instance, as it will never get destroyed because it is a root component. Secondly, by being a root, the service instances created by it will be accessible to all the child components.
*   We learned that for configuring the root injector, an **`Injectable`** decorator is preferred more than a **`NgModule`** decorator, as an Injectable decorator leads to tree shaking. Tree shaking is a step in the Angular build process that ensures all the unused services are removed from the final bundle.
*   We learned that we could leverage the HTML power in Angular by using the HTML’s **`autofocus`** attribute, **`form`** tag, and **`submit`** button.

*   We have encapsulated the **`IsAddNew`** and **`Customers`** with the observable, which will be subscribed by **`CustomerComponent`**.

You can find almost all the logic in **`CustomerComponent`** is now part of **`CustomerService`**.

Let’s finish the **`CustomerComponent`** code, and then it will become more apparent.

**Step 44: Check the output**

Execute the application. Do a successful login with the proper credentials. Once **`CustomerComponent`** is displayed, click on **`AddNew`**. Unfortunately, that won’t work.

Check the developer’s tools to check if there is any run-time error. To your surprise, there won’t be any.

# Discussion – Continued

Let’s troubleshoot the preceding code and try to understand the reason for it.

**Step 1**

Let’s examine the current value emitted by **`IsAddNew$`**.

In the **`CustomerComponent`** template, just after the **`nav`** element, add the **`h1`** tag as follows:

1.  `<h1>IsAddNew value is {{IsAddNew$|async}}</h1>`

Now, check the output.

![](images/Image51020.jpg)

**Figure 15.1**

It proves that **`IsAddNew$`** observable is properly subscribed as the emitted values are displayed in the **`h1`** tag.

**Step 2**

Next, set the default value to **`false`** in the service and see the output.

1.  `  constructor() {`
2.  `     this.IsAddNew$ = of(true);`
3.  `…….`

![](images/Image51203.jpg)

**Figure 15.2**

As expected, **`true`** is displayed, and as you can see, the visibility is also toggled now – *The customer list* is switched with the *Customer data entry*.

It proves that our **`IsAddNew$`** is working as expected.

Then, what could be the reason? Undo all the troubleshooting code, and let’s talk about it.

Our troubleshooting proved that it works correctly at the beginning, but not after the **`AddNew`** clicks.

Let’s analyze the code in the **`AddNew`** method.

1.  `AddNew() {`
2.  `     this.customerService.setAddNew();`
3.  `  }`

In the **`AddNew`** method, we have simply invoked the **`setAddNew`** method of **`CustomerService`**.

1.  `  setAddNew(){`
2.  `     this.IsAddNew$ = of(true);`
3.  `  }`

In the **`setAddNew`** method, we have simply reassigned the **`IsAddNew$`** with a new observable.

**Reason for the error**

It’s a simple *object-oriented programming* concept. The observable is a *class,* and so it is a *reference type*.

We have a variable **`IsAddNew$`** in the **`CustomerService`**. It points to an **`Observable`** object which is subscribed by the **`CustomerComponent`**, and it emits **`false`** in the beginning.

1.  When the **`AddNew`** button is clicked, **`CustomerService`** creates a ***new*** *Observable* using the *of* method and assigns it to the **`IsAddNew$`** variable.
2.  Technically, **`CustomerComponent`** has subscribed to the old observable object. **`CustomerComponent`** has no connection with this new object.

![](images/Image51210.jpg)

**Figure 15.3**

**Solution**

Instead of creating a new observable, the solution is to emit the value from the existing *observable*.

Technically, an **`Observable`** class in RxJS won’t expose any methods for updating the values.

I think you know the solution. Think about it.

Do you remember our previous discussion about the *Subject*?

As the *Subject* is both the *Observable* and the *Observer,* it can serve our purpose.

The following are the steps to accomplish it:

1.  **`CustomerService`** maintains a *Subject*.
2.  **`CustomerComponent`** will subscribe to the observable exposed by the preceding *Subject*.
3.  **`CustomerService`** emits the new value using the same *Subject*.

![](images/Image51255.jpg)

**Figure 15.4**

Now, let’s implement this logic in the code.

# Lab – Continued

**Step 45: Subject in the Service**

Create an object of **`Subject`** in the **`CustomerService`** and implement the logic mentioned earlier, as follows:

1.  `  export class CustomerService {`
2.  `   private Customers: Array<Customer>;`

4.  `   private IsAddNewSubject: Subject<boolean>;`
5.  `   IsAddNew$: Observable<boolean>;`
6.  `   Customers$: Observable<Array<Customer>>;`
7.  `   constructor() {`
8.  `     this.IsAddNewSubject = new Subject<boolean>();`
9.  `     this.IsAddNew$ = this.IsAddNewSubject.asObservable();`

11.  `     this.Customers = new Array<Customer>();`
12.  `………`
13.  `  }`

15.  `   setAddNew() {`
16.  `     this.IsAddNewSubject.next(true);`
17.  `  }`

19.  `   setList() {`
20.  `     this.IsAddNewSubject.next(false);`
21.  `  }`

23.  `   Save(customer: Customer) {`
24.  `     this.Customers.push(customer);`
25.  `     this.Customers$ = of(this.Customers);`
26.  `     this.IsAddNewSubject.next(false);`
27.  `  }`

**Step 46: Check the output**

After the preceding step, you will find **`AddNew`** working as expected. Now, click on **`Cancel`**.

**Discussion – Continued**

Although everything seems perfect, there is a small glitch in our lab. To find it out, let’s do a quick temporary change in the code.

In the **`CustomerComponent`** template immediately after the **`nav`** tag, add the **`h1`** tag as follows:

1.  `<h1>IsAddNew: {{IsAddNew$|async}}</h1>`

Now, check the output.

**Initial output**

![](images/Image51299.jpg)

**Figure 15.5**

As you can see, nothing is displayed.

**On Add New**

Click on the **`Add New`** button and check the output.

![](images/Image51348.jpg)

**Figure 15.6**

As expected, **`true`** is displayed.

**On Cancel**

Next, click on **`Cancel`** and check the output.

![](images/Image51390.jpg)

**Figure 15.7**

Strangely, now it is displaying **`false`**, which was also expected at the beginning. Instead, in the beginning, we got nothing.

**Did you realize the reason for such a weird behavior?**

Let’s do one more experiment.

Replace our experimental code with the following:

1.  `<h1>IsAddNew: {{(IsAddNew$|async).toString()}}</h1>`

This time, you will see everything broken. Check the developer tool. You will find the following run-time error:

![](images/Image51461.jpg)

**Figure 15.8**

*I***`sAddNew$`** observable emits the value only when it is fed with some data using the **`IsAddNewSubject.next`** method, and at the beginning, there no value is fed, and hence it is **`null`**.

That is why, we are getting the preceding run-time error.

**Reason for the empty value in the beginning**

The Angular interpolation displays empty if the value is **`null`**.

**How come** ***thelistsection*** **is invisible, and** ***thedataentrysection*** **is hidden at the beginning?**

In the **`CustomerComponent`** template, the **`list`** section is configured to become hidden when **`IsAddNew$`** emits **`true`**. Check the following code:

1.  `<div class=”card” [hidden]=”IsAddNew$|async”>`

At the beginning, **`IsAddNew$`** is emitting nothing, so **`IsAddNew$|async`** leads to the *null* value, which is not **`true`**; hence **`listsection`** remains visible.

On the other side, *the data entry section* is configured to become visible when **`IsAddNew$`** emits false with the following code’s help:

1.  `  <div class=”card” [hidden]=”!(IsAddNew$|async)” ngForm #myForm=”ngForm”>`

Do you know what is **`!null`** in JavaScript? Try to find out by displaying it in the console or alert.

You will find it as *true*, and hence the *data entry section* remains hidden at the beginning.

With that, you learned the glitch in the current application and also the reason behind it.

**Do we need to do anything about it?**

You can ignore this glitch as we are getting the output as expected, but it should be solved as a best practice.

It can be achieved using **`BehaviorSubject`** instead of the **`Subject`**.

Unlike **`Subject`**, the **`BehaviorSubject`** constructor accepts a parameter. That value will be emitted as soon as it is subscribed.

In simple words, with **`BehaviorSubject`***,* we will get the opportunity to assign (emit) the default values.

Let’s do the same.

# Lab – Continued

**Step 47: Change the Subject**

Open **`CustomerService`** and replace the **`Subject`** with **`BehaviorSubject`** as follows:

1.  `……`
2.  `   private Customers: Array<Customer>;`

4.  `   private IsAddNewSubject: BehaviorSubject<boolean>;`
5.  `   IsAddNew$: Observable<boolean>;`
6.  `   Customers$: Observable<Array<Customer>>;`
7.  `   constructor() {`
8.  `     this.IsAddNewSubject = new BehaviorSubject<boolean>(false);`
9.  `     this.IsAddNew$ = this.IsAddNewSubject.asObservable();`
10.  `……`

As you can see in the preceding code, we have passed false as the parameter to the **`BehaviorSubject`** constructor as we want the default value to be false.

# Discussion – Continued

Are we done or still missing something?

Unfortunately, there is one more issue that can easily be ignored but shouldn’t be. To find out the same, I would like to share the new way of debugging in Angular.

Follow the following steps one by one:

**Step 1: Select Customer Component**

Open the developer tools in chrome, navigate to the **`Elements`** tab, and finally select **`CustomerComponent`**. (You have to click it for selecting.)

![](images/Image51469.jpg)

**Figure 15.9**

**Step 2: Display the selected Component**

Now, get into the **`console`** tab and type *$0*.

![](images/Image51519.jpg)

**Figure 15.10**

It returns the currently selected DOM element.

(It will only work in the development environment.)

**Step 3: Get the Component**

Next, write the following code in the console:

![](images/Image51553.jpg)

**Figure 15.11**

Cool, isn’t it? It displays the instance of **`CustomerComponent`** attached to the selected element.

**Step 4: Explore** ***IsAddNew$***

First, expand **`CustomerComponent`** and then select **`IsAddNew$`**.

You will find a property called a **`source`**.

It is **`BehaviorSubject`**.

Expanding it, you will find a property called **`observers`**, representing the subscribers subscribed to the **`Observable`**.

![](images/Image51588.jpg)

**Figure 15.12**

In our case, you can see the two subscribers.

It means we have subscribed to **`IsAddNew$`** two times.

**Do you remember subscribing to it twice?**

Cross-check all your code. Forget about the two times. We haven’t **manually** subscribed to it even once.

Manually is bolded in the previous statement, as it does get auto-subscribed.

**Do you remember** ***asyncpipe*** **and us writing** ***{IsAddNew$|async}* in the template?**

That’s what was responsible for the auto subscription.

We wrote it twice in the template.

Once for *showing* and once for *hiding*. Every time an *async pipe* is used, the observables will be automatically subscribed.

As a best practice, we should avoid the unnecessary subscriptions.

It can be achieved by manually subscribing and unsubscribing, instead of using an *async pipe*. That would be too much manual work. There is another way, and that is, **`ngIf`** *and* **`async`** *as syntax*.

***ngIf and** ***async as***

To understand the solution, look at the following diagram:

![](images/Image51625.jpg)

**Figure 15.13**

First of all, let’s understand what it is, and then let’s talk about the use cases where this solution can be implemented:

1.  In this approach, we will attach the *****`ngIf`** directive to an element and assign it to the observable with the *async pipe* followed by *an alias name*. In the preceding diagram, we have a parent **`div`** element to which we have attached the *****`ngIf`** directive.
2.  When the observable emits a value, it will be stored in a variable, and that variable will only be accessible within the element. In our case, *XYZ* will hold the latest value emitted by the observable.

This approach’s drawback is that the observable must emit some value, and that value cannot be *false*, or else the elements will not render:

*   Let’s say *X$* observable in the preceding example returns a *boolean* value *false*. The element will not even get rendered in such a case. That’s how *****`ngIf`** works.
*   If the observable emits the *Boolean* value *true* or any non-null value, then the element gets rendered, and that value will be accessible using the variable *XYZ*. The scope of the *XYZ* variable is the element that is attached to the *****`ngIf`** directive.

Considering that fact, in the current scenario of **`IsAddNew$`***,* this solution won’t fit because, in our case, **`IsAddNew$`** emits *false* at the beginning. So, the entire parent element itself won’t render.

![](images/Image51665.jpg)

**Figure 15.14**

**Are you thinking about negating the value while using** ****ngIf*?**

Well, that’s not possible because negation won’t work with **`async`** *as* syntax.

***async as*** **may seems like a useless feature to you.**

It is not useless. If the initial value were *true*, we would have used it without any trouble.

**The new solution - *ngIf and else approach**

In this approach, we will use *****`ngIf`** with **`else`**. We already have an example of *****`ngIf`**.

Well, there is some extension to it. We can specify the *else* part, as follows:

1.  `<div class=”card” *ngIf=”!(IsAddNew$|async);else AddNewContainer”…`

Are you wondering what **`AddNewContainer`** is? It references the element that needs to be displayed when **`IsAddNew$`** emits a **`false`** value. In our case, it will be a reference to the *data entrydiv*.

In short, in Angular terms, it is a *template reference variable*.

**The problem in attaching the template reference variable to data entry div**

We have already used a *template reference variable* with that **`div`** element. Check the code:

1.  `<div class=”card” ngForm #myForm=”ngForm”>`

As you can see, the *template reference variable* is holding the reference of the **`ngForm`** directive attached to the element.

**Possible solutions**

*   So, the solution is to have another parent element surrounding the preceding **`div`** element. In doing so, we may disrupt the HTML structure, and our UI may get affected.
*   Fortunately, Angular has a provision to create a virtual parent element; the element which won’t affect the UI and just act as a parent. It is the **`ng-template`**. We can call it a silent parent.

So, without further delay, let’s implement it.

# Lab – Continued

**Step 49: Apply *ngIf with else**

Change the **`CustomerComponent`** template as follows:

1.  `……`
2.  `<div class=”container-fluid pt-3” >`
3.  `   <div class=”card” *ngIf=”!(IsAddNew$|async);else AddNewContainer”>`
4.  `     ……`
5.  `   </div>`
6.  `   <ng-template #AddNewContainer>`
7.  `     <div class=”card” ngForm #myForm=”ngForm”>`
8.  `     ……`
9.  `     </div>`
10.  `   </ng-template>`
11.  `</div>`

Finally, you will find the 100% working output. Also, check the number of subscriptions taking place, using the procedure followed in the previous discussion.

Before jumping to the next step, I want you to try adding a new record. Put some values into **`FirstName`** and **`LastName`** textboxes and save a new **`Employee`**. Fortunately, that will work perfectly fine too.

Take some break. Go and have some free time for yourself before reading the next statement.

# Discussion – Continued

Now there is confusion.

**Isn’t the same** ***IsAddNew$*** **theory applies to *Customers$*** **as well?**

**`Customers$`** is a variable in the **`CustomerService`** pointing to an observable object subscribed by the **`CustomerComponent`**. When the **`Save`** button is clicked, the new observable object is created and assigned to the **`Customers$`** variable.

In short, the one observable subscribed by **`CustomerComponent`** and the one pointed by **`Customers$`** after save are different. In a short statement, it should exhibit the same issue as the **`IsAddNew$`** issue we had before.

The confusing part is that it will not exhibit any issues. *save* will work without any problem.

![](images/Image51711.jpg)

**Figure 15.15**

Let’s dig more into it.

Let’s start with **`IsAddNew$`** and understand what was happening before the *Subject* or *BehaviorSubject* solution was implemented:

1.  As concluded before, the **`IsAddNew$`** variable was pointing to the two sets of observables, one at the beginning and one after the **`Add New`** action.
2.  Both of those observables were emitting the *Boolean* values.
3.  **`CustomerComponent`** has subscribed to the first observable, and hence it will get the values only emitted by the first **`observable`** and not the second.

Look at the complete flow in the following diagram:

![](images/Image51768.jpg)

**Figure 15.16**

The story of **`Customers$`** is a little different. (Check out the preceding diagram.)

1.  In the **`CustomerService`**, a class-level variable of type *Array of Customer* is defined.
2.  We also have an **`Observable`** in **`CustomerService`** called **`Customers$`**, which will emit the preceding class-level variables.
3.  **`CustomerComponent`** has subscribed to this previously mentioned **`Customers$`**, and so it will get the *reference* of the *Array of Customer* defined in the **`CustomerService`**.

    Please note that **`CustomerComponent`** will get the reference, not the value, as the *Array* is a reference type.

4.  In the **`Save`** method of **`CustomerService`**, we are adding a new **`Customer`** element to the *Array of Customer*. (Don’t forget that both the **`CustomerComponent`** and the **`CustomerService`** have references to this *Array*.)
5.  Then, reinitialize the **`Customers$`** variable with a new observable object. So now, there are two observable objects, and **`CustomerComponent`** has subscribed to the old one and not the new one. (We had a similar condition in the case of **`IsAddNew$`** -- hope you can recollect it. The solution that we implemented was using the Subject and making the same observable emit the new value.)
6.  But you know what, the preceding point (point no 5) doesn’t even matter. It doesn’t matter if we reinitialize it or use the **`Subject`** and make the old observable emit the updated Array.

    The reason is, with **`IsAddNew$`***,* we had a boolean, which was a value type, and this time it is an *Array*, which is a reference type, and both the **`CustomerComponent`** and the **`CustomerService`** are sharing that reference (check point no 3). Hence, if any *Array* gets changed, then the **`CustomerComponent`** can see that change.

![](images/Image51845.jpg)

**Figure 15.17**

That is the reason why everything was working as per the expectation without using the **`Subject`**.

*   We learned about RxJSBehaviorSubject in Angular.

*   We saw ***`ngIf`** **`with else`** in action.
*   We discussed the challenge we faced in emitting the reference types from the service.

# Discussion – Continued

Let’s analyze the current code step by step:

1.  **`CustomerComponent`** has a reference to **`Customer$`** observable in **`CustomerService`** with the help of the following code:
    1.  `this.Customers$ = this.customerService.Customers$;`
2.  This observable is subscribed using the **`asyncpipe`** as the following:
    1.  `……`
    2.  `<tr *ngFor=”let item of (Customers$|async)”>`
    3.  `……`
3.  **`Customer$`** is initialized in the constructor of **`CustomerService`**.
    1.  `this.Customers$ = of(this.Customers);`

The time when the preceding code executes, the **`Customers`** variable is not set with any value, or the better statement will be the **`Customers`** variable holds an **`undefined`** value.

4.  Hence, the subscriber will get the **`undefined`** as the emitted value. That’s the reason why the Customer *list* is showing empty.
5.  Next, in the *constructor* of the **`CustomerComponent`**, we have made the *API call* to get the customer collection from the server./p>
    1.  `this.httpClient`
    2.  `get<Array<Customer>>(``‘http://localhost:4000/customers’, {`
    3.  `headers: {‘x-access-token’: localStorage.token},`
    4.  `})`
    5.  `.subscribe(……);`
6.  After that, the **`Customers`** variable in the **`CustomerService`** is re-initialized with the API call response.
    1.  `subscribe((x) => {`
    2.  `this.Customers = x;`
    3.  `});`

Reinitializing the **`Customers`** variable doesn’t emit new values to the subscriber of the **`Customers$`** observable. Hence, the list remains empty.

![](images/Image52104.jpg)

**Figure 16.6**

We need a solution where as soon as the API returns the response, that response should be emitted to the **`CustomerComponent`**.

Do you know how to do it? Think about it for a while, as I believe you know how to do it.

# Lab – Continued

**Step 57: Create the Subject and the Observable in CustomerService**

Remove the **`Customers`** variable from the **`CustomerService`**.

Then, create a variable **`CustomersSubject`** of type **`Subject`** as follows:

1.  `……`
2.  `export class` `CustomerService {`
3.  *`// private Customers: Array<Customer>;`*

5.  `   private IsAddNewSubject: BehaviorSubject<boolean>;`
6.  `   IsAddNew$: Observable<boolean>;`
7.  `   private CustomersSubject: Subject<Array<Customer>>;`
8.  `   Customers$: Observable<Array<Customer>>;`
9.  `   constructor(private httpClient: HttpClient) {`
10.  `……`

**Step 58: Initialize the Subject and the Observable**

Initialize both the **`CustomersSubject`** and the **`Customers$`** in the **`CustomerService`** constructor, just like we did **`IsAddNewSubject`** and **`IsAddNew$`**.

1.  `constructor(private httpClient: HttpClient) {`
2.  `     this.IsAddNewSubject = new BehaviorSubject<boolean>(false);`
3.  `     this.IsAddNew$ = this.IsAddNewSubject.asObservable();`
4.  `     this.CustomersSubject = new Subject<Array<Customer>>();`
5.  `     this.Customers$ = this.CustomersSubject.asObservable();`

**Step 59: Emit Customers**

Emit the response retrieved from the API call using the next method of the **`Subject`** class as follows:

1.  `     this.httpClient`
2.  `       .get<Array<Customer>>(‘http://localhost:4000/customers’, {`
3.  `         headers: {‘x-access-token’: localStorage.token},`
4.  `      })`
5.  `       .subscribe((x) => {`
6.  `         this.CustomersSubject.next(x);`
7.  `      });`

2.  *`// this.Customers.push(customer);`*

4.  *`// this.IsAddNewSubject.next(false);`*
5.  `  }`

# Discussion – Continued

In the preceding output, you can see three rows. It means the API has returned a list with three records. This time let’s troubleshoot the problem in a new way.

Open the **`CustomerComponent`** template and then create the **`div`** element after the **`nav`** element as follows:

1.  `……`
2.  `</nav>`
3.  `<div>`
4.  `   {{Customers$|async}}`
5.  `</div>`
6.  `<div class=”container-fluid pt-3”>`
7.  `……`

Now check the output.

![](images/Image52214.jpg)

**Figure 16.8**

It’s not helping. It just proved that we are getting three objects but it’s not giving any details on those three.

What if I say there is a way to display its JSON-format representation?

That can be possible using the *json* pipe

*json pipe converts a value into its JSON-format representation.*

Apply the *json* pipe after the **`asyncpipe`** as follows:

1.  `<div>`
2.  `   {{Customers$|async|json}}`
3.  `</div>`

The preceding code indicates that the **`asyncpipe`** will be attached to **`Customer$`***,* and the **`jsonpipe`** will be attached to the response returned by the **`asyncpipe`**.

The sequence is crucial when multiple pipes are attached.

Now check the output.

![](images/Image52256.jpg)

**Figure 16.9**

**Were you able to understand why we were getting three empty rows?**

We are getting *firstName* and *lastName,* whereas, in the **`CustomerComponent`** template, we are displaying *FName* and *LName*. Our *model* and API response are not compatible. We have to transform the API response into our *model* before emitting it to the subscriber that is **`CustomerComponent`**. For that, we can use the **`map`** operator of *RxJS*.

# Lab – Continued

**Step 62: Use the map operator**

Use the map operator of RxJS to transform the actual response to the required response as follows:

1.  `……`
2.  `import {BehaviorSubject, Observable, Subject} from ‘RxJS’;`
3.  `import {map} from ‘RxJS/operators’;`
4.  `import {Customer} from ‘../Models/customer’;`
5.  `……`
6.  `     this.httpClient`
7.  `       .get<Array<{firstName: string, lastName: string}>>(‘http://localhost:4000/customers’, {`
8.  `         headers: {‘x-access-token’: localStorage.token},`
9.  `      }).`
10.  `       pipe(map(customerAPIResponse => {`
11.  `         return customerAPIResponse.map(customer => new Customer(customer.firstName, customer.lastName));`
12.  `      }))`
13.  `       .subscribe((x) => {`
14.  `         this.CustomersSubject.next(x);`
15.  `      });`
16.  `……`

# Discussion – Continued

**Are you wondering what Array <{firstName: string, lastName: string}> is?**

It represents an *Array of objects*—an object which will have two properties, **`firstName`** and **`lastname`**.

Please note that you may choose to create separate classes for holding the API responses, which will be the best practice.

But many a times in the application, we end up in a situation where we don’t want to create a separate class, but we need something dynamically. In such a case, we can use it in this way.

But for this scenario, it’s good to create a custom class for the same.

# Lab – Continued

**Step 63: Create CustomerItemResponse class**

Create a new file **`customer.response.ts`** in the **`Modes/HttpResponses`** folder with the following content:

1.  `export class CustomerItemResponse{`
2.  `   constructor(public firstName: string, public lastName: string) {`
3.  `  }`
4.  `}`

**Step 64: Use CustomerItemResponse class**

Rewrite our **`CustomerService`** API logic as follows:

1.  `……`
2.  `     this.httpClient`
3.  `       .get<Array<CustomerItemResponse>>(‘http://localhost:4000/customers’, {`
4.  `         headers: {‘x-access-token’: localStorage.token},`
5.  `      }).`
6.  `       pipe(map(customerAPIResponse => {`
7.  `         return customerAPIResponse.map(customer => new Customer(customer.firstName, customer.lastName));`
8.  `      }))`
9.  `       .subscribe((x) => {`
10.  `         this.CustomersSubject.next(x);`
11.  `      });`
12.  `……`

# Discussion – Continued

**Difference between the** **`Observable.pipe`** **and the** **`Observable.subscribe`**

The pipe method is for chaining *Observableoperators*, and the subscribe is for activating the *Observable* and listening for the emitted values.

*   In more exact words, we subscribe to the *observable* to get the emitted value. For that, the subscribe method is used.
*   Before it reaches the subscribe method, the emitted value pipe through the various operators who may alter the value or even stop further emitting. For that purpose, the *pipe* method is used.

    In the preceding case, we have configured the emitted record to pipe through the **`map`** operator. The **`map`** operator lets us transform the value to something else.

    As you can see, in the preceding code, we have transformed **`Array<CustomerItemResponse>`** to **`Array< customer>`**.

**Step 68: Save the Customer**

Make an API call in the **`Save`** method in **`CustomerService`** as follows:

1.  `Save(customer: Customer) {`
2.  *`// this.Customers.push(customer);`*
3.  *`// this.Customers$ = of(this.Customers);`*
4.  *`// this.IsAddNewSubject.next(false);`*
5.  `     const newCustomer: NewCustomer = new NewCustomer(`
6.  `       customer.FName,`
7.  `       customer.LName);`
8.  `     this.httpClient`
9.  `       .post(‘http://localhost:4000/customers’, newCustomer)`
10.  `       .subscribe(() => {`
11.  `         this.IsAddNewSubject.next(false);`
12.  `      });`
13.  `  }`

As you can see, this time, we have to use the **`post`** method instead of the **`get`** method of **`HttpClient`** as we want to post the data.

In the subscribe, we are emitting true value from the **`IsAddNewSubject`**, which will switch us back to the list screen.

**Step 69: Check the output**

Execute the application. Complete the login and get into **`CustomerComponent`**. Switch to the data entry screen by clicking on the **`Add New`** button. Enter some dummy values and click the **`Save`**.

It won’t work for sure.

Can you guess the reason? Try to put some thoughts, and find out the reason for it.

If you couldn’t find out the reason, then open the developer tools. It will show you the reason – the API is protected from an unauthorized access. We need to pass the token.

**Step 70: Pass the token**

Pass the token in the header similar to what we did during the get request.

1.  `this.httpClient`
2.  `       .post(‘http://localhost:4000/customers’, newCustomer, {`
3.  `         headers: {‘x-access-token’: localStorage.token},`
4.  `      })`
5.  `       .subscribe(() => {`
6.  `         this.IsAddNewSubject.next(false);`
7.  `      });`

**Step 71 – Check the output**

Try to create a new Customer with the first name as Gabbar and the last name as Singh. After Save, you will have a weird output like the following:

![](images/Image52365.jpg)

**Figure 16.11**

What just happened? Let’s discuss this.

# Discussion – Continued

Before you read further, I recommend you do a hard refresh (*F5*) in your browser.

Strangely, you will see the new record added, and therefore displayed in the list.

Let’s understand what is going on here, step by step.

Let’s start exploring what we get at the beginning:

1.  **`IsAddNew$`** is subscribed by the **`CustomerComponent`** using the **`asyncpipe`**, and the *****`ngIf`** directive will use the emitted value, which will be *false* at the beginning.
2.  The *****`ngIf`** directive lets the element get created, only when the value is **`true`**. Hence, only the list section gets rendered at the beginning, and the *form section* won’t even get rendered into the DOM.
3.  In the service, a list of customers is obtained from the API and emitted via **`Subject`**.
4.  That emitted list of customers gets displayed in the view.

![](images/Image52420.jpg)

**Figure 16.12**

Now, let’s understand what happens when we click on the **`Add New`** button.

![](images/Image52486.jpg)

**Figure 16.13**

1.  **`IsAddNew$`** emits **`false`**, making the list section get removed from the DOM and the **`form section`** get rendered.
2.  Now, no one is subscribed to **`Customers$`***.* At this point, if it emits something, there will not be anyone to catch it.

Finally, let’s take a look at the picture after *Save/Cancel*:

1.  **`IsAddNew$`** again emits *true*.
2.  Thus, the *form section* gets removed from DOM, and the *list section* gets re-created.
3.  Now the surprise is, this newly created *listsection* is not as the same old one.

    The old *list section* and the corresponding subscription were already gone completely when the **`Add New`** button was clicked.

    The new *list section* is also subscribed to **`Customer$`***,* but it won’t get the previously emitted value as it’s a new subscription.

![](images/Image52497.jpg)

**Figure 16.14**

The solution will be making a new API call after *Save* and emitting the whole list again.

# Lab – Continued

**Step 72: Create the LoadCustomers method**

As the logic for reading new customers will be the same as before (the one in the constructor), let’s create a reusable function and use it in both the places.

1.  `……`
2.  `     constructor(private httpClient: HttpClient) {`
3.  `     this.IsAddNewSubject = new BehaviorSubject<boolean>(false);`
4.  `     this.IsAddNew$ = this.IsAddNewSubject.asObservable();`
5.  `     this.CustomersSubject = new Subject<Array<Customer>>();`
6.  `     this.Customers$ = this.CustomersSubject.asObservable();`
7.  `     this.LoadCustomers();`
8.  `  }`
9.  `   private LoadCustomers(): void{`
10.  `     this.httpClient`
11.  `     .get<Array<CustomerItemResponse>>(‘http://localhost:4000/customers’, {`
12.  `       headers: {‘x-access-token’: localStorage.token},`
13.  `    }).pipe(`
14.  `       map((customerAPIResponse) => {`
15.  `         return customerAPIResponse.map(`
16.  `           (customer) => new Customer(customer.firstName, customer.lastName)`
17.  `        );`
18.  `      })`
19.  `    ).subscribe((x) => {`
20.  `       this.CustomersSubject.next(x);`
21.  `    });`
22.  `  }`
23.  `……`

**Step 73: Update the Save method**

Change the **`Save`** method in the **`CustomerService`** as follows:

1.  `  Save(customer: Customer) {`
2.  `     const newCustomer: NewCustomer = new NewCustomer(`
3.  `       customer.FName,`
4.  `       customer.LName`
5.  `    );`
6.  `     this.httpClient`
7.  `       .post(‘http://localhost:4000/customers’, newCustomer, {`
8.  `         headers: {‘x-access-token’: localStorage.token},`
9.  `      }).subscribe(() => {`
10.  `         this.LoadCustomers();`
11.  `         this.IsAddNewSubject.next(false);`
12.  `      });`
13.  `  }`

**Step 74: Check the output**

Finally, if your output is not as per the expectation, cross-check all the steps mentioned earlier, and we are on track again. You will find a similar issue with cancel too. Finish it as an assignment.

# [Summary](#toc.xhtml_s113a)

So finally, after so much thinking and coding, we were successful in implementing **`CustomerService`**. Let’s list all the topics we have covered in this chapter:

*   We used the **`HttpClient`** service again in our application.
*   We used the generic version of the **`HttpClient.get`** and the **`post`** method.
*   We saw the demonstration of the **`Subject`** again.
*   We discussed the token-based authentication and implemented the same in the application.
*   We discussed the json pipe. Using this pipe, we can display the content of an object using interpolation. They were only used for troubleshooting.
*   We learned that when it comes to multiple pipes, a sequence is essential.
*   We saw the demonstration of using an anonymous object as a generic parameter. It is not recommended but may be seen in the projects.
*   We learned that we use the subscribe method to get the emitted values and use the pipe method to pipe the values through the various operators that may alter the values or even stop further emitting.
*   We discussed the map operator in RxJS. Using it, we can transform the actual HTTP response to the desired one.

# [CHAPTER 17](#toc.xhtml_c17)

# [Token Interceptor](#toc.xhtml_c17)

# [Introduction](#toc.xhtml_s114a)

Is there anything that can be improved in the application that we have right now?

Go through the complete code once again as a revision before moving further. If you can find room for improvement, then excellent.

The problem is adding a token. Moving further, we have to do it in almost every API call. Do we have any better way to do it?

Yes, and that is the HTTP Interceptors. Using it, we can transform every outgoing request or incoming response in between.

We will create and register an interceptor in our demo, which will transform every outgoing request by adding a token in the header.

Excited to see it in action, let’s do it.

# [Lab – Continued – Token interceptor](#toc.xhtml_s115a)

**Step 75: Create TokenInterceptorService**

Inside the **`Services`** folder, create a new folder **`Interceptors`** and then create a new file, **`http.interceptor.ts`**, as follows:

1.  `import {HttpEvent, HttpHandler, HttpInterceptor, HttpRequest}`
2.  `                   from ‘@angular/common/http’;`
3.  `import {Injectable} from ‘@angular/core’;`
4.  `import {Observable} from ‘RxJS’;`

6.  `@Injectable()`
7.  `export class TokenInterceptorService implements HttpInterceptor {`

9.  `   constructor() {}`
10.  `   intercept(req: HttpRequest<any>, next: HttpHandler): Observable<HttpEvent<any>> {`

12.  `      const token = localStorage.token;`
13.  `      let newHeaders = req.headers;`
14.  `      if (token) {`
15.  *`// if token is not undefined`*
16.  `         newHeaders = newHeaders.append(‘x-access-token’, token);`
17.  `      }`

19.  *`// HttpRequests are immutable, so it cannot be altered`*
20.  *`// Hence we are creating a clone out of it along with adding headers`*
21.  `      const authReq = req.clone({headers: newHeaders});`
22.  `      return next.handle(authReq);`
23.  `  }`
24.  `}`

# Discussion – Continued

**Does it look like a service to you?**

It should be because it is a service. In the next step, we will configure the Injectors for it.

**Understand the code**

It is a simple code.

We have a class implementing the **`HttpInterceptor`** interface, which forces us to define the **`intercept`** method.

Inside the **`intercept`** method, we are *cloning* the existing *HTTP request* because we cannot modify the HTTP request directly as they are *immutable*. *Immutable* means they cannot be changed.

As you can see, we have passed the custom header to the **`clone`** method. Thus, the new request object will have the authentication headers attached to it.

**Which injector should be configured for TokenInterceptorService?**

As I said, we need to configure the injector for **`TokenInterceptorService`***. O*therwise, the Angular DI system won’t be able to inject the **`TokenInterceptorService`** instance. The question is, which injector should we configure?

Let’s revise our learning about Angular DI.

1.  So far, we have created a couple of services and configured both the module and the element injectors.
2.  We have used an Injectable decorator for configuring the module injector, and for the element injectors, we have used a **`Component`** decorator.
3.  So far, we have only one *Angular Module* in action, and that is our root Module. Hence, the only module injector we have with us is the root Injector, and it creates an application-wide singleton instance.
4.  We have configured the element injector of **`CustomerComponent`**. It creates a service instance with a scope of component life span.

In the future chapters, we will create more components and more modules and talk about the injectors once again.

Based on whatever you have learned about services, what do you think, which injector should be chosen for *Interceptors*?

It should be the root module injector*,* but this time there is a twist.

We cannot use an **`Injectable`** decorator for this purpose.

Do you remember how we configured the Injector for AuthenticationService at the very beginning?

1.  `providers:[{provide: AuthenticationService, useClass: AuthenticationService}`

Later, we learned that the short-syntax could be used instead of the long-syntax when both **`provide`** and **`useClass`** have the same value.

1.  `Providers: [AuthenticationService]`

Although an injectable decorator is the recommended approach because of the tree-shaking provision, we cannot use it as it won’t allow us to explicitly specify the DI token.

**Why do we need to specify the DI token explicitly for the Token interceptors?**

As we have discussed before, the interceptor lets us modify the request-response in between. Creating an interceptor is like hooking into the Angular’s default execution flow. Configuring the injectors for the interceptor won’t be as simple as it is for any regular Angular service. In the case of Interceptors, we need to specify the DI token (**`HTTP_INTERCEPTORS`**) explicitly.

**How to configure the root injector without using an Injectable decorator?**

So finally, you will see the configuring of the module injector with the help of the **`NgModule`** decorator.

# Lab – Continued

**Step 76: Configure the Injector**

Configure the root module injector in **`AppModule`** as follows:

1.  `import {HttpClientModule, HTTP_INTERCEPTORS} from ‘@angular/common/http’;`
2.  `……`
3.  `import {TokenInterceptorService} from ‘./Services/Interceptors/http.interceptor’;`
4.  `@NgModule({`
5.  `   ……`
6.  `   providers: [{provide: HTTP_INTERCEPTORS, useClass: TokenInterceptorService}],`
7.  `   bootstrap: [AppComponent],`
8.  `})`
9.  `export class AppModule {}`

**Step 77: Check the output**

As we are done with everything, it’s time to check the output. Switch to the browser and check the output.

You get the best output so far. “*A white screen*.”

![](images/Image52550.jpg)

**Figure 17.1**

Check developer tools. You will find a new kind of error.

![](images/Image52622.jpg)

**Figure 17.2**

We will talk about the multi providers in the next chapter. For now, let’s finish this lab.

**Step 78: Convert the regular provider to a multi-provider**

For that, attach the multi attribute to the provider with the value as true.

1.  `……`
2.  `   providers: [{provide: HTTP_INTERCEPTORS,`
3.  `               useClass: TokenInterceptorService, multi:true}],`
4.  `   bootstrap: [AppComponent],`
5.  `})`
6.  `export class AppModule {}`

**Step 79: Cleanup CustomerService**

We are not required to inject the token into the request explicitly as the token-interceptor is in place. With that, cleanup the **`CustomerService`** methods as follows:

1.  `……`
2.  `  private LoadCustomers(): void{`
3.  `     this.httpClient`
4.  `     .get<Array<CustomerItemResponse>>(‘http://localhost:4000/customers’).pipe(`
5.  `       map((customerAPIResponse) => {`
6.  `         return customerAPIResponse.map(`
7.  `           (customer) => new Customer(customer.firstName, customer.lastName));`
8.  `      })`
9.  `    ).subscribe((x) => {`
10.  `       this.CustomersSubject.next(x);`
11.  `    });`
12.  `  }`
13.  `……`
14.  `   Save(customer: Customer) {`
15.  `     const newCustomer: NewCustomer = new NewCustomer(`
16.  `       customer.FName,customer.LName);`
17.  `     this.httpClient`
18.  `       .post(‘http://localhost:4000/customers’, newCustomer).subscribe(() => {`
19.  `         this.LoadCustomers();`
20.  `         this.IsAddNewSubject.next(false);`
21.  `      });`
22.  `  }`
23.  `……`

**Step 80: Check the output**

Execute and confirm that both the customer list and the data entry screen are working correctly.

# [Summary](#toc.xhtml_s116a)

Let’s list all the topics we have covered in this chapter:

*   We learned how to create and configure the HTTP interceptors.
*   We spoke about the *HttpInterceptor* interface, which we need to implement for creating the interceptors.
*   We learned that we could not directly modify an HTTP request in the interceptor as they are immutable. Hence, we need to clone the existing request. We attach the headers to the cloned request and then pass that cloned request further in the pipeline.
*   We saw a demonstration of both the long and the short syntax used while configuring the injectors.
*   We learned that we explicitly need to specify the DI token – **`HTTP_INTERCEPTORS`** for configuring the injectors for the HTTP interceptors.
*   We learned that, although an injectable decorator is the recommended approach because of the tree-shaking provision, we cannot use it all the time for configuring the module injectors as it won’t allow us to specify the DI token explicitly.
*   We also learned the benefit of configuring the injectors using the **`NgModule`** decorator. It provides more configuration options compared to the **`Injectable`** decorator.
*   We learned that the injector needs to be configured with multi providers for the HTTP interceptors.

# [CHAPTER 18](#toc.xhtml_c18)

# [Multi Providers](#toc.xhtml_c18)

# [Introduction](#toc.xhtml_s117a)

In this chapter, we will discuss multi providers. This chapter can be skipped for now if you want.

# [Regular providers](#toc.xhtml_s118a)

After looking at the previous error in *[Chapter 17: Token Interceptor](#c17.xhtml)*, you must have understood that there is more than one kind of provider in Angular.

There is a regular provider, and then there is a multi-provider.

The provider we defined for **`CustomerService`**, **`AuthenticationService`**, and **`AuthGuard`** all falls under the regular providers.

It means, for one DI Token, one provider will be defined.

# [Multi Providers](#toc.xhtml_s119a)

For one DI token, multiple providers will be defined. When one DI token is expected to be injected in the application, the injector injects an array.

Let’s do a quick RND to understand the same.

Create a new service **`MultiRndService`** as follows:

1.  `@Injectable()`
2.  `export class MultiRndService{`
3.  `     m1(){`
4.  `       console.log(‘MultiService1’);`
5.  `    }`
6.  `}`

Now, create one more service, **`MultiRndService2`**, as follows:

1.  `@Injectable()`
2.  `export class MultiRndService2{`
3.  `     m2(){`
4.  `       console.log(‘MultiService2’);`
5.  `    }`
6.  `}`

Now, configure the root Injector with a regular provider as follows:

1.  `…….`
2.  `   providers: [{provide: HTTP_INTERCEPTORS,`
3.  `            useClass: TokenInterceptorService, multi: true},`
4.  `     {provide:MultiRndService, useClass: MultiRndService},`
5.  `     {provide:MultiRndService, useClass: MultiRndService2},`
6.  `  ],`
7.  `   bootstrap: [AppComponent],`
8.  `})`
9.  `export class AppModule {}`

As you can see, we have used the same DI token for both the providers.

Next, specify a parameter of the type **`MultiRndService`** in the constructor of **`Customer Component`**.

1.  `……`
2.  `constructor(private customerService: CustomerService,`
3.  `               private authenticationService: AuthenticationService,`
4.  `               private rndService: MultiRndService`
5.  `              ) {`
6.  `     const decodedToken = jwt_decode(localStorage.token);`
7.  `……`

Now, put a breakpoint inside the constructor, and let’s check what gets injected.

![](images/Image52720.jpg)

**Figure 18.1**

You can see that the instance of the *MultiRndService2* class got injected.

Before we proceed, I want to make your life easier by explaining how an incompatible type (MultiRndService2) was injected.

Well, remember the injection happens at runtime, and at runtime, everything is *JavaScript*, so no matter what you expect, anything can be injected.

Hence, the value in the *provide* and *useClass* doesn’t need to be compatible with each other.

In a real-time system, I would have defined a *base class* or an *interface* on top of both the *types* and used it as *a DI token*.

Now, let’s get back to our research. Attach the *multi* attribute with the *true* value to both the *providers* and check the output.

1.  `providers: [{provide: HTTP_INTERCEPTORS,`
2.  `  useClass: TokenInterceptorService, multi: true}`
3.  `     ,{provide:MultiRndService, useClass: MultiRndService, multi: true},`
4.  `     {provide:MultiRndService, useClass: MultiRndService2, multi: true},`
5.  `  ],`

![](images/Image52805.jpg)

**Figure 18.2**

As you can see, this time, the instances of both the classes got injected in the form of an array.

**What happens if we specify one of them as a multi-provider and one as a regular?**

We will get a “*cannot mix multi providers and regular providers*” error in such a case.

**Why are we getting the “cannot mix…” error while configuring the Injector for HTTP_INTERCEPTOR?**

We created an HTTP interceptor in *[Chapter 17: Token Interceptor](#c17.xhtml)* and then configured the module injector. The important point is we did it only once.

We have not created the multiple providers for **`HTTP_INTERCEPTOR`**.

If that is the case, why did we get the “*cannot mix*…” error when we created a regular provider?

If you remember, in one of the previous labs, we imported **`HttpClientModule`** in our **`AppModule`**.

(Check the imports section of the **`NgModule`** decorator in **`AppModule`**.)

When we import one Angular module into another, providers get merged. So in our case, all the providers defined in the **`HttpClientModule`** got merged to the providers of **`AppModule`**.

Angular has already configured the injector for the DI token **`HTTP_INTERCEPTOR`** with a multi-provider in the **`NgModule`** decorator of **`HttpClientModule`**.

That’s why we got a “*cannot mix*…” error when we configured the injector with the regular provider.

**Don’t you think rndService parameter of constructor should be declared as an Array?**

The question is, aren’t we supposed to write the **`CustomerComponent`** constructor as follows:

1.  `……`
2.  `   constructor(private customerService: CustomerService,`
3.  `               private authenticationService: AuthenticationService,`
4.  `               private rndService: Array<MultiRndService>`
5.  `              ) {`
6.  `……`

If we pursue it, we will get the following error:

![](images/Image52812.jpg)

**Figure 18.3**

We need to define a provider for **`Array`**. Though it seems logical for any strongly typed language developer, it will make our life more complicated.

In such cases, we use the Injection Token or the String Token instead of the Type Token.

**DI Tokens**

Do you remember our discussion of the DI token?

We learned that the injector uses a token to identify the correct provider when injecting in the application.

We also specified three kinds of tokens – *Type Token*, *String Token*, and *Injection Token*.

So far, we were using the type token.

But there are scenarios where we won’t be able to specify the type as a token. Such as the one mentioned earlier.

So in such cases, we use the String token or the Injection token.

**String token example**

Configure the injector with a provider but instead of the type token, use the string token.

1.  `……`
2.  `   providers: [{provide: HTTP_INTERCEPTORS,`
3.  `useClass: TokenInterceptorService, multi: true}`
4.  `     , {provide: “RND_VALUE”, useClass: MultiRndService, multi: true},`
5.  `     {provide: “RND_VALUE”, useClass: MultiRndService2, multi: true},`
6.  `  ],`
7.  `   bootstrap: [AppComponent],`
8.  `})`
9.  `export class AppModule {}`

Next, change the component’s constructor as follows:

1.  `constructor(private customerService: CustomerService,`
2.  `               private authenticationService: AuthenticationService,`
3.  `               @Inject(“RND_VALUE”)x: Array<MultiRndService>`
4.  `              ) {`

You will notice a specialized decorator is attached to the constructor parameter now – **`Inject`**.

**`@Inject()`** is a manual mechanism for letting Angular know that a parameter must be injected.

The best thing is the parameter type. It is an *Array of MultiRndService*.

Debug like earlier, and you will notice both the **`Service`** instances are injected as an Array.

**Injection token example**

Using the magic strings is always considered a bad practice in programming. Using a String token is also regarded as a bad practice, as there is a chance of spelling mistakes and therefore, run time errors.

So the recommended approach is using the **`Injection`** token instead.

To do that, first of all, declare a const variable of the type **`InjectionToken`** as follows. (It’s just RND, so define it in the same file where you have created **`MultiRNDService`**.)

1.  `export const RND_TOKEN = new InjectionToken<MultiRndService[]>`
2.  `(‘CollectionOfRNDService’);`

Next, change the provider as follows:

1.  `……`
2.  `import {MultiRndService, MultiRndService2, RND_TOKEN} from ‘./Services/multi-rnd.service’;`
3.  `@NgModule({`
4.  `   ……`
5.  `   providers: [{provide: HTTP_INTERCEPTORS,`
6.  `       useClass: TokenInterceptorService, multi: true}`
7.  *`// ,{provide: “RND_VALUE”, useClass: MultiRndService, multi: true},`*
8.  *`// {provide: “RND_VALUE”, useClass: MultiRndService2, multi: true},`*
9.  `     ,{provide: RND_TOKEN, useClass: MultiRndService, multi: true},`
10.  `     {provide: RND_TOKEN, useClass: MultiRndService2, multi: true},`
11.  `  ],`
12.  `   bootstrap: [AppComponent],`
13.  `})`
14.  `export class AppModule {}`

There is no more hard coded string anywhere. If there is any spelling mistake, we will get a compile error.

Finally, change the component constructor as follows:

1.  `constructor(private customerService: CustomerService,`
2.  `               private authenticationService: AuthenticationService,`
3.  *`// @Inject(“RND_VALUE”)x: Array<MultiRndService>`*
4.  `               @Inject(RND_TOKEN)x: Array<MultiRndService>`
5.  `              ) {`

Do the same debugging to check the output.

**Why do we need a multi-provider?**

Now you know what a multi-provider is.

You know how to create a multi-provider.

The final question is why do we need it?

We need it sometimes when we are creating reusable services.

Now, let’s say that a part of the service logic will be defined as a part of the library itself, and a part of it will be provided by the one who uses the library. Using the multi-providers becomes essential.

# [Summary](#toc.xhtml_s120a)

Let’s review the topics that we have learned in this chapter:

*   We learned that it is possible to define more than one *provider* for a single *DI token*. This is referred to as a *multi-provider*.
*   We learned that if the multiple *regular providers* are defined for a single *DI token*, one *provider* will replace the other based on the order.
*   We learned that if multiple *multi-providers* are defined for a single *DI token*, the injector will inject an array of services.
*   We learned that while defining a *provider*, the *DI token* and the actual services are not expected to be compatible. They can have a completely different structure.
*   We learned that we could not define both the *regular provider* and the *multi-provider* for a single *DI token*. Either all the providers should be *regular*, or all should be *multi* for a single *DI token*.
*   We learned that using the *String* or *Injection token* with *multi-providers* is recommended as *DI token* instead of *Type token*.
*   We learned about the *Inject* decorator that lets us manually tell Angular that the parameter has to be injected. It will be used in the case of *String* and *Injection token*.
*   We learned that a *multi-provider* could be very useful sometimes if we are creating a reusable service.

# [CHAPTER 19](#toc.xhtml_c19)

# [Compile-time Configuration](#toc.xhtml_c19)

# [Introduction](#toc.xhtml_s121a)

If you have any application development experience, you must have already figured out the drawback of the current application. The API URL is embedded directly inside the app, which will trouble us a lot in the long run.

We may have different API URLs in the production mode. The current code will be too difficult as we have to change it in every place. We need some configuration mechanisms in the project, and that’s what we will be learning to do in this chapter.

# [Compile-time versus run-time configuration](#toc.xhtml_s122a)

Compile-time configuration means that the configuration will be done at the time of compilation, and the run-time configuration means it will be done at the time of deployment.

If you’re confused, don’t worry. Let’s do the demo and understand both.

# [Lab – Continued – Compile time configuration](#toc.xhtml_s123a)

**Step 81: Create the second server**

The first step will be creating another server to confirm that the configuration changes are working.

For that, quickly create a new JavaScript file in the server folder and call it **`newServer.js`**.

Copy everything from the existing **`server.js`** and put it into the **`newServer.js`**.

Don’t worry about the code inside it. Just change the port and the default customer collection to some other value.

1.  `……`
2.  `const port = process.env.PORT || 5000;`
3.  `……`
4.  `let customers = [`
5.  `   {customerId: 1, firstName: “A”, lastName: “B”},`
6.  `   {customerId: 2, firstName: “C”, lastName: “D”},`
7.  `   {customerId: 3, firstName: “E”, lastName: “F”},`
8.  `];`

**Step 82: Run server**

Now open a new Command Prompt, navigate to the **`server`** folder, and run this new server by executing the following command:

1.  `node newServer.js`

Now, we have two servers running—one with port 4000 and the second with 5000; both of which are returning a different set of values.

# Discussion – Continued

Angular has a provision for compile-time configuration. Inside the **`src`** folder, there is a folder called **`environments`**. It contains two files, **`environment.ts`***,* and **`environment.prod.ts`***,* with the following content:

1.  *`// environment.ts`*
2.  `export const environment = {`
3.  `   production: false`
4.  `};`

1.  *`// environment.ts`*
2.  `export const environment = {`
3.  `   production: true`
4.  `};`

As you can see, it exports a constant object called the **`environment`** with a single property – **`production`**.

# Lab – Continued

**Step 83: Create an apiURL key in the environment**

Create a new key **`apiURL`** in both the **`environment.ts`** and the **`environment.prod.ts`** files, as follows:

1.  *`// environment.ts`*
2.  `export const environment = {`
3.  `   production: false,`
4.  `   apiUrl: ‘http://localhost:4000’,`
5.  `};`

1.  *`// environment.prod.ts`*
2.  `export const environment = {`
3.  `   production: true,`
4.  `   apiUrl: ‘http://localhost:5000’,`
5.  `};`

As you can see, we are pointing to the old server in one file and the new one in another.

**Step 84: Get Api Url dynamically in service**

Change the **`LoadCustomers`** method in **`CustomerService`** as follows:

1.  `private LoadCustomers(): void{`
2.  `     this.httpClient`
3.  ``     .get<Array<CustomerItemResponse>>(`${environment.apiUrl}/customers`)``
4.  `     .pipe(`
5.  `       map((customerAPIResponse) => {`
6.  `         return customerAPIResponse.map(`
7.  `           (customer) => new Customer(customer.firstName, customer.lastName)`
8.  `        );`
9.  `      })`
10.  `    ).subscribe((x) => {`
11.  `       this.CustomersSubject.next(x);`
12.  `    });`
13.  `  }`
14.  `……`

As you can see, instead of a fixed API URL, now we have a dynamic URL coming from the configuration.

Open the browser and check http://localhost:3000.

**Step 88: Rebuild the application in production mode**

Stop **`lite-server`**, navigate the root folder of the application, and rebuild the app again. But this time, use the **`--prod`** flag. It will create an optimized production-ready application build.

![](images/Image53051.jpg)

**Figure 19.3**

This time **`dist\lab4-ng`** folder will contain fewer items than before.

# Discussion – Continued

*   One difference between the production build and the non-production build is, in the production build, the *source map files* (files with extension **`map.js`**) are missing. The source-map files contain the information about the source TypeScript files. The source map files are the reason why we are able to debug the TypeScript files directly in the browser.
*   The second difference is, the **`vendor.js`** file is not generated in the production-build. This file was meant to contain the third-party libraries imported into the app.

    The main bundle is optimized during the production-build. All the unused code is removed from it while generating the final bundle; hence, for the production-build, the vendor build is integrated into the main bundle.

**Step 89 – Check the application**

Once again, start *lite-server* and check the output. This time, you will see the customer records coming from the server running on port 5000 port.

This proves that instead of *environment.ts*, *environment.prod.ts* got considered.

It is referred to as a *compile-time configuration*. At the compile-time, the application is configured with an API URL. (Also modify the *Save* function and do the test.)

# [Summary](#toc.xhtml_s124a)

Let’s review the topics that we have learned in this chapter:

*   We began our chapter by understanding the need for configuration in the application.
*   We learned the difference between the compile-time and the run-time configuration. The compile-time configuration means that the configuration will be done at the time of compilation, and the run-time configuration means it will be done at the time of deployment.
*   We learned about *the environment.ts* file and how it gets replaced with *the environment.prod.ts* file in the production mode.
*   We generated the non-production mode and production mode builds of our Angular application using *the ng build* and *ng build --prod* command, respectively.
*   We learned that in the non-production build, Angular CLI also generates the source map files; thus, we can directly debug the TypeScript files at run-time.
*   We learned that we wouldn’t get a separate *vendor.js file* in the production build as it is integrated into the *main* bundle.
*   We saw a demonstration of an Angular module called lite-module that can be used as a development purpose web-server for our static files.

# [CHAPTER 20](#toc.xhtml_c20)

# [Run-time Configuration](#toc.xhtml_c20)

# [Introduction](#toc.xhtml_s125a)

In the previous chapter, we spoke about the compile-time configuration.

But in some situations, the run-time configures suits better. In this chapter, we will learn how to do it.

# [Lab – Continued – Run-time configuration](#toc.xhtml_s126a)

**Step 90: Create a configuration file**

Create a new file **`config.json`** in the **`src/assets`** folder with the following content:

1.  `{`
2.  `   “apiUrl”: “http://localhost:4000”`
3.  `}`

# Discussion

**Why the *assets* folder?**

You must be wondering why we explicitly put our file inside the **`assets`** folder.

As you know, in the current lab, we are planning to bring the run-time configuration into action.

For that, we need the **`config.json`** file as it is in the final build.

A fantastic thing about the **`ng build`** command is that the *assets* folder will be replicated into the final build as it is.

Are you wondering why the assets folder is so special?

You will find the answer to that question in the **`angular.json`** file.

![](images/Image53136.jpg)

**Figure 20.1**

Did you notice the *assets* section inside the *build* section?

Inside it, we have specified our *assets* folder as one of the values. All the folders and the files defined inside this section will be copied into the final build by Angular CLI as it is.

# Lab – Continued

**Step 91: Create AppConfigService**

Next, create a file with the name **`app-config.service.ts`** file in the **`Services`** folder as follows:

1.  `import {HttpClient} from ‘@angular/common/http’;`
2.  `import {Injectable} from ‘@angular/core’;`
3.  `import {Subscription} from ‘RxJS’;`
4.  `@Injectable({`
5.  `   providedIn: ‘root’`
6.  `})`
7.  `export class AppConfigService {`
8.  `   apiUrl: string;`
9.  `   constructor(private http: HttpClient) {}`
10.  `   loadConfig(): Subscription {`
11.  `     return this.http`
12.  `       .get<{apiUrl: string}>(‘./assets/config.json’)`
13.  `       .subscribe(config => {`
14.  `         this.apiUrl = config.apiUrl;`
15.  `      });`
16.  `  }`
17.  `}`

**Step 92: Get API URL dynamically in service**

Inject **`AppConfigService`** into the **`CustomerService`** as follows:

1.  `……`
2.  `export class CustomerService {`
3.  `……`
4.  `   constructor(private appConfigService: AppConfigService,`
5.  `               private httpClient: HttpClient) {`
6.  `……`
7.  `  }`
8.  `……`

**Step 93: Make API call with dynamic URL**

Next, change the **`LoadCustomers`** method as follows:

1.  `……`
2.  `   private LoadCustomers(): void{`
3.  `     this.httpClient`
4.  ``     .get<Array<CustomerItemResponse>>(`${this.appConfigService.apiUrl}``
5.  ``/customers`)``
6.  `     .pipe(`
7.  `       map((customerAPIResponse) => {`
8.  `         return customerAPIResponse.map(`
9.  `           (customer) => new Customer(customer.firstName, customer.lastName)`
10.  `        );`
11.  `      })`
12.  `    )`
13.  `     .subscribe((x) => {`
14.  `       this.CustomersSubject.next(x);`
15.  `    });`
16.  `  }`
17.  `……`

As you can see, now, the API URL is not hardcoded anymore.

**Step 94: Check the output**

Check the output in the browser. Do you get an empty table? Of course, you did. 😊

Check the developer tool. You will see the following error:

![](images/Image53143.jpg)

**Figure 20.2**

# Discussion – Continued

**Why did we get this error?**

As you know, the **`LoadConfig`** method of **`AppConfigService`** is what sets the value to the **`apiURL`** property. Surprisingly, we have not invoked that method anywhere so far. Hence, **`apiURL`** is *undefined*.

**Do you know what exactly we want?**

We want the **`apiUrl`** property to be updated with a value in the **`config.json`** file before any external API calls are made.

**How can we do that?**

We have to invoke the **`LoadConfig`** method before the Angular marks the application as initialized. That way, we get 100% assurance that the **`apiURL`** property will be updated before any component is loaded.

In short, we need a provision to hook into the Angular’s initialization process and write our custom code.

**Is this possible?**

In Angular, we have a provision for hooking into the Angular’s bootstrap process.

We can configure a function that gets injected into an application startup and executed during the application initialization. In case that function returns a *promise*, the initialization does not complete until the *promise* is resolved.

**How to hook the custom logic into the Angular’s initialization process?**

Did you notice the word *injected* in the preceding explanation? Go through it again, if you didn’t notice.

Angular has exposed a DI token of type *InjectionToken* called **`APP_INITIALIZER`**.

We provide our function to this token, and it automatically gets hooked into the Angular Initialization process.

**How to inject function into the DI token?**

We have already seen an example of creating the providers using *useClass*. It is when we want to provide *type* as the value. The Angular injector creates an instance of that type and injects it.

But there is more than *useClass*:

*   *useFactory* – This lets us provide a method as a value. It is used when we want to control the object creation manually.

    The Angular injector invokes the factory method every time it wants to do the injection. The factory method is always expected to return an object. Whatever the factory method returns, that will be injected into the application.

*   *useValue* – This lets us inject a static value. A value can be any scalar value or an object. It’s best to inject constant values.
*   *useExisting* – It is the alias provider. It is used when we want to use the new provider in place of the old provider.

In our current case, the *useFactory* fits well. Let’s see it in action.

# Lab – Continued

**Step 95: Create initializeConfig method**

Create a new file **`app-initializer.ts`** in the **`Services`** folder and create a function called **`initializeConfig`** inside it as follows:

1.  `export function initializeConfig() {`
2.  `   return () => {`
3.  `     return new Promise((resolve) => {`
4.  `       setTimeout(() => {`
5.  `         console.log(‘Testing’);`
6.  `         resolve();`
7.  `      }, 3000);`
8.  `    });`
9.  `  };`
10.  `}`

Please take some time and analyze the code.

It’s a simple function that is returning another function, and that another function is returning a *promise*, and finally, the *promise* gets resolved after 3 seconds.

**Step 96: Configure injector to inject initializer function**

In the **`AppModule`**, configure the root injector as follows:

1.  `import {HttpClientModule, HTTP_INTERCEPTORS} from ‘@angular/common/http’;`
2.  `import {APP_INITIALIZER, NgModule} from ‘@angular/core’;`
3.  `……`
4.  `import {initializeConfig} from ‘./Services/app-initializer’;`
5.  `……`
6.  `@NgModule({`
7.  `   declarations: [AppComponent, CustomerComponent, LoginComponent],`
8.  `   imports: [……],`
9.  `   providers: [`
10.  `     {provide: APP_INITIALIZER, useFactory: initializeConfig,multi:true},`
11.  `     {provide: HTTP_INTERCEPTORS, useClass: TokenInterceptorService, multi:true}`
12.  `……`

**Step 97: Check the output**

Execute the application, and you will notice a 3-second pause before **`CustomerComponent`** or **`LoginComponent`** gets displayed. If you have not clearly understood yet, then increase the period from 3 seconds to a higher number. You will find that only after the promise is resolved, the application will be considered initialized, and only after that, the component is rendered.

**Step 98: Return promise from AppConfigService**

The **`loadConfig`** method in **`AppConfigService`** is returning the subscription object as the subscribe method of the observable returns the subscription object. Let’s modify it to return the promise object.

1.  `  loadConfig(): Promise<void> {`
2.  `     return this.http`
3.  `       .get<{apiUrl: string}>(‘./assets/config.json’).toPromise()`
4.  `       .then(config => {`
5.  `         this.apiUrl = config.apiUrl;`
6.  `      });`
7.  `  }`

**Step 99: Inject AppConfigService to initializeConfig method**

Inject **`AppConfigService`** into **`InitalizeConfig`** by adding it as a parameter to the method. Then, invoke the **`loadConfig`** method inside it as follows:

1.  `import {AppConfigService} from ‘./app-config.service’;`
2.  `export function initializeConfig(appConfigService: AppConfigService) {`
3.  `   return () => {`
4.  `     return appConfigService.loadConfig();`
5.  `  };`
6.  `}`

**Step 100: Check the output**

Once again, we will get a “*white screen of death*.”

In the developer tools, we will find the following error:

![](images/Image53200.jpg)

**Figure 20.3**

# Discussion – Continued

**What exactly happened in the preceding case?**

Angular, as expected, invoked the factory function during the initialization process, which is **`initializeConfig`**.

The problem is the parameter of the **`initializeConfig`** function.

In the preceding case, when the DI system invoked the factory method in Angular, no parameter was passed, and hence **`appConfigService`** is undefined, leading to the preceding error.

**What to do?**

We have to tell Angular somehow that our factory function is dependent on **`AppConfigService`** so that before invoking the factory function, it creates the instance of **`AppConfigService`** class and passes it, then passes it as a parameter while invoking. In short, we have to tell Angular to inject the instance of the **`AppConfigService`** class into the factory function.

That can be done using *deps*.

# Lab – Continued

**Step 101: Define the dependency**

In the **`AppModule`**, while defining the factory function for **`APP_INITIALIZER`**, set the deps property to indicate that our factory function is dependent on **`AppConfigService`**.

1.  `……`
2.  `   providers: [`
3.  `     {provide: APP_INITIALIZER, useFactory: initializeConfig, multi: true, deps:[AppConfigService]},`
4.  `……`
5.  `  ],`
6.  `   bootstrap: [AppComponent],`
7.  `……`

**Step 102: Check the output**

Finally, we will get the table displaying the dynamically returned customers from the server.

# Discussion – Continued

Now I know, despite getting the proper output, you are not feeling comfortable.

You must be wondering why we have to explicitly specify the dependency using the **`deps`** property when it comes to **`useFactory`***.* It was automatic with the **`useClass`**.

The answer is, in the case of **`useClass`**, if you remember, I said, “every consumer of the service must be decorated with the *Injectable* decorator.” That Injectable decorator does that automation for us. It adds the metadata to the Service class specifying other services that it depends on.

In the case of **`useFactory`**, we don’t add any such decorator. Hence, we have to do it manually, and for that, we have *deps*.

2.  `authenticate(user: User): void {`
3.  *`// Change no 3 - Observable is subscribed`*
4.  `     this.httpClient`
5.  `       .post<AuthenticationResponse>(`
6.  ``         `${this.appConfigService.apiUrl}/signin`,user).subscribe(……``
7.  `……`
8.  `……`*`//CustomerService`*
9.  `Save(customer: Customer) {`
10.  `     const newCustomer: NewCustomer = new NewCustomer(`
11.  `       customer.FName,`
12.  `       customer.LName`
13.  `    );`
14.  `     this.httpClient`
15.  ``       .post(`${this.appConfigService.apiUrl}/customers`, newCustomer)``
16.  `       .subscribe(……`
17.  `……`
