# [Summary](#toc.xhtml_s127a)

With this lab, we finally reached Station 9\. I hope that you have now gotten a clearer picture of the run-time configuration and how it can be achieved in Angular.

It was the longest journey of all. Take a good break and give yourself a relaxing sleep. See you in the next chapter, where we will begin our journey to the next station.

In this chapter, we covered the following topics:

*   We learned that the assets folder located in the SRC folder of the Angular application would be copied to the final build.
*   We learned that we could hook into the Angular’s initialization process by configuring the root injector with a DI token provider – **`APP_ INITIALIZER`**.
*   We learned that we could provide a method as a value to the DI token using **`useFactory`**.
*   We spoke about the toPromise method of the observable class that creates a promise out of the observable.
*   We learned that while creating the providers, using the deps property, we can specify the current provider’s dependency. It is a must to explicitly define the dependency with **`useFactory`**.
*   We learned that in the case of **`useClass`**, we are not required to explicitly specify the dependent services, as an Injectable decorator does that for us. That is why, we have to attach an Injectable decorator to every service class, no matter what injector is configured for it.

# [CHAPTER 21](#toc.xhtml_c21)

# [Error Handling](#toc.xhtml_c21)

# [Introduction](#toc.xhtml_s128a)

In this chapter, we will begin our journey to Station 10, and by the end of this station, you will learn everything about the run-time error handling in Angular.

Before we dive into the lab, let’s understand the different kinds of run-time exceptions that an Angular application can have:

1.  **Client-side errors**: These are unhandled run-time errors in the client-side logic.
2.  **Server-side errors**: It can further be categorized into the following three areas:
    *   Unhandled run-time error
    *   Non-authorized error from the server
    *   Server not connected error – when an HTTP call is attempted, and the server is not available

In this chapter, we will learn to handle all the four kinds of errors.

# [Lab – Error handling in Angular](#toc.xhtml_s129a)

**Step 1: Rename the application**

Start renaming the application from lab4-NG to lab5-NG.

**Step 3: Create a component for testing errors**

5.  `(click)=”throwUnAuthorizedError()”>`

As you can see, we have three buttons – each button leads to one kind of error discussed initially.

8.  `……`
9.  `})`

**Step 6: Prepare client-side logic error**

Next, define **`throwClientError`** in **`ErrorTestComponent`** as follows:

1.  `throwClientError() {`
2.  `    throw new Error(‘Testing client side error !!!’);`
3.  `}`

**Step 7: Prepare server-side logic error**

Next, define **`throwServerError`** in **`ErrorTestComponent`** as follows:

1.  `   constructor(private appConfigService: AppConfigService,`
2.  `               private httpClient: HttpClient) {`
3.  `  }`
4.  `……`
5.  `   throwServerError(){`
6.  ``     this.httpClient.get(`${this.appConfigService.apiUrl}/getError`).subscribe();``
7.  `}`

As you can see in the preceding code, we are making an API call that will throw a run-time error on the server-side.

**Step 8: Prepare unauthorized error**

Define **`throwUnAuthorizedError`** in **`ErrorTestComponent`** as follows:

1.  `   throwUnAuthorizedError(){`
2.  ``     this.httpClient.get(`${this.appConfigService.apiUrl}/authTest`).``
3.  `subscribe();`
4.  `}`

Technically, the preceding code is also making an API call, but the users have to be authenticated first for this API.

![](images/Image53448.jpg)

# Discussion – Continued

So, we have our component ready, which will throw an error and help test the error handling logic.

Next, let’s decide what we want to do when an unexpected error is encountered in the system:

*   For unexpected run-time errors (both the client and the server), the most followed involves the following two steps:
    1.  Redirect the user to some custom-designed page.
    2.  Persist (log) error in some storage medium, so that later it can be examined for troubleshooting.
*   For unauthorized errors, the preferred approach is, redirecting to the login screen.
*   For the failure generated because of the server’s unavailability, the preferred approach is displaying some custom messages.

So, now we know what has to be done for each kind of error.

# Lab – Continued

**Step 10: Create the Custom error component**

Create a new folder called *Error* in the app folder and then create a component called **`ErrorComponent`** as follows:

1.  `import {Component} from ‘@angular/core’;`

3.  `@Component({`
4.  `   selector: ‘app-error’,`
5.  `   templateUrl: ‘./error.component.html’`
6.  `})`

8.  `export class ErrorComponent {`

2.  `<h2>Unexpected Error <b>:(</b></h2>`

5.  `     <div class=”bar”></div>`
6.  `     <div class=”bar”></div>`
7.  `     <div class=”bar”></div>`
8.  `   </div>`
9.  `   <div class=”gear two”>`
10.  `     <div class=”bar”></div>`

# Discussion – Continued

Now that all the ingredients are set for action, it’s time to learn how to handle the errors.

Fortunately, Angular has a centralized exception handling already set in place. The default implementation prints the error messages to the console.

The default behavior can be overridden by using a custom Angular service and injecting it into the Angular pipeline as a new error handler. Let’s do the same.

# Lab – Continued

**Step 15: Create the custom Error Handler**

Create a new file **`app-error-handler.service.ts`** inside the **`Services`** folder with the following content:

1.  `import {HttpErrorResponse, HttpResponseBase} from ‘@angular/common/http’;`
2.  `import {ErrorHandler, Injectable, NgZone} from ‘@angular/core’;`
3.  `import {Router} from ‘@angular/router’;`

5.  `@Injectable()`
6.  `export class AppErrorsHandler implements ErrorHandler {`
7.  `   constructor(`
8.  `     private router: Router,`
9.  `     private zone:NgZone`
10.  `  ) {}`

12.  `   handleError(error: Error | HttpErrorResponse) {`
13.  `     if (error instanceof HttpResponseBase) {`
14.  `this.HandleServerSideError(error);` *`// Indicates its a server side error`*
15.  `    } else {`
16.  `this.HandleClientSideError(error);` *`// Indicates its a client side error`*
17.  `    }`
18.  `  }`

20.  `   private HandleServerSideError(error: HttpErrorResponse) {`
21.  `     if (error.status === 0) {`
22.  `       alert(‘Server is not running’);`
23.  `    } else if (error.status === 401) {`
24.  `       this.zone.run(() => this.router.navigate([‘/login’]));`
25.  `    } else {`
26.  `       localStorage.error = error.message;`
27.  `       this.zone.run(() => this.router.navigate([‘/error’]));`
28.  `    }`
29.  `  }`
30.  `   private HandleClientSideError(error: Error) {`
31.  `     localStorage.error = error.message;`
32.  `     this.zone.run(() => this.router.navigate([‘/error’]));`
33.  `  }`
34.  `}`

# Discussion – Continued

Let’s understand the preceding code.

The first thing you must have noticed is the **`ErrorHandler`** interface. To create a custom error handler, we must implement our class with the **`ErrorHandler`** interface and define the **`handleError`** method.

**`handleError`** method is self-explanatory. You can see the *if…else* condition. Out of the four errors discussed, three are handled in the *if* block, whereas the remaining one was handled in the *else* block.

**Note**: In our current case, we are only storing the latest error in our local storage. In the real-time scenario, you may follow a more persistent medium, such as a Database, and keep all the errors.

**zone**

I am sure, **`zone.run`** in the preceding code must have caught your eyes.

We have an encapsulated **`router.navigate`** inside the **`zone.run`** call.

To understand the same, look into the following code:

![](images/Image53790.jpg)

**Figure 21.7**

The preceding code is a part of the original Angular source code. **`onHandleError`** gets executed when the Angular application throws an unhandled exception.

Angular, in response to the error, makes a call to the **`zone.runOutsideAngular`** method.

**zone.runOutsideAngular**

In Angular, the zone provides the execution context for the application.

**`zone.runOutsideAngular`** makes the code execute outside the Angular’s execution context so that the change detection won’t kick-off. If you recall, we spoke about the change detection once earlier. We said it is responsible for updating the UI, and no change detection means no UI update.

Sometimes, to control the Angular application’s performance, we purposely execute some code outside the Angular’s execution context. That can be done using the **`runOutsideAngular`** method.

By default, Angular is only interested in displaying the exception detail in the console and not in the UI update. Hence, in the centralized error handler, it executes the code using the **`runOutsideAngular`** method.

**zone.run**

When you are outside of the Angular’s execution context, you can re-enter the execution zone using the **`zone.run`** method.

The preceding code depicts that the error handler executes outside the Angular context. Hence, we have used the **`zone.run`** method so that our **`router.navigate`** method performs in the original execution context of our application, and therefore we see the updated UI.

Don’t stress much to understand the preceding theory as we hardly use **`runOutsideAngular`** in real-time.

My advice is, don’t forcefully use the **`runOutsideAngular`** method in your project.

When needed, you will realize it, and secondly, once you use it, you may need to use the *run* method to come back to the original world.

# Lab – Continued

**Step 16: Configure Injector**

The preceding step will be configuring the root injector with our custom error handler. Open the **`app.module.ts`** file, and modify the code to the following:

1.  `import {HttpClientModule, HTTP_INTERCEPTORS} from ‘@angular/common/http’;`
2.  `import {APP_INITIALIZER, ErrorHandler, NgModule} from ‘@angular/core’;`
3.  `import {FormsModule, ReactiveFormsModule} from ‘@angular/forms’;`
4.  `import {BrowserModule} from ‘@angular/platform-browser’;`
5.  `import {RouterModule} from ‘@angular/router’;`
6.  `……`
7.  `import {AppErrorsHandler} from ‘./Services/app-error-handler.service’;`
8.  `……`
9.  `@NgModule({`
10.  `……`
11.  `   providers: [`
12.  `     {provide: APP_INITIALIZER, useFactory: initializeConfig, multi: true, deps:[AppConfigService]},`
13.  `     {provide: HTTP_INTERCEPTORS, useClass: TokenInterceptorService, multi: true},`
14.  `     {provide:ErrorHandler, useClass:AppErrorsHandler},`
15.  `……`
16.  `})`
17.  `export class AppModule {}`

# Discussion – Continued

In the preceding code, you can see that we have configured the root injector using the **`NgModule`** decorator instead of the **`Injectable`** decorator. An **`Injectable`** decorator cannot be used in this case as it won’t allow us to explicitly specify the DI token. For replacing the error handler with a custom error handler, we need to provide a DI token (**`ErrorHandler`**) explicitly.

# [Summary](#toc.xhtml_s130a)

So, we have now reached station 10\. In this journey, we covered the following topics:

*   We learned that we could attach a component-specific style to a component. For that, we will use the **`styleUrls`** parameter of the Component decorator.
*   We learned that Angular has a centralized exception handling already set in place. The default implementation prints the error messages to the console.
*   We learned that the default behavior could be overridden using a custom Angular service and injecting it into the Angular pipeline as a new error handler. For that, we have to configure the root injector for the DI token – ErrorHandler.
*   We learned that we have to implement the service with the **`ErrorHandler`** interface for creating a custom error handler service.
*   We learned about **`zone.runOutsideAngular`** method that lets us execute code outside the Angular’s execution context.
*   We learned about the **`zone.run`** method that lets us get back to the Angular’s execution context.

# [Section 5](#toc.xhtml_c21b)

# [Summary](#toc.xhtml_c21b)

In [Section 5](#p05.xhtml), we explored the Angular services in detail and covered two stations – Station 9 and Station 10 – which completes our journey half way. Let’s revise each chapter in this section.

**Chapter 12 – Authentication Service**

*   We revised the DI concept again and learned that it makes our code loosely coupled, leading to more maintainable and testable code.
*   We spoke about the Angular Injector, which is responsible for injecting the service instances into the Angular application.
*   We discussed the two kinds of Injectors – Module and Element.
*   We learned that both the Injectors maintain a hierarchy.
*   We learned how to configure the service using an Injectable decorator and a Component decorator.
*   We learned that both the **`NgModule`** and the **`Injectable`** decorator let us configure the module injectors, whereas the **`Component`** decorator configures the element injector.
*   We learned that we have to add the Injectable decorator to every Angular service dependent on some other service regardless of what injector is configured.
*   We learned that the root Injector creates a global singleton instance, whereas the element injector creates the instances that will be specific to the component instance (and its children).
*   We spoke about the short syntax for configuring the injector when both the provide and the **`useClass`** have the same value.
*   We learned about the reactive programming and implementing it in Angular with the help of RxJS.
*   We learned that in reactive programming, the Observable is subscribed by a **`Subscriber`**, and the **`Observer`** feeds the data into the Observables.
*   We have seen a demonstration of the RxJS Subjects, which represents both the Observable and the Observer in the reactive programming.
*   We learned that the RxJS subjects would be marked as private in the real-time project, and the underlying observable will be exposed as a public member.

**Chapter 13 – Unsubscribe**

*   We learned that unsubscribing is very important as it will lead to a memory leak otherwise.
*   We learned how to unsubscribe from the RxJS subscriptions.
*   We learned about the **`ngOnDestroy`** life cycle event of the Angular component
*   We had a quick talk on the Angular pipe. It is used to encapsulate the transformation logic in Angular (a separate chapter is dedicated to it).
*   Finally, we discussed the async pipe.

**Chapter 14 – Logout and Route Guard Cleanup**

*   We learned about the importance of writing logic in the services by moving the existing code into the services.
*   We again revised the element and the module injector in this chapter. As the element injector was not serving our requirement, we switched it to the module injector.
*   We learned that the element injector of **`AppComponent`** would indirectly create a singleton global instance, as it will never get destroyed because it is a root component. Secondly, by being a root, the service instances created by it will be accessible to all the child components.
*   We learned that for configuring the root injector, an Injectable decorator is preferred more than the **`NgModule`** decorator as an Injectable decorator leads to tree shaking. Tree shaking is a step in the Angular build process that ensures all the unused services are removed from the final bundle.
*   We learned that we could leverage the HTML power in Angular by using the HTML’s autofocus attribute, form tag, and submit button.

**Chapter 15 – Customer Service**

*   We learned how to create the custom observables using the RxJS **of** operator
*   We saw a demonstration of configuring the element injector once again.
*   We had a revision of the RxJS Subject in Angular.
*   We learned about the RxJS Behavior Subject in Angular.
*   We learned a new way of troubleshooting/debugging the Angular apps in the developer tools using the ng object exposed by Angular.
*   We saw *async as* in action.
*   We saw **`*ngIf with else`** in action.
*   We discussed the challenge we face in emitting the reference types from the service.

**Chapter 16 - HTTP Service**

*   We used the **`HttpClient`** service again in our application.
*   We used the generic version of the **`HttpClient.get`** and the post method.
*   We saw the demonstration of the Subject again.
*   We discussed the token-based authentication and implemented the same in the application.
*   We discussed the json pipe. Using this pipe, we can display the contents of an object using the interpolation. They were only used for troubleshooting.
*   We learned that when it comes to multiple pipes, a sequence is essential.
*   We saw the demonstration of using an anonymous object as a generic parameter. It is not recommended but may be seen in the projects.
*   We learned that we use the subscribe method to get the emitted values and use the pipe method to pipe the values through the various operators that may alter the values or even stop further emitting.
*   We discussed the map operator in RxJS. Using it, we can transform the actual HTTP response to the desired one.

**Chapter 17 - Token Interceptor**

*   In this chapter, we learned how to create and configure the HTTP interceptors.
*   We spoke about the HttpInterceptor interface, which we need to implement for creating the interceptors.
*   We learned that we could not directly modify an HTTP request in the interceptor as they are immutable. Hence, we need to clone the existing request. We attach the headers to the cloned request and then pass that cloned request further in the pipeline.
*   We saw a demonstration of both the long and the short syntax used while configuring the injectors.
*   We learned that we explicitly need to specify the DI token – **`HTTP_INTERCEPTORS`** for configuring the injectors for the HTTP interceptors.
*   We learned that, although an injectable decorator is the recommended approach because of the tree-shaking provision, we cannot use it all the time for configuring the module injectors as it won’t allow us to specify the DI token explicitly.
*   We also learned the benefit of configuring the injectors using the **`NgModule`** decorator. It provides more configuration options compared to the Injectable decorator.
*   We learned that the injector needs to be configured with the multi providers for the HTTP interceptors.

**Chapter 18 – Multi providers**

*   In this chapter, we learned that it is possible to define more than one provider for a single DI token. This is referred to as a multi-provider.
*   We learned that if the multiple regular providers are defined for a single DI token, then one provider will replace the other based on the order.
*   We learned that if the multiple multi-providers are defined for a single DI token, then the injector will inject an array of services.
*   We learned that while defining a provider, the DI token and the actual services are not expected to be compatible. They can have a completely different structure.
*   We learned that we could not define both the regular provider and the multi-provider for a single DI token. Either all the providers should be regular, or all should be multi for a single DI token.
*   We learned that using the String or the Injection token with the multi-providers is recommended as the DI token instead of the Type token.
*   We learned about the Inject decorator that lets us manually tell Angular that the parameter has to be injected. It will be used in the case of the String and the Injection token.
*   We learned that the multi-provider could be very useful sometimes if we are creating a reusable service.

**Chapter 19 – Compile-time configuration**

*   We begin this chapter by understanding the need for configuration in the application.
*   We learned the difference between the compile-time and the run-time configuration. The compile-time configuration means the configuration will be done at the time of compilation, and the run-time configuration means it will be done at the time of deployment.
*   We learned about the environment.ts file and how it gets replaced with the **`environment.prod.ts`** file in the production mode.
*   We generated the non-production mode, and the production mode builds of our Angular application using the **`ng build`** and **`ng build --prod`** command, respectively.
*   We learned that in the non-production build, Angular CLI also generates the source map files; thus, we can directly debug the TypeScript files at run-time.
*   We learned that we wouldn’t get a separate vendor.js file in the production build as it is integrated into the main bundle.
*   We saw a demonstration of an Angular module called the lite-module that can be used as a development purpose web-server for our static files.

**Chapter 20 – Run-time configuration**

*   We learned that the assets folder located in the SRC folder of the Angular application would be copied to the final build.
*   We learned that we could hook into the Angular’s initialization process by configuring the root injector with a DI token provider – **`APP_ INITIALIZER`**.
*   We learned that we could provide a method as a value to the DI token using **`useFactory`**.
*   We spoke about the toPromise method of the observable class that creates a promise out of the observable.
*   We learned that while creating the providers, using the deps property, we can specify the current provider’s dependency. It is a must to explicitly define the dependency with **`useFactory`**.
*   We learned that in the case of **`useClass`**, we are not required to explicitly specify the dependent services, as the Injectable decorator does that for us. That is why, we have to attach an Injectable decorator to every service class, no matter what injector is configured for it.

**Chapter 21 – Error Handling**

This chapter was the last in Section 5, and thus lets us reach Station 10.

*   We had a detailed discussion on the Error handling in Angular.
*   We learned that we could attach a component-specific style to a component. For that, we will use the **`styleUrls`** parameter of the Component decorator.
*   We learned that Angular has a centralized exception handling already set in place. The default implementation prints the error messages to the console.
*   We learned that the default behavior could be overridden using a custom Angular service and injecting it into the Angular pipeline as a new error handler. For that, we have to configure the root injector for the DI token – **`ErrorHandler`**.
*   We learned that we have to implement the service with the ErrorHandler interface for creating a custom error handler service.
*   We learned about the **`zone.runOutsideAngular`** method that lets us execute the code outside the Angular’s execution context.
*   We learned about the zone.run method that lets us get back to the Angular’s execution context.

# [Section 6](#toc.xhtml_p06)

# [CHAPTER 22](#toc.xhtml_c22)

# [The Component-Oriented Style in Action](#toc.xhtml_c22)

# [Introduction](#toc.xhtml_s131a)

This chapter is going to be a fascinating one. We will take up the previous project and improve it by actually following a component-oriented style. With this chapter, you will finish your journey to Station 11.

# [Component-oriented style](#toc.xhtml_s132a)

**Component oriented style** is a UI designing approach that will let us create our UI as a collection of reusable and composable web components.

Look at the following UI. What comes to your mind when you see it?

![](images/Image54019.jpg)

**Figure 22.1**

Isn’t it merely a composition of two labels, two text boxes, and two buttons?

What precisely are those labels, text boxes, and buttons? They are **components**.

Now, look at the following one. What do you see?

![](images/Image54071.jpg)

**Figure 22.2**

You may say – dropdown.

I won’t deny it. It’s indeed a dropdown.

*   But It can also be considered a combination of a textbox, a button, and a dynamic box displaying multiple items.
*   A button is placed using some CSS so that it looks like a part of the text box.
*   When that button is clicked, some code is executed, making a dynamic box appear at runtime, showing a couple of line items. When those line items are clicked, it gets displayed in the textbox.

What I meant to say is, a dropdown is a component, but it is a composition of other smaller elements or better referred to as components.

Based on whatever lab we have finished so far, I believe by now you must have a clear idea about the components.

A component represents a piece of the entire UI. We put those components together, form a giant component, and keep on doing it until the whole UI is done.

So far, in our application, we have seen a couple of components in action, such as **`LoginComponent`**, **`CustomerComponent`**, **`ErrorComponent`** and **`ErrorTestComponent,`** and so on.

# [Component oriented style and Angular](#toc.xhtml_s133a)

You may be amazed to learn that Angular does not coin a component-oriented style.

You can see a similar approach, followed by the other UI technologies such as Polymer, ReactJS, and so on.

It’s a standard approach/practice introduced with the emergence of HTML5.

This approach makes our code maintenance easy and lets multiple UI developers work parallelly.

# [A problem in the current application](#toc.xhtml_s134a)

Do you think we have followed the component-oriented style in the real manner in the current project that we have developed so far?

Well, partially yes and partially no.

We broke down the application into multiple components where each component had its role – Login, Customer, ErrorTest, and Error.

These top-level components will be identified based on the features of the project. Such components are referred to as the **Feature components**.

More features introduce more components, and those components will be accessed mostly using routing.

Once a feature component is identified and ready to be developed, the next step will be determining how each feature component will be designed. Creating one huge feature component won’t be a good idea as it makes the code **unreadable** and **unmanageable**. We should break it down into multiple smaller components.

In our example, the **`CustomerComponent`** is expected to be broken down into multiple smaller components. We haven’t done it, and that is the problem.

# [Container-Presentation pattern](#toc.xhtml_s135a)

When it comes to breaking down a feature component, the Container-Presentation pattern is widely used and proved.

In this approach, we will break our feature component into multiple components. One of them will be called a container component, and the others a presentation component.

Some people call it differently.

The **container components** will be referred to as the **smart** components. They will be the ones who will interact with the external APIS for retrieving and updating the data. They will be the ones responsible for the application state changes.

The **presentation components** are considered to be the **dumb** components. They are responsible for dealing with only one kind of functionality. They will take care of one part of the entire feature component logic.

They will rely on the container components for any interaction with the outside world, such as interacting with the external APIs, updating the application state, and so on.

You will understand it very well after the next demo.

# [Lab – Upgrade project to follow container-presentation pattern](#toc.xhtml_s136a)

Next, we need to make everything work again step by step. Let’s start with the list data. If you remember, earlier we obtained it from the service.

Now, as per the container-presentation pattern, the presentation component should not deal with the Angular services. It should be purely meant for the presentation purposes. Interacting with an Angular service will be the container component’s task.

**Then how does CustomerListComponent get the data?**

We will pass it from the CustomerComponent to the CustomerListComponet, and for that, we will use the **Input properties**.

We have already seen the input properties in action. It’s time to create our custom Input property, which can be done using the **`Input`** decorator.

# Discussion – Continued

**What were we doing earlier on AddNew?**

We were invoking the **`setAddNew`** method of **`CustomerService`**.

**Can we do the same now?**

Yes, we can. Get the **`CustomerService`** instance injected into **`CustomerListComponent`**, and then invoke the method.

**Should we do it?**

No, because **`CustomerListComponent`** is a presentation component. The presentation component should not deal with any logic. It should ask the container component for such things.

The final question is how to invoke the container component function when the presentation component’s button is clicked.

That can be done by creating a custom output property in the **`CustomerListComponent`**, and for that, we will use an *output* decorator.

Let’s do the demo and understand the same.

2.  `……`
3.  `export class CustomerListComponent {`
4.  `   ……`
5.  `   @Output()`
6.  `   OnAddNew:EventEmitter<void> =new EventEmitter<void>();`
7.  `……`

**Step 20 – Initialize OnCancel in CustomerComponent**

It has two steps. First, we need to refactor the existing **`Cancel`** method in the **`CustomerComponent`**.

Let’s have a look at the existing implementation of the CustomerComponent’s **`Cancel`** method.

1.  `Cancel() {`
2.  `     this.customerService.setList();`
3.  `     this.Reset();`
4.  `  }`
5.  `   Reset() {`
6.  `     this.CustomerForm.reset();`
7.  `  }`

**`CustomerForm`** is not part of the **`CustomerComponent`** anymore, and hence it will be undefined. Therefore, remove the **`Reset`** method from **`CustomerComponent`** and redefine the **`Cancel`** method as follows:

1.  `   Cancel() {`
2.  `     this.customerService.setList();`
3.  `  }`

(You will find the **`Reset`** method invoked in the **`Save`** function as well. Make sure to remove that statement as well to avoid the compile error.)

After that, change the **`CustomerComponent`** template as follows:

1.  `……`

3.  `    <customer-add (OnCancel)=”Cancel()”></customer-add>`
4.  `   </ng-template>`
5.  `</div>`

In the preceding code, **`OnCancel`** is the output property of **`CustomerAddComponent`**, whereas **`Cancel`** is the method defined in the **`CustomerComponent`**.

Next, let’s finish the Save functionality.

**Step 22 – Declare the output property for Save in CustomerAddComponent**

Create a new property **`OnSave`** in **`CustomerAddComponent`** with the output decorator as follows:

1.  `……`
2.  `import {Customer} from ‘../Models/customer’;`
3.  `……`
4.  `   @Output()OnSave: EventEmitter<Customer> = new EventEmitter<Customer>();`
5.  `……`

You can see that the generic argument of EventEmitter is not void anymore – it is the Customer.

We need it because while raising the event, we will pass the customer as data.

**Step 23 – Raise the event**

Change the **`Save`** method in **`CustomerAddComponent`** as follows:

1.  `……`
2.  `   Save() {`
3.  `     this.OnSave.emit(new Customer(this.FirstName,this.LastName));`
4.  `     this.Reset();`
5.  `  }`
6.  `……`

You can see that while emitting, we are passing the customer object.

**Step 24 – Initialise OnSave in CustomerComponent**

Attach **`OnSave`** event handler to **`CustomerAddComponent`** in **`CustomerComponent`** as follows:

1.  `<ng-template #AddNewContainer>`
2.  `    <customer-add (OnCancel)=”Cancel()” (OnSave)=”Save($event)”></customer-add>`
3.  `   </ng-template>`

Have you noticed the **`$event`** keyword in the preceding code? It represents the values emitted in the previous step.

**Step 25 – Save method in CustomerComponent**

Change the **`Save`** method in **`CustomerComponent`** to the following:

1.  `…..`
2.  `   Save(c: Customer) {`
3.  `     this.customerService.Save(c);`
4.  `  }`
5.  `……`

# Discussion – Continued

**Revision on the Container-Presentation pattern**

With the preceding step, we completed our demo on the Container-Presentation pattern.

Let’s quickly discuss how we shall decide what is to go where.

What part of our code should be considered in a container, and what should be the presentation?

All the operations related to the presentation will be a part of the **Presentation component**.

It includes sorting, paging, validating fields, formatting data, resetting the form, etc.

All the smart operations, such as the decision making, routing, and, most importantly, the coordination with the Angular service, will be a part of the **Container component**.

**Pending issues**

Before we end this chapter, let’s solve two pending issues in the lab – autofocus in the first control and the default-enter. Let’s start with autofocus.

If you are thinking about using the autofocus attribute in HTML, you will be disappointed.

The HTML autofocus attribute sets focus on the element when the page is loaded for the first time.

Try it out quickly:

*   Set the **`autofocus`** attribute on the **`firstName`** textbox and execute the application.
*   Click on **`AddNew`**, you will find the focus on the **`firstName`** textbox.
*   Click on **`Cancel`**, and then click on **`AddNew`** again. Unfortunately, you won’t find the focus set back.

To solve this problem, we will use the **`ViewChild`** decorator and the template reference variable. Using these two, we can get the view element access in the component class.

The next question is, when will we set the focus? We have to set the focus as soon as the component is loaded, and the control is ready to focus.

Let’s try with the constructor.

# Lab – Continued

Let’s attach a template reference variable to the firstname textbox.

1.  `   <input #firstName class=”form-control”`
2.  `[ngClass]=”{‘is-valid’:F.valid,’is-invalid’:F.invalid}”`
3.  `type=”text” name=”FName”[(ngModel)]=”this.FirstName” required`
4.  `minlength=”3” #F=”ngModel”>`

In the preceding code, you can notice a template reference variable firstname is attached to the textbox.

Open the **`CustomerAddComponent`** class and create a variable **`FirstNameControl`** of the type **`ElementRef`** as follows:

3.  `from ‘@angular/core’;`
4.  `……`
5.  `export class CustomerAddComponent{`
6.  `……`
7.  `   @ViewChild(“firstName”) FirstNameControl: ElementRef;`
8.  `……`

Set the focus using the following code in the constructor:

2.  `……`

4.  `     this.FirstNameControl.nativeElement.focus();`
5.  `  }`
6.  `……`

# Discussion – Continued

**Error handler issue**

Is it annoying to check the errors like this?

Whenever there is an error, you will get redirected to an error page. Then, you have to find out the error in the local storage.

That may be an ideal way in the production environment, but in the development environment, I think the default approach makes more sense where the red error gets displayed in the console.

Is there a better way? Yes, there is. Open your AppErrorHandler and add the following block of code at the beginning of the **`handleError`** function:

1.  `   if(!environment.production){`
2.  `       throw error;`
3.  `    }`

The complete **`handleError`** function looks like the following:

1.  `……`
2.  `import {Router} from ‘@angular/router’;`
3.  `import {environment} from ‘src/environments/environment’;`

5.  `@Injectable()`
6.  `export class AppErrorsHandler implements ErrorHandler {`
7.  `……`
8.  `   handleError(error: Error | HttpErrorResponse) {`
9.  `     if(!environment.production){`
10.  `alert(‘Run-time error occured.` `Kindly check developer tools’)`
11.  `       throw error;`
12.  `    }`
13.  `     if (error instanceof HttpResponseBase) {`
14.  `this.HandleServerSideError(error);` *`// Indicates its a server side error`*
15.  `    } else {`
16.  `this.HandleClientSideError(error);` *`// Indicates its a client side error`*
17.  `    }`
18.  `  }`
19.  `……`

After this change, your custom error handler comes to the picture only if the current environment is a production environment.

**How to solve the focus issue?**

Now the question is, how to tackle the focus issue? Writing the logic insider constructor is not working. A constructor is invoked when the instance of **`CustomerAddComponent`** got created. The textbox does not even exist at that moment.

One solution is the Angular life cycle events. Angular lets us hook into the Angular execution process by exposing a lot of life cycle methods. We can put our custom logic into those methods and do custom things.

One such life cycle method is **`ngOnInit`**. It executes once the component is initialized.

Let’s try it out.

# Lab – Continued

**Step 32 – Implement ngOnInit and add focus logic**

For that, implement **`CustomerAddComponent`** with **`OnInit`** interface, which will force us to define the **`ngOnInit`** method. In **`ngOnInit`**, set the focus on the **`firstName`** textbox.

1.  `import {Component, ElementRef, EventEmitter, OnInit, Output, ViewChild}`
2.  `from ‘@angular/core’;`
3.  `……`
4.  `@Component({`
5.  `   selector: ‘customer-add’,`
6.  `   templateUrl: ‘./customer-add.component.html’`
7.  `})`
8.  `export class CustomerAddComponent implements OnInit  {`
9.  `   ……`
10.  `   @ViewChild(“firstName”) FirstNameControl: ElementRef;`
11.  `   ngOnInit(){`
12.  `     this.FirstNameControl.nativeElement.focus();`
13.  `  }`
14.  `……`

**Step 33 – Check the output**

Unfortunately, this won’t work either.

# Discussion – Continued

Unfortunately, ngOnInit was not helpful. It gets invoked once the component is initialized. At this point, the controls are not rendered, hence, **`this.FirsNameControl`** will be undefined.

***Note:*** Kindly understand that the constructor component was not even initialized, whereas, during ngOnInt, it is initialized but not rendered.

We have a perfect life cycle method for this situation – **`ngAfterViewInit`**. It is an ideal candidate for the logic that needs access over the rendered controls.

Let’s use the same.

# Lab – Continued

**Step 34 – Implement ngAfterViewInit**

Implement **`CustomerAddComponent`** with **`AfterViewInit`** interface and then define the **`ngAfterViewInit`** method as follows:

1.  `import {AfterViewInit, Component, ElementRef, EventEmitter,`
2.  `Output, ViewChild} from ‘@angular/core’;`
3.  `……`
4.  `export class CustomerAddComponent implements AfterViewInit  {`
5.  `…….`
6.  `   @ViewChild(“firstName”) FirstNameControl: ElementRef;`

**Step 35 – Check the output**

Finally, you will find it working. You will notice that the **`firstName`** textbox will have the focus every time **`CustomerAddNewComponent`** is accessed.

# Discussion – Continued

So, with the preceding step, we were successful with the focus requirement. Next, let’s solve the default enter issue.

If you remember, previously, we achieved it by putting the entire section inside a *form* tag and changing the regular *button* with a *submit* button.

We will follow the same methodology this time.

# Lab – Continued

**Step 36 – Implement default enter**

Replace the **`containerdiv`** element in the**`CustomerAddComponent`** with the form tag and the regular button to submit the button as follows:

1.  `   <form class=”card” #myForm=”ngForm”>`
2.  `     <h5 class=”card-header”>Add New Customer</h5>`
3.  `……`
4.  `       <input type=”submit” class=”btn btn-success” value=”Save”`
5.  `       (click)=”Save()” [disabled]=”myForm.invalid”> &nbsp;`
6.  `……`
7.  `     </div>`
8.  `   </form>`

**Step 37 – Check the output**

Enter some values in both the text boxes and just press enter. It will trigger the **`Save`** function automatically, and we will have achieved our target.

# Discussion – Continued

Though everything looks fantastic, there is something different compared to previous time. Did you notice it?

The **`ngForm`** directive was explicitly attached to the **`div`** tag before.

Did you notice that we have not attached the **`ngFormdirective`** explicitly to our **`form`** tag? Then, how come everything is working?

Well, in the **`template-driven forms`**, the **`ngFrom`** directive gets automatically attached to the *form* elements—no need to do it explicitly.

That is the reason why we removed it when we changed **`div`** to *form*.

# [Summary](#toc.xhtml_s137a)

We have now completed our journey to Station 11\. Let’s quickly revise what we have learned in this chapter:

*   We discussed a new UI development approach in the modern world – component-oriented style. It is a UI designing approach that will let us create our UI as a composition of multiple smaller and reusable components.
*   We learned that Angular is not the pioneer of the component-oriented approach. It is just following it. The other technologies, like react and polymer, follow a similar process.
*   We learned that this approach makes our code maintenance easy and lets multiple UI developers work parallelly.
*   We spoke about the components that represent the application’s main components and are mostly accessed via routing.
*   We learned that sometimes it is considered to break down a feature component into smaller components to make the code readable and manageable. The container-presentation pattern is one of the commonly followed patterns in industry for this purpose.
*   We had a quick discussion on the difference between the container and the presentation components. The container component is usually the smart one, whereas the presentation components are termed as the dumb components and they deal with the UI presentation.
*   We created the custom input property for passing the data from the outer component to the inner component.
*   We created the custom output property for raising even from the inner component that can be handled in the outer component.
*   We revisited the Viewchild decorator and template reference variable and learned to reference the component class’s view element.
*   We learned about using the environment again in this chapter. We made our error handler code behave differently for the different environments (dev and production).
*   We revisited **`ngOnInit`** and learned how it is best suited for the initialization logic.
*   We learned about the **`ngAfterViewInit`** life cycle, a perfect candidate for logic that needs access over the rendered controls.
*   We learned that we are not required to explicitly assign the **`ngForm`** directive to the form tag in the template-driven forms.

# Discussion – Continued

Now, we have something interesting. We have the following three problems to address:

1.  We want to create a menu for navigating between the preceding components.

    **The question is, in which component will we add the menu?**

    If we add it to the AppComponent, then it will be visible even on the login screen.

    Explicitly doing it in each component will not be feasible as it will violate the DRY principle. (Don’t repeat yourself – once the code is written, don’t repeat it).

2.  Secondly, check the preceding route configuration code. Have you noticed the AuthGuard attached to each route? Again, that is a violation of the DRY principle. It is our **second problem**.
3.  Finally, in one of our previous chapters, we added logged in user’s name and logout link in the customer component. As an application, it has to be present on every page. That is the **third problem**.

The solution for all the three problems is **nested routes**.

We will introduce another component – AuthComponent, the parent component for the menus, the user’s name, the logout link, and all the other authorize-required-components.

# Lab – Continued

**Step 4 – Create AuthComponent**

Create a new folder in the app folder called **`Auth`** and then create a new component called **`AuthComponent`** as follows:

3.  `import * as jwt_decode from ‘jwt-decode’;`
4.  `@Component({`
5.  `   selector: ‘auth-root’,`
6.  `   templateUrl: ‘./auth.component.html’`
7.  `})`
8.  `export class AuthComponent {`
9.  `   LoginUserFirstName = ‘DummyFName’;`

11.  `   constructor(private authenticationService: AuthenticationService) {`
12.  `       const decodedToken = jwt_decode(localStorage.token);`
13.  `       this.LoginUserFirstName = decodedToken.firstName;`
14.  `  }`
15.  `   logout() {`
16.  `     this.authenticationService.logout();`
17.  `  }`
18.  `}`

As you can see, the username and the logout code from **`CustomerComponent`** is shifted to **`AuthComponent`**.

(Please ensure that the corresponding code is removed from **`CustomerComponent`** as it is not relevant anymore.)

# Discussion – Continued

Let’s try to understand the code:

*   There are two top-level routes – *login*, responsible for **`LoginComponent`**, and *auth* route, responsible for **`AuthComponent`**.
*   *The router-outlet* in the **`AppComponent`** becomes the place-holder for the **`LoginComponent`** and **`AuthComponent`**, depending on the route.
*   *router-outlet* in the **`AuthComponent`** becomes a place-holder for the other components based on the child routes of the *auth* route.

# Discussion – Continued

There are the following three problems which we need to address:

1.  **Page-Refresh**

    Did you notice something strange in the preceding output?

    Did you notice the page refreshed every time the link was clicked?

    If the page is refreshing, then it is not an SPA. We have to achieve the same output without a page refresh, and that can be accomplished using a **`routerLink`** directive.

2.  **Highlighting the current menu**

    As of now, the current menu is not highlighted. It can be solved using the **`routerLinkActive`** directive.

3.  **Empty URL**

    The preceding problem is, when the URL is empty, we get a blank screen. (Execute the application. Complete the login and check what you get – a blank white screen.)

Let’s solve all of them.

# Discussion – Continued

Although everything is working as per the expectation, there is one big problem in the preceding lab, that is, eager loading.

All the components are loaded into the project from the very beginning.

Open the developer tools and check the source tab. You will find **`main.js`**. Please open it and scroll up and down to see the customer template content, supplier template content, and product template content. Everything is bundled into one.

![](images/Image54825.jpg)

**Figure 23.3**

![](images/Image54860.jpg)

**Figure 23.4**

Let’s build the project and confirm if the generated bundle file has the same problem.

Stop the current execution by pressing *Ctrl + C* in the Command Prompt.

Then create the project build using the **`ng build`** command.

![](images/Image54867.jpg)

**Figure 23.5**

As you can see, only one application related bundle got generated – **`main.js`**.

Now, search for the **`customer`** keywords in it. Then, search for the **`supplier`** followed by the **`product`***.*

You will find everything in that one single JavaScript file.

Imagine the situation of a project with hundreds of such components. Imagine the bundle’s size and imagine the load on the browser to download that huge bundle file.

The solution is lazy loading. We will configure our route to load the components lazily.

For that, first, we have to encapsulate each component inside an independent Angular Module.

# Lab – Continued

**Step 14 – Create an Angular Module**

Create a new Angular module **`CustomerModule`** inside the **`Customer`** folder. Then, include all the customer related components inside this **`CustomerModule`**.

1.  `import {NgModule} from ‘@angular/core’;`
2.  `import {CustomerAddComponent} from ‘./customer-add.component’;`
3.  `import {CustomerListComponent} from ‘./customer-list.component’;`
4.  `import {CustomerComponent} from ‘./customer.component’;`
5.  `@NgModule({`
6.  `   declarations: [CustomerComponent,CustomerListComponent,CustomerAddComponent]`
7.  `})`
8.  `export class CustomerModule {}`

Once it is done, make sure that you remove all of the preceding components from the **`AppModule`**.

![](images/Image54948.jpg)

**Figure 23.6**

# Discussion – Continued

As you can see, **`CustomerComponent`**, **`CustomerListComponent`**, and **`CustomerAddComponent`** are added to the **`declaration`** section of **`CustomerModule`**.

*   But you can notice that there is no *bootstrap* section in the **`NgModule`** decorator.

    *The bootstrap* section will be needed only in the root module to declare the root component.

*   You must have noticed the missing **`BrowserModule`** in the imports section.

    That’s because the services registered in the **`BrowserModule`** needed to be a singleton, and hence it is imported only in the root module.

Don’t worry much about it. In the next chapter, we will revise everything we’ve learned about the Angular module regarding Services, Components, Routing, and so on.

# Lab – Continued

Again, don’t forget to remove the respective components from **`AppModule`**.

**Step 16 – Reconfigure the route for lazy loading**

Open the **`app.route.ts`** file and modify your routes to the following:

1.  `export const AppRoutes: Routes = [`

13.  `          ),`
14.  `      },`

18.  `           import(‘src/app/Supplier/supplier.module’).then(`
19.  `             (x) => x.SupplierModule`
20.  `          ),`
21.  `      },`

23.  `         path: ‘product’,`

25.  `           import(‘src/app/Product/product.module’).then(`
26.  `             (x) => x.ProductModule`
27.  `          ),`

As you can see, we have removed the references for all the components, and instead, now we are referring to the modules.

Also, while defining the routes, instead of the **`component`**, we are using **`loadChildren`**.

**Step 17 – Define child routes**

There is one final step that we need to perform to make the lazy loading work. Define the child routes and configure them in the respective Angular modules as follows:

1.  `const childRoute: Routes = [{path: ‘’, component: CustomerComponent}];`

5.  `   declarations: [`
6.  `     CustomerComponent,`
7.  `     CustomerListComponent,`
8.  `     CustomerAddComponent,`
9.  `  ],`
10.  `})`

3.  `@NgModule({`
4.  `   imports: [RouterModule.forChild(childRoute)],`
5.  `   declarations: [`
6.  `     SupplierComponent`
7.  `  ],`
8.  `})`

1.  `const childRoute: Routes = [{path: ‘’, component: ProductComponent}];`

3.  `@NgModule({`
4.  `   imports: [RouterModule.forChild(childRoute)],`
5.  `   declarations: [`
6.  `     ProductComponent`
7.  `  ],`
8.  `})`

I am not sure if you had a chance to look into your Command Prompt where the Angular application was executed. You will find a bunch of errors been thrown by **`CustomerAddComponent`** and **`CustomerListComponent`**.

![](images/Image55004.jpg)
