# Discussion – Continued

As you know, a component in Angular belongs to an Angular.

The same rule applies to the directives and the pipes. (Soon, we will create the custom directives and pipes, and then you will see it in action.)

We have already spoken about the Modules once earlier in this book – in ES6 learning and TypeScript learning. The idea of the module is the same everywhere.

Whatever is defined inside one module will be, by default, private to that module and won’t be accessible outside. If one module wants to access the stuff defined in another module, then the second module needs to import the first one.

**ngModule**

If you remember, in our earlier code **`CustomerAddComponent`** was part of the AppModule, and we did import **`FormsModule`** in the **`AppModule`**. That is how we were able to use the directives such as **`ngModel`** in **`CustomerAddComponent`**.

In the new code, **`CustomerAddComponent`** is defined as part of **`CustomerModule`**. We never imported **`FormsModule`** in the **`CustomerModule`**. That is why, we are getting such an error.

Importing **`FormsModule`** in **`CustomerModule`** will solve a whole bunch of errors thrown in **`CustomerAddComponent`**.

***ngFor,*ngIf, and ngClass**

But this will not resolve the error thrown by **`CustomerListComponent`** about **`*ngFor`**.

**`*ngFor`***,* **`*ngIf`***,* **`ngClass`** are the directives defined inside *CommonModule*.

To solve the error about those directives, we need to import *CommonModule* in the respective module.

Now, I am sure that there is one big question that is troubling you. **How was it working earlier?**

Earlier, the CustomerListComponent was part of AppModule. I know you will immediately say, “but we haven’t imported **`CommonModule`** in **`AppModule`**.” It is true, but you have imported **`BrowserModule`**, which internally imported **`CommonModule`**. (You will learn more about the Angular modules in the next chapter.)

**Then why are we not importing BrowserModule in CustomerModule?**

**`BrowserModule`** also configures the injectors for some services that are responsible for the DOM manipulations and many other browser-related services. Those services are expected to be singleton at the application level. Re-importing it again will throw the following error:

![](images/Image55012.jpg)

**Figure 23.8**

# Lab – Continued

**Step 19 – Import Forms and CommonModule**

Import both **`FormsModule`** and **`CommonModule`** in **`CustomerModule`**.

1.  `@NgModule({`
2.  `   imports: [RouterModule.forChild(childRoute),CommonModule,FormsModule],`
3.  `   declarations: [`
4.  `     CustomerComponent,`
5.  `     CustomerListComponent,`
6.  `     CustomerAddComponent,`
7.  `  ],`
8.  `})`
9.  `export class CustomerModule {}`

**Step 20 – Check the output**

Make sure that all the routes are working as they were earlier. After that, open the developer tools and check the source tab.

![](images/Image55058.jpg)

**Figure 23.9**

You will find main.js, but along with that, you will also find another JavaScript file related to the customer.

Next, click on the supplier link, and you will find that the JavaScript file related to the supplier gets loaded. The same goes for the product.

![](images/Image55098.jpg)

**Figure 23.10**

This is called lazy loading. Based on the need, **`components/modules`** are loaded.

**Step 21 – Build the project**

Build the project using the **`ng build`** command and check the generated files. You will find one bundle for each lazily loaded module.

# What are lazily loaded – Modules or Components?

When you go through this concept, sometimes you will hear modules are lazily loaded and sometimes components. So, what exactly loaded lazily?

The answer is both. The modules are lazily loaded, and so are their corresponding components.

# [Is bundling good or bad?](#toc.xhtml_s140a)

I am sure you must be wondering about one thing now.

*Earlier, while learning JavaScript, we spoke about bundling’s advantages. We said, we need bundling, and now we are finding a way to create multiple bundles.*

Let me clarify it. Bundling is indeed good, but the bundle should not be huge. If it is too big, then break it down into multiple.

Most of my clients always have this issue. I have seen projects with a bundle size of more than 30MB.

# [Does that mean we should lazy load every module?](#toc.xhtml_s141a)

In our case, I configured my route in a way that each module will load lazily. Hence, for each module, one bundle gets generated, but that won’t be the case in a real-time project.

If you do that, then there is no point in bundling at all. It would be like loading everything on demand.

# [Hybrid approach](#toc.xhtml_s142a)

In real-time, make that decision wisely. Load a couple of modules eagerly, and load some of them lazily.

For instance, look at the following sample routing:

1.  `export const AppRoutes: Routes = [`
2.  `   {path: ‘login’, component: LoginComponent},`
3.  `   {path:’’,component:AuthComponent,children:[`
4.  `     {`
5.  `       path:’config’,`
6.  `loadChildren:import(“src/app/master/maser.module”).then(x=>x.MasterModule)`
7.  `    },`
8.  `     {`
9.  `       path:’exec’,`
10.  `loadChildren:import(“src/app/transaction/transaction.module”).then(x=>x.TransactionModule)`
11.  `    }`
12.  `  ]},`
13.  `];`

15.  `@NgModule({`
16.  `   declarations: [LoginComponent,HeaderComponent,AuthComponent],`
17.  `})`
18.  `export class InitialModule {}`

20.  `@NgModule({`
21.  `   imports:[InitialModule,RouterModule.forRoot(AppRoutes)],`
22.  `   declarations: [AppComponent],`
23.  `})`
24.  `export class AppModule   {}`

*   You can see four Angular modules in action in the preceding code – **`AppModule`**, **`InitialModule`**, **`MasterModule`**, and **`TransactionModule`**.
    *   Assume that **`LoginComponent`**, **`HeaderComponent`**, and **`AuthComponent`** are declared as part of **`InitialModule`**.
    *   Assume that **`MasterModule`** has **`CustomerComponent`** and **`ProductComponent`**.
    *   Assume that **`TransactionModule`** has **`CheckoutComponent`** and **`CheckInComponent`**.
*   **`AppComponent`** is the root component, and **`AppModule`** is the root module. So it will be loaded during the application initiation time, and it will be included in the main bundle.
*   **`InitialModule`** is directly imported into the **`AppModule`**. So it will be eagerly loaded, and therefore be included in the main bundle.
*   **`MasterModule`** will be lazily loaded, so a separate bundle will be created for this module, containing both **`CustomerComponent`** and **`ProductComponent`**.
*   **`TransactionModule`** will be lazily loaded, so a separate bundle will be created for this module, containing both **`CheckoutComponent`** and **`CheckInComponent`**.

Let’s explore each of these two modules.

1.  `export const configRoutes: Routes = [`
2.  `   {path: ‘customer’, component: CustomerComponent},`
3.  `   {path: ‘product’, component: ProductComponent},`
4.  `];`
5.  `@NgModule({`
6.  `   imports:[RouterModule.forChild(configRoutes)],`
7.  `   declarations: [CustomerComponent,ProductComponent],`
8.  `})`
9.  `export class MasterModule  {}`

**`MasterModule`** has configured the routes for two of its components.

1.  `export const execRoutes: Routes = [`
2.  `   {path: ‘cout’, component: CheckoutComponent},`
3.  `   {path: ‘cin’, component: CheckInComponent},`
4.  `   {path: ‘bal’, component: BalanceSheetComponent},`
5.  `   {path: ‘pnl’, component: ProfitAndLossComponent},`
6.  `];`
7.  `@NgModule({`
8.  `   declarations: [BalanceSheetComponent,ProfitAndLossComponent],`
9.  `})`
10.  `export class ReportModule {}`
11.  `@NgModule({`
12.  `   imports:[ReportModule],`
13.  `   declarations: [CheckoutComponent,CheckInComponent],`
14.  `})`
15.  `export class TransactionModule  {}`

Look at the **`TransactionModule`**. It has not only configured the routes for each of its components but the components of **`ReportsModule`** too. **`TransactionModule`** has imported the **`ReportsModule`**. Hence, **`ReportsModule`** gets loaded when **`TransactionModule`** is loaded. On top of that, all the components in the **`ReportsModule`** become part of the same bundle.

In short, **`ReportModule`** and **`TransactionModule`** will be included in the same module.

So overall, the preceding example has three lazyily loaded modules – main, master, and transaction.

# [Pre-loading strategy](#toc.xhtml_s143a)

Lazy loading is a nice feature as it makes the bundles smaller and reduces the initial load—the Angular loads the module when the user requests it for the first time.

The Angular allows us to optimize our app further using a technique called PreLoading – loading the lazy-loaded Modules in the background asynchronously while the user is interacting with the app. This will help boost up the loading time of the app.

When we configure our routes using the **`forRoot`** method, we have a second argument to specify a strategy.

1.  `RouterModule.forRoot(AppRoutes, {preloadingStrategy: NoPreloading}))`

It can have two possible values.

**NoPreloading**: It is the default one. It means you don’t want to pre-load, and so the modules configured to be lazyily loaded will be downloaded on demand.

**PreloadAllModules**: It means you want to load all the lazyily loaded modules asynchronously in the background.

By Pre-loading the lazy loaded module, the user does not have to wait for the module to be downloaded. All the modules will already be downloaded in the background.

Try executing our project using both the strategies. It would be pretty straightforward. Just run and in the developer tools, find out what is loaded and what is not.

Are you confused between eager loading and lazy loading with PreloadAllModules?

**Eager loading**

Make the modules part of one bundle, and that will be loaded at the beginning.

**Lazy loading with NoPreloading**

A separate bundle for each lazy-loaded module will be created, which will be loaded on demand.

**Lazy loading with PreloadAllModules**

A separate bundle for each lazy-loaded module will be created, and all of them will be loaded asynchronously in the background.

# [Custom pre-loading strategy](#toc.xhtml_s144a)

We can even create a custom preloading strategy to match our requirements. For that, we have to create a custom class inherited from **`PreLoadingStrategy`** class and then define the **`preload`** method.

1.  `export class CustomPreloadStrategy implements PreloadingStrategy {`
2.  `   preload(route: Route, load: () => Observable<any>): Observable<any> {`
3.  `     if (true){`
4.  `       return load();`
5.  `    }`
6.  `     else{`
7.  `       return of(null);`
8.  `    }`
9.  `  }`
10.  `}`

The **`pre-load`** method will be called once for every lazy loaded route configured in the application.

As you can see, the first parameter of the pre-load method is the active route. We can use this to extract the information about the route, which is being loaded.

The second parameter is the **`Observable`** function, which we need to return if we want to pre-load this module. We can return **`Observable`** of null if we do not wish to pre-load the module.

# [Summary](#toc.xhtml_s145a)

This concludes our learning of Routing, and with that, we have completed our journey to Station 12. Let’s revise what we learned in this chapter:

*   We learned how we could use the template property instead of templateUrl when the component HTML is simple.
*   We revisited the router-outlet component that displays the different components for different URLs.
*   We spoke about the **`routerLink`** directive that lets us navigate between the routes without a full page refresh.
*   We learned about the **`routerLinkActive`** that lets us add CSS class if a specific route is active.
*   We spoke about the default routes that will get loaded if the URL is empty.
*   We learned that we could define the default routes at both the root level and the child level.
*   We did a detailed study on lazy loading and learned how it reduces the initial load time.
*   We spoke about the pre-loading strategies that let us decide if a lazy-loaded module needs to be loaded asynchronously or not.

# [CHAPTER 24](#toc.xhtml_c24)

# [Angular Modules](#toc.xhtml_c24)

# [Introduction](#toc.xhtml_s146a)

The journey to Station 13 is going to be the best of all. With this, you will get an answer to almost every question that was unanswered so far.

We will talk about everything on the Angular Modules. We will explore why we need them? We will discuss how they work with the components, services, and routing.

# [Angular Modules and Angular Components](#toc.xhtml_s147a)

After going through 23 chapters and completing the journey till Station 12, you must have a clear picture about the Angular Component. They are the basic building block of an Angular application and represents a self-contained piece of the whole UI.

The components are encapsulated inside an Angular Module.

Let’s answer a couple of questions related to it.

# [Question 1 – Why do we group the Angular Components into the Angular Modules?](#toc.xhtml_s148a)

We know very well by now that in a real-time project, we create multiple feature-based components.

Think about an application having ten features.

How many components will it have?

If you say ten, then it is not the correct answer. It will have more than that.

We may develop some reusable components.

We may even plan each feature component as multiple smaller components, making it more readable and manageable. Remember our **`CustomerComponent`**? We broke it down into **`CustomerList`**, **`CustomerAdd`**, and **`CustomerComponent`**.

In short, we may end up with a lot of components.

Components have selectors. How will you make sure that you put a unique selector to every component? Practically, that’s not possible. That’s why we have the Angular Modules.

Let’s do a quick demo on the same.

# [Lab 8 – Multiple Angular modules and components](#toc.xhtml_s149a)

**Step 1 – Create a new Angular Project**

Using Angular CLI, create a new Angular project as follows:

1.  `ng new myapp`

**Step 2 – Create Feature Components**

For this demo, we need two components – **`Customer`** and **`Product`**.

This time, we won’t do it manually. We will do it using Angular CLI.

Make sure that you are inside the **`myapp`** folder, and then execute the following command:

1.  `ng g c Customer`

As you can see, CLI did all the hard work for us.

It created the customer folder.

![](images/Image55153.jpg)

**Figure 24.1**

It created the component for you. It connected the component class to its template and CSS.

1.  It set the selector as the **`app-customer`**.
2.  It also added the component to the declarations section in the **`NgModule`** decorator of **`AppModule`**.

**Step 3 – Create ProductComponent**

Using the same approach, create **`ProductComponent`**.

![](images/Image55215.jpg)

**Figure 24.2**

**Step 4 - CreateHeaderComponent for Customer**

Now, imagine that the developer of the **`CustomerComponent`** decides to break it down into smaller ones. He/she concluded that he/she needs the **`HeaderComponent`**, so he/she can create it as shown in the preceding figure.

![](images/Image55252.jpg)

**Figure 24.3**

As you can, we have instructed CLI to create a new component inside the **`customer`** folder.

Secondly, by default, the CLI creates a new folder for every component. By using the **`--flat`** flag, we instructed CLI to skip the custom folder creation.

Open the component and check the selector.

1.  `@Component({`
2.  `   selector: ‘app-header’,`
3.  `……`

**Step 5 – CreateHeaderComponent for Product**

The developer of **`ProductComponent`** goes through the same thought process. He too creates the **`HeaderComponent`** as follows:

![](images/Image55287.jpg)

**Figure 24.4**

Did you notice something interesting with this component? You will find that **`AppModule`** didn’t update this time.

CLI failed to add the new **`HeaderComponent`** to the **`AppModule`** as the class name conflicted with the **`HeaderComponent`** created in the previous step.

**Step 6 – Add new HeaderComponent to module**

Let’s do it manually using the TypeScript alias as follows:

1.  `……`
2.  `import {HeaderComponent as ProductHeaderComponent} from`
3.  `‘./product/header.component’;`
4.  `import {HeaderComponent  } from ‘./customer/header.component’;`
5.  `import {ProductComponent} from ‘./product/product.component’;`
6.  `import {CustomerComponent} from ‘./customer/product.component’;`

8.  `@NgModule({`
9.  `   declarations: [`
10.  `     AppComponent,`
11.  `     CustomerComponent,`
12.  `     ProductComponent,`
13.  `     HeaderComponent,`
14.  `     ProductHeaderComponent,`
15.  `  ],`
16.  `……`

If you wonder how the **`HeaderComponent`** of the Product became the **`ProductHederComponent`**, you have missed the Typescript import statement. Check it out. You will find that we specified an alias while importing so that both the **`HeaderComponent`** types can be used simultaneously.

**Step 7 – Change the header content**

Open **`header.component.html`** in the **`customer`** folder and put the following content:

1.  `<b>I am Customer Header</b>`

Then, change the content of the **`header.component.html`** file in the product folder to the following:

1.  `<b>I am Product Header</b>`

**Step 8 – Use the component**

Open **`customer.component.html`** and change the content to the following:

1.  `<p>customer works!</p><br>`
2.  `<app-header></app-header>`

Open **`product.component.html`** and change the content to the following:

1.  `<p>product works!</p><br>`
2.  `<app-header></app-header>`

**Step 9 – Use Customer and ProductComponent**

Open **`app.component.html`** and replace its content with the following:

1.  `<app-customer></app-customer><hr>`
2.  `<app-product></app-product>`

**Step 10 – Check the output**

Execute the application using the **`ng serve`** command.

You will get the run-time error as follows:

Try to imagine a big enterprise project where multiple developers are working.

![](images/Image55334.jpg)

**Figure 24.5**

Getting the preceding error is possible in such projects if we follow a single Angular module approach. Every developer cannot keep an eye on every other component’s selector in the application.

That is why, we follow the practice of having one Angular module for every feature in the application. We group all the components related to one feature into one Angular module.

**Step 11 – Create Customer module**

Create a new Angular module **`CustomerModule`** using Angular CLI as follows:

![](images/Image55378.jpg)

**Figure 24.6**

It creates **`CustomerModule`** inside the **`customer`** folder.

![](images/Image55414.jpg)

**Figure 24.7**

I believe you know why it has **`CommonModule`** in the **`imports`** section.

CommonModule is an Angular module that encapsulates the directives such as **`*ngIf`***,* **`*ngFor`***,* **`ngClass`***,* and so on.

Angular CLI is smart enough to understand that usually, every module needs these directives. Hence, importing it is a part of the CLI design process. You can remove it if you want, but remember that the common directives and pipes defined inside the CommonModule won’t work afterward.

**Step 12 – Move Customer related component to CustomerModule**

Remove **`CustomerComponent`** and **`HeaderComponent`** from the declarations section of **`AppModule`**.

And put them in the declarations section of **`CustomerModule`** as follows:

![](images/Image55474.jpg)

**Figure 24.8**

![](images/Image55482.jpg)

**Figure 24.9**

**Step 13 – Create ProductModule**

Next, create **`ProductModule`** using Angular CLI.

![](images/Image55519.jpg)

**Figure 24.10**

**Step 14 – Move Product related component to ProductModule**

Move both **`ProductComponent`** and **`HeaderComponent`** to **`ProductModule`**.

![](images/Image55608.jpg)

**Figure 24.11**

![](images/Image55621.jpg)

**Figure 24.12**

After this step, **`AppModule`** will be left with only one component, and that will be **`AppComponent`**.

**Step 15 – Check the output**

Execute the application using the **`ng serve`** command. You will notice the following error:

![](images/Image55658.jpg)

**Figure 24.13**

The preceding error was expected. The reason is as follows:

*   At this stage, **`CustomerComponent`** belongs to **`CustomerModule`**.
*   The rule says that every component will be private to its module by default. So **`CustomerComponent`** is private to **`CustomerModule`** by default.
*   Hence, it cannot be used in any component which is not part of **`CustomerModule`**.
*   **`AppComponent`** is not part of **`CustomerModule`**. It is a part of **`AppModule`**. Thus, the **`CustomerComponent`** cannot be used in **`AppComponent`**.

To make it work, we have to mark **`CustomerComponent`** as public in the **`CustomerModule`** explicitly.

**Step 16 – Make CustomerComponent visible to AppComponent**

It will be a two-step process. First, add **`CustomerComponent`** into the exports section of CustomerModule and then import **`CustomerModule`** in the **`AppModule`** as follows:

![](images/Image55720.jpg)

**Figure 24.14**

![](images/Image55727.jpg)

**Figure 24.15**

In Angular, when one Angular module imports another Angular module, the first one gets access to every exported member in the second module.

![](images/Image55735.jpg)

**Figure 24.16**

**Step 17 – Make ProductComponent visible to the AppComponent**

Follow the preceding steps for **`ProductComponent`**.

**Step 18 – Check the output**

Execute the application to get the expected output.

Hence, creating one module for every feature is the recommended approach in any Angular application. That answered our first question.

# [Question 2 – Is it a must to export every component from the Angular Module?](#toc.xhtml_s150a)

No, and you should not do it.

Set only those components in the exports section, which you want to use in some other component’s template.

Keep the other components private by not declaring them in the *exports* section.

(Please remember – the components of one module will always be visible to each other. They will be invisible outside that module unless marked as public.)

# [Question 3 – What about Routing?](#toc.xhtml_s151a)

A component can be accessed through routing, even if it is private.

# [Question 4 – What happens when one Angular module imports another?](#toc.xhtml_s152a)

All the exported components in the second module become visible in the first module.

# [Question 5 – What about the directives and pipes?](#toc.xhtml_s153a)

Everything you learned about components also applies to the directives and pipes.

Every directive and pipe will be put in the declarations section of the **`NgModule`** decorator.

After that, the same public and private logic will be applicable.

# [Question 6 – What happens to the components that never get used?](#toc.xhtml_s154a)

The angular build process is brilliant.

Do you remember the *Injectable* decorator and *Tree-shaking*?

We learned that using an *Injectable* decorator is recommended for configuring the module injectors.

*Tree shaking* is the process that takes place during the Angular build process. It makes sure that the services decorated with the *Injectable* decorator are removed from the final build if it is not used in the application.

The same tree-shaking process takes care of the unused components, directives, and pipes. If there is a component that has never been used in any template, it will be removed from the final build.

**Please note:** I said, “never used in any template.” I never said, “not declared in an Angular module.” The component will be removed from the bundle even if it is a part of an Angular module, considering it is not used anywhere in any other component template.

Because of the tree-shaking, the overall size of the bundle gets reduced.

You, as a developer, are not required to do anything about tree shaking. Just create your application and build it. Angular CLI will take care of all the hard work for you.

# [Question 7 – Is there any drawback because of the tree shaking?](#toc.xhtml_s155a)

Yes, there is. There are some scenarios where you will never use the components in any template, but you will still want them to be a part of the bundle.

**What to do in such a case?**

We can deal with such a situation. The **`NgModule`** section has a special section called **`entryComponents`**.

Just declare that component in the **`entryComponents`** section, and that’s it. That component will become part of the final bundle, no matter if it is used in any other template or not.

1.  `@NgModule({`
2.  `   declarations: [`
3.  `     AppComponent,`
4.  `     ProductComponent,`
5.  `     ProductHeaderComponent, DontDeleteComponent`
6.  `  ],`
7.  `   imports: [BrowserModule,CustomerModule,ProductModule],`
8.  `   providers: [],`
9.  `   bootstrap: [AppComponent],`
10.  `   entryComponents:[DontDeleteComponent]`
11.  `})`
12.  `export class AppModule {}`

**Now you may be wondering, why should we be doing it?**

There are some scenarios where we will be loading the components dynamically in the code instead of using them in the template.

Can you think of one such scenario? I will give you a hint. You already did one such demo.

It was Routing. Did we ever use those components anywhere in the template? Still, they are a part of the bundle. How? The answer is **`entryComponents`**.

You may argue that you never actually added any of the components in the **`entryComponents`** section.

I agree with you. You never did, but Angular did. Every component configured into routing will get automatically added to the **`entryComponents`** array.

# [Question 8 – Any scenario where we have to put the component in the entryComponents section explicitly?](#toc.xhtml_s156a)

One of the use cases is the third party libraries. Sometimes, you may end up in some third-party Angular library that expects you to create a component and add them to the **`entryComponents`** array.

# [Question 9 – What about the hierarchical import?](#toc.xhtml_s157a)

The following two scenarios will answer this question:

![](images/Image55773.jpg)

**Figure 24.17**

In this scenario, Module3 won’t be able to access the Module1 members unless Module3 explicitly imports Module1.

![](images/Image55817.jpg)

**Figure 24.18**

In this scenario, Module2 re-exports the members of Module1 again. Hence, Module3 will get access to the Module1 components as well.

# What if Module1 is exporting a lot of members and Module2 wants to re-export all of them back?

The answer is, in such a case, instead of the members, we can directly export the module.

![](images/Image55876.jpg)

**Figure 24.19**

It means, along with the Module2PublicComponent, every other exported member in the Module1 is again exported by Module2.

You have already experienced it once before.

*   BrowserModule internally imports CommonModule.
*   CommonModule contains **ngFor, *ngIf*, and the other commonly used directives.
*   BrowserModule re-exported the entire CommonModule.
*   So, every module that imports BrowserModule also gets access to the directives exposed by the Common Module.

# [Angular Modules and Services](#toc.xhtml_s158a)

We saw the role of the Angular Module with the components. Next, it’s time for the Services.

Let’s do some revision.

In one of the chapters on services, we spoke about the Angular Injectors:

*   As an application developer, we never create Injectors. Angular does that for us. We only configure it with the provider.
*   The injector creates the instance of service using that provider.
*   There are two kinds of injectors that Angular creates – the element injector and the module injectors.
*   Angular creates an Element Injector for every component and maintains a tree hierarchy similar to the application’s component tree. In that tree, we have our root component injector on the top.
*   When a dependency is expected in the Angular application, the first request will be sent to the connected component’s element injector. If it cannot be resolved, it will be forwarded to the parent component’s element injector, and so on, until the dependency is resolved.
*   If all the root element injector fails to create the instance, it will be forwarded to the Module injector.

![](images/Image55912.jpg)

**Figure 24.20**

So far, in this book, we spoke about the element injectors in detail, but when it comes to the module injectors, we only talked about the root injectors. Now it’s time to talk about the *child module injectors*.

# [Child Module Injectors](#toc.xhtml_s159a)

When the application is bootstrapped, Angular creates a *module injector*, referred to as the *root injector*.

After that, when the other Angular modules are lazily loaded, the child module injectors get created, and thus a new tree hierarchy is created.

Let’s answer a few questions regarding the Angular modules with respect to the services. For that, we need a demo application. So let’s create one first.

# [Lab 9 – Hierarchical module injectors](#toc.xhtml_s160a)

**Step 1 – Create a project**

Create a new project using the **`ng new`** command.

When prompted for routing, say, *Yes*.

![](images/Image55950.jpg)

**Figure 24.21**

**Step 2 – Create more modules**

Create six modules as follows:

![](images/Image55988.jpg)

**Figure 24.22**

**Step 3 – Create components**

Create **`CustomerComponent`** and include it into **`CustomerModule`** as follows:

![](images/Image56001.jpg)

**Figure 24.23**

“**`ng g c Customer`**” – we already saw that in action in the previous demo.

This time, it is a little different. Can you see that special **`-m`** flag with the customer as the value? It means, add a newly created component to **`CustomerModule`**.

Just like that, create the Product, Checkout, and CheckinComponent in the respective modules.

# Discussion – Continued

In the real-time projects, to keep the root module cleaner, a separate module will usually be created for routing, and then that module will be imported into the root Module.

When you create an Angular project with the routing configured, you will find a file gets created, called **`app-routing.module.ts`**. You will find the following content inside it:

1.  `import {NgModule} from ‘@angular/core’;`
2.  `import {RouterModule, Routes} from ‘@angular/router’;`

4.  `const routes: Routes = [];`

6.  `@NgModule({`
7.  `   imports: [RouterModule.forRoot(routes)],`
8.  `   exports: [RouterModule],`
9.  `})`
10.  `export class AppRoutingModule {}`

If you check AppModule, you will find **`AppRoutingModule`** in the **`imports`** section there.

The following two important things happened when an **`AppModule`** imported **`AppRoutingModule`**:

1.  Everything exported by **`AppRoutingModule`** becomes visible inside **`AppModule`**.

    In the current case, it is **`RouterModule`** – that makes everything exported by **`RouterModule`** visible to AppModule’s components. For instance, the components such as the *router-outlet* and the directive such as the *routerLink* can be used in the **`AppComponent`** template.

2.  The providers defined in the **`AppRoutingModule`** will get merged with the providers in the **`AppModule`**.

    Do you see any providers in **`AppRoutingModule`**?

    No, but that doesn’t make the **`AppRoutingModule`** providers empty.

    **`AppRoutingModule`** has imported **`RouterModule`**. Because of that, the providers defined in the **`RouterModule`** get merged with the providers of the **`AppRoutingModule`**. One of the providers defined in the **`RouteModule`** is for the *RouterService*, which we have seen in action in the *[Chapter 11](#c11.xhtml)* Authentication chapter.

    So ultimately, we registered the root injector for RouterService.

Reread this discussion after the end of this chapter.

**Step 4 – Configure root routes**

Redefine the routes variable in the **`app-routing.module.ts`** as follows:

1.  `const routes: Routes = [`

4.  `     loadChildren: () =>`
5.  `       import(‘src/app/master/master.module’).then((x) => x.MasterModule),`
6.  `  },`
7.  `   {`

10.  `       import(‘src/app/transaction/transaction.module’).then((x) => x.TransactionModule),`
11.  `  },`
12.  `];`

It means, we are going to load both the **`MasterModule`** and the **`TransactionModule`** lazily.

It means, the new child module injectors will get created for them, and they will be children of the root injector.

Next, configure the child routes in **`MasterModule`** and **`TransactionModule`** as follows:

11.  `  }`

16.  `})`
17.  `export class MasterModule {}`

It means, we are going to load both the **`CustomerModule`** and the **`ProductModule`** lazily.

It means, the new child module injectors will get created for them, and they will be the children of the master module injector.
