**What do we have?**

We have an application where a hierarchy of the module injector exists as follows:

![](images/Image56172.jpg)

**Figure 24.25**

# Question 1 – What exactly is the purpose of having a hierarchy of injectors?

*   When a dependency is expected in the Angular application, the first request will be sent to the connected component’s element injector. If it cannot be resolved, it will be forwarded to the parent component’s element injector and so on, until the dependency is resolved.
*   Finally, if the *root element injector* fails to resolve the dependency, it will be forwarded to the connected module’s module injector. If it fails to resolve, it will be forwarded to the parent module injector and so on until the dependency is resolved. Finally, if the root module injector also fails to resolve the instance, Angular will throw a “no provider…” error.

Let’s give it a try.

Create a service and use it in the **`CusomerComponent`**.

For creating a service, you can use the following command:

![](images/Image56249.jpg)

**Figure 24.26**

It will create the service as follows:

1.  `@Injectable({`
2.  `   providedIn: ‘root’`
3.  `})`
4.  `export class MyTestService {`
5.  `   constructor() {}`
6.  `}`

Remove the provider in the root as we are going to do the demo with **`NgModule`**.

Then do the following trials:

1.  Execute the application, and you should get a “no provider…” error.
2.  Then, put the provider in the **`CustomerModule`** as follows:

    1.  `@NgModule({`
    2.  `   declarations: [CustomerComponent],`
    3.  `   imports: [CommonModule, RouterModule.forChild(routes)],`
    4.  `   providers:[MyTestService]`
    5.  `})`
    6.  `export class CustomerModule {}`

    Check the output. It should work.

3.  Then, remove the provider from **`CustomerModule`** and put it in the **`MasterModule`**. It should work.
4.  Then, remove it from **`MasterModule`** and put it in **`AppModule`**. It should work.
5.  Now, instead of **`CustomerComponent`**, use the service in **`CheckInComponent`**. It should work this time, too, because **`AppModule`** is the root of all.
6.  Now, remove it from **`AppModule`**, put the provider in **`MasterModule`**, and keep using the service in **`CheckInComponent`**. It should again break with “no provider…error.”
7.  Now, put the provider in both **`CustomerModule`** and **`ProductModule`**. (Remove it from everywhere else.) Use the service in both the **`Customer`** and the **`ProductComponent`**. It will work.
8.  Now, keep using the service in both the **`Customer`** and the **`ProductComponent`** but put the provider **only** in the MasterModule. It will work.

Feel free to try more combinations. Note down every result somewhere to analyze it properly.

The difference between the 7th and the 8th trial is the number of service instances:

*   In the 7th trial, two instances of the service got created.

    To confirm the same, put a breakpoint in the constructor of the service. You will see the breakpoint get hit twice.

*   Whereas, in the 8th trial, only one got created. The injector located in the hierarchy’s higher position can create and inject into all the components below. So, the same instance was shared between both the components. This time the breakpoint will get hit only once.

The service instances created by the root module injector will be singleton throughout the application.

The second level module injector creates an instance that will be singleton across all the modules’ components and children.

So the answer to the question is that it will help us control the provider’s scope.

# Question 2 – What will happen if the module injectors are configured with the same providers at multiple levels?

# Let me simplify this question.

Let’s say, in the current sample project, you put the providers for **`MyTestService`** in both **`AppModule`** and **`CustomerModule`**. What will happen in that case?

One instance of **`MyTestService`** will be created in such a case, and it will be available throughout the application. The same instance will be injected wherever it is expected except for **`CustomerComponent`**. For **`CustomerModule`**, one more independent instance will be created and will be injected into **`CustomerComponent`**.

It is similar to the overriding concept in the object-oriented programming. All get the same instance as it is the root, but the customer receives it’s own instance.

(Make sure to try it out by yourself.)

# Question 3 – When we import one module in another, does it create a hierarchy?

Please note, importing the modules doesn’t create any kind of hierarchy.

Angular won’t create a separate module injector for the imported modules.

The child injectors will be created when the modules are lazy-loaded.

# Question 4 – In that case, what happens to the providers of the imported module?

We discussed it once in this chapter already – the providers get merged to the parent module’s providers.

For instance, the providers of all the modules imported by **`AppModule`** get merged to the providers of **`AppModule`**.

Try the following demo by yourself:

*   Create one more Angular module. Call it **`ServiceModule`**.
*   Then, in that **`ServiceModule`**, define the providers for **`MyTestService`**.
*   Make sure that there is no providers anywhere else in the application.

Now, try both the question 1 and the question 2 trials again, but instead of specifying the providers, import **`ServiceModule`**, and **`test`**.

You can see the same output as the providers get merged.

# Question 5 – How is configuring using the NgModule decorator different from the Injectable decorator?

When configured with an Injectable decorator, we get the advantage of tree shaking, which ensures that if the service is not used anywhere in the application, it will be removed from the final bundle and thus reduce the overall bundle size.

# Question 6 – Is it possible to configure the other child module injectors using the Injectable decorator?

We have seen the **`Injectable`** decorator demo in the previous labs, where we configured the root injectors using the **`providedIn`** root approach.

The same **`Injectable`** decorator can be used for configuring the child module injectors. Check the following code:

1.  `@Injectable({`
2.  `   providedIn: TransactionModule`
3.  `})`
4.  `export class MyTestService {`
5.  `}`

The preceding code configures the module injector associated with **`TransactionModule`**. This time, the service instance will only be created, and it will be singleton across **`TransactionModule`**.

It won’t be accessible to any other modules.

I recommend you to try all the eight trials in question 1 and question 2 with this approach.

# Question 7 – If a particular requirement is possible with both the approaches, that is, with both the NgModule decorator and the Injectable decorator, then in such a case, which one is the recommended approach?

The **`Injectable`** decorator is the answer because of tree shaking.

# Question 8 – In that situation, configuring the NgModule decorator seems useless; why do we have it?

It is because of the following two reasons:

1.  An injectable decorator won’t allow us to explicitly specify the **`DI token`**. When we have such a requirement, we have to go with the **`NgModule`** decorator. Try to recollect *[Chapter 17: Token Interceptor](#c17.xhtml),* and *[Chapter 21: ErrorHander](#c21.xhtml)* from this book.
2.  Let’s say you are creating an Angular library that the others can download and use.

    Let’s say that the library contains a service called **`NotificationService`**, which the others will use. **`NotificationService`** is dependent on **`EmailService`**, which is, in turn, dependent on **`ConfigService`**.

    In this case, the one who wants to use **`NotificaionService`** has to download the library and then configure the injector for **`NotificationService`**, **`EmailService`**, and **`ConfigService`**.

    It is quite challenging for a developer to know all the dependencies, especially when it comes to the third party libraries.

    Try to imagine when the dependency level of the service is even more complicated.

    A better way will be, the one developer who creates the service will also make an Angular module and configuring it with the providers for all the three services.

    The one who wants to use the service will import that module in their module, and that’s it.

    Do you remember **`HTTPClientModule`**? We imported it in our module, which made the root module configured for **`HTTPService`** and all the other dependent services.

These two use-cases cannot be covered with the **`Injectable`** decorator.

If you want a module injector, my advice is that you go with an **`Injectable`** decorator whenever you can.

If you are a library developer or want to specify a DI Token, then go with **`NgModule`** decorator explicitly.

# [Summary](#toc.xhtml_s161a)

That finishes our *[Chapter 24](#c24.xhtml)* and brings us to Station 13. We have 7 more stations to cover before you can call yourself an Angular developer. Let’s revise the topics we covered in this chapter:

*   We learned to create the components and the modules using Angular CLI.
*   We learned how the Angular modules make the component development easier. We grouped the related components in the modules and then exported only a few of them as public. We learned that the non-exported components in an Angular module becomes private to that module.
*   We learned that the Angular modules also group the directives and pipes that become private to that module unless explicitly exported.
*   We learned about the **`ServiceModules`** that are the Angular modules used specifically for configuring the injectors for the services and the dependent services. **`HttpClientModule`** is an example of such **`ServiceModule`**.
*   We already discussed the third advantage of the Angular modules in the previous chapter – lazy loading.
*   We learned that even the private components of an Angular module could be accessed via routing.
*   We learned that **`BrowserModule`** imports **`CommonModule`** and re-exports it. Hence, the directives exported by **`CommonModule`** will be visible inside **`AppModule`**.
*   We learned that the tree-shaking mechanism in the Angular build process makes sure that all the unused components are removed from the final build, unless they are referred via routing. The component will be considered unused if it is not used in any other component’s template.
*   We learned that Angular creates a child module injector when a module is lazy-loaded.

# [CHAPTER 25](#toc.xhtml_c25)

# [Angular Pipes](#toc.xhtml_c25)

# [Introduction](#toc.xhtml_s162a)

Well, on our journey to Station 14, we will learn about the Angular Pipes.

You have already seen the two inbuilt pipes in action so far – async and JSON.

# [What are the pipes?](#toc.xhtml_s163a)

In Angular, we use pipes for transforming the data.

Let’s say, we have the data that needs a transformation before being displayed. That’s when you need an Angular pipe. Whenever you feel that the data needs to be transformed and displayed in the project, create a pipe for that.

The following are commonly used built-in pipes for the data formatting:

*   **DatePipe**: Formats a date value according to the locale rules.
*   **UpperCasePipe**: Transforms text to all upper case.
*   **LowerCasePipe**: Transforms text to all lower case.
*   **CurrencyPipe**: Transforms a number to a currency string, formatted according to the locale rules.
*   **DecimalPipe**: Transforms a number into a string with a decimal point, formatted according to the locale rules.
*   **PercentPipe**: Transforms a number to a percentage string, formatted according to the locale rules.

You already know the syntax for using pipes.

I strongly recommend you do a quick demo yourself with a couple of pipes listed earlier – declare a variable in the view class and display it in the view.

1.  `Name= “Sukesh Marla”;`
2.  `…`
3.  `{{Name | uppercase}}`

It would be interesting if we created a custom pipe. So, let’s do it.

# [Lab 10 – Custom pipe in action – Demo 1](#toc.xhtml_s164a)

In this demo, we will create a custom pipe that will transform a byte to KB, MB, GB…

**Step 1 – Create a project**

Create a new Angular project using Angular CLI.

1.  `ng new lab10-ng`

**Step 2 – Modify AppComponent**

Declare a numeric property in the view class with some initial value and display it in the template using the interpolation.

1.  `export class AppComponent {`
2.  `   SomeValue: number = 2456;`
3.  `}`
4.  `{{SomeValue}}`

**Step 3 – Check the output**

Execute the application using the **`ng serve`** command and make sure that everything is OK.

![](images/Image56345.jpg)

**Figure 25.1**

**Step 4 – Create the pipe**

Create the new pipe using the following command:

![](images/Image56352.jpg)

**Figure 25.2**

It will generate a new file **`file-size.pipe.ts`** with the following content:

1.  `import {Pipe, PipeTransform} from ‘@angular/core’;`

3.  `@Pipe({`
4.  `   name: ‘fileSize’`
5.  `})`
6.  `export class FileSizePipe implements PipeTransform {`

8.  `   transform(value: unknown, …args: unknown[]): unknown {`
9.  `     return null;`
10.  `  }`
11.  `}`

# Discussion – Continued

**Let’s explore the code generated after executing the CLI command.**

The command created a new file *file-size.pipe.ts* and made some changes in *app.module.ts*. Let’s explore both.

file-size.pipe.ts

*   We have a TypeScript class called FileSizePipe.
*   Class is implementing an interface *PipeTransForm* – **the first step for creating pipe**.
*   Implementing the interface forces us to define the *transform* method. (Ignore *return null* for now.)
*   The class is decorated with the *Pipe* decorator – **the second step for creating a pipe**.
*   Check the *name* property in the *Pipe* decorator. That is the one we will be using in the template later in the stage.

app.module.ts

Finally, open AppModule, and you will see the *FileSizePipe* included in the *declarations* section of the *NgModule* decorator.

Similar to components, every pipe in Angular is grouped inside an Angular module. If you create a pipe inside one module and want it to be used inside another module, then the first module needs to export it.

**Common Module**

The commonly used Angular inbuilt pipes such as *uppercase*, *async*, *json* are a part of *CommonModule*.

**Custom pipes and AppModule**

In real-time projects, we won’t add the custom pipes to the AppModule. We will have a separate module that will be imported wherever needed. Such modules are referred to as SharedModules.

**unknown datatype**

I know the developer inside you is troubling you with the *unknown* data type in the preceding code.

There are two particular data types in TypeScript that are meant for the dynamic coding – *any* and *unknown*.

The similarity between them are as follows:

Anything can be assigned to both of them.

let a: any = 100;      // We can assign anything to any

let b: unknown =  1000; // We can assign anything to the unknown just like any

The difference between them are as follows:

*any* can be assigned to anything. The *unknown* cannot be assigned to anything.

let s1: string = a;     // any is assigable to anything

let s2: string = b; // Invalid, we can’t assign *b* to any other type (without an explicit assertion)

# Lab – Continued

**Step 5 – Change the pipe logic**

Change the transform method as follows:

1.  `  transform(value: number, …args: number []): string {`
2.  `     return ‘Just Compile’;`
3.  `  }`

Did you notice that we changed the *unknown* to the other data types? It’s always the best practice to specify the exact data types.

**Step 6 – Apply the pipe**

Open the **`AppComponent`** template and apply the pipe as follows:

1.  `{{SomeValue |fileSize}}`

**Step 7 – Check the output**

Execute the application, and you should get the following output:

![](images/Image56437.jpg)

**Figure 25.3**

No matter what the SomeValue variable’s value is, you will always get the preceding output because the transform method is not returning anything dynamic.

**Step 8 – Change the pipe logic**

Now it’s time to put the real logic inside the pipe — change the transform method.

1.  `  transform(value: number, …args: number[]): string {`
2.  `     let data = ‘’;`
3.  `     if (value > (1024 * 1024 * 1024)) {`
4.  `         data = (value / 1024 / 1024 / 1024).toFixed(2) + ‘ GB’;`
5.  `    } else if (value > (1024 * 1024)) {`
6.  `         data = (value / 1024 / 1024).toFixed(2) + ‘ MB’;`
7.  `    } else if (value > 1024) {`
8.  `         data = (value / 1024).toFixed(2) + ‘ KB’;`
9.  `    } else {`
10.  `         data = value + ‘ B’;`
11.  `    }`
12.  `     return data;`
13.  `  }`

This time, instead of returning a fixed value, we are returning a dynamic value.

**Step 9 – Check the output**

Execute the application.

Do a couple of more tests. Change the value to some larger value and check the output.

![](images/Image56444.jpg)

**Figure 25.4**

# [Lab 11 – Custom pipe in action – Demo 2](#toc.xhtml_s165a)

This time we will create a pipe that will transform the data by concatenating it with the other values.

**Step 10 – Update AppComponent for demo**

Create three new properties of the type string in AppComponent and display them in the template as follows:

1.  `export class AppComponent {`
2.  `   SomeValue: number = 2456;`
3.  `   FirstName: string= “Sukesh”;`
4.  `   MiddleName: string= “Nagesh”;`
5.  `   LastName: string= “Marla”;`
6.  `}`

1.  `{{SomeValue |fileSize}}<br>`
2.  `Full name :{{FirstName}} - {{MiddleName}} - {{LastName}}`

**Step 11 – Check the output**

![](images/Image56522.jpg)

**Figure 25.5**

You can see that the three values are concatenated and displayed in the UI. As of now, the transformation logic or the concatenation logic is written as a part of our template. It is a bad practice. We need a pipe for such a situation.

**Step 12 – Create the pipe**

Create the concat pipe as follows:

![](images/Image56595.jpg)

**Figure 25.6**

**Step 13 – Implement the transform logic**

As you know, the first parameter in the transform method represents the data to which the pipe is attached.

Our current requirement expects more than one value, and that is where the second parameter comes into the picture.

Redefine the transform method as follows:

1.  `export class ConcatPipe implements PipeTransform {`
2.  `   transform(value: string, …args: string[]): string {`
3.  `     args.forEach((x) => {`
4.  `       value = value + ‘-’ + x;`
5.  `    });`
6.  `     return value;`
7.  `  }`
8.  `}`

**Step 14 – Apply the pipe**

Change the **`AppComponent`** template to the following:

1.  `……`
2.  `Full name :{{FirstName |concat: MiddleName: LastName  }}`

As you can see, we have applied the concat pipe to **`FirstName`** and then passed **`MiddleName`** and **`LastName`** as the parameter.

**Step 15 – Check the output**

You will get the same output, but this time the concatenation logic (transformation logic) is written inside the pipe.

# [My experience](#toc.xhtml_s166a)

Almost in every consulting assignment that I do, I find one common issue. Most of the time, the transformation logic is written either in the component class or in the template. Sometimes, I even find the transformation logic written as a part of a service. It is a bad practice. Use the pipes for any transformation logic.

# [Impure pipes](#toc.xhtml_s167a)

We are not done with the pipes yet. What we have seen so far are pure pipes. There is another kind of pipe – the impure pipes. Let’s understand it next.

*In Angular, the change detection is the process of updating the DOM when the data changes. We are going to talk about it in detail in the upcoming chapter. It executes when a DOM event happens, such as click, focus, etc., or when an HTTP request is completed or even after certain APIs complete the execution, such as the setTimeout and setInterval.*

If we talk about pipes, then the pipe will be executed only if the value to which it is attached gets changed**. It will not run on every change detection.**

Let’s talk about a scenario – we have the data, and we want to transform that data based on some data in the local storage. Let’s say there is a button in the UI that will change the local storage value. After the button click, the change detection will execute, but because the value to which the pipe was attached didn’t change, our pipe will not re-execute. So, we won’t get the desired output.

Don’t worry if the preceding paragraph is not exact. Let’s do the demo and understand the same.

# [Lab 12 – Understand impure pipe](#toc.xhtml_s168a)

**Step 16 – Create a pipe**

Create a new pipe called ImpureTest using CLI.

![](images/Image56606.jpg)

**Figure 25.7**

**Step 17 – Redefine transform**

Redefine the transform method as follows:

1.  `transform(value: number, …args: number[]): number {`
2.  `     if (localStorage.myValue) {`
3.  `       return value + parseInt(localStorage.myValue);`
4.  `    } else {`
5.  `       return value;`
6.  `    }`
7.  `  }`

**Step 18 – Setup AppComponent**

First, declare a simple variable in the **`AppComponent`** class as follows:

1.  `Counter: number = 20;`

Next, display that variable in the template and attach the **`impureTest`** pipe as follows:

1.  `Counter: {{Counter | impureTest}}`

Then, create a button in the template as follows:

1.  `<input type=”button” value=”Click Me” (click)=”HandleClick()”>`

Finally, define the **`HandleClick`** method as follows:

1.  `   HandleClick() {`
2.  `     if (!localStorage.myValue) {`
3.  `       localStorage.myValue = 1;`
4.  `    } else {`
5.  `       localStorage.myValue = parseInt(localStorage.myValue) + 1;`
6.  `    }`
7.  `  }`

Please note that every time the button is clicked, we are increasing the local storage value by one, but we are not changing the variable counter’s value.

**Step 19 – Check the output**

Execute the application. Initially, you will get to see a result based on the current local storage value.

After that, no matter how many times you click the button, the local storage value will change, but the UI will never change.

# Discussion – Continued

Don’t be under the impression that the change detection didn’t execute. It did.

The problem is that the pipe didn’t execute as the counter variable’s value didn’t change.

And that is because it is a pure pipe.

If you want the pipe to be executed on every change detection, you must make it *impure*.

# Lab – Continued

**Step 20 – Make the pipe impure**

Convert the pure pipe to impure as follows:

1.  `@Pipe({`
2.  `   name: ‘impureTest’,`
3.  `   pure:false`
4.  `})`
5.  `export class ImpureTestPipe implements PipeTransform {`

**Step 21 – Check the output**

Now, check the output. It will work.

That is everything about the pipe.

# [Summary](#toc.xhtml_s169a)

With this chapter, we get one more station closer to completing our journey. Station 15 – the Custom Directives journey will be awaiting you in the next chapter.

Let’s revise this chapter:

*   In this chapter, we learned about pipes that help us encapsulate the transformation logic in the application.
*   We learned that we need to create a class for creating pipes, implement it from the *PipeTransform* interface, and then attach the *Pipe* decorator.
*   We learned that the pipes are also encapsulated inside the Angular modules, just like the components.
*   We learned that the commonly used pipes in the Angular application such as *json*, *async*, *uppercase*, etc., are defined inside *CommonModule*.
*   We learned that the custom Angular modules (commonly referred to as SharedModule) are created to encapsulate all the custom pipes, and the other modules will import those modules in the real-time projects.
*   We spoke about the difference between the two data types in TypeScript – *any* and *unknown*. We learned that the variables of type *any* can be assigned to the other types, whereas the variables of the type *unknown* can be set to only the variables of the type *unknown*.
*   We saw the demonstration of the TypeScript REST parameter.
*   We learned that the pure pipes execute only at the beginning, and only when the input value gets changed.
*   We learned that the impure pipes execute after every change detection.

# [CHAPTER 26](#toc.xhtml_c26)

# [Angular Directives](#toc.xhtml_c26)

# [Introduction](#toc.xhtml_s170a)

In our journey to Station 15, we will talk about the Angular directives once again.

So far, we have explored a lot of inbuilt directives in Angular. Now it is time to create some of our own.

# [What are directives?](#toc.xhtml_s171a)

In the Angular world, it is a way to extend HTML.

In Angular, we will find one of the following three kinds of directives:

1.  Element directives
2.  Attribute directives
3.  Structural directives

The **Element directives** are also called the components in Angular. You have already seen many demos where we created an Angular component, creating a custom DOM element.

![](images/Image56697.jpg)

**Figure 26.1**

The **Attribute directives** are used to enhance the existing behavior of any DOM element. Ngclass and ngstyle are the best examples for them.

The **Structural directives** are used when we want to shape or reshape the existing DOM element’s structure, typically by adding, manipulating, or removing the elements. ngFor and ngIf are the best examples for the same.

Let’s do a demo on both the custom Attribute and Structural directives.

# [Lab 11 – Mouse Over Directive](#toc.xhtml_s172a)

In this lab, we will create a custom directive, which will add the ability to change the element’s background color on the mouseover.

**Step 1 – Create a new angular project**

Create a new angular project as follows:

1.  `ng new lab11-ng`

**Step 2 – Create a new directive**

Create a new Angular directive as follows:

![](images/Image56747.jpg)

**Figure 26.2**

It will create a TypeScript file with the following content:

1.  `import {Directive} from ‘@angular/core’;`
2.  `@Directive({`
3.  `   selector: ‘[appMouseOver]’`
4.  `})`
5.  `export class MouseOverDirective {`
6.  `   constructor() {}`
7.  `}`

# Discussion – Continued

The preceding code is pretty straight forward. There is a class which is decorated with the *Directive* decorator. Have you noticed a square bracket in the *selector*? That is very important. We will talk more about it after this demo.

# Lab – Continued

**Step 3 – Add the testing code**

Write a simple **`console.log`** statement in the constructor of the directive as follows:

1.  `export class MouseOverDirective {`
2.  `   constructor() {`
3.  `     console.log(‘Hello world’)`
4.  `  }`
5.  `}`

**Step 4 – Attach the directive**

Open AppComponent and create an HTML element and attach the preceding directive as follows:

![](images/Image56788.jpg)

**Figure 26.3**

**Step 5 – Execute the application**

Execute the application and check the output.

![](images/Image56826.jpg)

**Figure 26.4**

Can you find “Hello world” twice in the console?

We applied a directive to the two controls (one to the span element and one to the input element), and hence the two instances of the directive class got created, and therefore the message got displayed two times.

# Discussion – Continued

Next, we need to access the element to which the directive is attached, and for that, Angular has a service called – *ElementRef*. *ElementRef* provides the wrapper over the *nativeDOM element* to which the directive is attached.

Once the element reference is obtained, it can be manipulated using another Angular service called *Renderer2*.

# Lab – Continued

**Step 6 – Access and manipulate the element in the directive**

Inject both the services into class and then modify the background color of control as follows:

1.  `export class MouseOverDirective {`
2.  `   constructor(el: ElementRef, renderer: Renderer2) {`
3.  `     renderer.setStyle(el.nativeElement, ‘background’, ‘red’);`
4.  `  }`
5.  `}`

***Note:*** Though *ElementRef* allows manipulating the corresponding element directly, it is not recommended. For all the manipulation, using the *Renderer2* service is recommended.

**Step 7 – Check the output**

You will get the control with the updated color this time.

![](images/Image56863.jpg)

**Figure 26.5**

# Discussion – Continued

Let’s understand the next step.

As of now, we are hard coding the color in the directive. In the next step, let’s make it dynamic, and for that, we will use the *inputproperty*.

As I said before, the components are also directives. So, you will find a lot of similar features between them, such as the input and the output properties, life cycle events, etc.

# Lab – Continued

**Step 8 – Create an input property**

Create a new input property in the directive for the color and use it instead of the hardcoded value.

1.  `import {Directive, ElementRef, Input, Renderer2} from ‘@angular/core’;`
2.  `@Directive({`
3.  `   selector: ‘[appMouseOver]’`
4.  `})`
5.  `export class MouseOverDirective {`
6.  `   @Input() color:string= ‘red’;`
7.  `   constructor(el: ElementRef, renderer: Renderer2) {`
8.  `     renderer.setStyle(el.nativeElement, ‘background’, this.color);`
9.  `  }`
10.  `}`

**Step 9 – Assign value to input property**

Next, assign the value to the preceding input property in the **`app.component.html`** file as follows:

1.  `<span appMouseOver color=”blue”>My Span 1</span><br>`
2.  `<input type=”button” value=”I am button” appMouseOver color=”yellow”>`

# Discussion – Continued

The input property of a component is bound to a DOM property in the template.

By default, Angular considers the DOM property name and the input property name as the same.

That is why, the DOM property *color* of *input* control is mapped to the input property *color* in the MouseOverDirective.

# Lab – Continued

**Step 10 – Check the output**

Are you expecting the elements with the blue and yellow colors this time? If yes, it would be disappointing as both will continue to be displayed in the red color.

# Discussion – Continued

Wondering why? We spoke about it once earlier.

*A constructor will be executed when the instance of a class is created.*

When it comes to components and directives, the *constructor* is not the perfect place to write the initialization logic. The *constructor* is executed as soon as the instance is created. At this point, the input properties are not set to the values passed from the parent component.

*ngOnInit* is the perfect place for such kind of logic.

# Lab – Continued

**Step 11 – Implement ngOnInit**

Next, define the **`ngOnInit`** method in the component as follows:

1.  `import {Directive, ElementRef, Input, OnInit, Renderer2}`
2.  `from ‘@angular/core’;`
3.  `……`
4.  `export class MouseOverDirective implements OnInit {`
5.  `   @Input() color:string= ‘red’;`
6.  `   constructor(private el: ElementRef, private renderer: Renderer2) {`
7.  `  }`
8.  `   ngOnInit(): void {`
9.  `     this.renderer.setStyle(this.el.nativeElement, ‘background’, this.color);`
10.  `  }`
11.  `}`

**Step 12 – Execute the application.**

This time you will get to see the controls with the new colors.

# Discussion – Continued

Are you happy with the approach that we are following for assigning the value to the input property?

How about if we can achieve something like the following?

1.  `<span mouse-over-color=”blue”>My Span 1</span><br>`
2.  `<input type=”button” value=”I am button”  mouse-over-color=”yellow”>`

Does it look better? If yes, then that’s what we will be doing next.

# Lab – Continued

**Step 13 – Redefine the input property**

First of all, pass the parameter to the input decorator as follows:

1.  `   @Input(‘mouse-over-color’) color:string= ‘red’;`

After the preceding code, we have to change the **`AppComponent`** code to the following:

1.  `<span appMouseOver mouse-over-color=”blue”>My Span 1</span><br>`
2.  `<input type=”button” value=”I am button” appMouseOver mouse-over-color=”yellow”>`

# Discussion – Continued

By having the parameter to the Input decorator, we instructed the Angular to have a different mapping.

Now the input property *color* is mapped to the DOM property *mouse-over-color*. We use a parameterized Input decorator to expose the directive’s properties to the outside world with a different name.

# Lab – Continued

**Step 14 – Check the output**

Recheck your update. It must be the same as before.

**Step 15 – Change the selector**

Next, redefine the directive selector to the mouse-over-color as follows:

1.  `……`
2.  `@Directive({`
3.  `selector: ‘[mouse-over-color]’` *`// (Make sure not to miss square brackets.)`*
4.  `})`
5.  `export class MouseOverDirective implements OnInit {`
6.  `   @Input(‘mouse-over-color’) color:string= ‘red’;`
7.  `……`

# Discussion – Continued

Did you notice that our selector and Input property are the same now?

If yes, then you are paying attention to everything, or else you need to improve the focus. 😊

# Lab – Continued

**Step 16 – Change AppComponent**

Next, change the **`AppComponent`** code to the following:

1.  `<span mouse-over-color=”blue”>My Span 1</span><br>`
2.  `<input type=”button” value=”I am button” mouse-over-color=”yellow”>`

**Step 17 – Check the output**

Recheck your update. It must be the same as earlier.

# Discussion – Continued

What next? Does the output look like the one that was our target? Aren’t we expecting the color to be changed on mouseover? Let’s put the final nail to the coffin.

In Angular, we can handle the host events using a decorator called *HostListener*.

Here, the host means the one who uses it.

In the case of directives, it is the element to which the directive is attached.

# Lab – Continued

**Step 18 – Handle mouse over and mouse leave events**

Define the two methods in the directive class and attach the HostListener decorator on both of them as follows:

1.  `ngOnInit(): void {`
2.  `  }`
3.  `   @HostListener(‘mouseover’)`
4.  `   OnMouseOver(){`
5.  `     this.renderer.setStyle(this.el.nativeElement, ‘background’, this.color);`
6.  `  }`
7.  `   @HostListener(‘mouseleave’)`
8.  `   OnMouseLeave(){`
9.  `     this.renderer.setStyle(this.el.nativeElement, ‘background’, ‘’);`
10.  `  }`

Using the *HostListener* decorator, we can listen to the events of the host of the directive.

**Step 19 – Check the output**

This time you will find the color gets changed when you take the cursor on that element.

It was a simple demonstration of creating a custom attribute directive that enhanced the existing HTML element’s existing behavior. That’s called the **Attribute Directive**.

# [Different types of Directive selectors](#toc.xhtml_s173a)

I know you are waiting for an answer on those square brackets we used with the selectors.

The thing is, the square bracket is one of the ways to specify the selectors – there are more.

**Attribute selector**

In our previous example, we specified a selector for our directive as [mouse-over-color].

It is an example of an Attribute selector – the directive will be attached to every DOM element, with a mouse-over-color attribute.

**CSS selector**

Look at the following code:

1.  `@Directive({`
2.  `   selector: ‘.abc’`
3.  `})`
4.  `export class MouseOverDirective`

It is the CSS selector. The directive will be attached to every element in DOM, which has CSS class abc. For example, it will be attached to the following element:

1.  `<input type=”button” value=”I am button” class=”abc”>`

**Element selector**

Look at the following code:

1.  `@Directive({`
2.  `   selector: ‘input’`
3.  `})`
4.  `export class MouseOverDirective{}`

This one is the Element selector. The directive will be attached to every input element in DOM.

For example, it will be attached to both the following elements:

1.  `<input type=”button” value=”I am button”>`
2.  `<input type=”text” value=”I am button”>`

**Id selector**

Look at the following code:

1.  `@Directive({`
2.  `   selector: #MyId`
3.  `})export class MouseOverDirective{}`

This one is the Id selector. The directive will be attached to every element with the id attribute and the value as **`MyId`**.

For example, it will be attached to the first input element as follows:

1.  `<input id=”MyId” type=”button” value=”I am button”>`
2.  `<input type=”tex” value=”I am button”>`

# [Lab 12 – Create OnlyIfUserIsAuthorizedDirective Directive](#toc.xhtml_s174a)

In Lab 12, we will create a structural directive that will replace an *HTML element* with the custom *HTML div element* if the current logged user role is not the authorized user.

**Step 1 – Create a new directive**

Create a new angular directive, **`OnlyIfUserIsAuthorizedDirective`**, as follows:

![](images/Image57103.jpg)

**Figure 26.6**

**Step 2 – Execute the application**

Add a new input button in **`AppComponent`** and attach the preceding directive to it.

1.  `<input type=”button” value=”Add new record” appOnlyIfUserIsAuthorized >`

Then, put a “*hello world*” message in the constructor of the directive and confirm that everything is proper so far (similar to the previous lab testing).

**Step 3 – Inject the necessary services**

As we have already discussed, the requirement is to remove the button element and put the HTML span element when the user is not authorized. To get it done, inject the ElementRef and Renderer2 service to the directive like done earlier.

1.  `export class OnlyIfUserIsAuthorizedDirective implements OnInit {`
2.  `   constructor(private ele: ElementRef,private renderer: Renderer2) {……}`

**Step 4 – Define the authorization logic**

Put the following code inside the **`ngOnInit`** method as follows:

1.  `  ngOnInit(): void {`
2.  `     const isAuthorized = true;`
3.  `     if (localStorage.IsAuthorized !== ‘true’) {`
4.  `       const pNode = this.renderer.createElement(‘div’);`
5.  `       const txtNode = this.renderer.createText(Not authorized!!!’);`
6.  `       this.renderer.appendChild(pNode, txtNode);`
7.  `       this.renderer.appendChild(this.ele.nativeElement.parentElement, pNode);`
8.  `       this.renderer.removeChild(this.ele.nativeElement.parentElement,this.ele.nativeElement);`
9.  `    }`
10.  `  }`

# Discussion – Continued

As per the code, an element will be replaced with an *HTML div element* if the local storage contains a *false* value. Let’s analyze the code step by step:

1.  First, we created an *HTML div element*.
2.  Then we created a simple text.
3.  Then we appended the text into the *div element*.
4.  Next, we appended the *HTML div* element to the parent element of the current element. (The current element means the one to which we have attached the directive.)
5.  Finally, we removed the current element.

(We are assuming that the local storage holds a value indicating if a user is authorized or not.)

# Lab – Continued

**Step 5 – Execute the application**

Execute the application and confirm that the button element is automatically removed from the DOM when the local storage contains a false value.

![](images/Image57173.jpg)

**Figure 26.7**

Explicitly change the value in the local storage and refresh the page to see different outputs.

(We don’t have any screen which will change the value of the local storage to true or false. Do it manually using the chrome developer tools? For that, open the developer tools, move to the **`Application`** tab, and select the **`Local Storage`** from the left section.)

# Discussion – Continued

**Does it look like an attribute directive to you with a different logic?**

If your answer is yes, then you are right.

**Did we say we will be creating a structural directive at the beginning of this lab?**

Yes, we did.

Logically, both the attribute and the structure directives are attached to the existing elements as attributes. They are differentiated based on the purpose. One adds some behavior, and the other modifies the structure. Usually, you will find all the structural directives are written with the asterisk (***) symbol.

We will come to the secret behind the asterisk (***) soon.

# Lab – Continued

**Step 6 – Add the button to change authorization**

Manually changing the value of the local storage must be very irritating. So let’s create an input button in **`AppComponent`** for that purpose, as follows:

1.  `  <input type=”button” value=”Change Authorization” (click)=”Change()”>`
2.  `CurrentValue:{{CurrentAuthorization}}<br>`
3.  `<input type=”button” value=”Add new record” appOnlyIfUserIsAuthorized >`

1.  `……`
2.  `export class AppComponent {`
3.  `   title = ‘lab11-ng’;`
4.  `   CurrentAuthorization = localStorage.IsAuthorized;`
5.  `   Change(): void {`
6.  `       localStorage.IsAuthorized =`
7.  `localStorage.IsAuthorized === ‘true’ ?` `false : true;`
8.  `      this.CurrentAuthorization = localStorage.IsAuthorized;`
9.  `  }`
10.  `}`

It is a simple code. Every time the button is clicked, the value in the local storage is inversed.

**Step 7 – Execute the application**

You will be amazed to see that after the button click, the authorized section in the DOM won’t change. Do a hard refresh, and you will see the changes.

![](images/Image57180.jpg)

**Figure 26.8**

# Discussion – Continued

**What exactly is the reason for the preceding case?**

As of now, we have written the DOM manipulation logic in the *ngOnInit*.

*ngOnInit* is an Angular life cycle event that is executed when an Angular directive is initialized. Initialization happens only once at the beginning, and that means *ngOnInit* executes only once. After the button click, that code won’t run, and so the UI won’t change.

We need something else. There is an event called *ngOnChanges*. Let’s try that once.

# Lab – Continued

**Step 8 – Implement ngOnChanges**

Instead of implementing from the **`OnInit`** interface, implement the directive from the **`OnChanges`** interface. After that, define the **`ngOnChanges`** method as follows:

1.  `ngOnChanges(changes: SimpleChanges): void {`
2.  `     const isAuthorized = true;`
3.  `     if (localStorage.IsAuthorized !== ‘true’) {`
4.  `       const pNode = this.renderer.createElement(‘div’);`
5.  `       const txtNode = this.renderer.createText(‘Not authorized!!!’);`

7.  `       this.renderer.appendChild(pNode, txtNode);`
8.  `       this.renderer.appendChild(this.ele.nativeElement.parentElement, pNode);`
9.  `       this.renderer.removeChild(this.ele.nativeElement.parentElement,this.ele.nativeElement);`
10.  `    }`
11.  `  }`

**Step 9 – Check the output.**

This will not serve the purpose, either. After the preceding change, the output won’t change.

# Discussion – Continued

**ngOnChanges**

*ngOnChanges* executes once at the beginning after the *ngOnInit method,* and after that, when the directive’s data-bound property gets changed, it will be executed.

Right now, our directive doesn’t have any data-bound property, and hence *ngOnChanges* executes only at the beginning.

**ngDoCheck solution**

We do have a unique life-cycle method meant for such kind of situation – *ngDoCheck*.

It will be executed on every change-detection.

Don’t worry about the change-detection for now. Just understand that the change detection is responsible for updating the DOM, and it happens automatically after the DOM events.

Let’s revise every life cycle event we have seen so far.

*   *Constructor* and *ngOnInit* will be executed only once at the beginning when the component or the directive is constructed.

    (Please note that the ngIf directive and the routing destroys and creates the component/directive on demand. These two methods execute every time a component/directive is created.)

*   *ngOnChanges* runs once at the beginning after the *ngOnInit method,* and after that, when the directive’s data-bound property gets changed, it will be executed.
*   *ngAfterViewInit* is executed once the view is completely initialized. Use it when you need to access the DOM elements in the component. (Remember the focus example in one of the *[chapter 22](#c22.xhtml)*?)
*   *ngDestroy* is executed when the component/directive is destroyed and used for the cleanup operations. (Remember the example where we destroyed our subscriptions?)
*   *ngDoCheck* is executed on every change detection.

For our situation, *ngDoCheck* fits best.

**Step 10 – Implement ngDoCheck**

Remove **`ngOnChanges`** and put **`ngDoCheck`** as follows:

1.  `export class OnlyIfUserIsAuthorizedDirective implements DoCheck {`
2.  `   constructor(`
3.  `     private ele: ElementRef,`
4.  `     private renderer: Renderer2`
5.  `  ) {}`
6.  `   ngDoCheck(): void {`
7.  `     const isAuthorized = true;`
8.  `     if (localStorage.IsAuthorized !== ‘true’) {`
9.  `       const pNode = this.renderer.createElement(‘div’);`
10.  `       const txtNode = this.renderer.createText(‘Not authorized!!!’);`

12.  `       this.renderer.appendChild(pNode, txtNode);`
13.  `       this.renderer.appendChild(this.ele.nativeElement.parentElement, pNode);`
14.  `       this.renderer.removeChild(this.ele.nativeElement.parentElement,this.ele.nativeElement);`
15.  `    }`
16.  `  }`
17.  `}`

**Step 11 – Check the output.**

Now test the application as follows:

1.  Open the developer tools and change the value of **`IsAuthorized`** local storage to *true*.
2.  Now refresh the browser. You should be able to see the authorized section.
3.  Now click on the **`Change Authorization`** button. It will inverse the local storage value, making the authorized section get replaced with a “**`Not authorized!!!`**” message.
4.  Now click on the **`Change Authorization`** button again. Strangely, you won’t get the authorization section back. If you check the value in the local storage, you will find it updated as *true*.
5.  Click on the **`Change Authorization`** button again. You will find the following error in the console:

    ![](images/Image57341.jpg)

    **Figure 26.9**

# Discussion – Continued

**What exactly happened here?**

When the user is not authorized, the element gets **removed** from DOM, and the new element is inserted.

**Removed** is an important word here. As it is removed, it won’t be available the next time, and that’s what is causing this strange output that was shown earlier.

The best way to deal with the DOM manipulation scenarios with the directives is by using the *ViewContainerRef* and *TemplaeRef* service.

Let’s see it in action first and then have a detailed discussion on the same.

# Lab – Continued

**Step 12 – Inject the new services into directives**

Inject **`ViewContainerRef`** and **`TemplateRef`** in the directive and remove the code in the **`ngDoCheck`** as follows:

1.  `constructor(`
2.  `     private vc: ViewContainerRef, private tc: TemplateRef<any>) {}`

4.  `   ngDoCheck(): void {`

6.  `  }`

**Step 13 – Wrap authorize content in ng-template**

In **`AppComponent`**, wrap the authorize content inside the ng-template as follows:

1.  `<ng-template>`
2.  `   <input type=”button” value=”Add new record” >`
3.  `</ng-template>`

Don’t worry about the **`appOnlyIfUserIsAuthorized`** directive for now. We will bring it back into action very soon.

**Step 14 – Check the output**

You will find that the content inside the ng-template won’t get rendered on the screen no matter whether the user is authorized or not.

You can argue that the contents are supposed to be rendered by default.

It is not, and let’s discuss why.

# Discussion – Continued

**ng-template, TemplateRef, and ViewContainerRef**

The **`ng-template`** directive represents an Angular template.

In general, a template refers to a DOM structure that won’t have any existence by itself. They will be at run-time composed together with the other DOM elements.

**Is the definition difficult to digest?**

Don’t worry. Just remember that the contents defined inside the **`ng-template`** won’t become a part of the DOM automatically. It will be used manually in the code and get inserted somewhere.

**TemplateRef and ViewContainerRef – two heroes**

**`TempalteRef`** service will provide a reference to the content defined inside the **`ng-template.`**

**`ViewContainrRef`** represents a container where one or more views can be attached to a component.

Continue with the demo; things will become much simpler afterwards.

# Lab – Continued

**Step 15 – Attach the directive**

Attach the **`appOnlyIfUserIsAuthorized`** directive to **`ng-template`** as follows:

1.  `……`
2.  `<ng-template appOnlyIfUserIsAuthorized >`
3.  `   <input type=”button” value=”Add new record” >`
4.  `</ng-template>`

If you remember, the earlier **`appOnlyIfUserIsAuthorized`** directive was attached to the button. Now we shifted it to ng-template.

**Step 16 – define ngDoCheck**

Implement **`ngDoCheck`** as follows:

1.  `……`
2.  `   ngDoCheck(): void {`
3.  `     const isAuthorized = true;`
4.  `     if (localStorage.IsAuthorized !== ‘true’) {`
5.  `       this.vc.clear();`
6.  `    }`
7.  `     else{`
8.  `       this.vc.clear();`
9.  `       this.vc.createEmbeddedView(this.tc);`
10.  `    }`
11.  `  }`

As you can see, the template is dynamically embedded into the view container when the user is authorized, and the view container is cleared if the user is not authorized.

The **`TemplateRef`** service gave us the reference of **`ng-template`** to which the directive was attached, and **`ViewContainerRef`** gave us the reference of the container, where we can add more views dynamically.

**Step 17 – Check the output.**

Just check the output, and you will see “*Add new record*” when **`IsAuthorized`** has the true value, and it will be gone when **`IsAuthorized`** has a false value.

# Discussion – Continued

There is a big issue in the output. Did you notice it? If you think it is the missing message when the user is not authorized, then that’s not what we are talking about. We will solve that problem soon in a much better way. This is even a bigger problem**, which is, *ngDoCheck* executes on every change detection.**

Is that a problem? Isn’t that what we wanted all this time?

Maybe yes, but not during all the change detection.

What does that mean?

Did you notice that we have invoked the *clear* method in both the *if* and *else* block? Did you ask yourself why it is so?

The *if* block makes sense as that represents the unauthorized section, and in that case, we expect it to be emptied, but why does the *else* block have it?

If we don’t invoke the *clear* method in the *else* block, every time the change detection happens, a new element is inserted into the container. Check it out yourself.

Remove that *clear method* call from the *else* block.

In the development mode, Angular executes the change detection two times at the beginning. So, you will find the two buttons at the start. (Don’t worry about that now.)

Considering that you still have your MouseOverDirective in action, take your cursor on that one element to which that directive is attached. You will notice that every time you take the cursor, one more element gets created. The same happens when you take out the cursor.

That is the reason why the *clear* method is invoked in both the *if* and *else* block.

Is it a proper way to do it?

No, removing an element on every change detection and recreating it again is too bad from the performance point of view. We need a more elegant way.

It is a logical issue and can be solved using a simple *Boolean* variable.

# Lab – Continued

**Step 18 – Change ngDoCheck**

Declare a boolean variable **`IsViewCreated`** with the initial value as false and use it in **`ngDoCheck`**.

1.  `   IsViewCreated = false;`
2.  `   ngDoCheck(): void {`
3.  `     const isAuthorized = true;`
4.  `     if (localStorage.IsAuthorized !== ‘true’ && this.IsViewCreated) {`
5.  `       this.vc.clear();`
6.  `       this.IsViewCreated = false;`
7.  `    } else if (localStorage.IsAuthorized !== ‘false’ && !this.IsViewCreated) {`
8.  `       this.vc.createEmbeddedView(this.tc);`
9.  `       this.IsViewCreated = true;`
10.  `    }`
11.  `  }`

**Step 19 – Check the output**

Everything should be working fine now.

During my consulting experience, I found this one issue very often – unnecessary DOM manipulation, which leads to a slow application and sometimes can be avoided using a simple logic.

# Discussion – Continued

Next, we have to bring back that missing message if the user is not authorized.

To get it done, we will first create a custom component and then load it dynamically.

Time for learning something new – **dynamically loading component**.

It can be done using an Angular service called *ComponentFactoryResolver*.

Let’s bring it into action.

# Lab – Continued

**Step 20 – Create a CustomMessageComponent**

Create a new Angular component called **`CustomMessageComponent`** using Angular CLI and then create an **`Input`** property inside it called **`Message`** and use it in the template.

1.  `……`
2.  `export class CustomMessageComponent implements OnInit {`
3.  `   @Input() Message: string = ‘Default message’;`
4.  `……`

1.  `<b>{{Message}}</b>`

**Step 21 – Inject ComponentFactoryResolver into the directive.**

1.  `import {`
2.  `   ComponentFactoryResolver,`
3.  `   ……`
4.  `} from ‘@angular/core’;`
5.  `……`
6.  `export class OnlyIfUserIsAuthorizedDirective implements DoCheck {`
7.  `   constructor(`
8.  `     private vc: ViewContainerRef,`
9.  `     private tc: TemplateRef<any>,`
10.  `     private resolver: ComponentFactoryResolver,`
11.  `  ) {}`
12.  `……`

**Step 22 – Redefine ngDoCheck**

It’s time for some magic.

1.  `ngDoCheck(): void {`
2.  `     const isAuthorized = true;`
3.  `     if (localStorage.IsAuthorized !== ‘true’ && this.IsViewCreated) {`
4.  `       this.vc.clear();`
5.  `       const MessageComponentFactory = this.resolver.resolveComponentFactory(`
6.  `         CustomMessageComponent`
7.  `      );`
8.  `       const MessageComponent = this.vc.createComponent(MessageComponentFactory);`
9.  `       MessageComponent.instance.Message = ‘Not Authorized!!!’;`
10.  `       this.IsViewCreated = false;`
11.  `    } else if (localStorage.IsAuthorized !== ‘false’ && !this.IsViewCreated) {`
12.  `       this.vc.createEmbeddedView(this.tc);`
13.  `       this.IsViewCreated = true;`
14.  `    }`
15.  `  }`

Just check the **`if`** block in the preceding code. It has all the magic.

Just two lines of code and the component is dynamically created and loaded.

You will find a call to a clear method again in the **`else`** block. That’s needed as we need to remove the dynamically created component. (But our boolean variable assures that the logic won’t execute on every unnecessary change detection.)

**Step 23 – Check the output.**

Excellent, isn’t it. The preceding code worked with a charm. You will get your “*Not Authorized!!!*” back to the action.

# Discussion – Continued

I am very sure that the developer inside you is still not happy. He/she is still hungry. He/she is still thinking about that special asterisk (***) symbol that we used with the *ngIf* and *ngFor* directive.

Let me show it, instead of explaining.

# Lab – Continued

**Step 24 – Change AppComponent.**

Open **`AppComponent`** and change your authorized section as follows:

1.  `<input type=”button” value=”Add new record” *appOnlyIfUserIsAuthorized >`

(Please note: there is no **`ng-template.`**)

**Step 25 – Check the output.**

Nothing will change. You will still get the same output.

# Discussion – Continued

When we put the asterisk (*) with the directive, Angular automatically creates *ng-template*, attaches the directive, and put the contents (input button in our case) inside *ng-template*.

In short, it converts the preceding code to the following code:

1.  `<ng-template appOnlyIfUserIsAuthorized >`
2.  `   <input type=”button” value=”Add new record” >`
3.  `</ng-template>`

Using the asterisk (*) is called the micro syntax. It makes our life easy when it comes to using structural directives.

# [Summary](#toc.xhtml_s175a)

With this chapter, we have completed an essential concept in Angular. Without the custom directives, I cannot expect any Angular project. That concludes our journey to Station 15\. We are almost near the end of our trip, with only five stations pending. Let’s revise what have we learned in this chapter:

*   We learned about the directives in this chapter. They extend the existing HTML.
*   We learned that there are three kinds of directives that Angular has.
*   The first one, referred to as the Element directives, lets us create the new HTML tags. The element directives can also be termed as Components in Angular.
*   The second one is the Attribute directives that lets us extend the behavior of the existing HTML elements. **`ngClass`** and **`ngStyle`** are typical examples of such directives.
*   The third one is the structural directives that lets us change the structure of the existing HTML elements. Structural directives, similar to the Attribute directives, will be attached to an HTML element as an attribute.
*   We learned that usually, the structural directives would be attached to the HTML element with the prefix ***. For example – **`*ngFor`**, **`*ngIf.`**
*   We learned about the different ways to specify a selector to a directive – the Attribute selector, Id selector, CSS selector, and Element selector.
*   We saw a demonstration of the **`ElementRef`** service in Angular to reference the HTML element to which the directive is attached.
*   We learned about the **`Render2`** service in Angular that will provide the various methods to deal with the DOM manipulation.
*   Once again, we understood how the **`ngOnInit`** method is preferred for the initialization logic. We learned that a *constructor* would be executed when a class’s object is created, whereas **`ngOnInit`** is executed once the component/directive is initialized. Here, Initialized means, the instance is created, and all the properties are initialized with the values passed by the parent component.
*   We learned about a parameterized Input decorator that lets us expose the component/directive property to the outside world with a different name.
*   We learned about the **`HostListner`** decorator that lets us handle the element’s events to which the directive is attached.
*   We learned about *the* **`ngOnChanges`** life cycle event of Angular that executes whenever the value of a component’s data-bound property is changed.
*   We learned about the **`ngDoCheck`** life cycle event of Angular that executes on every change detection.
*   We spoke about the **`ng-template`** that is used to create the Angular templates. The Angular templates won’t get rendered automatically. They will be used and rendered inside some other DOM elements at run-time with some custom logic.
*   We learned about the **`TemplateRef`** service that gives us the reference of contents defined inside the **`ng-template`**.
*   We saw the **`ViewContainerRef`** service in action, which gave us the reference of a container to which one or more views can be attached.
*   We learned about the **`ComponentFactoryResolver`** service that can be used to load the components at the run-time dynamically.
*   We spoke about the micro-syntax with the directives that removes the overhead of explicitly creating the **`ng-template`** element.

When it comes to learning any technology, I firmly believe that RND plays a critical role.

When we get stuck somewhere, we search for the answers on the internet:

*   That will land us to some forums or articles or videos.
*   That will introduce some new unknown and unaware concepts to us.
*   That will take our skills to the next level.

Hence, I very much believe that the RND plays a very important role when it comes to learning and technology.

Considering that, I was hoping you would create something like the following by yourself:

![](images/Image57433.jpg)

**Figure 27.1**

In real-time projects, template-driven is not recommended for such complex forms. Template-driven is useful if you have a small form without many validations.
