# [CHAPTER 28](#toc.xhtml_c28)

# [Angular Styles](#toc.xhtml_c28)

# [Introduction](#toc.xhtml_s177a)

In our journey to Station 17, we will learn how to deal with the stylesheets in Angular.

# [External stylesheets](#toc.xhtml_s178a)

You must have noticed a file called **`angular.json`** in your Angular application. It is the Angular CLI configuration file. Commands such as **`ng serve`**, and **`ng build`** are executed using the settings inside this file.

![](images/Image57444.jpg)

**Figure 28.1**

One of the essential sections defined inside this file is the builds/styles section.

By default, you will find an entry for **`style.css`** in that section.

It acts as a global CSS file for our Angular application. The styles defined inside it will be available throughout the application. We have already seen it in action in one of the previous chapters.

# [Can we have more than one external stylesheet files?](#toc.xhtml_s179a)

Yes, and there are the following three ways to do it:

1.  By including it in **`Index.html`** using a link tag.
2.  By using CSS @import.
3.  By importing in the **`angular.json`** file.

The first one is pretty straight forward. Include the external CSS file in the **`index.html`** using the **`link`** tag.

1.  `<link rel=”stylesheet” href=”mystyle.css”>`

Let’s look into the other two ways.

**Step 1 – Create a project.**

Create a new angular project **`lab15-ng`** using the **`ng`** command.

**Step 2 – Create a component.**

Create a new component called **`ExternalCSSTestComponent`**, and then include it in the **`AppComponent`**. (Remove the existing content of **`AppComponent`**.)

1.  *`<!-- External CSS Test Component -->`*
2.  `<p class=”MyClass1 MyClass2”>Hello World!!!</p>`

1.  *`<!-- App Component -->`*
2.  `<app-external-csstest-component></app-external-csstest-component>`

**Step 3 – Create a new CSS file.**

Create a new CSS file called **`mystyle.css`** in the **`src`** folder and put the following content:

1.  `.MyClass2{`
2.  `   font-weight: bold;`
3.  `}`

**Step 4 – Update style.css**

Import **`mystyle.css`** in **`style.css`** using the CSS @Import rule and then create a new CSS class as follows:

1.  `@import “mystyle.css”;`
2.  `.MyClass1{`
3.  `   border:1px solid black;`
4.  `}`

**Step 5 – Test the output**

Execute the application. You will get a bold “*Hello World!!!*” with a black border around it – bold because of mystyle.css and the border because of *style.css*.

**Step 6 – Build the application.**

Build the application using the **`ng build --prod`** command.

After executing the command, you will find a single *CSS* file with the name **`style.***.css`**.

Open that file and check the contents of it.

1.  `.MyClass2{font-weight:700}.MyClass1{border:1px solid #000}`

As you can see, the contents of both **`style.css`** and **`mystyle.css`** are bundled into a file now.

Let’s see what happens if we import more than one CSS file.

**Step 7 – Create a new CSS file.**

Create a new CSS file **`color.css`** inside the **`src`** folder.

1.  `.MyClass3{`
2.  `   color: red;`
3.  `}`

**Step 8 – Update ExternalCSSTestComponent**

Apply this new CSS class in the **`ExternalCSSTestComponent`** as follows:

1.  *`<!--  External CSS Test Component -->`*
2.  `<p class=”MyClass1 MyClass2 MyClass3”>Hello World!!!!</p>`

**Step 9 – Import the new CSS file**

Similar to the previous time, import color.css in the **`style.css`** as follows:

1.  `import “mystyle.css”;`
2.  `@import “color.css”;`

4.  `.MyClass1{`
5.  `   border:1px solid black;`
6.  `}`

**Step 10 – Execute the application**

Execute the application. You will get a bold and red “*Hello World!!!*” decorated with all the three CSS classes.

**Step 11 – Build the application**

Now build the application.

Angular CLI will bundle all the three CSS files into one file.

After building, you will get one CSS file **`style.***.css`** encapsulating the contents of all the three files.

1.  `.MyClass2{font-weight:700}.MyClass3{color:red}.MyClass1{border:1px solid #000}`

That was the example of CSS @import. On ng build, Angular CLI bundles the **`style.css`** and all its dependent stylesheets into one bundle.

Next, let’s look at the **`angular.json`** way.

**Step 12 – Import color.css in angular.json**

First, remove the import statement for color.css in **`style.css`**.

1.  `import “mystyle.css”;`
2.  `.MyClass1{`
3.  `   border:1px solid black;`
4.  `}`

Next, include **`color.css`** in the **`angular.json`** file as follows:

1.  `“build”: {`
2.  `           ……`
3.  `             “styles”: [`
4.  `               “src/styles.css”,`
5.  `               “src/color.css”`
6.  `            ],`
7.  `             “scripts”: []`
8.  `          },`

**Step 13 – Execute the application**

Any change in the **`angular.json`** file requires re-execution of the **`ng serve`** command.

So make sure that you do the same. (For that, press Ctrl+C to stop the existing execution and execute the ng serve command again.)

After the execution, you will get the same output as earlier. You will get “*Hello World!!!*” in red color with bold formatting and a black border.

**Step 14 – Build the application**

Build the application using the ng build --prod command, and surprisingly you will not find anything significantly different. You will find a single CSS file getting generated containing the contents from all the three CSS files like earlier.

1.  `.MyClass2{font-weight:700}.MyClass1{border:1px solid #000}.MyClass3{color:red}`

It seems like nothing has changed. Both CSS @import and **`angular.json`** behave the same.

# Discussion – Continued

When it comes to the external stylesheets, we can include them either using the *CSS @import* or *angular.json*. In a real-time project, you may see any of them in action or maybe both of them.

In the preceding case, we used both.

You may be wondering which one to follow. Well, both have pros and cons:

*   The *CSS @import* one makes the development life easy as every time we think of adding or removing an external stylesheet from the application, we don’t need to re-execute the *ng serve* command.
*   On the other hand, *angular.json* lets us create multiple bundles if needed. In case you feel like the generated CSS bundle is too large and needs to be broken down into multiple files, then *angular.json* is the best way. Let’s see this one in action.

# Lab – Continued

**Step 15 – Configure angular.json for multiple bundles**

Open **`angular.json`** and change the styles section as follows:

1.  `“styles”: [`
2.  `               “src/styles.css”,`
3.  `               {`
4.  `                 “input”: “src/color.css”,`
5.  `                 “bundleName”: “color”`
6.  `              }`
7.  `            ],`

**Step 16 – Execute the application**

Re-run the **`ng serve`** command. There will be no change in the output.

**Step 17 – Build the application**

Build the application again. This time two CSS bundles get generated – **`style.***.css`** & **`color.***.css`**

The first one contains the contents of **`styles.css`** and **`mystyle.css`**.

The second one contains the contents of **`color.css`**.

# [Component styles](#toc.xhtml_s180a)

When we talk about components, we call it a combination of two things – a template class and a template. But that’s not 100% true. Sometimes, it can also have a CSS file of its own.

When you create an Angular Component using Angular CLI, you must have noticed such files. If not, don’t worry about that. We will look into it now and also understand how that CSS file is different from **`style.css`**.

# [Lab – 15 – Understand component style](#toc.xhtml_s181a)

**Step 1 – Comment ExternalCSSTestComponent**

Comment **`ExternalCSSTestComponent`** in **`AppComponent`** as follows:

1.  *`<!-- App Component -->`*
2.  *`<!-- <app-external-csstest-component></app-external-csstest-component> -->`*

**Step 2 – Create Components**

Create four components – Customer, CustomerList, Product, and ProductDashboard.

**Step 3 – Use Components**

Use these components inside each other as follows:

1.  *`<!-- App Component -->`*
2.  *`<!-- <app-external-csstest-component></app-external-csstest-component> -->`*
3.  `<div class=”DivContainer”>`
4.  `   <p>Hello From App Comonent</p>`
5.  `   <app-customer></app-customer>`
6.  `   <app-product></app-product>`
7.  `</div>`

1.  *`<!-- Customer Component -->`*
2.  `<div class=”DivContainer”>`
3.  `   <p>Hello From Customer</p>`
4.  `   <app-customer-list></app-customer-list>`
5.  `</div>`

1.  *`<!-- Product Component -->`*
2.  `<div class=”DivContainer”>`
3.  `   <p>Hello From Product</p>`
4.  `   <app-product-dashboard></app-product-dashboard>`
5.  `</div>`

1.  *`<!-- Customer List Component -->`*
2.  `<div class=”DivContainer”>`
3.  `   <p>Hello From Product-List</p>`
4.  `</div>`

1.  *`<!-- Product Dashboard Component -->`*
2.  `<div class=”DivContainer”>`
3.  `   <p>Hello From Product-Dashboard</p>`
4.  `</div>`

**Step 4 – Define styles**

Now the magic begins. Define a new style inside **`style.css`** as follows:

1.  `……`
2.  `.DivContainer {`
3.  `   border:1px solid black;`
4.  `   margin: 5px;`
5.  `}`

![](images/Image57587.jpg)

**Figure 28.2**

**Step 5 – Check the output**

Execute the application to get the following output.

As expected, the same CSS got applied in every component.

**Step 6 – Define component styles**

Next, define the new styles in the **`app.component.css`** file as follows:

1.  `.DivContainer {`
2.  `   border:2px black dashed;`
3.  `}`

**Step 7 – Check the output**

You will find that the style defined in the **`app.component.css`** file only affected the **`div`** element in the **`AppComponent`**. The other components are still displaying a solid black border that is attached because of **`style.css`**.

![](images/Image57699.jpg)

**Figure 28.3**

Can you see the dashed border? That’s because of the **`app.component.css`** file, which is attached to **`AppComponent`**.

**Step 8 – Define the component style**

Next, let’s define the style in the **`customer-list.component.css`** file as follows:

1.  `.DivContainer {`
2.  `   border:3px red dotted;`
3.  `}`

**Step 9 – Check the output**

This time you will notice that the new style only impacted the **`div`** element inside **`CustomerListComponent`**.

Do you see the red dotted border in the previous output? That’s because of the new style we added in the **`customer-list.component.css`** file.

![](images/Image57707.jpg)

**Figure 28.4**

That’s what the component style is all about – styles are encapsulated inside the component.

**Step 10 – Build the application**

Let’s build the application, and let’s exactly understand what happens to these component styles after the build.

# Discussion – Continued

You will be amazed to see that no specific CSS file gets generated for any component after building.

Open *style.***.css* and *color.***.css* and check if the component styles are bundled inside those files. You won’t find the component styles inside those files either.

**Then where are the component styles?**

The component CSS styles are encapsulated into the component’s view itself, which is why it doesn’t affect the rest of the application.

The Angular CLI bundles all the custom application code into a bundle file called *main. ***.js* file.

Open that *main.***.js* file and search for *3px*. If you remember, the CustomerList CSS had *3px border*.

You will find it inside the file.

![](images/Image57784.jpg)

**Figure 28.5**

At run-time, Angular will dynamically inject those styles into the DOM as the inline styles when the respective components are loaded. You can even check them in the developer tool.

![](images/Image57826.jpg)

**Figure 28.6**

***Note:*** As you know, in the case of lazy loading, multiple such bundles will be generated. So, you will find the component styles inside the respective bundles instead of the main bundle.

**Is *styleUrls* the only way to create the component-specific styles?**

*styleUrls* lets us include the external CSS files into the component, but that’s not the only way to have the component-specific style. We have another method – the inline styles. It can be done as follows:

1.  `@Component({`
2.  `   selector: ‘app-customer-list’,`
3.  `   templateUrl: ‘./customer-list.component.html’,`
4.  *`// styleUrls: [‘./customer-list.component.css’]`*
5.  ``   styles: [`.DivContainer {``
6.  `     border:3px red dotted;`
7.  ``  }`]``
8.  `})`
9.  `export class CustomerListComponent implements OnInit {……`

Using *styleUrls* over *styles* is recommended, but you will still find some places in your project where the latter is used.

# [View encapsulation](#toc.xhtml_s182a)

We have said it a couple of times – the component CSS styles are encapsulated into the component’s view itself.

We can control this encapsulation per-component basis by setting the encapsulation mode in the component decorator.

There are the following three possible values as of now:

# 1\. Emulated

It is the default one. In this mode, you will find that each DOM element is attached with some unique attributes.

1.  `@Component({`
2.  `   selector: ‘app-root’,`
3.  `   templateUrl: ‘./app.component.html’,`
4.  `   styleUrls: [‘./app.component.css’],`
5.  `   encapsulation: ViewEncapsulation.Emulated,`
6.  `})`
7.  `export class AppComponent {`

Execute the application and open the developer tools, and open the **`Elements`** tab.

**Can you see _ngcontent-pij-c15 in the preceding image?**

As you can see, it is attached to every element in **`AppComponent`**.

**Can you find_ngcontent-pij-c12?**

As you can see, it is attached to every element of the **`CustomerComponent`**.

![](images/Image57833.jpg)

**Figure 28.7**

Angular generates a unique string for every component and adds it to every child element of that component.

**Are you wondering why Angular does so?**

By doing so, Angular makes those styles component-specific.

![](images/Image57877.jpg)

**Figure 28.8**

To understand it, check the head section. You will find multiple style tags.

Angular dynamically created those style tags at run-time.

Two of them are global styles, and then after that, every style tag represents one component.

Expand the third *style* tag. It represents the AppComponent style.

**Do you see something interesting?**

CSS selector also got renamed.

Renaming will ensure that these CSS settings are attached to only those elements that have the corresponding attribute.

![](images/Image57910.jpg)

**Figure 28.9**

# 2\. None

This one is not useful in reality. Setting this value indicates that we don’t want any view encapsulation, making the component style a global style.

1.  `@Component({`
2.  `   selector: ‘app-root’,`
3.  `   templateUrl: ‘./app.component.html’,`
4.  `   styleUrls: [‘./app.component.css’],`
5.  `   encapsulation: ViewEncapsulation.None,`
6.  `})`
7.  `export class AppComponent {`

You will notice that after setting it to None, any style defined in AppComponent will apply globally.

# 3\. ShadowDOM

I have already said once earlier that the Angular components are inspired by the **`Web Components`** concept introduced in HTML5. One of the core features of **`WebComponent`** is ShadowDOM.

**Let me show you what ShadowDOM is?**

Create an HTML file and put the following content inside it. We are demonstrating an HTML concept, so the Angular project is not needed. You can have a simple HTML file.

1.  `<html>`
2.  `<head>`
3.  `     <title>Datepicker</title>`
4.  `</head>`
5.  `<body><input type=”date”></body>`
6.  `</html>`

Execute the file and open the developer tools.

![](images/Image57951.jpg)

**Figure 28.10**

**Do you know the specialty of this HTML 5 date element?**

It is a Web Component. Like our Angular components, it is an encapsulation over some HTML (better-called template), formatting (CSS), and behavior (logical code). Do you want to see the underlying template?

Click on the setting icon in the developer tools.

![](images/Image57991.jpg)

**Figure 28.11**

It will launch the settings panel. In the Elements subsection, check the “*Show user agent shadow DOM option*”.

Then, close the setting panel and recheck the **`Elements`** section. This time you will be able to expand the date element.

![](images/Image58031.jpg)

**Figure 28.12**

**Do you see #shadow-root?**

That’s what ShadowDOM is. The styles included inside it will not leak to the outside world, so the outside world remains unaffected, but that’s not the end. The web components’ content will stay untouched by the external world’s CSS as well. It means any CSS formatting attached to the div element by the outside world will not affect the div element of the date element.

![](images/Image58073.jpg)

**Figure 28.13**

**ShadowDOM in Angular**

In Angular, by setting the encapsulation to *ShadowDOM*, we will get similar behavior.

# Demo 1

**Step 1 – Set encapsulation**

For demonstration, set the encapsulation mode to ShadowDOM for **`AppComponent`** and **`CustomerListComponent`**. Then, remove the styleUrls from **`AppComponent`** and the styles from **`CustomerListComponent`**.

1.  `@Component({`
2.  `   selector: ‘app-customer-list’,`
3.  `   templateUrl: ‘./customer-list.component.html’,`
4.  *`// styleUrls: [‘./customer-list.component.css’]`*
5.  *``// styles: [`.DivContainer {``*
6.  *`//   border:3px red dotted;`*
7.  *``//}`],``*
8.  `   encapsulation: ViewEncapsulation.ShadowDom,`
9.  `})`
10.  `export class CustomerListComponent implements OnInit {`

1.  `@Component({`
2.  `   selector: ‘app-root’,`
3.  `   templateUrl: ‘./app.component.html’,`
4.  *`//styleUrls: [‘./app.component.css’],`*
5.  `   encapsulation: ViewEncapsulation.ShadowDom,`
6.  `})`
7.  `export class AppComponent {`

**Step 2 – Set encapsulation**

Set the encapsulation mode of the Product, ProductDashboard, and CustomerComponent as ShadowDOM.

**Step 3 – Execute the application**

Now execute the application. You will get a screen with no border around anything.

![](images/Image58111.jpg)

**Figure 28.14**

It proves that the external CSS has no impact on the components with the view encapsulation set to ShadowDOM. (No CSS in **`style.css`** impacted these components.)

# Demo 2

**Step 1 – Define Component styles**

Add the component styles to both **`CustomerListComponent`** and **`AppComponent`** as follows:

1.  `@Component({`
2.  `   selector: ‘app-root’,`
3.  `   templateUrl: ‘./app.component.html’,`
4.  `   styleUrls: [‘./app.component.css’],`
5.  `   encapsulation: ViewEncapsulation.ShadowDom,`
6.  `})`
7.  `export class AppComponent`

1.  `@Component({`
2.  `   selector: ‘app-customer-list’,`
3.  `   templateUrl: ‘./customer-list.component.html’,`
4.  *`// styleUrls: [‘./customer-list.component.css’]`*
5.  ``   styles: [`.DivContainer {``
6.  `     border:3px red dotted;`
7.  ``  }`],``
8.  `   encapsulation: ViewEncapsulation.ShadowDom,`
9.  `})`
10.  `export class CustomerListComponent implements OnInit {`

**Step 2 – Change encapsulation**

Remove the encapsulation from all the components except **`AppComponent`**.

(In other words, encapsulation to Emulated for **`AppComponent`**.)

**Step 3 – Execute the application**

Execute the application and check both the output and DOM using the developer tools.

![](images/Image58156.jpg)

**Figure 28.15**

![](images/Image58163.jpg)

**Figure 28.16**

AppComponent is rendered as a ShadowDOM, and so its styles are directly rendered inside it.

Now let’s check the output.

You will find a dashed border around AppComponent. That is obvious because that’s how the AppComponent style was written.

You will find a dotted red border around CustomerListComponent. That is obvious because that’s how CustomerListComponent style was written.

**Why the other components have a dashed border?**

Are you disappointed to see the dashed border around the other components?

When the encapsulation was set to *emulated,* the AppComponent style was limited to AppComponent. CustomerComponent did not inherit it.

That is not the case this time. This time, the AppComponent style is inherited to all the child components, and hence all the components except the CustomerList component have a dashed border. The customer list component has a dotted red border because it has its component style.

You may say that it is *violating the rule of ShadowDOM* – the outside style will not affect contents inside ShadowDOM, and the styles defined inside the ShadowDOM will not affect anyone else.

The answer is no. It is not violating. Look at the generated DOM again. AppComponent is rendered as a ShadowDOM, but what about the other components? They are rendered as ordinary children of AppComponent, and their encapsulation is still set as emulated (default). So, they will follow the same old methodology of the unique attribute.

That is the reason why all of them are getting a dashed border.

**Step 4 – Change encapsulation**

Set *encapsulation* to *ShadowDOM* for all the components.

![](images/Image58230.jpg)

**Figure 28.17**

After doing so, you will get the following output.

This time every component is rendered as a ShadowDOM. Hence, none of them are affected by the external CSS.

*ShadowDOM is best suited when it comes to creating reusable independent components.*

*For real-time application, emulated is the recommended and best approach*. With it, we can take advantage of the global stylesheet, and when the component-specific style is needed, we have the provision for that too.

# [Summary](#toc.xhtml_s183a)

*   We spoke about the three ways of including the external stylesheets in an Angular application – using the **`link`** tag in the **`index.html`** file, using *CSS @import*, and using the **`angular.json`** file.
*   We learned that the **`angular.json`** file has a section called *builds/styles* used to declare the external CSS files in the project. By default, the Angular boilerplate project comes with an entry for the **`style.css`** file.
*   We saw *CSS @import’s* demonstration and learned that on **`ng build`**, the Angular CLI bundles **`style.css`** and every stylesheet file it is dependent on and creates one bundle.
*   We saw a demonstration of importing the stylesheet files using the **`angular.json`** file and learned that all the stylesheet files mentioned in the **`angular.json`** file are bundled into one file on **`ng build`**.
*   We learned that importing the stylesheet files using the **`angular.json`** instead of *CSS @import* is considered a better approach. It gives us a way to instruct Angular CLI to generate multiple bundles, if needed.
*   We learned to attach the styles to individual components using the *Component* decorator’s *styleURL* or *styles* attribute. We learned that these styles wouldn’t get bundled inside any CSS bundle after the build; instead, they become part of the component’s view itself.
*   We learned about *ViewEncapsulation* that lets us control how the styles will be encapsulated into the component view.
*   We spoke about the *Emulated* encapsulation that is the default mode. This mode attaches a unique attribute to each DOM element of the component and renames each CSS selector so that the style becomes specific to only that component.
*   We spoke about the *Native* encapsulation that follows the *ShadowDOM* approach of HTML 5. With this mode, we assure that no external CSS affects the component’s contents, and the component-specific-style won’t affect the outer world. It is the best approach for the reusable components.
*   We spoke about the *None* encapsulation that makes every component-specific style a global style.

# [CHAPTER 29](#toc.xhtml_c29)

# [Angular Change Detection](#toc.xhtml_c29)

# [Introduction](#toc.xhtml_s184a)

Have you ever questioned yourself about how the template displays the latest data all the time? How is it updated every time the data in the component class gets changed? Change detection is the answer to that, and in this chapter, we will explore change detection in detail.

# [What is change detection?](#toc.xhtml_s185a)

Change Detection is a mechanism in Angular that runs over the bindings, evaluates expressions, compares them to the earlier values, and updates the DOM if necessary.

In short, it is the one responsible for keeping your UI up to date.

# [Which components are considered in change detection?](#toc.xhtml_s186a)

As we have discussed many times earlier, Angular maintains a tree hierarchy of components. This tree will have a Root Component at the top.

In change detection, every component in the component hierarchy will be checked and re-rendered if needed.

(You will see it in action in our demo very soon.)

# [When does Angular perform change detection?](#toc.xhtml_s187a)

Angular performs change detection during the following events in the application lifetime.

1.  **After the application initialization** – That is after the Angular application is bootstrapped, and Angular loads the bootstrap component.
2.  **After the DOM event listeners** – After the browser events such as click, focus, blur, and so on, are handled.
3.  **After the HTTP Data Request** – That is after the Ajax request is processed (response from the server is received) – response can be an error response or a success response.
4.  **MacroTasks** – That is after the macro-tasks such as **`setTimeout()`** or **`setInterval()`** are completed. These macro tasks are considered completed once the callback function passed to them completes the execution.
5.  **MicroTasks** – That is after the micro-tasks such as Promise are completed – a Promise will be considered complete once it is resolved or rejected.
6.  **Other async operations** – That is after the other async operations such as **`WebSocket.onMessage`** and **`Canvas.toBlob`** complete the execution.

# [How does Angular trigger the change detection?](#toc.xhtml_s188a)

Angular has *monkey patched* all the preceding APIS using a library called **`zone.js`**.

It has replaced all these low-level APIS with the newer versions. These newer versions have some additional functionality – such as after the callback is called, it makes sure that the changes are detected and the UI is updated if needed, that is, change detection.

# [Change Detection in development mode](#toc.xhtml_s189a)

One important thing you must understand is that the change detection is executed two times in the development mode. To prove that, let’s do a quick demo.

**Step 1 - Create a project**

Create a new Angular project **`lab16-ng`** using the **`ng new`** command.

**Step 2 - Create a new component**

Create a new Angular component called **`TwiceDetectionTestComponent`** using the **`ng`** command and then put the following content inside it:

1.  `<h1>Testing twice change detection call - {{getData()}}</h1>`
2.  `<input type=”button” value=”Click Me” (click)=”HandleClick()” >`

1.  `@Component({`
2.  `   selector: ‘app-twice-detection-test-component’,`
3.  `   templateUrl: ‘./twice-detection-test-component.component.html’,`
4.  `   styleUrls: [‘./twice-detection-test-component.component.css’]`
5.  `})`
6.  `export class TwiceDetectionTestComponentComponent   {`
7.  `   getData(): string {`
8.  `     console.log(“getData invoked”)`
9.  `     return “Just Compile LLP”`
10.  `  }`
11.  `   HandleClick(){`
12.  `  }`
13.  `}`

**Step 3 – Include the component in the root component**

Open **`app.component.html`** and replace the existing content with the following:

1.  *`<!-- app.component.html -->`*
2.  `<app-twice-detection-test-component></app-twice-detection-test-component>`

**Step 4 – Execute the application**

Execute the application using the **`ng serve`** command and check the output.

![](images/Image58286.jpg)

**Figure 29.1**

But more than the output, you should be looking at the developer tool console.

![](images/Image58351.jpg)

**Figure 29.2**

# Discussion

Did I say change detection executes two times in the development mode?

Does it look like two times to you? It is four times.

Let’s talk about it after some time. For now, continue with the next step.

# Lab – Continued

**Step 5 – Click the button**

Clear the developer console and click on the **`Click Me`** button in our component. As I said earlier, the button click will trigger change detection. It would be interesting to check how many times the change detection executes.

![](images/Image58410.jpg)

**Figure 29.3**

Clear the console and try again. You will notice that every time the button is clicked, the console is populated with two messages.

# Discussion – Continued

I am sure you are still worried about the four-time execution at the beginning. Don’t worry about that.

With the preceding step, we proved that the change detection is executed twice when the button is clicked.

Are you wondering about the reason for it? Let’s discuss.

Open the developer tools, put a breakpoint inside the getData method, and check the call stack.

During the first breakpoint, you will get the following call stack.

![](images/Image58418.jpg)

**Figure 29.4**

The second time, it will be as follows:

![](images/Image58486.jpg)

**Figure 29.5**

Can you find out the difference between the both?

During the first execution, Angular updated the DOM, and that’s what the detectchanges method does.

In the second call, Angular ensures that nothing has changed after the previous DOM re-rendering, and that’s what the checkNoChanges method does. It will throw an exception if it finds a changed binding or determines that DOM should be updated. Let’s prove it.

# Lab – Continued

**Step 6 – Make the DOM change after the first call**

For that, change the **`getData`** method as follows:

1.  `getData(): number {`
2.  `     const data = Math.random() * 100;`
3.  `     console.log(data);`
4.  `     return data;`
5.  `  }`

**Step 7 – Recheck the application**

Ignore the first-time execution as earlier. Clear the console and click on the **`Click Me`** button. Your developer console should look like the following:

![](images/Image58535.jpg)

**Figure 29.6**

We will get an exception – ExpressionChangedAfterItHasBeenChecked.

Angular did the change detection twice. During the first execution, some output was generated, and during the second, Angular finds a different value to be rendered. Hence, we got that error.

Please note that this two-time execution will only be seen in the development mode. In the production mode, the change detection occurs only once.

**Step 8 – Execute in production mode**

Re-run the application using the **`ng serve --prod`** command. Check the developer tools. You will find two outputs getting displayed. (Earlier, in the development mode, it was four times.)

Clear the console and click on the button. You will find only one value getting displayed. (Earlier, in the development mode, it was two times.)

# [Why is it double during the initialization time?](#toc.xhtml_s190a)

Based on the previous lab, we have the following combinations:

*   Development mode + application initialization time = change detection will be four times.
*   Development mode + other change detection triggers (such as event handler, macro tasks, etc) = change detection will be two times.
*   Production mode + application initialization time = change detection will be two times.
*   Production mode + other change detection triggers (such as event handler, macro tasks, and so on) = change detection will execute only once.

We learned that in the development mode, Angular does the change detection twice. During the first run, DOM is rendered with the latest component state, and in the second run, it confirms that DOM is not changed after the previous re-rendering. If it did, we will get a run-time error – *ExpressionChangedAfterItHasBeenChecked*.

Now, the question is why it is two times after the application initialization?

*   During the initialization of the application, Angular, by default, makes a call to the tick method, which leads to the change detection. (In the development mode, it happens twice – once **`changedetection`** and once **`checkNoChanges`**.)
*   Once that is done, because of the zone.js, the tick method again gets executed. That will lead to a change detection cycle run for the second time. (In the development mode, it happens twice – once **`changedetection`** and once **`checkNoChanges`**.)

# [Disconnect zone.js from Angular](#toc.xhtml_s191a)

Earlier, we understood that Angular uses **`zone.js`** to monkey patch the native low-level APIS. Those monkey patchings assure that once those low-level APIs complete, the change detection is fired, and so the DOM is updated.

**Does that mean there is no change detection without zone.js?**

That will not be true.

**Change Detection** is a mechanism in Angular that runs over the bindings, evaluates the expressions, compares them to the earlier values, and updates the DOM if necessary.

By monkey patching the low-level APIs, such as addEventListener, setTimeout, and so on, **`zone.js`** makes sure that the change detection happens automatically.

If we take out **`zone.js`**, it won’t remove the change detection ability of Angular. It will just remove the automation.

In Angular, there is a provision to disconnect the default zone from the application. Let’s do that.

**Step 1 – Create a new project**

The change detection learning gets too confusing sometimes. Hence, I always recommend creating a new project for every new demo.

Create a new Angular project **`lab17-ng`** using the **`ng`** command.

**Step 2 – Change AppComponent**

Change the **`AppComponent`** content as follows:

1.  `export class AppComponent   {`
2.  `   Counter: number=0;`
3.  `   Increement(){`
4.  `     this.Counter++;`
5.  `  }`
6.  `}`

1.  `<input type=”button” value=”Increement” (click)=”Increement()” /> {{Counter}}`

**Step 3 – Execute the application**

Execute the application using the **`ng serve`** command. Make sure that the value is updating after every button click.

The current application is configured to run with **`zone.js`** that fires the change detection after every click, by default.

**Step 4 – Disconnect the zone.js**

In the **`src`** folder, you will find a file called **`polyfill.ts`**.

Scroll to the bottom, and you will find a line importing the zone library. Comment on that statement or remove it.

1.  *`//import ‘zone.js/dist/zone’; // Included with Angular CLI.`*

**Step 5 – Execute the application**

Upon execution, you will get a blank page. Open the developer tools to check the following error:

![](images/Image58660.jpg)

**Figure 29.7**

# Discussion – Continued

As we have removed zone.js, there is no more zone in the Angular application. The zone is the execution context for the Angular application. Nothing much to worry about – just take note of one thing – Angular needs the zone, or else it will break the application, and to make it work, we need to provide our custom zone object.

Another alternate way is *NoopNgZone* – the noop zone is shipped with Angular, and that does nothing unlike the zone object provided by zone.js, which monkey patches the low-level APIs.

# Lab – Continued

**Step 6 – Provide the noop zone**

For that, change the bootstrap statement in the **`main.ts`** file to the following:

1.  `platformBrowserDynamic().bootstrapModule(AppModule,{ngZone: ‘noop’})`
2.  `   .catch(err => console.error(err));`

**Step 7 – Execute the application**

Execute the application. You will find the counter value displayed as “0”.

Next, click on the button. You won’t see any DOM change. That proves that no change detection is happening after the DOM events.

# Discussion – Continued

Angular does the change detection once at the beginning after the application is initialized. This is the reason why the initial value of the counter is displayed correctly.

But as the **`zone.js`** is disconnected, the auto-trigger of the change detection is no more active.

# Lab – Continued

**Step 8 – Manually detect the changes**

We removed **`zone.js`**, and so there will not be any auto change detection in the application, but surely, we can do it manually by invoking the tick method explicitly.

Get the reference of **`ApplicationRef`** service in **`AppComponent`** and then invoke the **`tick`** method as follows:

1.  `export class AppComponent {`
2.  `   Counter: number = 0;`
3.  `   constructor(private app: ApplicationRef) {}`
4.  `   Increement() {`
5.  `     this.Counter++;`
6.  `     this.app.tick();`
7.  `  }`
8.  `}`

**Step 9 – Check the output**

This time you will find the expected output.

Following a zoneless Angular approach can drastically improve the performance as it restricts the change detections, but it can increase the application’s complexity. So, choose wisely.

# [polyfill.ts](#toc.xhtml_s192a)

I am sure that you are trying to figure out about the **`polyfill.ts`** file after our lab.

**What are polyfills?**

Polyfills are libraries which add some additional features to the existing JavaScript and so makes the incompatible browsers compatible.

Let’s do a quick demo of one such polyfill called **`classlist.js`**.

**Step 1 – Create a new HTML file**

Create a new HTML file **`index.html`** and put the following content:

1.  `<!DOCTYPE html>`
2.  `<html>`
3.  `<head>`
4.  `     <style>`
5.  `         .mystyle {`
6.  `             width: 300px;`
7.  `             height: 50px;`
8.  `             border: 1px solid black;`
9.  `        }`
10.  `     </style>`
11.  `</head>`
12.  `<body>`
13.  `     <button onclick=”addStyle()”>Add Style</button>`
14.  `     <div id=”myDIV”>`
15.  `         I am a DIV element`
16.  `     </div>`
17.  `     <script >`
18.  `         function addStyle() {`
19.  `             document.getElementById(“myDIV”).classList.add(“mystyle”);`
20.  `        }`
21.  `     </script>`
22.  `</body>`
23.  `</html>`

**Step 2 – Test the HTML file**

Open the HTML file in any browser of your choice.

(Make sure that if you are using IE, it should be version 10 or higher)

Click on the button, and you should get a black border around your DIV element.

**Step 3 – Test the HTML file in IE9 or lower**

Now, open the same HTML file in IE9 or some lower version of IE.

You won’t find the demo working as the classList is not supported in IE9 and lower.

**Step 4 – Download the polyfill**

To make it work, we will use a polyfill called **`classlist.js`**.

It can be downloaded using the **`npm install`** **`classlist.js`** command.

**Step 5 – Include the polyfill**

Once downloaded, include the downloaded library file in **`index.html`** as follows:

1.  `<!DOCTYPE html>`
2.  `<html>`
3.  `<head>`
4.  `     <script  src=”./node_modules/classlist.js/classList.js”></script>`
5.  `     <style>`
6.  `         .mystyle {`
7.  `……`

**Step 6 – Re-test the HTML in IE9 or lower**

This time you will find it working.

**What is polyfill.ts in the Angular project?**

It is a file where we will include all the polyfills that we expect to be loaded before the app.

By default, it has only one entry – entry for **`zone.js`**.

The angular library is built on the latest standards of the web platform. We cannot assure you that all those standards work on all the browsers. Suppose you plan to execute the Angular application in the older browsers. In that case, you may need some polyfill libraries for sure, and as a standard, after downloading the polyfill, it has to be included in the **`polyfill.ts`** file.

All the libraries imported in the **`polyfill.ts`** are bundled and minified as **`polyfill.***.js`** when the application is built using the **`ng build`** command.

# [Change detection with the hierarchy of components](#toc.xhtml_s193a)

In our fourth demo, we will learn about the change detection having a hierarchy of components.

**Step 1 – Create a new project**

Create a new Angular project **`lab18-ng`**.

**Step 2 – Create components**

Create the Customer, CustomerList, CustomerAdd, and Product components using the **`ng`** command.

**Step 3 – Use components**

*   Use CustomerList and CustomerAddComponent inside the Customer component.
*   Use the Product and Customer component inside the AppComponent.
*   Also, create a button in all the components and handle their click events.
*   Create a method GetMessage in all the component classes and invoke it inside the interpolation syntax in the respective templates.

1.  `export class AppComponent {`
2.  `   GetMessage(): void {`
3.  `     console.log(‘App Component message’);`
4.  `  }`
5.  `   HandleClick(): void  {}`
6.  `}`
7.  `<!-- app.component.html -->`

11.  `   <app-customer></app-customer>`
12.  `   <app-product></app-product>`
13.  `</div>`

1.  `export class CustomerComponent  {`
2.  `   GetMessage(): void {`
3.  `     console.log(‘Customer Component message’);`
4.  `  }`
5.  `   HandleClick(): void  {}`
6.  `}`

1.  *`<!-- customer.component.html -->`*
2.  `<div>`
3.  `   {{GetMessage()}}`
4.  `   <input type=”button” value=”CustomerButton” (click)=”HandleClick()”>`
5.  `   <app-customer-list></app-customer-list>`
6.  `   <app-customer-add></app-customer-add>`
7.  `</div>`

1.  `export class ProductComponent{`
2.  `   GetMessage(): void {`
3.  `     console.log(‘Product Component message’);`
4.  `  }`
5.  `   HandleClick(): void  {}`
6.  `}`

1.  *`<!-- product.component.html -->`*
2.  `<div>`
3.  `   {{GetMessage()}}`
4.  `   <input type=”button” value=”ProductButton” (click)=”HandleClick()”>`
5.  `</div>`

1.  `export class CustomerAddComponent {`
2.  `   GetMessage(): void {`
3.  `     console.log(‘Customer Add Component message’);`
4.  `  }`
5.  `   HandleClick(): void  {}`
6.  `}`

1.  *`<!-- customer-add.component.html -->`*
2.  `<div>`
3.  `   {{GetMessage()}}`
4.  `   <input type=”button” value=”CustomerAddButton” (click)=”HandleClick()”>`
5.  `</div>`

1.  `export class CustomerListComponent{`
2.  `   GetMessage(): void {`
3.  `     console.log(‘Customer List Component message’);`
4.  `  }`
5.  `   HandleClick(): void  {}`
6.  `}`

1.  *`<!-- customer-list.component.html -->`*
2.  `<div>`
3.  `   {{GetMessage()}}`
4.  `   <input type=”button” value=”CustomerListButton” (click)=”HandleClick()”>`
5.  `</div>`

**Step 4 – Define a style**

Open *style.css* and add the following content:

1.  `div{`
2.  `   padding: 20px;`
3.  `   margin-top: 10px;`
4.  `   border: 1px solid black;`
5.  `}`

**Step 5 – Execute the application**

To learn and understand the change detection properly, execute the application in the production mode using the **`ng serve --prod`** command.

![](images/Image58744.jpg)

**Figure 29.8**

Open the developer tools. You will find a message from each component. You will find it two times as the change detection executes twice at the beginning.

![](images/Image58779.jpg)

**Figure 29.9**

**Step 6 – Click the button**

Clear the console and click the button in any of the components.

You will find that no matter which button is clicked, the message from every component gets displayed – it means every component is re-rendered.

![](images/Image58821.jpg)

**Figure 29.10**

**Step 7 – Disconnect the application from zone.js**

Let’s disconnect the application from **`zone.js`**.

For that, remove the import statement from the **`polyfills.ts`** file and modify the **`main.ts`** content to the following:

1.  `platformBrowserDynamic().bootstrapModule(AppModule,{ngZone:’noop’})`
2.  `   .catch(err => console.error(err));`

**Step 8 – Test the output**

Execute the application. The initial messages are still unaffected.

Clear the developer console and click the buttons.

You will get no messages as there is no more auto-trigger for the change detection as **`zone.js`** is removed in the previous step.

**Step 9 – Implement the manual change detection using the tick method**

Inject the ApplicationRef service in the CustomerListComponent and inside the HandleClick method, execute the tick method.

1.  `export class CustomerListComponent{`
2.  `   constructor(private app: ApplicationRef){`

4.  `  }`
5.  `   ……`
6.  `   HandleClick(): void  {`
7.  `     this.app.tick();`
8.  `  }`
9.  `}`

**Step 10 – Test the output**

Click on the button in the CustomerListComponent.

Although you get the output, it won’t be a happy output. After the button click, every component is rendered as the **`ApplicationRef.tick`** method triggers the change detection for every component in the entire hierarchy.

What if we want to trigger the change detection of only a specific component, not the entire hierarchy? That can be done using an Angular service called **`ChangeDetectorRef`**.

**Step 11 - Implement the manual change detection using the ChangeDetectionRef service**

Inject the **`ChangeDetectionRef`** service in the **`CustomerAddComponent`** and trigger the change detection inside the **`HandleClick`** method as follows:

1.  `export class CustomerAddComponent {`
2.  `   constructor(private cd: ChangeDetectorRef){`
3.  `  }`
4.  `……`
5.  `   HandleClick(): void  {`
6.  `     this.cd.detectChanges();`
7.  `  }`

**Step 12 – Test the application**

This time, click on the button inside the **`CustomerAddComponent`**. It will only display the CustomerAddComponet message, which means only CustomerAddComponent is rendered.

# [Detaching the change detection from a particular component](#toc.xhtml_s194a)

By making the application zoneless, we impacted the entire application. What if we want to do it for a particular component?

It can be done using the **`detach`** method of the **`ChangeDetectorRef`** class.

**Step 1 – Prepare the project**

Create a new project lab19-ng and then create the Customer, CustomerList, CustomerAdd, and Product components like the previous project.

**Step 2 – Detach the ChangeDetection**

Detach the change detection from the CustomerListComponent as follows:

1.  `export class CustomerListComponent implements OnInit {`
2.  `   constructor(private cd: ChangeDetectorRef) {}`
3.  `   ngOnInit(): void {`
4.  `     this.cd.detach();`
5.  `  }`
6.  `……`

**Step 3 – Test the application**

Execute the application in the production mode.

**Initial messages**

Detaching the change detector and the zoneless Angular are two different things.

The change detector is responsible for detecting changes in the Angular components, whereas the zoneless Angular is all about disabling the change detection’s auto-triggering.

![](images/Image58830.jpg)

**Figure 29.11**

This time we have detached the change detector itself. Hence, you will not get the CustomerListComponent message in the console when the application is loaded.

(I believe you know why the messages are displayed twice. We spoke about it several times.)

**Output after button click**

Now, click the buttons inside the component. No matter what button you click, the output will always be as follows:

![](images/Image58877.jpg)

**Figure 29.12**

You may be a little confused about one thing.

**Why are the other components rendering when the button inside the CustomerListComponent is clicked?**

Our application still has zone.js in place, and so after every event, the tick method of the ApplicationRef service gets called automatically. That will trigger the change detection for every component except CustomerListComponent (as the change detector is detached).

**Step 4 – Manually detect changes**

Manually detect the changes inside the **`HandleClick`** method of the CustomerListComponent as follows:

1.  `   HandleClick(): void {`
2.  `     this.cd.detectChanges();`
3.  `  }`

**Step 5 – Test the application**

After the preceding change, the button click event inside the CutomerList component behaves a little differently. Upon clicking, you will see the CustomerListComponent message inside the console along with the others.

# [OnPush change detection strategy](#toc.xhtml_s195a)

There is one more way to control the change detection at the component level. That is, by changing the change detection strategy. The default strategy makes the change detection run all the time.

By setting it to OnPush, we limit it to only the following specific cases:

*   When the event is triggered within the component.
*   When the reference of the input property of a component is changed.
*   When the observable consumed using the async pipe emits a new value.

**Step 1 – Set OnPush as ChangeDetectionStrategy**

Open CustomerAddComponent and set it’s change detection to OnPush as follows:

1.  `@Component({`
2.  `   selector: ‘app-customer-add’,`
3.  `   templateUrl: ‘./customer-add.component.html’,`
4.  `   styleUrls: [‘./customer-add.component.css’],`
5.  `   changeDetection:ChangeDetectionStrategy.OnPush`
6.  `})`
7.  `export class CustomerAddComponent {`

**Step 2 – Execute the application**

You will get the following output:

**Initial execution**

![](images/Image58961.jpg)

**Figure 29.13**

The change detection was initially fired for the application by Angular.

In one of the earlier steps, we have detached the change detector from the **`CustomerListComponent`**.

Hence, we get a message from every component except the **`CustomerListComponent`**.

After that, **`zone.js`** makes the change detection execute again. This time, you will also find the **`CustomerAddComponent`** message missing as it has the OnPush strategy.

**Button click – All except CustomerAddComponent**

Click all the buttons except the **`CustomerAddComponent`** button. You will get the following output:

![](images/Image58996.jpg)

**Figure 29.14**

As you can see, no CustomerList and CustomerAddComponents are displayed as we have detached the change detector from the one, and the other one has the OnPush as the default strategy.

**`CustomerAddComponent`** **button click**

You will get the following output:

![](images/Image59062.jpg)

**Figure 29.15**

After the internal event from the CustomerAddComponent, we got the **`CustomerAddComponent`** message.

That proves the first point of OnPush: the change detection is fired for a component when the event is fired within the component.

**Step 3 – Create a class to hold data**

Create a simple class **`CustomerAddData`** as follows:

1.  `export class CustomerAddData{`
2.  `   CustomerName: string;`
3.  `}`

**Step 4 – Create input property**

Next, create an input property inside **`CustomerAddComponent`** as follows:

1.  `export class CustomerAddComponent {`
2.  `   @Input() cData: CustomerAddData;`
3.  `……`

**Step 5 – Change CustomerComponent**

Then, create an instance of **`CustomerAddData`** in the **`CustomerComponent`** and set it to the input property of **`CustomerAddComponent`** as follows:

1.  `export class CustomerComponent  {`
2.  `   CustomerData: CustomerAddData={CustomerName:”Sukesh”};`
3.  `……`

1.  *`<!-- customer.component.html -->`*
2.  `<div>`
3.  `   {{GetMessage()}}`
4.  `   <input type=”button” value=”CustomerButton” (click)=”HandleClick()”>`
5.  `   <app-customer-list></app-customer-list>`
6.  `   <app-customer-add [cData]=”CustomerData”></app-customer-add>`
7.  `</div>`

**Step 6 – Redefine HandleClick method**

Redefine the **`HandleClick`** method in the CustomerComponent as follows:

1.  `HandleClick(): void  {`
2.  `     this.CustomerData.CustomerName=”ABCD”;`
3.  `  }`

**Step 7 – Test the application**

If you expect to see the CustomerAddComponent re-rendered again after the button in the CustomerComponent is clicked, then that means you haven’t read the second point of the change detection properly. It says, the change detection will be fired when the reference of a component’s input property is changed, and the current code is not doing that. It is only mutating the object.

**Step 8 – Change the reference**

Redefine the **`HandleClick`** method in the CustomerComponent as follows:

1.  `   HandleClick(): void  {`
2.  `     this.CustomerData.CustomerName=”ABCD”;`
3.  `  }`

**Step 9 – Test the output**

This time, you will find the CustomerAddComponent message get displayed after the button in the CustomerComponent is clicked.

**Step 10 – Revise your skills**

Proving the third point of change detection will be an assignment for you.

We have done so many labs so far. I think, now you are smart enough to get that on your own.

*   Create a service.
*   Expose a Subject from it.
*   Subscribe to that Subject in CustomerAddComponent using the async pipe.
*   Make that Subject emit new data in some other component.

# [Summary](#toc.xhtml_s196a)

In this chapter, we spoke about the change detector in detail. Not everything in this chapter is expected to be remembered by you. Just remember that making the Angular application *zoneless* and *OnPush*, the change detection can improve the application performance significantly. That concludes our journey to Station 18\. Before we start our journey to Station 19, let’s do a quick revision of what we have learned in this chapter:

*   We spoke about the change detection, which runs over the bindings, evaluates the expressions, compares them to the earlier values, and updates the DOM if necessary.
*   We learned that, by default, during every change detection cycle, every component in the hierarchy is checked for the changes and re-rendered if needed.
*   We learned that Angular does the change detection the first time after the application is initialized. After that, every time a DOM event is triggered or an HTTP request is completed, or a macro task such as **`setTimeout`** and **`setInterval`** finishes the callback method, or microtasks such as *Promise* is completed, the change detection will be triggered.
*   We learned that Angular has monkey patched all the low-level JavaScript APIS and explicitly performed the change detection by the end of the APIs. For the monkey patching, Angular has used the **`zone.js`** library.
*   We learned that the change detection is executed by default twice in the development mode.
*   We learned that in the development mode, Angular does the change detection twice. During the first run, DOM is rendered with the latest component state, and in the second run, it confirms that DOM is not changed after the previous re-rendering. If it did, we will get a run-time error – **`ExpressionChangedAfterItHasBeenChecked`**.
*   We learned that the change detection is executed twice during the application initialization time. It creates the following possibilities:
    *   Development mode + application initialization time = change detection will be four times.
    *   Development mode + other change detection triggers (such as event handler, macro tasks, etc) = change detection will be two times.
    *   Production mode + application initialization time = change detection will be two times.
    *   Production mode + other change detection triggers (such as event handler, macro tasks, etc) = change detection will execute only once.
*   We made it clear that **`zone.js`** has nothing to do with how the change detection is performed, or with the internal logic of change detection. Its core purpose is monkey patching the low-level APIs so that the change detection is auto-triggered when needed.
*   We learned that we could disconnect **`zone.js`** from the Angular application, and after that, there will be no automatic change detection trigger.
*   We learned that the Angular application must need a zone to execute the application, and so removing **`zone.js`** will break the application. We demonstrated **`NoopNgZone`** – it is a zone object provided by Angular that won’t monkey patch the low-level APIs, unlike **`zone.js`**.
*   We learned about the **`ApplicationRef`** service that gives us the **`tick`** method using which we could trigger the change detection explicitly in the application.
*   We discussed polyfills – the libraries that add some additional features to the existing JavaScript and make the incompatible browsers compatible.
*   We spoke about the **`pollyfill.ts`** file where we will import the polyfills that we expect the application to have. By default, it will contain only an entry statement when the application is created, for **`zone.js`***.*
*   We spoke about the **`ChangeDetectorRef`** service that lets us trigger the change detection for the individual component.
*   We learned that we could detach the change detector from a particular component using the **`detach`** method of ChangeDetectorRef class.
*   We looked into the **`OnPush`** change detection strategy that limits the change detection to trigger only during some of the following instances:
    *   When the event is triggered within the component.
    *   When the reference of the input property of a component is changed.
    *   When the observable consumed using the async pipe emits a new value.

# [CHAPTER 30](#toc.xhtml_c30)

# [Angular Elements](#toc.xhtml_c30)

# [Introduction](#toc.xhtml_s197a)

In our journey to Station 19, we will discover one of the not-so-famous but useful features called the Angular Elements.

# [What are Angular Elements?](#toc.xhtml_s198a)

Did you ever think about using your Angular component in a non-Angular project?

If yes, then the Angular element is the way for it. The Angular Elements are the Angular Components, which can be packaged as the custom elements, aka the web components.

With this, you will get an opportunity to use your component anywhere, such as React, Vue, and so on.

# [Lab 20 – Creating Angular elements](#toc.xhtml_s199a)

**Step 1 – Create a new Angular project**

Create a new Angular project **`lab20-ng`**.

**Step 2 – Create a new component**

Create a new Angular component **`MyReusable`**.

1.  `export class MyReusableComponent {`
2.  `   Message = ‘Initial Message’;`
3.  `   constructor() {}`

5.  `   ChangeMessage(): void {`
6.  `     this.Message = ‘Changed Message’;`
7.  `  }`
8.  `}`

1.  `<h2>Message is : {{Message}}</h2>`
2.  `<input type=”button” value=”Change Message” (click)=”ChangeMessage()”>`

**Step 3 – Use component**

Use the preceding component inside **`AppComponent`**.

1.  *`<!-- app.component.html -->`*
2.  `<app-my-reusable></app-my-reusable>`

**Step 4 – Test the application**

Execute the application using the **`ng serve`** command and make sure that everything is working as expected.

**Initial output**

![](images/Image59143.jpg)

**Figure 30.1**

**After button click**

![](images/Image59180.jpg)

**Figure 30.2**

**Step 5 – Configure the project for the Angular elements**

As you know, this project aims to create an Angular element.

To do that, we need a library called **`document-register-element`**.

It can be downloaded using the **`npm install`** command and then included in the app by importing it in the **`polyfill.ts`** file.

There is another simplified way to do it. We can do it using the **`ng add @angular/elements`** command as follows:

![](images/Image59223.jpg)

**Figure 30.3**

The **`ng add`** command is specialized to work with the third-party libraries. It downloads the libraries and updates the **`package.json`** and also does the other necessary configuration needed in the application required by the third-party library.

**Step 4 – Configure the Angular component to be the AngularModule**

For that, define a constructor in the **`AppModule`** as follows:

1.  `import {Injector, NgModule} from ‘@angular/core’;`
2.  `import {createCustomElement} from ‘@angular/elements’;`
3.  `import {BrowserModule} from ‘@angular/platform-browser’;`
4.  `import {AppComponent} from ‘./app.component’;`
5.  `import {MyReusableComponent} from ‘./my-reusable/my-reusable.component’;`
6.  `export class AppModule {`
7.  `   constructor(private injector: Injector) {`
8.  `     const custom = createCustomElement(MyReusableComponent, {`
9.  `       injector: this.injector,`
10.  `    });`
11.  `     customElements.define(my-custom-angular-element’, custom);`
12.  `  }`
13.  `}`

In the preceding code, we have injected the root module injector instance in the **`AppModule`** and then configured **`MyReusableComponent`** as an Angular element.

**Step 5 – Build the project**

Build the project using the **`ng build`** command as usual, but this time with an additional flag.

![](images/Image59263.jpg)

**Figure 30.4**

Did you realize what --**`output-hashing noneflag`** did?

Do you remember having a hash appended to the name of every file generated after the build command? For instance - **`main.3a82886dd6c280a056fd.js.`**

That hash is not there anymore.

We don’t have any particular reason for removing the hash from here. It is to make the names easier.

**Step 6 – Sample HTML file for the project**

For testing the preceding Angular element, we don’t need an Angular project.

![](images/Image59272.jpg)

**Figure 30.5**

To make sure that you understand the concept correctly, create an independent folder called Test-Angular-Elements somewhere in the machine.

Then create a new HTML file inside that folder called **`index.html`** and put the following content inside it:

1.  `<!doctype html>`
2.  `<html lang=”en”>`
3.  `<head>`
4.  `     <title>Angular Elements Demo</title>`
5.  `</head>`
6.  `<body>`
7.  `     <my-custom-angular-element></my-custom-angular-element>`
8.  `     <script src=”../lab20-ng/dist/lab20-ng/runtime.js” ></script>`
9.  `     <script src=”../lab20-ng/dist/lab20-ng/polyfills.js” ></script>`
10.  `     <script src=”../lab20-ng/dist/lab20-ng/main.js” ></script>`
11.  `</body>`
12.  `</html>`

**Step 7 - Test the HTML**

Open the **`index.html`** file created in the preceding step in any browser of your choice.

**Before button click**

![](images/Image59356.jpg)

**Figure 30.6**

**After button click**

![](images/Image59390.jpg)

**Figure 30.7**

Although it is a happy output, there is a small bug in the same. Open the developer tools to check the same.

![](images/Image59398.jpg)

**Figure 30.8**

Remember, we still have **`AppComponent`** as the bootstrap component in **`AppModule`**.

**Step 8 – Remove the bootstrap component**

Our target was creating an Angular component. There is no point in having a root/bootstrap component here. So, remove the bootstrap section from the **`AppModule`** as follows:

1.  `@NgModule({`
2.  `   declarations: [AppComponent, MyReusableComponent],`
3.  `   imports: [BrowserModule],`
4.  `   providers: [],`
5.  `})`
6.  `export class AppModule {`

**Step 9 – Rebuild the application**

Again, build the application as before.

**Step 10 – Test the HTML**

Re-execute the **`index.html`** file. This time you will find the following error in the console:

![](images/Image59434.jpg)

**Figure 30.9**

Angular expects to have a bootstrap component specified in the root module unless the **`ngDoBoostrap`** method is defined in the **`AppModule`**.

**Step 11 – Define ngDoBoostrap method**

Define an empty **`ngDoBoostrap`** method in the AppModule as follows:

1.  `   ngDoBootstrap (){}`

**`ngDoBoostrap`** method is mainly used if we want to load the bootstrap component manually based on some condition. Let’s not worry about that now.

**Step 12 – Rebuild the application**

Once again, rebuild the application using the same flags.

**Step 13 – Test the HTML file**

Finally, you will get an error-free and working output.

# [Summary](#toc.xhtml_s200a)

That concludes our journey to Station 19\. Finally, in the next chapter, we will see the journey to our final Station – Station 20\. In that Station, we will discuss the pending topics. Before that, as usual, let’s revise our learnings of this chapter:

*   In this chapter, we learned to create the Angular Elements that are the Angular Components packaged as the custom elements, aka the web components.
*   We learned that the Angular element is the way to use the Angular element in the non-Angular project.
*   We saw a demonstration of the **`ng add`** command. This command is specialized to work with the third-party libraries. It downloads the libraries and updates the **`package.json`** and also does the other necessary configuration needed in the application required by the third-party library.
*   We covered **`--output-hashing none`** flag that can be used with the **`ng build`** command for instructing Angular CLI that hashing is not needed while naming bundle.
*   We learned that there is no point in having a root/bootstrap component in the Angular project intended for the Angular elements.
*   We learned that it is compulsory to specify a bootstrap component in every Angular project. That applies to the project targeting Angular elements. To solve this dilemma, we introduced the **`ngDoBootstrap`** method, which lets us bootstrap the components manually.

# [ViewChildren](#toc.xhtml_s202a)

Do you remember the **`ViewChild`** decorator used in one of our previous labs? It will let us get the reference of the template elements in the component class. Let’s do a quick revision on the same.

**Step 1 – Create a new project**

Create a new Angular project using the **`ng`** command.

**Step 2 – Change AppComponent content**

Change the **`AppComponent`** content to the following:

1.  `export class AppComponent {`
2.  `   @ViewChild(“MyTextbox”) element: HTMLInputElement;`
3.  `   ChangeValue(): void{`
4.  `     this.element.value=”My Value”;`
5.  `  }`
6.  `}`

1.  `<input type=”text” #MyTextbox>`
2.  `<input type=”button” value=”Change Value” (click)=”ChangeValue()”>`

**Step 3 – Execute the application**

Execute the application using the **`ng serve`** command. You will get the following output:

**Before button click**

![](images/Image59525.jpg)

**Figure 31.1**

**After button click**

![](images/Image59534.jpg)

**Figure 31.2**

**Step 4 – Change the AppComponent**

Now, change the **`AppComponent`** template to the following:

1.  `<input type=”text” #MyTextbox><br><br><input type=”text” #MyTextbox><br><br>`
2.  `<input type=”button” value=”Change Value” (click)=”ChangeValue()”>`

**Step 5 – Test the output**

Now check the output. It will be as follows:

But when you click the button, only the first textbox gets populated with a value.

![](images/Image59572.jpg)

**Figure 31.3**

What if you want to get the reference of all the template elements? The answer is the **`ViewChildren`** decorator.

**Step 6 – Change the AppComponent code**

Change the **`AppComponent`** code to the following:

1.  `  @ViewChildren(‘MyTextbox’) elements: QueryList<ElementRef>;`
2.  `   ChangeValue(): void {`
3.  `     this.elements.forEach((x) => {`
4.  `       x.nativeElement.value = ‘My Value’;`
5.  `    });`
6.  `  }`

**Step 7 – Re-check the output**

This time, you will find both the textboxes updated with the new value.

# [Content projection](#toc.xhtml_s203a)

Content projection is an Angular concept that lets us project the HTML contents from the parent component to the child component.

In the child component, those projected contents can be accessed using an inbuilt Angular component called **`ng-content`**. Let’s understand with a demo.

**Step 1 – Get the project**

Create a new project **`lab22-ng`**.

**Step 2 – Create a new Component**

Create a new Angular component called **`MyBoxedContentComponent`**.

**Step 3 – Use the component**

Next, use the **`MyBoxedContentComponent`** in the **`AppComponent`** as follows:

1.  `<app-my-boxed-content>`
2.  `   <header>My Header</header>`
3.  `   <section>My Content<br>My Content again</section>`
4.  `</app-my-boxed-content>`

You can see something interesting in the preceding code. You can find some custom HTML content mentioned between our **`MyBoxedContentComponent`** selector.

That is called content projection – the HTML content is projected to the child component from the parent component.

**Step 4 – Access the projected component**

Finally, let’s access the projected component in the child component.

Redefine the **`MyBoxedContentComponent`** template to the following:

1.  `<div style=”border: 1px solid black;padding: 20px;”>`
2.  `   <ng-content></ng-content>`
3.  `</div>`

**Step 5 – Execute the project**

Execute the application. You will get the following output:

![](images/Image59633.jpg)

**Figure 31.4**

# Discussion – Continued

It was a simple example of Content projection.

But there is more to it. We have something called the content projection slots – they help us project the HTML content to the specific *ng-content* tags in the child component.

In short, we can have multiple *ng-content* tags in the child component, each pointing to a specific content in the projected content.

# Lab – Continued

**Step 6 – Update MyBoxedContentComponent**

Change the content in the **`MyBoxedContentComponent`** to the following:

1.  `<div style=”border: 1px solid black;padding: 20px;”>`
2.  `   <h1>`
3.  `     <ng-content select=”header”></ng-content>`
4.  `   </h1>`
5.  `   <div style=”border:1px dotted ;padding: 10px;”>`
6.  `     <ng-content select=”section”></ng-content>`
7.  `   </div>`
8.  `</div>`

**Step 7 – Check the output**

You will get the following output:

![](images/Image59716.jpg)

**Figure 31.5**

As you can see, the projected content is distributed into the different slots.

Check the developer tools for more clarity.

![](images/Image59769.jpg)

**Figure 31.6**

# [Content child](#toc.xhtml_s204a)

**`ContentChild`** is a decorator that lets us reference the matching element projected into the component. Let’s understand with a demo. For the demo, we will reuse the previous project.

**Step 1-Change the AppComponent**

Change the **`AppComponent`** content as follows:

1.  `<app-my-boxed-content>`
2.  `   <header #MyHeader>My Header</header>`
3.  `   <section>My Content <br>My Content again</section>`
4.  `</app-my-boxed-content>`

**Step 2 – Change the Child component**

Change the **`MyBoxedContentComponent`** content to the following:

1.  `<div style=”border: 1px solid black;padding: 20px;”>`
2.  `   <h1>`
3.  `     <ng-content select=”header”></ng-content>`
4.  `   </h1>`
5.  `   <div style=”border:1px dotted ;padding: 10px;”>`
6.  `     <ng-content select=”section”></ng-content>`
7.  `   </div>`
8.  `   <input type=”button” value=”ChangeStyle” (click)=”ChangeStyle()”>`
9.  `</div>`

The only change is the addition of a new button in the template – ChangeStyle.

**Step 3 – Use ContentChild in MyBoxedContentComponent**

Declare a property of the type **`ElementRef`** in the **`MyBoxedContentComponent`**, attach the **`ContentChild`** decorator to it, inject the Renderer2 service in the constructor, and finally define the ChangeStyle method as follows:

1.  `export class MyBoxedContentComponent implements OnInit {`
2.  `   @ContentChild(‘MyHeader’) HeaderElement: ElementRef;`
3.  `   constructor(private renderer: Renderer2) {}`
4.  `   ChangeStyle(): void {`
5.  `     this.renderer.setStyle(`
6.  `       this.HeaderElement.nativeElement,`
7.  `       ‘font-style’,`
8.  `       ‘italic’`
9.  `    );`
10.  `  }`
11.  `}`

**Step 4 – Test the application. Let’s say we want**

Execute the application and then click on the button. You will find the italic style attached to the header element.
