**Figure 31.7**

# [ContentChildren](#toc.xhtml_s205a)

The difference between the ContentChild and the ContentChildren is the same as the difference between the ViewChild and the ViewChildren. One returns the reference of the first template element matching the selector, and the other returns the QueryList.

# [Angular Life cycle](#toc.xhtml_s206a)

The angular component life cycle can also be referred to as the life cycle hooks that the developers can tap and write some custom logic.

We have already seen most of them in action in the previous labs. Let’s quickly revise them.

*   **Constructor** (called only once)

    It is not an Angular life cycle event/hook. It can be seen in any object-oriented language, and TypeScript is not different. It gets invoked as soon as a class’s instance gets created.

*   **ngOnChanges**

    It will be executed in two situations – Once at the beginning, after the *constructor*, and then every time a component’s data-bound input property is changed. This will be the first method in the life-cycle, which will let us use the component’s data-bound input properties. Before this (that is, in the constructor), the data-bound input properties will be unassigned. We have seen it in action in *[Chapter 26: AngularDirectives](#c26.xhtml)*.

*   **ngOnInit** (called only once)

    It will be executed after the first **`ngOnChanges`** and preferred for the initialization logic. We have seen it in action in *[Chapter 26: AngularDirectives](#c26.xhtml)*.

*   **ngDoCheck** (called multiple times)

    It will be executed every time the Angular change detection runs. We have seen it in action in *[Chapter 26: AngularDirectives](#c26.xhtml)*.

*   **ngAfterContentInit**

    It will be executed after Angular projects the external content into the component’s view.

    This is the perfect method if we want to access the component’s projected content in the initialization phase. **`ngOnInit`** won’t work for this purpose. **`ngAfterContentInit`** method is a perfect candidate for dealing with the template elements project into the component by the parent.

*   **ngAfterContentChecked**

    It will be executed after Angular checks the content projected into the component. This is the change detection check for the contents projected.

*   **ngAfterViewInit**

    It will be executed after the view, that is, a template of a component is initialized.

    **`ngAfterViewInit`** method is a candidate for dealing with a template in the view. (Do you remember how we set the focus on the textbox on this method in one of the previous labs?)

*   **ngAfterViewChecked**

    **`ngAfterVIewChecked`** is called every time Angular has finished running the change detection on a component, and its children.

*   **ngDestroy**

    It is the best method for cleaning up the resources. It will be invoked when the component/directive instance is destroyed.

# [Deployment files](#toc.xhtml_s207a)

We did build our project many times in this book so far. Let’s talk about the files that get generated after the **`ng build`** command.

*   **main.***.js or main.js**

    It is the bundled JavaScript file containing our code. All our application code is bundled into this one JavaScript file.

    Angular won’t create this bundle blindly. It performs tree-shaking to ensure that all the unused components, directives, services, and pipes are skipped. (In case of lazy loading, you will also find the feature-specific bundles.)

*   **polyfills.***.js**

    It is a bundled JavaScript file containing all the libraries imported in the **`polyfills.ts`**. By default, it will only include the **`zone.js`**.

    (We spoke about it in *[Chapter 29: Change detection](#c29.xhtml)*.)

*   **runtime.***.js**

    It is the webpack loader. This file contains the webpack utilities that are needed to load the other files.

*   **style.***.css**

    It is a bundle of all the CSS files specified in the **`angular.json`** file. (We spoke about it in *[Chapter 28: Angular styles](#c28.xhtml)*.)

    You may find multiple CSS bundles based on the *angular.json* configuration.

*   **script.***.js**

    Just like the CSS files, we can import the JavaScript files into our application using the **`angular.json`** file.

    The JavaScript files mentioned in this section will be bundled into the **`script.***.js`**.

    ![](images/Image59826.jpg)

    **Figure 31.8**

*   **vendor.***.js**

    Angular and the other 3rd party vendor scripts are bundled into this file.

    You will find this file missing in the production mode as it is bundled into **`main.***.js`**.

    You can instruct Angular CLI to create a separate bundle as follows:

    1.  `ng build --prod --vendor-chunk true`
*   *****.map.js**

    You will find that multiple files get generated in the development build with an extension as **`map.js`**.

    These files contain the information about the TypeScript source files, which enable direct debugging of the Typescript files at run-time.

# [More about Angular injectors](#toc.xhtml_s208a)

In our book, we spoke about a couple of ways to configure the injectors responsible for creating and injecting a service. Let’s revise it quickly.

![](images/Image59873.jpg)

**Figure 31.9**

I hope this diagram is self-explanatory. But there are two more ways to configure the Angular injectors.

# [Platform Injector and Null Injector](#toc.xhtml_s209a)

Previously, we learned that there are two kinds of injectors in Angular.

We have a *module injector* hierarchy at the top and an *element injector* below it.

There are two more above the *root module injector* – *platform injector* and *null injector*.

**Platform Injector**

The *platform injector* is positioned below the *null injector* and located above the *root module injector*.

I am sure that you are wondering why it exists.

**Can we have more than one root module injector in an Angular application**

No, we cannot. We will have only one root module injector, and that will be created when the application is initialized.

There is a big twist here. One application can have only one root module injector, but one page can contain more than one Angular application, and every application will have a root module injector of its own.

All those root injectors will be the child to the platform injector.

**Multiple Angular applications in real-time**

Do you remember the previous chapter on Angular Element? Every Angular Element is a self-contained component, and that acts like a mini-Angular project.

**How to configure platform injectors?**

For configuring platform injectors, we can use the Injectable decorator with the **`providedIn`** platform option.

1.  `@Injectable({providedIn: ‘platform’})`
2.  `export class MyService{…..`

**What is so special about the platform injector?**

*   When the service is expected to be injected, Angular will first ask the component’s *element injector*. If it fails, then the responsibility will be given to the *element injector* of the component located above it. It continues until the *root component injector* is reached.
*   After the *root component injector*, the responsibility will be delegated to the immediate *module injector*. If it fails, then it will be the turn for the *module injector* above it. That continues until the *root module injector* is reached.
*   After the root *module injector*, the responsibility will be delegated to the *platform injector*.

The platform injector creates the one global instance of the service that will be singleton at the platform level and is shared between all the applications on a page.

**Null injector**

The null injector is at the top of the tree, responsible for the “*No provider*” error. When the platform injector fails to do the injection, the Null injector comes to the picture, and it will simply throw the “*No provider error*.”

# [ProvidedIn – any](#toc.xhtml_s210a)

Using the **`providedIn`** any option of the **`Injectable`** decorator, we can configure the root module injector and every child module injector at once. It ensures that each lazy-loaded module gets its unique instances.

1.  `@Injectable({providedIn: ‘any’})`
2.  `export class MyService{…..`

For instance, let say we have an Angular application with the following Angular modules in our project – AppModule (Root Module), CustomerModule, ProductModule, SupplierModule, LoggerModule, and SecurityModule.

Assume that the application is configured as follows:

*   AppModule is the root module that will lazy-load the Customer, Product, and SupplierModule.
*   AppModule loads the LoggerModule eagerly. (Eager loading means, LoggerModule is imported in the AppModule.)
*   Finally, the SecurityModule is eager-loaded in the CustomerModule.

Considering the preceding facts, and considering that we have attached an **`Injectable`** decorator with **`providedIn`**: ‘any’, we will get the following output:

*   AppModule and LoggerModule will share a single instance across both of them.
*   A separate instance of the service will be created for ProductModule.
*   A separate instance of the service will be created for SupplierModule.
*   A separate instance of the service will be created for CustomerModule, and the same will be shared with SecurityModule.

# [Summary](#toc.xhtml_s211a)

With this, we have reached Station 20, and that concludes our final journey. Before the final note, let’s revise the learnings in this chapter:

*   We did a demo of the **`ViewChild`** decorator again. Using **`ViewChild`**, we get the reference of the element defined in the view.
*   We learned that the **`ViewChild`** decorator is only capable of providing the reference of the first matching element, whereas, using the **`ViewChildren`** decorator, we will be able to get the references for all the matching elements in the view.
*   We learned content projection in Angular that lets us project the HTML contents from the parent component to the child component. In the child component, that project content will be accessed using the **`ng-content`** tag.
*   We learned about the content projection slots that help us project the HTML content to the specific **`ng-content`** tags in the child component.
*   We learned about the **`ContentChild`** decorator that lets us reference the first matching element projected into the component.
*   We learned about the **`ContentChildren`** decorator that lets us reference all the matching elements projected into the component.
*   We revised all the Angular life cycle events again in this chapter.
*   We discussed that all the files get generated by the **`ng build`** command.
*   We revised the module and element injectors again in this chapter. We revised all the different ways of configuring the injectors and their use cases.
*   We spoke about *the platform injector* that creates the one global instance of the service that will be singleton at the platform level and is shared between all the applications on a page.
*   We learned that by using the *Injectable* decorator’s *providedIn platform* option, we configure the platform injector.
*   We spoke about *the null injector* at the top-most level, which is responsible for the “no provider” error.
*   We learned that by using the *providedIn any* option of the *Injectable* decorator, we can configure the root module injector and every child module injector at once. With this setting, all the eagerly loaded modules share one instance; however, each lazy-loaded module gets its unique instances.

# [Section 6](#toc.xhtml_c31b)

# [Summary](#toc.xhtml_c31b)

After this final section 6, with a total of 10 chapters, you became an Angular developer, and that was the end of our journey.

**Chapter 22 – The component-oriented style in action**

*   We discussed a new UI development approach in the modern world – the component-oriented style. It is a UI designing approach that will let us create our UI as a composition of multiple smaller and reusable components.
*   We learned that Angular is not the pioneer of the component-oriented approach. It is just following it. The other technologies, like react and polymer, follow a similar process.
*   We learned that this approach makes our **code maintenance easy** and **lets multiple UI developers work in parallel**.
*   We spoke about the feature-components that represent the main components in the application and are mostly accessed via routing.
*   We learned that sometimes it is considered to break down a feature component into smaller components to make the code readable and manageable. The container-presentation pattern is one of the commonly followed in the industry for this purpose.
*   We had a quick discussion on the difference between the container and the presentation components. The container component is usually the smart one, whereas the presentation components are termed as the dumb components and they deal with the UI presentation.
*   We created the custom input property for passing the data from the outer component to the inner component.
*   We created the custom output property for raising even from the inner component that can be handled in the outer component.
*   We revisited the **`Viewchild`** decorator and the *template reference variable* and learned to reference the component class’s view element.
*   We learned about using the *environment* again in this chapter. We made our error handler code behave differently for the different environments (dev and production).
*   We revisited **`ngOnInit`** and learned how it is best suited for the initialization logic.
*   We learned about the **`ngAfterViewInit`** life cycle, a perfect candidate for the logic that needs access over the rendered controls.
*   We learned that we are not required to explicitly assign the *ngForm* directive to the **`form`** tag in the *template-driven forms*.

**Chapter 23 – Angular Routing**

*   We learned how we could use the template property instead of the templateUrl when the component HTML is simple.
*   We revisited the router-outlet component that displays different components for different URLs.
*   We spoke about the **`routerLink`** directive that lets us navigate between the routes without a full-page refresh.
*   We learned about the **`routerLinkActive`** that lets us add the CSS class if a route is active.
*   We spoke about the default routes that will get loaded if the URL is empty.
*   We learned that we could define the default routes at both the root level and the child level.
*   We did a detailed study about lazy loading and learned how it reduces the initial load time.
*   We spoke about the pre-loading strategies that let us decide whether a lazy-loaded module needs to be loaded asynchronously or not.

**Chapter 24 – Angular Modules**

*   We learned to create the components and the modules using Angular CLI.
*   We learned how the Angular modules make the component development easier. We grouped related components in the modules and then exported only a few of them in public. The non-exported components in an Angular module becomes private to that module.
*   We learned that the Angular modules also group the directives and pipes that become private to that module unless they are explicitly exported.
*   We learned about ServiceModules that are the Angular modules used specifically for configuring the injectors for the services and the dependent services. HttpClientModule is an example of such ServiceModule.
*   We already discussed the third advantage of the Angular modules in the previous chapter – lazy loading.
*   We learned that even the private components of an Angular module could be accessed via routing.
*   We learned that BrowserModule imports CommonModule and re-exports it. Hence, the directives exported by CommonModule will be visible inside AppModule.
*   We learned that the tree-shaking mechanism in the Angular build process makes sure that all the unused components are removed from the final build unless they are referred via routing. The component will be considered unused if it is not used in any other component’s template.
*   We learned that Angular creates a child module injector when a module is lazy-loaded.

**Chapter 25 – Angular Pipes**

*   In this chapter, we learned about the pipes that help us encapsulate the transformation logic in the application.
*   We learned that we need to create a class for creating the pipes, implement it from the **`PipeTransform`** interface, and then attach the *Pipe* decorator.
*   We learned that the pipes are also encapsulated inside the Angular modules, just like the components.
*   We learned that the commonly used pipes in the Angular application such as *json*, *async*, *uppercase*, and so on, are defined inside **`CommonModule`**.
*   We learned that the custom Angular modules (commonly referred to as SharedModule) are created to encapsulate all the custom pipes, and the other modules will import those modules in the real-time projects.
*   We spoke about the difference between the two data types in TypeScript – *any* and *unknown*. We learned that the variables of the type *any* can be assigned to the other types, whereas the variables of the type *unknown* can be set to only the variables of the type *unknown*.
*   We saw the demonstration of the TypeScript REST parameter.
*   We learned that the pure pipes execute only at the beginning and only when the input value gets changed.
*   We learned that the impure pipes execute after every change detection.

**Chapter 26 – Angular directives**

*   We learned about the directives in this chapter. They extend the existing HTML.
*   We learned that there are three kinds of directives that Angular has.
*   The first one, referred to as the Element directives, lets us create the new HTML tags. The element directives can also be termed as Components in Angular.
*   The second one is the Attribute directives that lets us extend the behavior of the existing HTML elements. **`ngClass`** and **`ngStyle`** are typical examples of such directives.
*   The third one is the structural directives that lets us change the structure of the existing HTML elements. The structural directives, similar to the Attribute directives, will be attached to an HTML element as an attribute.
*   We learned that usually, the structural directives would be attached to the HTML element with the prefix asterisk (***). For example - **`*ngFor`**, **`*ngIf.`**
*   We learned about the different ways to specify the selector to a directive – Attribute selector, Id selector, CSS selector, and Element selector.
*   We saw a demonstration of the **`ElementRef`** service in Angular that will reference the HTML element to which the directive is attached.
*   We learned about the *Render2* service in Angular that will provide the various methods to deal with the DOM manipulation.
*   We once again understood how the **`ngOnInit`** method is preferred for the initialization logic. We learned that a *constructor* would be executed when a class’s object is created, whereas **`ngOnInit`** is executed once the component/directive is initialized. Here, Initialized means, the instance is created, and all the properties are initialized with the values passed by the parent component.
*   We learned about a parameterized Input decorator that lets us expose the component/directive property to the outside world with a different name.
*   We learned about the *HostListner* decorator that lets us handle the element’s events to which the directive is attached.
*   We learned about the **`ngOnChanges`** life cycle event of Angular that executes whenever the value of a component’s data-bound property is changed.
*   We learned about the **`ngDoCheck`** life cycle event of Angular that executes on every change detection.
*   We spoke about the **`ng-template`** that is used to create the Angular templates. The Angular templates won’t get rendered automatically. They will be used and rendered inside some other DOM elements at run-time with some custom logic.
*   We learned about the **`TemplateRef`** service that gives us the reference of the contents defined inside the **`ng-template`**.
*   We saw the **`ViewContainerRef`** service in action, which gave us the reference of the container to which one or more views can be attached.
*   We learned about the **`ComponentFactoryResolver`** service that can be used to load the components at run-time dynamically.
*   We spoke about the micro-syntax with the directives that removes the overhead of explicitly creating the **`ng-template`** element.

**Chapter 27 – Angular Forms**

It was an assignment chapter. In this chapter, you were expected to do a sample demo in both the template and the reactive approach.

**Chapter 28 – Angular Styles**

*   We spoke about the three ways of including the external stylesheets in an Angular application – using the *link* tag in the **`index.html`** file, using *CSS @import*, and using the **`angular.json`** file.
*   We learned that the **`angular.json`** file has a section called **`builds/styles`** that is used to declare the external CSS files in the project. By default, the Angular boilerplate project comes with an entry for the **`style.css`** file.
*   We saw the *CSS @import’s* demonstration and learned that on **`ng build`**, the Angular CLI bundles **`style.css`** and every stylesheet file it is dependent on, and creates one bundle.
*   We saw a demonstration of importing the stylesheet files using the **`angular.json`** file and learned that all the stylesheet files mentioned in the **`angular.json`** file are bundled into one file on **`ng build`**.
*   We learned that importing the stylesheet files using the **`angular.json`** instead of **`CSS @import`** is considered a better approach. It gives us a way to instruct Angular CLI to generate multiple bundles if needed.
*   We learned to attach the styles to the individual components using the **`Component`** decorator’s *styleURL* or *styles* attribute. We learned that these styles wouldn’t get bundled inside any CSS bundle after the build; instead, they become part of the component’s view itself.
*   We learned about **`ViewEncapsulation`** that let us control how the styles will be encapsulated into the component view.
*   We spoke about the *Emulated* encapsulation that is the default mode. This mode attaches a unique attribute to each DOM element of the component and renames each CSS selector so that the style becomes specific to only that component.
*   We spoke about the *Native* encapsulation that follows the *ShadowDOM* approach of HTML 5. With this mode, we assure that no external CSS affects the component’s contents, and the component-specific-style won’t affect the outer world. It is the best approach for the reusable components.
*   We spoke about the *None* encapsulation that makes every component-specific style a global style.

**Chapter 29 – Change detection**

*   We spoke about the change detection, which runs over the bindings, evaluates the expressions, compares them to the earlier values, and updates the DOM if necessary.
*   We learned that by default, during every change detection cycle, every component in the hierarchy is checked for the changes and re-rendered if needed.
*   We learned that Angular does the change detection the first time after the application is initialized. After that, every time a DOM event is triggered or an HTTP request is completed, or a macro task such as **`setTimeout`** and **`setInterval`** finishes the callback method, or microtasks such as *Promise* is completed, the change detection will be triggered.
*   We learned that Angular has monkey patched all the low-level JavaScript APIS and explicitly performed the change detection by the end of APIs. For the monkey patching, Angular has used **`zone.js`** library.
*   We learned that the change detection is executed by default twice in the development mode.
*   We learned that in the development mode, Angular does the change detection twice. During the first run, DOM is rendered with the latest component state, and in the second run, it confirms that DOM is not changed after the previous re-rendering. If it did, we will get a run-time error – **`ExpressionChangedAfterItHasBeenChecked`**.
*   We learned that the change detection is executed twice during the application initialization time. It creates the following possibilities:
    *   Development mode + application initialization time = change detection will be four times.
    *   Development mode + other change detection triggers (such as event handler, macro tasks, etc) = change detection will be two times.
    *   Production mode + application initialization time = change detection will be two times.
    *   Production mode + other change detection triggers (such as the event handler, macro tasks, and so on) = change detection will execute only once.
*   We made it clear that **`zone.js`** has nothing to do with how the change detection is performed, or with the internal logic of the change detection. Its core purpose is monkey patching the low-level APIs so that the change detection is auto-triggered when needed.
*   We learned that we could disconnect **`zone.js`** from the Angular application, and after that, there will be no automatic change detection trigger.
*   We learned that the Angular application must need a zone to execute the application, and so removing **`zone.js`** will break the application. We demonstrated **`NoopNgZone`** – it is a zone object provided by Angular that won’t monkey patch the low-level APIs, unlike **`zone.js`**.
*   We learned about the **`ApplicationRef`** service that gives us the *tick* method using which we could trigger the change detection explicitly in the application.
*   We discussed polyfills – the libraries that add some additional features to the existing JavaScript and make the incompatible browsers compatible.
*   We spoke about the **`pollyfill.ts`** file where we will import the polyfills that we expect the application to have. By default, it will contain only an entry statement when the application is created, for **`zone.js`***.*
*   We spoke about the **`ChangeDetectorRef`** service that lets us trigger the change detection for the individual component.
*   We learned that we could detach the change detector from a particular component using the **`detach`** method of the ChangeDetectorRef class.
*   We looked into the **`OnPush`** change detection strategy that limits the change detection to trigger only during some following instances:
    *   When the event is triggered within the component.
    *   When the reference of the input property of a component is changed.
    *   When the observable consumed using the async pipe emits a new value.

**Chapter 30 – Angular Elements**

*   In this chapter, we learned to create the Angular Elements that are the Angular Components packaged as the custom elements, aka the web components.
*   We learned that the Angular element is the way to use the Angular element in the non-Angular project.
*   We saw a demonstration of the **`ng add`** command. This command is specialized to work with the third-party libraries. It downloads the libraries and updates the **`package.json`** and also does the other necessary configuration needed in the application required by the third-party library.
*   We covered the **`--output-hashing none`** flag that can be used with the **`ng build`** command for instructing Angular CLI that the hashing is not needed while naming the bundle.
*   We learned that there is no point in having a root/bootstrap component in the Angular project intended for the Angular elements.
*   We learned that it is compulsory to specify a bootstrap component in every Angular project. That applies to the project targeting the Angular elements. To solve this dilemma, we introduced the **`ngDoBootstrap`** method, which lets us bootstrap the components manually.

**Chapter 31 – Leftovers**

*   We did a demo of the **`ViewChild`** decorator again. Using **`ViewChild`**, we get the reference of the element defined in the view.
*   We learned that the **`ViewChild`** decorator is only capable of providing the reference of the first matching element, whereas, using the **`ViewChildren`** decorator, we will be able to get the references for all the matching elements in the view.
*   We learned content projection in Angular that lets us project the HTML contents from the parent component to the child component. In the child component, that project content will be accessed using the **`ng-content`** tag.
*   We learned about the content projection slots that help us project the HTML content to the specific **`ng-content`** tags in the child component.
*   We learned about the **`ContentChild`** decorator that lets us reference the first matching element projected into the component.
*   We learned about the **`ContentChildren`** decorator that lets us reference all the matching elements projected into the component.
*   We revised all the Angular life cycle events again in this chapter.
*   We discussed all the files that get generated by the **`ng build`** command.
*   We revised the module and the element injectors again in this chapter. We revised all the different ways of configuring the injectors and their use cases.
*   We spoke about *the platform injector* that creates the one global instance of the service that will be singleton at the platform level and is shared between all the applications on a page.
*   We learned that by using the *Injectable* decorator’s *providedIn platform* option, we configure the platform injector.
*   We spoke about *the null injector* at the top-most level which is responsible for the “*no provider*” error.
*   We learned that using the *providedIn any* option of the *Injectable* decorator, we can configure the root module injector and every child module injector at once. With this setting, all the eagerly loaded modules share one instance; however, each lazy-loaded module gets its unique instances.

# [It is not a goodbye](#toc.xhtml_bm)

I am glad that you have reached so far. I hope you have enjoyed this long journey, and I hope your skills have now reached the next level.

*Learning* and *playing games* are two of the hobbies from a long list of hobbies that I have.

An extensive experience in these two hobbies taught me that both the things need repetition to reach mastery.

If you are a gamer, you know this very well too. If you want to get through any game, play it twice. The first time, finish the game, and the second time, reveal all the secrets.

The same principle applies to learning. You may plan to reread this book and revisit everything you have learned so far. Or at least, you can plan to review the summary of every section.

With that, I want to thank you, as well as congratulate you for completing this journey.

Though, let me assure you that this is not over. Learning never ends, and so does the Angular. For now, I want to say, “*stay tuned, this is not the only Angular book written by me.*”

I hope you have enjoyed this entire journey. You can connect with me via my Twitter handle, @sukeshmarla, or my email id, [SukeshMarla@Gmail.com](mailto:SukeshMarla@Gmail.com). Bye for now, but it is not a goodbye!!!!
